# Chapter 9: The Magic Seat and Cargo Tricks

## What you will learn
- What the Magic Seat is and why the Fit's cargo space is unusual for such a small car
- How to fold the rear seat flat for cargo (Utility Mode)
- How to flip the rear seat cushions up for tall items (Tall Mode)
- How to make one long flat surface for lumber or surfboards (Long Mode)
- How to recline the front seats flat to rest (Refresh Mode)

## The short version
The Magic Seat is Honda's name for the Fit's 60/40-split rear seat, split so each side folds on its own <!-- F-WP05-028 -->. It can fold four different ways beyond normal seating, called Utility, Tall, Long, and Refresh mode <!-- F-WP05-028 -->. Each mode takes a few minutes and no tools, but each one also has real safety rules you must follow before you start.

## The full explanation
Honda built the Magic Seat as a 60/40-split rear seat, meaning the two sides split unevenly <!-- F-WP05-028 -->. This lets you fold one side and leave a passenger seated on the other <!-- F-WP05-028 -->. Honda describes five total seating and cargo setups: the standard five-passenger mode, plus refresh mode, tall object mode, long object mode, and utility mode <!-- F-WP05-028 -->. This chapter covers the four non-standard modes as step-by-step procedures.

With every seat up in standard mode, the Fit holds 90.1 cubic feet of passenger space and 21.3 cubic feet of cargo space behind the back seat <!-- F-WP05-029 -->. Fold the rear seat-backs down flat for Utility Mode, and cargo space grows to 56.8 cubic feet <!-- F-WP05-030 -->.

Two basic seat motions make up all four modes. First, a rear seat cushion can fold up and lock against the seat-back, which is the base move behind Tall Mode <!-- F-WP05-031 -->. Second, a rear seat-back can fold down flat, which is the base move behind Utility Mode and part of Long Mode <!-- F-WP05-032 -->. Long Mode and Refresh Mode add more steps on top of these two basic motions.

## Step by step

### Utility Mode: fold the rear seat down flat for cargo
Utility Mode gives you a flat load floor from the tailgate forward, for boxes, groceries, or bikes <!-- F-WP05-032 -->.

**Safety warnings, before you start:**
- Always remove any items from the rear seat before you fold it.
- Always make sure nothing sits on the floor before you fold the seat-back down.
- Always keep cargo below the bottom of the windows, so it does not block the side curtain airbags (airbags built into the roof rail above the windows).
- Always tie down loose cargo so it cannot fly forward during hard braking.
- Do not put heavy items directly on a folded seat-back.
- Do not let a passenger sit on a rear seat you have folded down; return each side fully upright, locked, and belted first.

**Steps:**
1. Decide which side of the 60/40 rear seat you are folding: driver's side, passenger's side, or both <!-- F-WP05-032 -->.
2. Remove everything from that rear seat.
3. Move the matching front seat as far forward as it will go <!-- F-WP05-032 -->.
4. Lock the rear outer head restraint (the padded support behind your head) on that side in its lowest position <!-- F-WP05-032 -->.
5. If you are folding the driver's side, also set the rear-center head restraint to its lowest position <!-- F-WP05-032 -->.
6. If you are folding the driver's side, pull the latch plate to release the center seat belt from its anchor, and let the belt retract into the ceiling holder <!-- F-WP05-032 -->. Store the buckle there too <!-- F-WP05-032 -->.
7. Pull up the release lever on the outside of that seat-back <!-- F-WP05-032 -->.
8. Fold the seat-back forward until it lies flat <!-- F-WP05-032 -->.
9. Repeat steps 2 through 8 on the other side if you need the full-width flat floor.
10. Load your cargo, keeping it below window height and tied down.

**How you know it worked:** The seat-back lies flat and level, forming a flat load floor from the tailgate forward. It does not spring back up when you let go.

### Tall Mode: flip the rear cushions up for tall items
Tall Mode opens floor space in front of the seat-back, for items too tall to fit under a normal seat <!-- F-WP05-031 -->.

**Safety warnings, before you start:**
- Always remove any items from the rear seat before you fold the cushion up.
- Always make sure the cushion locks firmly against the seat-back before you load anything against it.
- Do not stack items higher than the top of the seat-backs; they can block your rear view and be thrown around the car in a crash.
- Do not place cargo where it blocks your view through the rear window.

**Steps:**
1. Remove everything from the rear seat cushion you are flipping up.
2. Pull up on the rear seat cushion <!-- F-WP05-031 -->.
3. Fold the seat leg down as the cushion comes up <!-- F-WP05-031 -->.
4. Push the seat cushion firmly against the seat-back until it locks in place <!-- F-WP05-031 -->.
5. Repeat for the other side if you want both cushions up.
6. Load your tall item into the open floor space in front of the seat-back.
7. To return to normal seating later, make sure the floor under the seat is empty first <!-- F-WP05-031 -->.
8. Pull the seat leg up fully <!-- F-WP05-031 -->.
9. Slowly push the seat cushion back down while holding it with both hands <!-- F-WP05-031 -->.
10. Set the seat leg into its floor guide; a latch appears when it seats correctly <!-- F-WP05-031 -->.

**How you know it worked:** The seat cushion stays locked upright against the seat-back on its own. It does not fall back down when you let go.

### Long Mode: carry long items like lumber or surfboards
Owners report that Long Mode combines the Tall and Utility motions to create one flat path from the tailgate to the dashboard <!-- F-WP05-034 -->. We could not confirm an official Honda step list for this exact combined mode on the 2008 GD3; check your owner's manual before loading anything long or heavy <!-- F-WP05-034 -->.

**Safety warnings, before you start:**
- Always keep at least one working seat belt and seating position free for every person riding in the car. Do not use more seats than are actually available once you reconfigure them.
- Always tie down long cargo. An unsecured long item can fly forward with great force during hard braking, which can seriously injure or kill someone in the front seat.
- Do not let cargo extend into the space where an airbag needs to open.
- Do not drive with cargo blocking your view through the rear window or a side mirror.
- We could not fully confirm these exact steps against the 2008 GD3 owner's manual. Follow your own owner's manual's cargo-loading warnings if they differ from this book <!-- F-WP05-034 -->.

**Steps (community-reported):**
1. Fold the passenger-side rear seat cushion up, as in Tall Mode steps 1 through 4, or fold that seat-back down flat, as in Utility Mode <!-- F-WP05-034 -->. Pick whichever gives you the flattest path to your item <!-- F-WP05-034 -->.
2. Move the front passenger seat as far forward as it will go <!-- F-WP05-034 -->.
3. Recline the front passenger seat-back forward until it rests against the folded rear seat, forming one flat surface from the tailgate to the dashboard on that side <!-- F-WP05-034 -->.
4. Leave the driver's-side rear seat upright if you still need to carry a rear passenger <!-- F-WP05-034 -->.
5. Load the long item, such as lumber, a surfboard, or a ladder, along the flattened path.
6. Tie the item down using the cargo area's tie-down anchors so it cannot slide or shift.

**How you know it worked:** The item lies flat and secured along one continuous surface from the tailgate to the dashboard. It does not shift when you push on it by hand.

### Refresh Mode: recline the front seats flat to rest
Refresh Mode reclines the front seats flat, level with the rear cushions, for resting when the car is parked <!-- F-WP05-033 -->. This is not a driving position.

**Safety warnings, before you start:**
- Always park the car, set the parking brake, and turn off the engine before you recline the seats this far.
- Always reinstall both front head restraints and lock both seats securely upright again before anyone drives or rides in the car.
- Do not drive the car, or let anyone ride in a moving car, with the front seats in this reclined position. Seat belts are not designed to protect a reclined occupant (a person lying back in the seat).
- Do not let a child use this mode unsupervised; the fold-down lever and mechanism could pinch fingers.

**Steps:**
1. Park the car, set the parking brake, and turn off the engine.
2. Recline the rear seat-backs as far back as they will go <!-- F-WP05-033 -->.
3. Remove both front head restraints and store them somewhere secure so they do not roll around <!-- F-WP05-033 -->.
4. Using the seat slide lever, move both front seat cushions as far forward as they will go <!-- F-WP05-033 -->.
5. Pull up the seat-back angle adjustment lever on each front seat <!-- F-WP05-033 -->.
6. Pivot each front seat-back backward until it is level with the rear seat cushions, creating one large cushioned area <!-- F-WP05-033 -->.
7. To return to the normal driving position, hold each seat-back with your hand first.
8. Pivot each seat-back back upright, controlling the motion so it does not come up too fast.
9. Reinstall both head restraints <!-- F-WP05-033 -->.
10. Lock both seats securely before anyone drives or rides in the car.

**How you know it worked:** The front seat-backs lie flat and level with the rear seat cushions, forming one continuous cushioned surface. Each seat-back stays down on its own without you holding it.

## What can go wrong

### Utility Mode
- Forgetting to move the front seat forward first stops the folded seat-back from lying flat.
- Leaving the head restraint up blocks the seat-back from folding flush.
- Skipping the center seat belt's anchor release on the driver's side can strain or damage that belt.
- Not checking the passenger airbag-off indicator after you restore the seat can leave you unsure if the front passenger's weight sensor still reads correctly.

### Tall Mode
- Not pushing the cushion firmly enough against the seat-back means it is not truly locked, and it can fall during driving.
- Forgetting to fold the seat leg down can leave the mechanism loose.
- Loading an item so tall that it blocks the rear window can also interfere with the seat-backs later, when you want to return the seat to normal.

### Long Mode
- Forgetting to move the front passenger seat forward before reclining it leaves a gap instead of a flat surface.
- Not tying the item down lets it slide during braking or turns.
- Loading an item so long that it sticks out past the rear bumper without a proper reflective flag or marker is unsafe, and may be illegal in some states.

### Refresh Mode
- Forgetting to remove the head restraints first stops the seat-back from lying fully flat.
- Letting go of the seat-back too quickly when you return it upright is risky; it can spring up faster than expected, so control it by hand.
- Forgetting to relock the seats and reinstall the head restraints before driving again leaves the car unsafe for the next trip.

## Quick check
1. What are the names of the four non-standard Magic Seat modes?
2. Before you fold the driver's-side rear seat-back down, what must you do with the center seat belt?
3. Why should you never stack cargo higher than the tops of the seat-backs in Tall Mode?
4. Why is Refresh Mode only safe when the car is parked with the engine off?
5. Why does this book label the Long Mode steps as "community-reported" instead of manual-confirmed?

<!-- Fact IDs used: F-WP05-028, F-WP05-029, F-WP05-030, F-WP05-031, F-WP05-032, F-WP05-033, F-WP05-034 -->

# Chapter 10: Fuel

## What you will learn
- What fuel grade your Fit needs
- How big the fuel tank is, and roughly how far it will take you
- What the low-fuel light means, and what happens if you ignore it
- How to fill the tank correctly and safely
- What the "CHECK FUEL CAP" message means and how to clear it

## The short version
Your 2008 Fit needs 87 AKI (pump octane) regular unleaded gasoline or higher <!-- F-WP05-035 -->. The tank holds 10.8 US gallons (41 liters) <!-- F-WP05-036 -->. Fill it outdoors, away from any flame or spark, and never try to add fuel again after the pump nozzle clicks off <!-- F-WP05-040 -->.

## The full explanation

### Fuel grade
Use 87 AKI (pump octane, the number posted on the gas station pump) unleaded gasoline or higher <!-- F-WP05-035 -->. Using a lower-octane fuel can cause a heavy, persistent metallic knocking sound in the engine, and that knock can damage the engine over time <!-- F-WP05-035 -->. Honda also recommends gasoline with detergent additives (chemicals that help keep the engine clean) <!-- F-WP05-035 -->. Where you can find it, choose gasoline without manganese-based additives such as MMT <!-- F-WP05-035 -->. Those additives can trigger the malfunction indicator lamp, the dashboard warning light also called the "check engine" light <!-- F-WP05-035 -->. Your Fit is designed for gasoline blended with up to 10% ethanol or up to 15% MTBE (a fuel additive; short for methyl tertiary-butyl ether) by volume, but never gasoline containing methanol <!-- F-WP05-035 -->.

### Tank size and range
The fuel tank holds 10.8 US gallons, or 41 liters <!-- F-WP05-036 -->. The owner's manual and this book do not give an exact miles-per-gallon figure in this chapter's sources. Your real-world range will depend on how and where you drive, so use your fuel gauge, not a mental estimate, to judge how far you can go.

### The low-fuel light
The low fuel indicator (a dashboard light) turns on when about 1.7 US gallons (6.3 liters) remain in the tank, before the needle reaches E <!-- F-WP05-037 -->. A small extra reserve remains even once the needle reaches E, but treat that reserve as a safety margin, not fuel to drive on normally <!-- F-WP05-037 -->. Running the tank out of fuel can make the engine misfire and can damage the catalytic converter (a part that cleans your exhaust) <!-- F-WP05-037 -->. Avoid driving with an extremely low fuel level <!-- F-WP05-037 -->.

### The fuel door and cap
The fuel fill door opens from outside the car; pull its notched edge to open it, since this trim has no separate release lever inside the car <!-- F-WP05-038 -->. The fuel fill cap stays tethered to the filler neck, so you cannot drop or lose it <!-- F-WP05-038 -->. You may hear a hissing sound as you remove the cap; that is normal tank pressure escaping <!-- F-WP05-038 -->. The cap's tether fits into a slit on the fuel fill door, so it can hang there while you refuel <!-- F-WP05-038 -->. Screw the cap back on until it clicks at least once; an improperly tightened cap can trigger the malfunction indicator lamp and a "CHECK FUEL CAP" message <!-- F-WP05-038 -->.

### The "CHECK FUEL CAP" message
If the fuel fill cap is loose or missing, a "CHECK FUEL CAP" message scrolls across the instrument panel's information display the next time you start the engine <!-- F-WP05-039 -->. You can press the trip-meter select/reset knob to clear the message from the display early <!-- F-WP05-039 -->. It comes back every time you restart the engine until you fix the cap <!-- F-WP05-039 -->. If the system keeps detecting a loose or missing cap, the malfunction indicator lamp also turns on <!-- F-WP05-039 -->. To fix it, turn the engine off, check that the cap is installed, and tighten it until it clicks at least once <!-- F-WP05-039 -->. The malfunction indicator lamp will not turn off right away; it can take several days of normal driving after you fix the cap <!-- F-WP05-039 -->. If it stays on longer than that, have a dealer inspect the car <!-- F-WP05-039 -->.

## Step by step: Refuel the car

**Safety warnings, before you start:**
- Gasoline is highly flammable and explosive. You can be burned or seriously injured while handling it <!-- F-WP05-040 -->.
- Always turn off the engine before you start fueling <!-- F-WP05-040 -->.
- Always keep heat, sparks, and open flame away from the fuel fill area <!-- F-WP05-040 -->.
- Always handle fuel outdoors only, never in an enclosed space <!-- F-WP05-040 -->.
- Always wipe up any spilled fuel right away <!-- F-WP05-040 -->.
- Do not try to "top off" the tank once the pump nozzle clicks off on its own; leave room for the fuel to expand as its temperature changes <!-- F-WP05-040 -->.

**Steps:**
1. Park with the driver's side of the car closest to the fuel pump.
2. Turn off the engine <!-- F-WP05-040 -->.
3. From outside the car, open the fuel fill door by pulling its notched edge <!-- F-WP05-038 -->.
4. Remove the fuel fill cap slowly; a hissing sound as tank pressure escapes is normal <!-- F-WP05-038 -->.
5. Fit the cap's tether into the slit on the fuel fill door, so the cap hangs there while you fuel <!-- F-WP05-038 -->.
6. Insert the pump nozzle and select 87 AKI regular unleaded fuel or higher <!-- F-WP05-035 -->.
7. Fill until the nozzle clicks off automatically, then stop; do not try to add more <!-- F-WP05-040 -->.
8. If the nozzle clicks off repeatedly before the tank feels full, try a different pump; this can mean a problem with the fuel vapor recovery system <!-- F-WP05-040 -->. A dealer visit may help if it keeps happening <!-- F-WP05-040 -->.
9. Screw the fuel fill cap back on until it clicks at least once <!-- F-WP05-038 -->.
10. Close the fuel fill door.

**How you know it worked:** The pump handles cleanly, the cap clicks at least once when you tighten it, and the fuel gauge needle moves up. No "CHECK FUEL CAP" message or malfunction indicator lamp appears on your next drive.

## What can go wrong
- Not tightening the fuel cap until it clicks can trigger a "CHECK FUEL CAP" message and the malfunction indicator lamp <!-- F-WP05-039 -->. This can take several days of driving to clear, even after you fix the cap <!-- F-WP05-039 -->.
- Trying to "top off" the tank past the automatic click-off risks a spill, and leaves no room for the fuel to expand <!-- F-WP05-040 -->.
- Forgetting to close the fuel fill door before you drive away leaves it open and exposed.
- Driving with the fuel gauge near empty risks a misfire and can damage the catalytic converter <!-- F-WP05-037 -->.

## Quick check
1. What octane rating does your 2008 Fit need?
2. About how many gallons remain in the tank when the low fuel indicator first turns on?
3. Why should you never try to "top off" the tank after the pump clicks off?
4. What does a "CHECK FUEL CAP" message mean, and how do you fix the underlying problem?
5. Why must you turn off the engine before you start fueling?

<!-- Fact IDs used: F-WP05-035, F-WP05-036, F-WP05-037, F-WP05-038, F-WP05-039, F-WP05-040 -->

# Part III: Driving It

---

# Chapter 11: First Drive for a New Driver

## What you will learn
- What is different about starting off in a manual Fit versus an automatic Fit
- The gear positions on the base-trim automatic transmission, and what each one does
- A short first-drive routine for each transmission type
- Where to find the full manual-transmission practice routine in this book

## The short version
A manual Fit needs your left foot on the clutch pedal before it will move. An automatic Fit only needs you to pick the right gear lever position and press the gas. Both transmissions reward a slow, gentle first drive in an empty parking lot.

## The full explanation

This chapter gives you a first look at driving your Fit, split by transmission type. If you do not know which one your car has, look at the floor. A clutch pedal on the far left, next to the brake and gas, means you have a manual. Only two pedals means you have an automatic.

### If your Fit has a manual transmission

A manual transmission asks you to pick every gear yourself using a lever and a clutch pedal. The clutch is the pedal that disconnects the engine from the wheels so you can change gears without grinding them.

The Fit's manual gearbox uses a standard five-speed "H" shift pattern.<!-- F-WP06-001 --> First and third gear sit in the top row of the pattern, and second, fourth, and reverse sit in the bottom row, with reverse next to fourth and fifth.<!-- F-WP06-001 --> The gearbox is synchronized in every forward gear, which means the gears are built to mesh smoothly as you shift.<!-- F-WP06-001 -->

This chapter's first-drive routine for a manual is short on purpose. Chapter 12 walks through the full sequence, step by step, with the exact clutch and gas technique to use, so read that chapter before your first real practice session.

### If your Fit has an automatic transmission

The base-trim automatic Fit has a full shift gate, meaning it offers more lever positions than only "Drive."<!-- F-WP06-004 --> The positions are Park, Reverse, Neutral, Drive, a position marked "D" with a small "3" (called D3), Second, and First.<!-- F-WP06-004 --> The Sport-model Fit has a shorter gate with paddle shifters instead, but the base trim you are learning on has all seven positions.<!-- F-WP06-004 -->

Here is what each position after Park, Reverse, and Neutral actually does:

- **Drive (D):** your normal, everyday driving position. The transmission picks a suitable gear on its own, from first through fifth, based on your speed and how hard you are pressing the gas.<!-- F-WP06-005 --> When the engine is cold, it may shift up at a higher engine speed than usual, to help the engine warm up faster.<!-- F-WP06-005 -->
- **D3:** works like Drive, but the transmission stays in first, second, or third gear and will not shift into fourth or fifth.<!-- F-WP06-006 --> Honda intends this position to give you engine braking on a steep downhill, and to stop the transmission from constantly shifting between third and fourth in stop-and-go traffic.<!-- F-WP06-006 --> Engine braking means using the drag of the engine, instead of only the brakes, to slow the car down.
- **Second (2):** locks the transmission in second gear, so it will not drop to first when you stop.<!-- F-WP06-007 --> The manual recommends this position for climbing a grade with more power, and for extra engine braking on a steep downhill.<!-- F-WP06-007 --> It also helps for starting out on a slippery surface or in deep snow, and for reducing wheel spin.<!-- F-WP06-007 -->
- **First (1):** locks the transmission in first gear.<!-- F-WP06-008 --> By moving the lever among First, Second, D3, and Drive as conditions call for it, you can shift the automatic somewhat like a manual, only without a clutch pedal.<!-- F-WP06-008 -->

For an ordinary first drive, you will spend almost all of your time in Drive. Save Second, First, and D3 for hills, snow, or heavy traffic, and remember to move the lever back to Drive once you no longer need them.

Note for parents and new drivers: this chapter's driving sources cover the gear lever positions themselves. They do not cover the exact steps for starting the engine and pulling out of a parking space on an automatic. Chapter 6 of this book, on starting and stopping the car, covers the engine-start sequence in full.

## Step by step

**Manual transmission — short version.** Full safety warnings and the full 11-step routine are in Chapter 12. In brief: fasten your seatbelt and adjust your seat and mirrors, then press the clutch fully to the floor and start the engine. Shift into first gear, then slowly release the clutch while pressing the gas gently until the car moves.<!-- F-WP06-001 --> Go to Chapter 12 before you try this for the first time.

**Automatic transmission — first drive.**
1. Fasten your seatbelt and adjust your seat and mirrors before you shift out of Park.
2. Press the brake pedal, and move the shift lever from Park to Drive.<!-- F-WP06-005 -->
3. Release the parking brake.
4. Slowly release the brake pedal, then press the gas pedal gently.
5. Let the transmission pick its own gears as your speed changes; you do not need to shift it yourself in Drive.<!-- F-WP06-005 -->
6. If you are climbing a long, steep hill and want more power, or descending a steep hill and want more engine braking, move the lever to D3.<!-- F-WP06-006 -->
7. Move the lever back to Drive once you are back on a normal road.

## What can go wrong
- **Manual:** letting the clutch pedal up too fast causes the engine to stall, which means it shuts off because it cannot keep running against the load. Chapter 12 covers this in detail.
- **Automatic — forgetting to shift back to Drive:** if you leave the lever in Second, First, or D3 after a hill or snow, the transmission stays stuck there.<!-- F-WP06-006 --><!-- F-WP06-007 --><!-- F-WP06-008 --> It will not use all five gears until you move the lever back.<!-- F-WP06-006 --> Get in the habit of checking the lever position once conditions are back to normal.
- **Automatic — confusing D3 with Drive:** D3 feels almost identical to Drive at low speed, since both use first through third gear in that range. The difference only shows up above the speed where Drive would normally shift into fourth or fifth.<!-- F-WP06-006 -->

## Quick check
1. How can you tell, by looking at the pedals alone, whether your Fit is a manual or an automatic?
2. Name the seven lever positions on the base-trim automatic Fit's shift gate.
3. What is the difference between Drive and D3?
4. Which position would you choose to help start out on deep snow or a slippery surface?
5. Where in this book should you go to learn the full manual-transmission first-drive routine?

<!-- Fact IDs used: F-WP06-001, F-WP06-004, F-WP06-005, F-WP06-006, F-WP06-007, F-WP06-008 -->

This fact is used instead in Chapter 12, because the task instructions for this book direct that the hill-start procedure and its sourcing note belong in Chapter 12 alongside the full manual-transmission walkthrough, not in this first-drive overview chapter. -->

# Chapter 12: Driving a Manual Transmission from Zero

## What you will learn
- How the clutch pedal works and why letting it up too fast stalls the engine
- The Fit's shift pattern, and how to get into reverse without grinding the gears
- The recommended speeds for shifting up, for the best fuel economy
- A full first-drive routine, plus a routine for starting off on a hill

## The short version
The clutch pedal disconnects the engine from the wheels so you can change gears. Let it up slowly and smoothly, with a little gas, and the car moves off without stalling. Practice in an empty lot before you try traffic or hills.

## The full explanation

A manual transmission gives you full control of which gear the engine is in, using a gear lever and a clutch pedal. The clutch pedal is the leftmost of the three pedals. Pressing it all the way down disconnects the engine from the wheels, so you can move the gear lever without grinding the gears.

### The shift pattern

The Fit's manual gearbox uses a standard five-speed "H" pattern.<!-- F-WP06-001 --> First and third gear are in the top row of the pattern; second, fourth, and reverse are in the bottom row, with reverse next to fourth and fifth.<!-- F-WP06-001 --> Every forward gear is synchronized, meaning the gearbox is built to let the gears mesh smoothly as you shift.<!-- F-WP06-001 -->

### Getting into reverse without grinding

The transmission has a built-in lockout that stops you from shifting straight from fifth gear into reverse.<!-- F-WP06-002 --> To shift into reverse without the gears grinding, come to a full stop and push the clutch pedal all the way down. Then either pause a few seconds before moving the lever into reverse, or shift briefly into a forward gear first.<!-- F-WP06-002 --> Either action gives the input shaft, the spinning part inside the gearbox that reverse gear must line up with, time to stop turning before reverse engages.<!-- F-WP06-002 -->

### Shifting for good fuel economy

The owner's manual gives recommended shift points for normal acceleration. Shift from first to second around 15 mph (24 km/h), and second to third around 27 mph (43 km/h).<!-- F-WP06-003 --> Shift from third to fourth around 39 mph (62 km/h), and fourth to fifth around 53 mph (85 km/h).<!-- F-WP06-003 --> As a general rule, drive in the highest gear that still lets the engine run and speed up smoothly. That gives you the best fuel economy and the lowest emissions.<!-- F-WP06-003 -->

### Starting off on a hill

Honda's owner's manual for this generation of Fit does not describe a dedicated hill-start procedure.<!-- F-WP06-009 --> This car also does not have a Hill Start Assist feature, which is a system that appears on some later Honda models.<!-- F-WP06-009 --> The manual's page on parking does cover parking on a hill. Turn your wheels, use first gear if you are facing uphill or reverse if you are facing downhill, and set the parking brake.<!-- F-WP06-009 --> It does not, however, describe how to pull away from a stop on an uphill grade.<!-- F-WP06-009 -->

Because of that gap, the hill-start routine below is a general three-pedal technique common to manual-transmission cars, built from this car's own documented clutch and parking-brake behavior. It is good general practice, not a Honda-published Fit procedure.

## Step by step

### Drive a manual transmission for the first time

**Safety warnings, before you touch a pedal:**
- Always practice first in an empty parking lot or a very quiet street, not in traffic.
- Always fasten your seatbelt and adjust your seat and mirrors before you start the engine.
- Always press the clutch pedal all the way to the floor before turning the key to start the engine; the starter will not engage otherwise.
- Do not rev the engine hard or ride the clutch pedal partly pressed. This wears the clutch out faster and can damage it.
- Always come to a full stop before shifting into reverse.
- Do not try to shift straight from fifth gear into reverse. The transmission has a lockout and needs a short pause, or a pass through a forward gear, first.<!-- F-WP06-002 -->

**Steps:**
1. Adjust your seat, mirrors, and seatbelt before starting the engine.
2. Push the clutch pedal all the way to the floor. The starter only works with the clutch down.
3. Turn the key to start the engine, then shift the lever into first gear.
4. Keep the brake pedal or parking brake applied while you slowly let the clutch pedal rise.
5. Feel for the "friction point," the moment the engine note changes and the car wants to creep forward.
6. Gently press the gas pedal while you keep easing the clutch pedal up.
7. Once the car is moving, finish releasing the clutch pedal and keep your foot off it.
8. Shift up around 15 mph into second, 27 mph into third, 39 mph into fourth, and 53 mph into fifth.<!-- F-WP06-003 -->
9. To shift up, press the clutch fully down, move the lever to the next gear, then ease the clutch back up.
10. To slow down, press the clutch down fully right before the engine would stall, then brake normally.
11. Once stopped, shift to neutral and take your foot off the clutch until you are ready to move again.

**How you know it worked:** you can pull away smoothly and shift up and down through the gears. You can also come to a full stop without the engine stalling or the car jerking.

### Start on a hill (manual transmission)

**Safety warnings, before you try this:**
- Always practice this first in an empty parking lot or a quiet street with a gentle slope, not on a busy or steep hill.
- Always keep your right foot ready to move to the brake pedal in case the car starts to roll backward.
- Do not practice on a hill with another car stopped close behind you until you are confident you will not roll back.
- Always turn on your hazard lights if you stall on a hill and are blocking traffic, then try again.
- Do not rest your foot on the clutch pedal once you are moving; this wears the clutch out faster.

**Steps:**
1. Come to a full stop on the hill using the brake pedal.
2. Pull the parking brake up firmly with your right hand.
3. Push the clutch pedal all the way to the floor.
4. Shift the lever into first gear.
5. Move your right foot from the brake pedal to the gas pedal.
6. Press the gas gently while slowly letting the clutch pedal rise, until you feel the engine pull against the car.
7. When you feel the car want to move forward, release the parking brake fully.
8. Keep easing the clutch pedal up while giving a little more gas, so the engine does not bog down or stall.
9. Once the car is moving smoothly forward, finish releasing the clutch pedal all the way.

**How you know it worked:** the car moves forward up the hill smoothly, does not roll backward at any point, and the engine does not stall.

## What can go wrong
- **Letting the clutch pedal up too fast.** This stalls the engine, meaning it shuts off because it cannot keep running against the sudden load.
- **Riding the clutch.** Holding the clutch pedal partway up while driving wears the clutch out faster.
- **Giving too little gas while releasing the clutch.** This also stalls the engine, especially on a hill.
- **Releasing the parking brake before the clutch has caught, on a hill start.** This lets the car roll backward.
- **Forgetting to fully release the parking brake once moving.** This can overheat the rear brakes.
- **Looking down at the shift lever instead of watching the road.** Learn the shift pattern by feel.
- **Trying to shift straight from fifth gear to reverse.** Pause first, or pass through a forward gear, instead.<!-- F-WP06-002 -->

## Quick check
1. What does the clutch pedal do, and where is it located?
2. What are the recommended speeds for shifting from first to second, and from fourth to fifth, for good fuel economy?
3. Why can you not shift straight from fifth gear into reverse, and what two things can you do instead?
4. What should your right foot be ready to do during a hill start, in case the car rolls backward?
5. Does the 2008 Fit have a Hill Start Assist feature?

# Chapter 13: Parking, Reversing, and Tight Spaces

## What you will learn
- Where reverse gear sits in the manual shift pattern, and how to get into it smoothly
- What the owner's manual says about parking on a hill
- Why this chapter is shorter than you might expect, and where to look for more

## The short version
Reverse sits next to fourth and fifth gear in the Fit's shift pattern, and the transmission has a lockout so you cannot grind into it from fifth by mistake. On a hill, turn your wheels, leave the car in gear, and set the parking brake. Beyond that, the sources for this book do not cover detailed parking or reversing technique for this specific car.

## The full explanation

### Reverse gear, on a manual Fit

In the Fit's five-speed "H" shift pattern, reverse sits in the bottom row of the pattern, next to fourth gear.<!-- F-WP06-001 --> The transmission has a lockout that prevents you from shifting straight from fifth gear into reverse.<!-- F-WP06-002 --> To get into reverse smoothly, come to a full stop and push the clutch pedal all the way down. Then either pause a few seconds or shift briefly into a forward gear before moving the lever into reverse.<!-- F-WP06-002 --> Either action gives the gearbox's spinning input shaft time to stop turning before reverse engages.<!-- F-WP06-002 --> On an automatic Fit, reverse is one of the seven positions on the base-trim shift gate, alongside Park, Neutral, Drive, D3, Second, and First.<!-- F-WP06-004 -->

### Parking on a hill

The owner's manual's page on parking covers what to do when you park on a hill. Turn your front wheels toward the curb or the edge of the road. Leave the transmission in first gear if you are facing uphill, or in reverse gear if you are facing downhill, and set the parking brake.<!-- F-WP06-009 --> This gives the parked car a second way to resist rolling, in addition to the parking brake, if it were to slip.

### What this chapter does not cover, and why

The sources used for this book do not include specific technique for parallel parking or backing into a space. They also do not cover using mirrors while reversing, or judging distances in tight spaces, for the 2008 Fit. We could not confirm Honda-specific guidance on these points. Rather than invent tips that are not in the source material, this book points you to general safe-driving practice instead. Go slowly, and check your mirrors and look over your shoulder before and during any reversing move. If you are unsure a space is tight, get out and look before you try it. A driving instructor or your state's driver's manual is a good place to learn parking and reversing technique, step by step.

## Quick check
1. Where does reverse gear sit in the Fit's manual shift pattern?
2. What must you do with the clutch pedal before shifting into reverse, and why?
3. According to the owner's manual, what gear should you leave the car in when parked facing uphill? Facing downhill?
4. Why does this chapter not include detailed parking or reversing tips?

No new fact card exists for detailed parking/reversing technique; this gap is stated honestly above rather than invented. -->

# Chapter 14: Highway, Rain, Snow, Heat, and Night

## What you will learn
- Why driving fast on the highway costs you fuel economy
- What kind of tires the owner's manual recommends for winter driving
- The rules for using tire chains on a Fit
- Which weather and lighting conditions this chapter's sources do not cover

## The short version
Speed above about 45 to 50 mph costs you fuel economy fast, because wind resistance rises sharply. For winter, "M+S" or all-season tires cover most conditions, and real snow tires or approved cable-type chains, on the front only, help further. The sources for this book do not cover rain, extreme heat, or night driving for this specific car.

## The full explanation

### Highway speed

Aerodynamic drag, the resistance of the air pushing back against the car, rises sharply above 45 mph (75 km/h).<!-- F-WP06-012 --> The EPA's general driving guidance says gas mileage usually drops quickly above 50 mph. Each 5 mph you drive over 50 mph acts like paying about $0.29 more per gallon, based on EPA's national average fuel price at the time that figure was published.<!-- F-WP06-013 --> This EPA guidance is written for gasoline cars in general, not for the Fit specifically, but it applies to any gasoline car including the Fit.<!-- F-WP06-013 --> Roof racks, carriers, and trailers add extra drag on top of that.<!-- F-WP06-012 -->

### Winter tires and traction

Tires marked "M+S," which stands for mud and snow, or marked "All Season" on the sidewall, are suitable for most winter driving.<!-- F-WP06-014 --> For the best traction in snow or ice, the owner's manual recommends installing dedicated snow tires or tire chains, and notes these may be legally required in some places.<!-- F-WP06-014 --> If you mount snow tires, they must be radial tires of the same size and load range as your originals. They must go on all four wheels, not only the drive wheels. Dry-road traction may be lower than with your original tires.<!-- F-WP06-014 --> Ask your tire dealer for a maximum speed recommendation for the snow tires you choose.<!-- F-WP06-014 -->

### Tire chains

Tire chains are allowed on the Fit, but with limits specific to this car's tight tire clearance.<!-- F-WP06-015 --> Mount chains only on the front tires, never the rear.<!-- F-WP06-015 --> Only SAE Class "S" cable-type traction devices with rubber chain tensioners are allowed. The manual says not to use metal link-style chains.<!-- F-WP06-015 --> Install chains only when conditions or local law require them, and follow the chain maker's instructions. Mount them as tight as possible and drive slowly. Check that they are not contacting the brake lines or suspension, and stop right away if you hear contact.<!-- F-WP06-015 --> Remove the chains as soon as the road is clear.<!-- F-WP06-015 -->

### Rain, heat, and night driving

This book's research did not find Honda-specific guidance for the 2008 Fit on driving in rain, in extreme heat, or at night. We could not confirm this for the 2008 Fit. Check your owner's manual, and until you find more specific guidance, use general safe-driving practice instead. Slow down in rain, since wet roads reduce traction. Watch your temperature gauge and coolant level more closely in extreme heat. Chapter 16 covers overheating in detail. Use your headlights and reduce speed at night, when your view of the road is shorter.

## Quick check
1. About how fast can you drive before aerodynamic drag starts costing you noticeably more fuel?
2. What two words does "M+S" stand for on a tire sidewall?
3. Which wheels should carry tire chains on a Fit, and what type of chain does the manual allow?
4. Does this chapter's source material cover night driving for the 2008 Fit? What should you do instead?

# Chapter 15: Getting Good Fuel Economy

## What you will learn
- The shift points that give the best fuel economy on a manual Fit
- What the owner's manual and the EPA say hurts and helps your fuel economy
- Why the break-in period matters for your car's long-term health
- Why you should not tow a trailer with this car

## The short version
Drive smoothly, keep your tires inflated, keep up with maintenance, and stay off the gas pedal in stop-and-go traffic. On a manual, shift up early. Above about 45 to 50 mph, wind resistance starts eating into your mileage fast.

## The full explanation

### Shifting for economy, on a manual

The owner's manual's recommended shift points, for normal acceleration, are: first to second around 15 mph (24 km/h), and second to third around 27 mph (43 km/h).<!-- F-WP06-003 --> Third to fourth comes around 39 mph (62 km/h), and fourth to fifth around 53 mph (85 km/h).<!-- F-WP06-003 --> The general rule is to drive in the highest gear that still lets the engine run and speed up smoothly.<!-- F-WP06-003 -->

### What the owner's manual says hurts and helps your fuel economy

Factors that lower fuel economy, according to the owner's manual, include aggressive driving, meaning hard acceleration or hard braking, and excess idling and stop-and-go acceleration and braking.<!-- F-WP06-012 --> Running the engine cold, driving with a heavy load or the air conditioning on, and under-inflated tires also hurt it.<!-- F-WP06-012 --> Tips to improve it include keeping up with maintenance, using the correct oil viscosity, and keeping your tires properly inflated.<!-- F-WP06-012 --> Avoid excess weight in the car, and keep the underside clean of snow or mud buildup.<!-- F-WP06-012 --> Drive moderately, meaning you avoid rapid acceleration, hard cornering, and hard braking, and observe the speed limit.<!-- F-WP06-012 --> Aerodynamic drag, the resistance of the air pushing back against the car, rises sharply above 45 mph (75 km/h).<!-- F-WP06-012 --> Roof racks, carriers, and trailers add extra drag.<!-- F-WP06-012 --> On a manual transmission, use the highest gear that still lets the engine run smoothly.<!-- F-WP06-012 -->

### What the EPA says, in general

The EPA's general driving-habit guidance applies to any gasoline car, including the Fit. It says aggressive driving, meaning speeding and hard acceleration or braking, can cut your gas mileage by roughly 15 to 30 percent at highway speeds.<!-- F-WP06-013 --> In stop-and-go traffic, aggressive driving can cut mileage by 10 to 40 percent.<!-- F-WP06-013 --> Gas mileage usually drops quickly above 50 mph. The EPA estimates that each 5 mph you drive over 50 mph acts like paying about $0.29 more per gallon.<!-- F-WP06-013 --> That figure is tied to national average fuel prices at the time it was published, and is not specific to the Fit.<!-- F-WP06-013 --> This is general EPA guidance for gasoline cars, not text written specifically about the Fit.<!-- F-WP06-013 -->

### The break-in period

Honda asks you to pay extra attention to your driving style for the vehicle's first 600 miles (1,000 km). This is called the break-in period.<!-- F-WP06-010 --> During this time, avoid full-throttle starts and rapid acceleration for the whole 600 miles, and avoid hard braking for the first 200 miles (300 km).<!-- F-WP06-011 --> Do not change the engine oil early. Wait until the regular scheduled maintenance interval.<!-- F-WP06-011 --> The same advice applies again after an engine overhaul or exchange, or after the brakes are replaced.<!-- F-WP06-011 --> Driving gently during break-in protects the engine and brakes, which pays off in reliability and economy for the life of the car.

### Towing

The owner's manual states that this vehicle is not built to tow a trailer at all, and that doing so can void the vehicle's warranties.<!-- F-WP06-016 --> No numeric trailer-towing weight rating is given anywhere in the manual, because the manual's position is that trailer towing should not be done with this car.<!-- F-WP06-016 --> A trailer adds weight and drag, and the manual already lists both as things that hurt fuel economy. So towing one would work against everything else in this chapter.<!-- F-WP06-012 -->

## Quick check
1. At what speed does the owner's manual recommend shifting from first to second gear, on a manual Fit?
2. Name three things the owner's manual says will hurt your fuel economy.
3. How long is the Fit's break-in period, and what should you avoid doing during the first part of it?
4. Is towing a trailer recommended for this car? What does the owner's manual say about it?

# Chapter 16: When Something Goes Wrong on the Road

## What you will learn
- What each red or important warning light means, and what to do about it
- How to change a flat tire safely, step by step
- How to jump-start a dead battery, step by step
- How to handle an overheating engine, step by step
- The rules for towing this car, and what the front towing hook is really for
- Why this chapter cannot give you a Honda-specific accident checklist, and what to do instead

## The short version
Pull over safely, turn on your hazard lights, and read the warning light before you decide what to do next. A flashing or steady red brake light, a low oil pressure light, or a stuck-on high temperature light all mean stop and deal with it, not keep driving. Keep the compact spare's speed limit and the flat-tow distance and speed limits in mind, since both are lower than normal driving speed.

## The full explanation

### Warning lights you may see on the road

The owner's manual only gives an explicit color label, "(Red)," to one indicator: the Parking Brake and Brake System Indicator.<!-- F-WP07-018 --> It comes on whenever you turn the ignition to ON, as a parking-brake reminder, and stays on until the parking brake is fully released.<!-- F-WP07-018 --> A beeper also sounds if you drive with the parking brake partly applied.<!-- F-WP07-018 --> If it stays on after you release the parking brake, or comes on while you are driving, something is wrong. The brake fluid level is probably low, or there is a brake system problem.<!-- F-WP07-018 --> Press the brake pedal lightly to check if it feels normal. If it feels normal, check the brake fluid level at your next stop. If the pedal does not feel normal, meaning it goes down much farther or needs much harder pressure, slow down by downshifting and pull over immediately.<!-- F-WP07-018 --> Do not keep driving; have the vehicle towed and repaired.<!-- F-WP07-018 --> If the ABS indicator, the light for the anti-lock brake system, comes on together with this brake indicator, have the vehicle inspected by a dealer right away.<!-- F-WP07-018 -->

The Low Oil Pressure Indicator should never come on while the engine is running.<!-- F-WP07-019 --> If it flashes or stays on, oil pressure has dropped very low. Pull over safely, shut off the engine, and turn on your hazard lights. Let the vehicle sit a minute, then open the hood and check the oil level, adding oil to the full mark on the dipstick if needed.<!-- F-WP07-019 --> Then restart the engine and watch the indicator. If it does not go out within 10 seconds, shut the engine off again; there is a mechanical problem that needs repair before you keep driving.<!-- F-WP07-019 --> This indicator is commonly described as a red, oil-can-shaped light, but that color could not be confirmed for the 2008 Fit specifically.<!-- F-WP07-019 -->

The Charging System Indicator means, if it comes on brightly while the engine is running, that the battery is not being charged.<!-- F-WP07-020 --> Immediately turn off all electrical accessories, and avoid using power windows and similar loads. Keep the engine running, since starting it again would drain the battery further, and go to a service station or garage for help.<!-- F-WP07-020 --> Owners report that this light is a red battery-shaped symbol, though the owner's manual text itself does not name a color for it.<!-- F-WP07-020 -->

The Malfunction Indicator Lamp, also called the check-engine light, means one of the engine's emissions-control systems may have a problem.<!-- F-WP07-021 --> The car may feel unchanged, but fuel economy can drop and emissions can rise. Continued driving may cause serious damage, so have the vehicle checked by a dealer as soon as possible if it comes on repeatedly.<!-- F-WP07-021 --> If it comes on right after you refuel, check for a loose or missing fuel cap first. The manual may also show a "CHECK FUEL CAP" message. Tightening the cap can take several days of driving to clear the light.<!-- F-WP07-021 --> This indicator does not demand pulling over right away, the way the brake, oil-pressure, or overheating indicators do, but the manual still says not to keep driving on it indefinitely.<!-- F-WP07-021 --> We could not confirm this indicator's color for the 2008 Fit specifically; the manual does not label it at all.<!-- F-WP07-021 --> Do not assume the check-engine light is a red light. Based on the manual's own wording, only the brake system indicator is confirmed red; the check-engine light is never given a color in the manual.<!-- F-WP07-022 -->

### Hazard lights and reflectors

The hazard warning button, also called the four-way flashers, is on the instrument panel.<!-- F-WP07-023 --> Pressing it flashes all four exterior turn signals and both instrument-panel turn indicators at the same time.<!-- F-WP07-023 --> Use the hazard lights if you need to park in a dangerous spot near heavy traffic, or if your vehicle is disabled.<!-- F-WP07-023 --> Every roadside procedure in this chapter, flat tire, overheating, and low oil pressure, tells you to turn on the hazard lights as an early step.<!-- F-WP07-023 -->

We could not confirm this for the 2008 Fit. A full read of the owner's manual found no mention anywhere of a warning triangle, reflective triangle, road flare, or roadside reflector. It is not listed as standard equipment or as a recommendation.<!-- F-WP07-024 --> Check your owner's manual, and consider carrying a reflective triangle or flares as extra roadside protection even though the Fit's manual does not require or mention one.<!-- F-WP07-024 -->

### Towing this car, and the towing hook

Honda names two approved ways to tow this vehicle for ordinary, non-emergency situations. Flat-bed equipment, where the whole car is loaded onto the back of a truck, is what the manual calls the best way to transport the vehicle.<!-- F-WP07-025 --> Wheel-lift equipment, where the tow truck's two arms lift the front tires off the ground while the rear tires stay down, is called an acceptable way to tow the vehicle.<!-- F-WP07-025 --> The manual says to call a professional towing service, and never to tow the car with only a rope or chain, since that is very dangerous.<!-- F-WP07-025 -->

The owner's manual does not offer flat towing, meaning all four wheels rolling on the ground, as a supported method for either transmission.<!-- F-WP07-026 --> It only allows front-wheels-on-the-ground towing as a damage-only contingency, when flatbed or wheel-lift equipment cannot be used.<!-- F-WP07-026 --> This method is capped at 50 miles (80 km) and 35 mph (55 km/h) maximum.<!-- F-WP07-029 --> For a manual transmission, release the parking brake, shift to neutral, and leave the ignition switch in the ACCESSORY position so the steering wheel does not lock.<!-- F-WP07-027 --> For an automatic transmission, release the parking brake, start the engine, shift to D, and hold for 5 seconds.<!-- F-WP07-028 --> Then shift to N, let the engine idle for 3 minutes, and turn it off. Leave the ignition switch in ACCESSORY.<!-- F-WP07-028 --> If you cannot shift the transmission or cannot start the engine, the vehicle must be transported with the front wheels off the ground instead. The front-wheels-down method does not apply in that case.<!-- F-WP07-028 --> Do not lift the vehicle, or tow it, using the bumpers. They cannot support the vehicle's weight and will be seriously damaged.<!-- F-WP07-030 --> Always confirm the steering wheel turns freely, with the ignition in ACCESSORY, before towing begins.<!-- F-WP07-030 -->

The car also carries a single detachable towing hook and its extension tool, stored in the tool kit in the cargo area.<!-- F-WP07-031 --> It mounts into a bolt hole behind a cover panel in the front bumper.<!-- F-WP07-031 --> The manual frames this hook as a "get unstuck" tool. It is meant for freeing the car from sand, mud, or snow over very short distances, or for a towing service to use for straight, flat-ground pulling only.<!-- F-WP07-032 --> It is not for loading the car onto a flatbed, and must not be used as a cargo tie-down.<!-- F-WP07-032 --> Do not tow at an angle with it, and call a towing service for anything beyond a very short pull.<!-- F-WP07-032 -->

### Accidents

We could not confirm this for the 2008 Fit. None of this book's research sources describe a Honda-specific accident checklist, meaning a list of what to do right after a collision. If you are ever in an accident, use general safe practice instead. Check for injuries first, and move to a safe spot off the road if the car can still move. Turn on your hazard lights, call emergency services if anyone is hurt, and exchange insurance and contact information with anyone else involved. Consult your state's driver's handbook or local authorities for the exact steps required where you live, since accident-reporting rules vary by state.

## Step by step

### Change a flat tire

**Safety warnings, before you touch the jack:**
- Always stop far away from traffic lanes, on firm, level, non-slippery ground, before you start.
- Always turn on the hazard warning lights before you get out of the car.
- Always get every passenger out of the car before you change the tire.
- Do not get under the car at any time. It can roll off the jack and crush you.
- Always follow the jacking steps in order. Do not skip the jacking-point check.
- Always handle wheel nuts and the brake hub carefully; they can be hot from driving.
- Never exceed 50 mph (80 km/h) on the compact spare tire. It is a temporary tire only.<!-- F-WP07-004 -->

**Steps:**
1. Pull well off the road, onto firm, level, non-slippery ground, away from traffic.
2. Turn on the hazard warning lights.
3. Put the transmission in Park (automatic) or reverse (manual). Set the parking brake.
4. Turn the ignition off. Have everyone get out of the car and stand away from traffic.
5. Open the tailgate and lift the cargo floor lid using its strap. Hook the strap to the head restraint leg to hold it open.<!-- F-WP07-002 -->
6. Take the tool kit out of the center of the spare tire.<!-- F-WP07-007 -->
7. Turn the jack's end bracket counterclockwise to loosen it, then lift the jack straight out.<!-- F-WP07-006 -->
8. Unscrew the wing bolt and lift the spare tire out of its well.<!-- F-WP07-002 -->
9. Loosen each wheel nut on the flat tire one half turn with the wheel nut wrench. Do not remove them yet.
10. On base-trim cars, pry off the wheel cover using the flat tip of the extension.<!-- F-WP07-010 -->
11. Find the jacking point nearest the flat tire. Look for the mark molded into the body underside.<!-- F-WP07-008 -->
12. Set the jack under that jacking point. Turn the end bracket clockwise until the jack meets the point.
13. Check that the jacking point's tab is resting inside the jack's notch before you raise the car.<!-- F-WP07-008 -->
14. Use the extension with the wheel nut wrench to raise the car until the flat tire is off the ground.
15. Remove the loosened wheel nuts, then pull the flat tire off. Set it down with the outside face up.
16. Wipe dirt off the wheel hub and the spare's mounting surface with a clean cloth.
17. Put the spare tire onto the hub. Thread the wheel nuts on finger-tight in a crisscross pattern.
18. Lower the car to the ground with the jack, then remove the jack.
19. Tighten the wheel nuts the rest of the way in the same crisscross pattern, to 80 lbf·ft (108 N·m).<!-- F-WP07-009 -->
20. Place the flat tire face down in the spare tire well and secure it with the wing bolt.
21. Store the jack and place the tool kit back in the center of the flat tire.<!-- F-WP07-007 -->
22. Store the wheel cover or center cap in the cargo area so it does not get scratched.
23. Unhook the strap, lower the cargo floor lid, and close the tailgate.
24. Drive to a service shop soon to have the wheel nut torque checked and the flat tire repaired.<!-- F-WP07-005 -->

**How you know it worked:** the spare tire sits flat on the ground, all wheel nuts are snug and torqued, and the car sits level. It drives straight with no pulling, wobble, or noise below 50 mph.<!-- F-WP07-004 -->

### Jump start the car

**Safety warnings, before you connect a cable:**
- Always keep sparks, open flames, and smoking materials away from the battery. It can explode.<!-- F-WP07-014 -->
- Do not jump-start a battery with slushy or frozen electrolyte, the liquid inside the battery. Let it thaw first.<!-- F-WP07-014 -->
- Do not connect the second jumper cable to the dead battery's negative terminal or to any other engine part. Use the grounding point, called "the stay" in the manual, instead.<!-- F-WP07-012 -->
- Always turn off all electrical accessories on both vehicles before connecting cables.<!-- F-WP07-014 -->
- Do not try to push- or pull-start a car with an automatic transmission. It will not work.<!-- F-WP07-014 -->
- Always keep loose jumper cable ends away from each other and from metal until fully disconnected.<!-- F-WP07-013 -->

**Steps:**
1. Open the hood and check the physical condition of your battery before doing anything else.<!-- F-WP07-011 -->
2. In cold weather, check that the battery's electrolyte is not slushy or frozen.<!-- F-WP07-014 -->
3. Turn off the heater, A/C, audio system, lights, and every other electrical accessory.<!-- F-WP07-014 -->
4. Put the transmission in neutral (manual) or Park (automatic), and set the parking brake.<!-- F-WP07-014 -->
5. If you are using another vehicle, have someone start it and run it at a fast idle.<!-- F-WP07-014 -->
6. Connect one jumper cable to the positive (+) terminal on your dead battery.<!-- F-WP07-012 -->
7. Connect the other end of that cable to the positive (+) terminal on the booster battery.<!-- F-WP07-012 -->
8. Connect the second jumper cable to the negative (-) terminal on the booster battery.<!-- F-WP07-012 -->
9. Connect the other end of the second cable to the grounding stay on your car, not the battery.<!-- F-WP07-012 -->
10. Start your engine. If cranking is slow, recheck that every cable clamp has a solid metal contact.
11. Once your car is running, disconnect the negative cable from your car first, then from the booster battery.<!-- F-WP07-013 -->
12. Disconnect the positive cable from your car, then from the booster battery.<!-- F-WP07-013 -->
13. Keep the loose cable ends apart and away from any metal until both cables are fully removed.<!-- F-WP07-013 -->

**How you know it worked:** the engine starts and keeps running on its own once both cables are disconnected, and the charging system indicator is off.

### The car is overheating

**Safety warnings, before you open the hood:**
- Do not open the hood if you see steam or spray coming from under it. Wait until it stops.<!-- F-WP07-017 -->
- Steam and spray from an overheated engine can scald you badly. Stay clear until things cool.<!-- F-WP07-017 -->
- Always let the engine and radiator cool down before removing the radiator cap.<!-- F-WP07-017 -->
- Do not remove the radiator cap while the engine is hot. Hot coolant, the liquid that keeps the engine's temperature down, can spray out and scald you.<!-- F-WP07-017 -->
- Do not keep driving with the high temperature indicator on. It can cause serious engine damage.<!-- F-WP07-015 -->

**Steps:**
1. Safely pull to the side of the road, away from traffic.<!-- F-WP07-016 -->
2. Shift to neutral (manual) or Park (automatic), and set the parking brake.<!-- F-WP07-016 -->
3. Turn off all accessories and turn on the hazard warning lights.<!-- F-WP07-016 -->
4. If you see steam or spray from under the hood, turn off the engine right away.<!-- F-WP07-016 -->
5. Wait until no more steam or spray is visible before you open the hood.<!-- F-WP07-016 -->
6. If there is no steam or spray, you may leave the engine running and watch the indicator.<!-- F-WP07-016 -->
7. If the indicator goes off within a few minutes, wait for it, then continue driving.<!-- F-WP07-016 -->
8. If the indicator stays on, turn off the engine and let it sit.<!-- F-WP07-016 -->
9. Look for an obvious coolant leak, such as a split hose. Everything is still very hot.<!-- F-WP07-016 -->
10. If you find a leak, do not drive again until it is repaired.<!-- F-WP07-016 -->
11. If you see no leak, check the coolant level in the radiator reserve tank.<!-- F-WP07-016 -->
12. Add coolant to the reserve tank if the level is below the MIN mark.<!-- F-WP07-016 -->
13. If the reserve tank was empty, let the engine and radiator cool fully before going further.<!-- F-WP07-016 -->
14. Using gloves or a heavy cloth, turn the radiator cap counterclockwise to the first stop only.<!-- F-WP07-016 -->
15. Let the pressure release fully, then push down on the cap and turn it off.<!-- F-WP07-016 -->
16. Start the engine and set the temperature dial to maximum heat.<!-- F-WP07-016 -->
17. Add coolant to the radiator up to the base of the filler neck.<!-- F-WP07-016 -->
18. If you have no coolant mix on hand, plain water is an acceptable emergency substitute.<!-- F-WP07-016 -->
19. Put the radiator cap back on tightly, then run the engine and watch the indicator.<!-- F-WP07-016 -->
20. If the indicator blinks or comes on again, stop driving. The engine needs repair.<!-- F-WP07-016 -->
21. If the temperature stays normal, top the reserve tank to MAX and close its cap tightly.<!-- F-WP07-016 -->
22. Have the cooling system drained and refilled with the correct coolant mixture soon.<!-- F-WP07-016 -->

**How you know it worked:** the high temperature indicator stays off during normal driving, the reserve tank sits between MIN and MAX, and you see no new coolant leaks.

## What can go wrong
- **Flat tire — loosening wheel nuts after the car is already jacked up.** The wheel spins in place instead of loosening. Loosen them a half turn first, while the tire is still on the ground.
- **Flat tire — setting the jack on bodywork instead of the marked jacking point.** Check for the molded mark before you raise the car.<!-- F-WP07-008 -->
- **Flat tire — driving the spare above 50 mph, or for a long distance, instead of repairing the flat promptly.** The compact spare is a temporary tire only.<!-- F-WP07-004 -->
- **Flat tire — skipping the shop visit to have wheel nut torque re-checked.** The manual calls for this after any fresh tightening.<!-- F-WP07-009 -->
- **Jump start — connecting the last cable to the dead battery's negative terminal instead of a ground point.** This raises the risk of sparks near battery gases.<!-- F-WP07-012 -->
- **Jump start — leaving accessories on while connecting cables, or trying to push-start an automatic.** Neither works safely.<!-- F-WP07-014 -->
- **Jump start — letting stray cable ends touch metal while still connected on one end.** Keep the ends apart until both cables are fully off.<!-- F-WP07-013 -->
- **Overheating — opening the hood while steam is still visible, or removing the radiator cap on a hot engine.** Both can cause serious scalding.<!-- F-WP07-017 -->
- **Overheating — continuing to drive after the indicator stays on, instead of pulling over.** This can cause serious engine damage.<!-- F-WP07-015 -->
- **Overheating — forgetting to have the correct coolant mixture put back in, after adding plain water in an emergency.** Plain water is a stopgap, not a permanent fix.<!-- F-WP07-016 -->
- **Towing — using the front towing hook for anything beyond a short, straight, flat-ground pull.** It is a "get unstuck" tool, not a general towing point, and must not be used to load the car onto a flatbed.<!-- F-WP07-032 -->
- **Towing an automatic without the D-then-N cycle.** Skipping the 5-second Drive step, the 3-minute idle in Neutral, and letting the engine cool before shifting can damage the transmission.<!-- F-WP07-028 -->

## Quick check
1. What color does the owner's manual explicitly confirm for a warning light, and which light is it?
2. What should you do if the brake pedal does not feel normal when you press it?
3. Name the maximum speed limit for the compact spare tire, and the distance and speed limit for front-wheels-on-the-ground towing.
4. In what order do you disconnect jumper cables once the engine is running?
5. Why should you never open the hood if you see steam coming from it?
6. Does this book have a Honda-specific accident checklist for the 2008 Fit? What should you do if you are in an accident?

# Part IV: Owning It (the paperwork)

---

# Chapter 17: Title, Registration, Insurance, Inspection, and Emissions Basics

## What you will learn
- What a **title** is, and why you should never leave it in the glovebox.
- What paperwork you should get when you buy from a dealer.
- What paperwork you should get when you buy from a private person.
- What the federal **Buyers Guide** sticker must tell you at a used car lot.
- Why registration, insurance, and inspection rules are different in every state, and where to check yours.

## The short version
A title is the legal paper that proves who owns a car, and it is the most important paper you will ever get with it. When you buy a car, you should also get a bill of sale and a written record of the mileage. Registration, insurance, and inspection rules are set by each state, so always check your state DMV's website for the exact steps.

## The full explanation

### Buying from a dealer
A **title** is the legal document that proves who owns a car <!-- F-WP08-001 -->. If you buy a used 2008 Fit from a licensed dealer, you should receive five things at the sale <!-- F-WP08-001 -->. First, the title, or proof the dealer can transfer it to you <!-- F-WP08-001 -->. Second, a **bill of sale**, which is the dealer's own sales contract <!-- F-WP08-001 -->. It shows the price, the taxes, and the fees <!-- F-WP08-001 -->. Third, a **Buyers Guide** window sticker, which becomes your copy once you buy the car <!-- F-WP08-001 -->. Fourth, an **odometer disclosure statement**, which is the mileage reading, signed by the seller <!-- F-WP08-001 -->. Federal law requires this statement for most cars under 20 model years old at the time of sale <!-- F-WP08-001 -->. Fifth, a temporary plate, or a finished application to title and register the car <!-- F-WP08-001 -->. This lets you drive it home legally while you wait for permanent plates <!-- F-WP08-001 -->.

### Buying from a private person
Buying from a private seller works a little differently <!-- F-WP08-002 -->. You should still get the title, but the seller must sign it over to you <!-- F-WP08-002 -->. The seller's name on the title must match their name exactly <!-- F-WP08-002 -->. If it does not match, you will need extra paperwork. A chain of title is a paper trail showing each past owner in order. A power of attorney is a signed paper letting one person sign for another <!-- F-WP08-002 -->. You should also get a bill of sale with both people's names, addresses, and signatures <!-- F-WP08-002 -->. It should list the car's year, make, and **VIN** <!-- F-WP08-002 -->. A VIN is the 17-character code that identifies one specific vehicle; you will use it again in Chapter 18 <!-- F-WP08-007 -->. You need a written record of the mileage at the time of sale too <!-- F-WP08-002 -->. Some states also want a release-of-liability form <!-- F-WP08-002 -->. This form tells the DMV that the seller is no longer responsible for the car after the sale <!-- F-WP08-002 -->.

### Why this book cannot give you exact state rules
Every state runs its own registration, insurance, and inspection system <!-- F-WP08-003 -->. Some states require a safety inspection or an emissions test <!-- F-WP08-003 -->. Some states require neither one <!-- F-WP08-003 -->. Some states want a notarized signature on the title, and some do not <!-- F-WP08-003 -->. Because these rules do not match from state to state, and because they can change, this book cannot list every one correctly and keep it current <!-- F-WP08-003 -->. Check your state DMV's website for the fees, deadlines, and inspection rules where you live <!-- F-WP08-003 -->.

### The Buyers Guide sticker
The Federal Trade Commission's Used Car Rule forces dealers to post a Buyers Guide sticker on every used car <!-- F-WP08-004 -->. Private sellers do not have to post one <!-- F-WP08-004 -->. The dealer must put the sticker in the window before you are even allowed to inspect the car for buying <!-- F-WP08-004 -->. The sticker must say whether the dealer offers a warranty <!-- F-WP08-004 -->. If it does, the sticker must state how long the warranty lasts, what share of repair costs the dealer pays, and which systems it covers <!-- F-WP08-004 -->. It must list the car's 14 major systems and the kinds of defects that can happen in them <!-- F-WP08-004 -->. It must suggest asking the dealer if you can get the car inspected by an independent mechanic first <!-- F-WP08-004 -->. It must warn you not to trust spoken promises that are not written down <!-- F-WP08-004 -->. Since a 2018 update, it must also recommend getting a vehicle history report <!-- F-WP08-004 -->. The dealer must hand you this same sticker as your copy when you buy the car <!-- F-WP08-004 -->.

## Quick check
1. What is a title, and why should you never leave it in the glovebox?
2. Name three of the five things you should get when buying from a dealer.
3. What must the Buyers Guide sticker tell you about a warranty?
4. Why can't this book list your state's exact registration and inspection rules?
5. Where should you go to find your state's exact rules?
