# Chapter 18: Recalls and Service Bulletins for This Exact Car, and How to Check for New Ones

## What you will learn
- What a **recall** is, and what a **campaign number** means.
- What a **TSB (Technical Service Bulletin)** is, and how it differs from a recall.
- All 8 confirmed NHTSA recalls for the 2007-2008 Honda Fit, in one table.
- Two recalls that sound like they apply to your car but do not.
- The top kinds of problems other 2008 Fit owners have reported to the government.
- How to check your exact car's VIN for open recalls, right now.

## The short version
A recall is a free, safety-related repair that the maker must offer because a part can fail in a dangerous way. Your 2008 Fit has 8 confirmed recalls, and you should check every one against your car's own VIN. You can check for free at nhtsa.gov/recalls using your car's 17-character VIN. Do this today, and check again every so often, not only once when you buy the car.

## The full explanation

### What a recall is
A **recall** is a safety fix that a car maker must offer for free, because a part on the car can fail in a way that could hurt someone <!-- F-WP08-007 -->. The National Highway Traffic Safety Administration, or **NHTSA**, is the US government agency that tracks recalls <!-- F-WP08-007 -->. Every recall gets a **campaign number**, which is a code that identifies that exact recall <!-- F-WP08-007 -->. A **TSB**, or Technical Service Bulletin, is different from a recall <!-- F-WP02-008 -->. A TSB is a repair instruction the maker sends to dealers for a known problem that is not considered a safety defect <!-- F-WP02-008 -->. A TSB is not free unless it is still covered by your warranty <!-- F-WP02-008 -->.

### The 8 confirmed recalls for your 2008 Fit
NHTSA's own database lists 8 recall campaigns for the 2007-2008 Honda Fit <!-- F-WP02-027 -->. Before you drive a used 2008 Fit, or even your own car if you have not checked lately, confirm every one of these shows as completed, not open <!-- F-WP08-011 -->.

| Campaign number | Date recall began | Component | Remedy |
|---|---|---|---|
| 10V033000 | March 19, 2010 | Power window master switch (driver's door) — water can enter through an open window and damage the switch, which can overheat or smoke <!-- F-WP02-001 --> | Dealer inspects the switch and installs a water cover. Dealer replaces the switch if it is already damaged. Free <!-- F-WP02-001 --> |
| 13V260000 | Parts ready October 18, 2013 | Power window master switch (driver's door) — a repeat of the same defect, because the 2010 fix did not fully solve it <!-- F-WP02-002 --> | Dealer replaces the power window master switch with an updated part. Free <!-- F-WP02-002 --> |
| 10V624000 | January 24, 2011 | Headlight low-beam wiring harness and combination switch — a bad connection can make low-beam headlights stop working <!-- F-WP02-003 --> | Dealer inspects the low-beam terminal and repairs it as needed. Free <!-- F-WP02-003 --> |
| 07V549000 | December 12, 2007 | Frontal airbag Occupant Detection System (ODS) wire harness — road salt tracked onto the floor can corrode this wire under the carpet. This one only applies if your car was ever sold or registered in one of 21 listed salt-belt states, or Washington, DC <!-- F-WP02-027 --> | Dealer inspects the wire harness and replaces it if needed. Free <!-- F-WP02-027 --> |
| 16V344000 | June 27, 2016 | Passenger frontal airbag inflator, made by a supplier named Takata — this covers both original and replacement inflators, and it can rupture and throw metal fragments <!-- F-WP02-028 --> | Dealer replaces the passenger airbag inflator. Free <!-- F-WP02-028 --> |
| 17V029000 | March 2017 | Passenger frontal airbag inflator — a later expansion of the same Takata defect as 16V344000 above <!-- F-WP02-029 --> | Dealer replaces the front passenger airbag inflator. Free <!-- F-WP02-029 --> |
| 19V501000 | On or about August 12, 2019 | Passenger frontal airbag inflator — this one only applies if the inflator had already been swapped once under an earlier Takata campaign <!-- F-WP02-004 --> | Dealer replaces the passenger airbag inflator again. Free <!-- F-WP02-004 --> |
| 20V770000 | NHTSA notified December 10, 2020 | Drive shaft — a coating defect can let road salt corrode the shaft until it breaks. This one only applies to manual-transmission 2008 Fits <!-- F-WP02-005 --> | Dealer inspects the drive shaft and replaces it if necessary. Free <!-- F-WP02-005 --> |

The ODS wire harness fix (07V549000) uses two related sensor systems, called ODS and OPDS <!-- F-WP02-027 -->. Together they detect a child seat or an out-of-position rider in the front passenger seat, so the airbag can be turned off for that seat when it should be <!-- F-WP02-027 -->. **Takata** was a company that made a type of airbag inflator that can explode over time as its propellant breaks down <!-- F-WP02-028 -->. That is why three separate campaigns above deal with a Takata passenger inflator <!-- F-WP02-013 -->. **Salt-belt states** are states that salt their roads heavily in winter, which speeds up rust on the underside of a car <!-- F-WP02-027 -->.

### Two look-alike recalls that do NOT apply to your car
It is easy to search for "Honda Fit recall" and find a campaign that looks like a match but is not <!-- F-WP02-006 -->. The 2009 Fit is a different car underneath a similar name <!-- F-WP02-006 -->. Campaign 18V268000 covers a passenger airbag installation error, but its own list of affected years is 2007 and 2009 through 2013 — 2008 is not on that list <!-- F-WP02-006 -->. Campaign 19V182000 covers a driver-side airbag inflator, but it names only the 2007 Fit, not the 2008 Fit <!-- F-WP02-007 -->. Your car's passenger-side inflator is still covered, through the three campaigns in the table above <!-- F-WP02-007 -->. This is why you should always check your own car's VIN, instead of only searching by year and model <!-- F-WP08-008 -->.

### One service bulletin worth knowing about
**Electric power steering (EPS)** is the electric motor that helps you turn the steering wheel <!-- F-WP02-008 -->. Honda Service Bulletin 14-058 extended the EPS warranty to 10 years or 150,000 miles, whichever came first <!-- F-WP02-008 -->. It applies to 2007-2008 Fits with a stored trouble code called a **DTC**, which stands for diagnostic trouble code <!-- F-WP02-008 -->. The specific codes are DTC 32-09 or DTC 61-04 <!-- F-WP02-008 -->. The symptom is steering that feels heavier than normal, especially when the car is not moving <!-- F-WP02-008 -->. By 2026 a typical 2008 Fit is past both limits of that warranty, so this repair is now an out-of-pocket cost, not a free warranty claim <!-- F-WP08-012 -->. A second bulletin, 13-021, covers a rocker arm oil pressure switch <!-- F-WP02-009 -->. It can trigger the **MIL**, or Malfunction Indicator Lamp, which is the dashboard light most people call the check engine light <!-- F-WP02-009 -->. It applies to 2007-2011 Fits and is fixed by replacing the switch and its seal <!-- F-WP02-009 -->.

### What other 2008 Fit owners have reported to NHTSA
NHTSA keeps a public database of consumer complaints <!-- F-WP02-011 -->. As of this research, it held 217 total complaint records for the 2008 Fit <!-- F-WP02-011 -->. A single complaint can be tagged with more than one category, so the counts below add up to more than 217 <!-- F-WP02-011 -->. Here are the top categories, in order, with the number of complaints in each: air bags, 57 <!-- F-WP02-011 -->. Structure, 43 <!-- F-WP02-011 -->. Power train, 24 <!-- F-WP02-011 -->. Engine, 23 <!-- F-WP02-011 -->. Electrical system, 18 <!-- F-WP02-011 -->. Exterior lighting, 17 <!-- F-WP02-011 -->. Vehicle speed control, 14 <!-- F-WP02-011 -->. Brake-related complaints combined, 22 <!-- F-WP02-011 -->. Air bags being the top category matches the recall list above <!-- F-WP02-011 -->. Three of your car's eight recalls involve airbags <!-- F-WP02-011 -->.

### How to check your own car's VIN, right now
Go to **nhtsa.gov/recalls** and use the "Search by YMM or VIN" option <!-- F-WP08-007 -->. Type in your car's full 17-character VIN and click Search <!-- F-WP08-007 -->. The VIN is printed on a small plate at the lower left corner of the windshield, visible from outside the car <!-- F-WP08-007 -->. It is also on your registration card and often your insurance card <!-- F-WP08-007 -->. NHTSA also lets you search by license plate and state instead <!-- F-WP08-007 -->. If your car has no open recalls, the tool says "0 unrepaired recalls associated with this VIN" <!-- F-WP08-008 -->. A search by year, make, and model instead of VIN gives you general recall and complaint information, not your specific car's repair status <!-- F-WP08-008 -->. Note that this VIN search shows unrepaired-recall status "for certain manufacturers," so it may not show every kind of record for every maker <!-- F-WP08-008 -->. American Honda's own owner site, owners.honda.com, also offers a VIN-based recall check, and Honda's customer service line, 1-800-999-1009, is another way to ask about recall or repair status <!-- F-WP08-009 -->. Using both the NHTSA tool and Honda's own site is a good habit, since a brand-new campaign can show up on one before the other <!-- F-WP08-009 -->. The exact page behavior on Honda's own site was not independently confirmed this pass, so treat it as a helpful backup, not your only check <!-- F-WP08-009 -->.

## Quick check
1. What is the difference between a recall and a TSB?
2. Name any three of your car's 8 recall campaign numbers and what part they fix.
3. Why doesn't campaign 19V182000 apply to your 2008 Fit, even though it covers a Fit?
4. Where on the car do you find the VIN, and what website do you type it into?
5. What is Takata, and why does it show up in three different recall campaigns?

# Chapter 19: Documents to Keep in the Glovebox and at Home

## What you will learn
- Which three or four papers belong in your car's glovebox.
- Which papers belong at home instead, and why.
- Why the title should never travel in the car.
- How keeping good records now helps you later, including when you sell the car.

## The short version
Keep your registration, your insurance card, and your inspection sticker in the glovebox, because you may need to show them at any time. Keep your title, your bill of sale, and your maintenance records at home instead, in a safe place. Never keep the title in the car, because a stolen title makes a stolen car much easier to resell.

## The full explanation

### What belongs in the glovebox
Keep your current **registration** in the glovebox <!-- F-WP08-005 -->. Registration is the state's record that your car is legally allowed on the road <!-- F-WP08-005 -->. Keep your proof of insurance card there too <!-- F-WP08-005 -->. If your state issues a safety or emissions inspection sticker or certificate, keep the current one in the car as well <!-- F-WP08-005 -->. Some owners also keep a copy of the Buyers Guide sticker from when they bought the car, described in Chapter 17 <!-- F-WP08-005 -->. A card listing an emergency contact, plus any medical conditions or allergies, is also worth keeping in the glovebox <!-- F-WP08-005 -->. Do **not** keep the title in the glovebox <!-- F-WP08-005 -->. A stolen title makes a stolen car much easier for a thief to resell <!-- F-WP08-005 -->.

### What belongs at home
Keep your **title** at home, in a safe place <!-- F-WP08-006 -->. The title is the single most important paper for this car, and it is not something you need on a daily drive <!-- F-WP08-006 -->. Keep your original bill of sale and purchase paperwork with it <!-- F-WP08-006 -->. Keep your Buyers Guide sticker copy from the sale too <!-- F-WP08-006 -->. Keep the owner's manual at home, or write down where the digital copy is saved <!-- F-WP08-006 -->. Keep your maintenance and repair receipts together <!-- F-WP08-006 -->. They matter for warranty claims, and they help prove the car's service history if you ever sell it <!-- F-WP08-006 -->. Keep any recall notice letters you receive from Honda or NHTSA, covered in Chapter 18 <!-- F-WP08-006 -->. Keep your auto insurance policy documents at home too, separate from the insurance card in the car <!-- F-WP08-006 -->.

### Why this matters later
Good records at home make it much easier to answer "what wears by then" questions if you ever sell the car <!-- F-WP08-006 -->. Chapter 20 covers this in detail for the 2008 Fit specifically <!-- F-WP08-006 -->.

## Quick check
1. Name three documents that belong in the glovebox.
2. Name three documents that belong at home instead.
3. Why should the title never stay in the car?
4. Why are maintenance receipts worth keeping, even for routine work?

# Chapter 20: Buying a Used 2008 Fit: Inspection Checklist and Known Trouble Spots

## What you will learn
- What a general **pre-purchase inspection (PPI)** covers on any used car.
- How to fold the 8 recalls from Chapter 18 into your buying checklist.
- The one service bulletin worth asking about before you buy.
- Eight trouble spots owners report on first-generation Fits, and how to check each one on a test drive.
- What a used 2008 Fit tends to cost in today's market, in a clear price table.
- Why two of the pricing sources disagree, and what a typical odometer reading looks like at this car's age.

## The short version
Before you buy a used 2008 Fit, run its VIN through the recall check from Chapter 18 and get a mechanic's inspection. Owners report eight specific wear spots on this car, so check each one on the test drive. Expect a wide price range depending on the source and the car's condition. Expect the odometer to show anywhere from about 100,000 to 250,000 or more miles.

## The full explanation

### A general pre-purchase inspection
A **pre-purchase inspection**, or **PPI**, is a check of a used car before you buy it, done by you or by a paid independent mechanic <!-- F-WP08-010 -->. A general PPI covers five main areas on any car <!-- F-WP08-010 -->. First, the exterior and body: look for crash damage, mismatched paint, rust, and signs of flood damage <!-- F-WP08-010 -->. Also check whether the car sits level, and check for cracked or chipped glass <!-- F-WP08-010 -->. Second, tires and brakes: check tread depth, the tire's age code on the sidewall, uneven wear, and the condition of the pads, rotors, and calipers <!-- F-WP08-010 -->. Third, the engine bay and the underside: check for fluid leaks, worn belts and hoses, and rust on the frame <!-- F-WP08-010 -->. A puddle under a parked car is worth investigating, and a transmission leak is especially expensive to ignore <!-- F-WP08-010 -->. Fourth, the interior and electronics: test the heat and air conditioning, and check the instrument cluster and warning lights <!-- F-WP08-010 -->. Confirm every gauge and light actually works <!-- F-WP08-010 -->. Fifth, a test drive that checks braking, steering feel, suspension, and acceleration <!-- F-WP08-010 -->. Also ask the mechanic to scan the car's computer for stored **diagnostic trouble codes (DTCs)** <!-- F-WP08-010 -->. A code can be cleared right before a sale, only to come back after you have driven the car for a while <!-- F-WP08-010 -->. A professional PPI from an independent shop commonly costs about $100 to $200 <!-- F-WP08-010 -->.

### Confirm every recall is closed
Run the car's VIN through the recall check described in Chapter 18 before you buy <!-- F-WP08-011 -->. Confirm all 8 recalls from that chapter's table show as completed, not open <!-- F-WP08-011 -->. Pay special attention to two of them <!-- F-WP08-011 -->. The drive shaft recall (20V770000) only applies if the car has a manual transmission <!-- F-WP08-011 -->. The ODS wire harness recall (07V549000) only applies if the car was ever sold or registered in a salt-belt state <!-- F-WP08-011 -->. Do not be alarmed if a general web search turns up campaign 19V182000, since that recall names only the 2007 Fit, not your 2008 Fit <!-- F-WP08-011 -->. Trust what the VIN search itself shows for your specific car, not a general search <!-- F-WP08-011 -->.

### Ask about the steering bulletin too
Ask the seller whether the check engine light has ever come on with a steering-related code <!-- F-WP08-012 -->. Also ask whether the steering has ever felt unusually heavy, especially when the car is stopped <!-- F-WP08-012 -->. This relates to Honda bulletin 14-058, covered in Chapter 18, which once extended the steering system's warranty to 10 years or 150,000 miles <!-- F-WP08-012 -->. By now that warranty window has passed on any 2008 Fit, so a repair would come out of your own pocket <!-- F-WP08-012 -->. Still, knowing the car's history helps you judge its condition <!-- F-WP08-012 -->.

### Eight trouble spots owners report on first-generation Fits
These eight items come from real owner reports on first-generation Fit forums <!-- F-WP08-013 -->. They are not from Honda or the government, so they are labeled as owner reports rather than confirmed defects <!-- F-WP08-013 -->.

1. Owners report that motor mounts wear out over time, causing a clunk when shifting into or out of gear, or vibration at idle <!-- F-WP08-013 -->.
2. Owners report that the timing chain tensioner can develop a ticking or rattling noise under load, though a healthy engine on this car is naturally a little "ticky" too <!-- F-WP08-013 -->.
3. The clutch master and slave cylinder are the small hydraulic parts that move the clutch when you press the pedal <!-- F-WP08-013 -->. On manual-transmission cars, owners report that one of these can suddenly feel spongy or stop working, sometimes with no visible fluid leak <!-- F-WP08-013 -->.
4. On automatic-transmission cars, owners report a shudder or hesitation holding a steady 55 to 65 miles per hour <!-- F-WP08-013 -->. More than one part can cause this, including worn spark plugs or ignition coils, not only the transmission <!-- F-WP08-013 -->.
5. Owners report air conditioning problems in two forms: a compressor that rattles when it engages, and cooling that starts strong but does not stay cold <!-- F-WP08-013 -->. Also check the condenser, which sits ahead of the radiator, for rock-strike damage <!-- F-WP08-013 -->.
6. Owners report a metallic clunk or knock from the front suspension over bumps, most often traced to worn sway-bar end links or strut top-mount bushings <!-- F-WP08-013 -->.
7. Owners report a dashboard or glovebox rattle, most often from a loose panel under the windshield or a part behind the factory radio <!-- F-WP08-013 -->. It tends to get worse in cold weather <!-- F-WP08-013 -->.
8. Owners report unexplained battery drain <!-- F-WP08-013 -->. Ask if the car has ever gone dead after sitting for a few days <!-- F-WP08-013 -->. A healthy 2008 Fit should draw roughly 15 to 17 **milliamps (mA)** of power with everything off <!-- F-WP08-013 -->. A milliamp (mA) is a small unit of electrical current <!-- F-WP08-013 -->.

Owners also report a specific rust spot worth checking, especially on a car that has spent winters in a state that salts its roads <!-- F-WP08-014 -->. Look where the rear quarter panel meets the rear bumper cover <!-- F-WP08-014 -->. Also check the seam where a plastic cover hides the joint between the quarter panel, the rocker panel, and the underside of the car <!-- F-WP08-014 -->. This pocket collects grit and moisture, and it is reported as the first place rust shows up on this car <!-- F-WP08-014 -->. Left unchecked, it can progress to holes in the driver's rear wheel well <!-- F-WP08-014 -->.

### One test drive, eight checks
You can check most of these trouble spots on a single test drive <!-- F-WP08-015 -->. Shift firmly into and out of gear and listen for a clunk, which points to worn motor mounts <!-- F-WP08-015 -->. Accelerate under moderate load and listen for a new ticking or rattling beyond the engine's normal light idle sound <!-- F-WP08-015 -->. On a manual car, push the clutch pedal fully and feel for sponginess or a change in pedal travel <!-- F-WP08-015 -->. On an automatic car, hold a steady 55 to 65 miles per hour and feel for shudder or hesitation <!-- F-WP08-015 -->. Run the air conditioning on max for several minutes and confirm it stays cold the whole time, not only for the first minute <!-- F-WP08-015 -->. Drive over a few bumps and rough pavement, listening for suspension clunks and dashboard rattles <!-- F-WP08-015 -->.

### What a used 2008 Fit costs in today's market
Different pricing sources measure different things, so their numbers do not match, and you should not read them as contradicting each other <!-- F-WP08-018 -->.

| Source | What it measures | Base-trim range |
|---|---|---|
| Kelley Blue Book (KBB) | Dealer retail price for the base "Hatchback 4D" trim | $4,575 to $5,850 <!-- F-WP08-016 --> |
| KBB | Estimated trade-in value (what a dealer might offer you for your own car) | About $2,550 to $2,825 <!-- F-WP08-016 --> |
| KBB | Estimated private-party value (selling car to car, not through a dealer) | About $3,925 to $5,125 <!-- F-WP08-016 --> |
| Edmunds | Appraisal-tool trade-in value | About $1,022 <!-- F-WP08-017 --> |
| Edmunds | Appraisal-tool private-party value | About $1,446 <!-- F-WP08-017 --> |
| CarGurus | Average price on cars actually listed for sale right now | About $6,521, with real listings ranging from roughly $2,600 to over $15,000 depending on the city <!-- F-WP08-017 --> |

Note: KBB's own figures for 2026 show the Sport trim pricing lower than the base "Hatchback 4D" trim <!-- F-WP08-016 -->. This book uses the Hatchback 4D numbers above for base-trim buying guidance, not the Sport numbers <!-- F-WP08-016 -->.

KBB and Edmunds are **appraisal-tool estimates**, meaning they assume an average, typical-mileage, typical-condition car, and both run lower than real asking prices <!-- F-WP08-018 -->. CarGurus reflects **actual asking prices** on real listings <!-- F-WP08-018 -->. That is why its number runs higher, and why it varies so much by city, mileage, and condition <!-- F-WP08-018 -->. This price gap between the two kinds of sources is a known, logged disagreement between them, not a mistake in one source or the other <!-- F-WP08-018 -->. A fair way to read all this: expect a rough floor around $1,000 to $3,000 for a high-mileage, rough-condition example <!-- F-WP08-018 -->. Expect a realistic asking price of roughly $3,000 to $10,000 or more for a clean, lower-mileage example <!-- F-WP08-018 -->. Use these numbers as a starting point, since used-car prices move over time and by region <!-- F-WP08-018 -->.

### What the odometer is likely to show
At the commonly cited average of about 12,000 miles driven per year, an 18-year-old car would show roughly 216,000 miles by simple math <!-- F-WP08-019 -->. The US Federal Highway Administration reports drivers actually average 13,400 to 14,300 miles per year <!-- F-WP08-019 -->. That would put the same math closer to 241,000 to 257,000 miles <!-- F-WP08-019 -->. Real 2008 Fit listings show much wider variation than either estimate. Two examples showed 206,169 miles and 128,493 miles <!-- F-WP08-019 -->. Many owners drive less than the national average, especially as a car gets older <!-- F-WP08-019 -->. Treat any reading from roughly 100,000 to 250,000 or more miles as plausible and normal for a car this age <!-- F-WP08-019 -->.

Routine maintenance, such as oil, filters, brakes, and tires, is covered elsewhere in this book <!-- F-WP08-020 -->. Beyond that, expect at least some of the eight trouble spots above to show wear, or to have already been fixed, on any real 100,000-plus-mile example <!-- F-WP08-020 -->. Clearcoat is the clear protective top layer over the paint. Also check the paint on horizontal surfaces like the roof and hood for clearcoat peeling, another item owners report on this car <!-- F-WP08-020 -->. Use what you find as a talking point for negotiating the price <!-- F-WP08-020 -->. Do not treat it as an automatic reason to walk away from an otherwise well cared for car <!-- F-WP08-020 -->.

## Quick check
1. Name the five or six areas a general pre-purchase inspection covers.
2. Which two recalls from Chapter 18 only apply under certain conditions, and what are those conditions?
3. Name three of the eight owner-reported trouble spots, and how you would check each one on a test drive.
4. Why do KBB's and CarGurus' prices for the same car look so different?
5. What odometer range should you consider normal for an 18-year-old Fit, and why is it so wide?

# Part V: Maintenance

---

# Chapter 21: The maintenance schedule

## What you will learn

- What the Maintenance Minder is, and why your Fit has no printed mileage chart for service
- How the Maintenance Minder decides when your car needs service
- What the codes "Item A," "Item B," and the numbered sub items mean
- Five fixed rules that apply no matter what the Maintenance Minder says
- What you, the owner, should check on your own, and how often
- An honest note on cost, since this chapter's sources do not cover shop prices

## The short version

Your 2008 Fit does not use a printed table of miles for oil changes and other service. <!-- F-WP09-001 --> Instead, a computer called the Maintenance Minder watches how you drive and shows a code on the dashboard display when something is due. <!-- F-WP09-001 --> A handful of items still follow a fixed schedule no matter what that display says, and this chapter lists every one of them. <!-- F-WP09-013, F-WP09-012, F-WP09-014, F-WP09-005, F-WP09-006, F-WP09-008 -->

## The full explanation

### What the Maintenance Minder is

The Maintenance Minder is a computer system built into your Fit. It tracks your driving and tells you when service is due, instead of relying on a fixed mileage chart. <!-- F-WP09-001 --> Many older Honda models, and some other cars, print a table with two columns: one for normal driving and one for severe driving, such as short trips or dusty roads. The driver has to decide which column applies. <!-- F-WP09-015 --> Your Fit's owner's manual has no such table. <!-- F-WP09-001, F-WP09-015 --> The Maintenance Minder computer factors driving severity into its own math, so you do not have to guess which column you fall into. <!-- F-WP09-001 -->

The system tracks something called oil life. Oil life is a percentage the computer calculates from your driving conditions and how many times your engine has turned over. <!-- F-WP09-001 --> As oil life drops, the display eventually shows a maintenance code telling you it is time for service. <!-- F-WP09-001 --> Chapter 7 shows you exactly what this looks like on your instrument panel and covers every other dashboard light and icon. <!-- F-WP09-001 -->

### Reading the codes: Item A, Item B, and sub items

When the Maintenance Minder decides service is due, it shows one main code and, sometimes, one or more numbered sub items.

Main Item A means it is time to replace your engine oil. <!-- F-WP09-002 --> Main Item B means more. It calls for a new engine oil and filter, plus a shop inspection. <!-- F-WP09-003 --> That inspection covers your front and rear brakes, your parking brake adjustment, and your tie rod ends, steering gear box, and boots. <!-- F-WP09-003 --> It also covers your suspension parts, driveshaft boots, and brake hoses and lines. <!-- F-WP09-003 --> Last, it covers all your fluid levels, your exhaust system, and your fuel lines. <!-- F-WP09-003 -->

Alongside Item A or B, a sub item number can appear for other jobs the Maintenance Minder tracks. These include rotating your tires, <!-- F-WP09-004 --> replacing your engine air filter, <!-- F-WP09-005 --> and replacing your cabin air filter, the filter that cleans the air coming through your heater and air conditioner. <!-- F-WP09-006 --> They also include inspecting and adjusting your drive belt, <!-- F-WP09-007 --> replacing your transmission fluid, <!-- F-WP09-008 --> replacing your spark plugs, <!-- F-WP09-009 --> and replacing your engine coolant, the liquid that keeps your engine from overheating. <!-- F-WP09-011 --> None of these sub items has a fixed mileage number printed in your owner's manual. The Maintenance Minder decides the timing for each one based on how you drive. <!-- F-WP09-004, F-WP09-005, F-WP09-006, F-WP09-007, F-WP09-008, F-WP09-009, F-WP09-011 -->

One more item rides along with services A, B, 1, 2, or 3: your valve clearance. Valve clearance is the small gap inside the engine that lets the valves open and close correctly. <!-- F-WP09-010 --> It gets checked at those visits, but it only gets adjusted if the engine sounds noisy. <!-- F-WP09-010 --> There is no separate mileage number for this adjustment. It is tied to whichever service visit comes up next. <!-- F-WP09-010 -->

### Five fixed rules that beat the Maintenance Minder

Most service on your Fit runs on the Maintenance Minder's own timing. But five items follow a fixed rule that applies no matter what the dashboard display says. These are the only true "fixed schedule" items in the whole book for this car, and every new driver and every parent should know all five.

1. **Brake fluid, every 3 years.** Change it every 3 years regardless of any message on the display. <!-- F-WP09-013 -->
2. **Idle speed, every 160,000 miles (256,000 km).** Have it inspected at this mileage, independent of the Maintenance Minder. <!-- F-WP09-012 -->
3. **Engine oil, at least once a year.** If a full 12 months pass since your last reset without the display asking for an oil change, change the oil anyway. <!-- F-WP09-014 -->
4. **Air filter and cabin filter, every 15,000 miles (24,000 km) if conditions are dusty or sooty.** If you drive in dusty conditions, replace your engine air filter at this interval instead of waiting for the display. <!-- F-WP09-005 --> If you drive mainly in urban areas with heavy soot from industry or diesel traffic, replace your cabin filter at this same interval. <!-- F-WP09-006 -->
5. **Automatic transmission fluid, on two special conditions.** Say you drive an automatic-transmission Fit regularly at low speed in mountains. At 60,000 miles (100,000 km), have the fluid changed. Repeat that change every 30,000 miles (48,000 km) from then on. <!-- F-WP09-008 --> Say instead that you tow a Fit behind a motorhome. Change the automatic transmission fluid every 2 years, or every 30,000 miles (48,000 km) — whichever happens first. <!-- F-WP09-008 --> Both of these rules apply to the automatic transmission only. <!-- F-WP09-008 -->

Outside of these five, your owner's manual names no other severe-condition trigger with a fixed number. <!-- F-WP09-015 --> If you are shopping for parts or fluid, note that the Fit's automatic transmission fluid, Honda ATF-Z1, has since been discontinued industry-wide. Ask your parts store for its replacement, Honda ATF DW-1, if ATF-Z1 is not on the shelf. <!-- F-WP09-029 -->

### What you check yourself, and how often

Between service visits, a few checks are yours to do, and none of them need a mechanic.

- Engine oil level and engine coolant level: check both every time you stop for fuel. <!-- F-WP09-060 -->
- Automatic transmission fluid level and brake fluid level: check monthly. <!-- F-WP09-060 -->
- Tire pressure and tread: check monthly. <!-- F-WP09-060 -->
- Headlights, parking lights, taillights, high-mount brake light, and license plate lights: check monthly that they all work. <!-- F-WP09-060 -->
- Battery condition: check monthly using the test indicator window on top of the battery. <!-- F-WP09-061 -->

### A note on cost

This chapter's sources are Honda's own owner's manual and parts-fitment pages. None of them give a dollar figure for what any of this costs, whether you do it yourself or pay a shop. We could not confirm DIY-versus-shop costs for the maintenance schedule from this work package's sources. Check current prices with a local shop or parts store, or look for cost information in Chapters 22 through 33, which cover each maintenance job on its own.

## Step by step: Making sense of your maintenance display

1. Look at your information display for an oil life percentage or a maintenance code. <!-- F-WP09-001 -->
2. If the code is Item A, plan to change your engine oil. <!-- F-WP09-002 -->
3. If the code is Item B, plan to change your engine oil and filter, and have the longer inspection list checked too. <!-- F-WP09-003 -->
4. Check for any sub item number next to the main code, and match it to the job list above. <!-- F-WP09-004, F-WP09-005, F-WP09-006, F-WP09-007, F-WP09-008, F-WP09-009, F-WP09-011 -->
5. Even with no code showing, change your oil once a year, change your brake fluid every 3 years, and check idle speed at 160,000 miles. <!-- F-WP09-014, F-WP09-013, F-WP09-012 -->
6. If you drive in dusty or sooty conditions, or tow, or drive slowly in mountains with an automatic, apply the matching fixed rule above instead of waiting on the display. <!-- F-WP09-005, F-WP09-006, F-WP09-008 -->

## What can go wrong

Because most of your Fit's maintenance runs on a computer instead of a printed chart, it is easy to assume the dashboard will catch everything. It will not. The five fixed rules in this chapter run on their own clock, separate from the Maintenance Minder, and the display will not necessarily remind you about them. <!-- F-WP09-013, F-WP09-012, F-WP09-014, F-WP09-005, F-WP09-006, F-WP09-008 --> A parent or new driver who only reacts to dashboard codes could miss a 3-year brake fluid change or a dusty-conditions air filter swap for years. Write these five rules down, or set your own calendar reminders, instead of relying on the display alone.

## Quick check

1. What does the Maintenance Minder do differently from an older car's printed service chart?
2. What is the difference between Main Item A and Main Item B?
3. Name all five fixed rules that apply no matter what the Maintenance Minder display shows.
4. How often should you check your tire pressure and your automatic transmission fluid level on your own?
5. Why can't this chapter tell you exactly how much a maintenance visit will cost?

# Chapter 22: Tools and Supplies for a Starter Garage

## What you will learn

- Every tool this book's maintenance chapters actually call for
- What each tool does, in plain words, the first time it comes up
- Which tools you need for almost every job, and which ones are for bigger jobs only
- A sensible order to buy tools in, so you are not buying everything at once
- Safety gear and shop supplies to keep on hand besides tools

## The short version

This chapter lists only the tools named in this book's own maintenance procedures, nothing extra. You do not need a full mechanic's toolbox to start. A handful of tools cover most jobs, and a few more only matter once you tackle brakes or oil changes.

## The full explanation

### Turning and tightening tools

Most jobs in this book start with turning a bolt. A **ratchet** is a handle that turns a socket, a metal cap that fits over a bolt head, in one direction without you lifting it off between turns. A **breaker bar** is a plain, longer handle with no ratcheting action, used when a bolt is stuck and needs extra force. <!-- P-WP11-1 --> You need a ratchet or breaker bar with sockets sized to your car's bolts for the oil drain plug and for many other jobs. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->

A **torque wrench** is a tool that tells you exactly how hard you are tightening a bolt, by feel or by a dial. It measures in pound-feet (lbf·ft) or newton-meters (N·m). <!-- P-WP11-1 --> You need one that reaches at least 80 lbf·ft (108 N·m), the torque for your wheel nuts. <!-- F-WP10-001 --> The same torque wrench should also reach down to 29 lbf·ft (39 N·m), the torque for your oil drain bolt. <!-- F-WP10-002, P-WP11-1 --> Guessing at torque by feel alone risks a leak or a stripped bolt, so this tool is worth buying early.

An **oil filter wrench** is a tool shaped to grip the round oil filter so you can twist it loose. The owner's manual itself calls this "a special wrench, available from your dealer." <!-- P-WP11-1 --> You also need a **lug wrench**, or a socket that fits your wheel nuts plus a breaker bar, any time you take a wheel off. <!-- P-WP11-5 --> A **flat-tip (flathead) screwdriver** and a **Phillips screwdriver** cover the clips and screws on your air filter housing. <!-- P-WP11-2, P-WP11-3 -->

### Lifting and support tools

A **floor jack** is a tool on wheels that lifts one corner or one end of the car using a hand pump or lever. <!-- P-WP11-22 --> Buy one rated for at least your car's weight. The small scissor jack that lives in your trunk is only rated for a roadside flat tire, not for working underneath the car. <!-- P-WP11-22 -->

**Jack stands** are metal supports you slide under the car once it is lifted, so the car's weight rests on solid steel instead of the jack. <!-- P-WP11-22 --> Buy at least two, and ideally four, each rated for at least your car's weight. <!-- P-WP11-1, P-WP11-5, P-WP11-22 --> Chapter 23 covers how to use a jack and jack stands safely, and that chapter's warnings apply every time you use these tools.

**Wheel chocks** are wedges you place against a tire that stays on the ground, so the car cannot roll while another corner is lifted. <!-- P-WP11-5, P-WP11-22 --> You need at least two.

### Tire and brake tools

A **tire pressure gauge** measures the air pressure inside a tire, in pounds per square inch (psi). <!-- P-WP11-4 --> The owner's manual recommends using your own gauge every time, instead of a different shop gauge each visit, so your readings stay consistent. <!-- P-WP11-4 --> You will also want a portable inflator, or access to a gas station air pump, to add air. <!-- P-WP11-4 -->

A **flashlight** lets you see the brake pad through the wheel spokes without removing the wheel; a ruler or a dedicated pad-thickness gauge is a helpful, optional extra. <!-- P-WP11-6 -->

Once you move on to replacing brake pads, a **C-clamp**, or a dedicated brake piston tool, pushes the caliper piston back into its housing to make room for new pads. <!-- P-WP11-7 --> Rear drum brakes need their own set of tools. Use large C-clamps or brake-spring pliers to compress the brake shoes, plus a drum brake spring and hold-down tool kit to handle the springs and pins safely. <!-- P-WP11-8 --> A **wire brush** and **brake-parts cleaner**, a solvent spray made for cleaning brake dust, are needed for both front pad and rear drum jobs. <!-- P-WP11-7, P-WP11-8 -->

Bleeding your brakes means pushing old fluid and trapped air out of the brake lines. <!-- P-WP11-9 --> For that job, you need a wrench sized to the small **bleeder screw** on each brake. <!-- P-WP11-9 --> You also need clear plastic tubing that fits snugly over it, and a clean catch bottle for the old fluid. <!-- P-WP11-9 --> A turkey baster or a fluid syringe helps remove old fluid from the reservoir before you start. <!-- P-WP11-9 -->

### Safety gear and shop supplies

Nitrile gloves and safety glasses protect your hands and eyes any time you work with oil, fresh or used. <!-- P-WP11-1 --> Keep a stack of shop rags on hand for wiping up drips and cleaning parts. <!-- P-WP11-1 --> You will also want a sealed container to carry used oil to a recycling center, since used oil cannot go in the trash or on the ground. <!-- P-WP11-1 --> For brake work, keep high-temperature brake grease on hand for pad backing and slide pins. <!-- P-WP11-7 --> For the parking-brake lever pivot on drum brakes, use a specific product called Molykote 44MA, or an equivalent high-temperature brake grease. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->

Chapters 24 through 27 name the exact oil, filters, pads, shoes, and fluid your car needs. This chapter only covers the tools that install them.

## Step by step: Building your toolkit in a sensible order

You do not need every tool on day one. Buy in this order, based on which jobs come first in this book.

1. Buy a tire pressure gauge first. It is cheap, and Chapter 26 asks you to check your tires often. <!-- P-WP11-4 -->
2. Add nitrile gloves, safety glasses, and shop rags. Every job after this uses at least one of them. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->
3. Add a ratchet or breaker bar with a socket set, a lug wrench, and a flashlight. These cover inspection jobs and light repairs. <!-- P-WP11-1, P-WP11-5, P-WP11-6 -->
4. Before your first oil change, add a torque wrench, an oil filter wrench, a drain pan, and a funnel. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->
5. Before you lift the car for any reason, add a floor jack, jack stands, and wheel chocks, and read Chapter 23 first. <!-- P-WP11-22 -->
6. Before a brake job, add the C-clamp or piston tool, wire brush, brake-parts cleaner, and, for rear drums, the spring and hold-down tool kit. <!-- P-WP11-7, P-WP11-8 -->
7. Before bleeding brakes, add the bleeder wrench, clear tubing, a catch bottle, and a turkey baster or syringe. <!-- P-WP11-9 -->

## What can go wrong

Several procedure cards behind this book flag one bolt size or another as unconfirmed, telling you to check your own car before buying a socket rather than guess. <!-- P-WP11-1, P-WP11-7, P-WP11-9 --> Buying the wrong socket size wastes a trip to the store, so measure or check the bolt on your own car first when a size is not given. A torque wrench is not optional if a job names a torque number. Tightening by feel alone can leave a bolt too loose, which can fail later, or too tight, which can strip threads or crack a part. <!-- F-WP10-001, F-WP10-002 --> Do not swap a floor jack alone for jack stands. The floor jack lifts the car, but only the jack stands are meant to hold its weight while you work underneath. <!-- P-WP11-22 -->

## Quick check

1. What is the difference between a ratchet and a breaker bar?
2. Why does this book recommend a torque wrench instead of tightening bolts by feel?
3. Name two tools you need before you can safely lift the car and work underneath it.
4. Why should you use your own tire pressure gauge every time instead of a different gauge at each stop?
5. What should you do if a procedure card does not give you an exact bolt size?

This chapter draws only on the "Tools" and "Parts and supplies" sections of procedures/WP-11-1.md through WP-11-9.md and WP-11-22.md, since the writer brief limits Chapter 22 to tools actually named across those cards. Facts from facts/WP-09.md and the rest of facts/WP-10.md are not used here because this chapter is about tools, not maintenance intervals or fitment numbers; F-WP10-001 and F-WP10-002 are the two torque exceptions, cited because the torque wrench's required range comes directly from them.

# Chapter 23: Safety First — Jacking, Jack Stands, Wheel Chocks, Hot Parts, Fluids

## What you will learn

- The one rule that matters most in this whole chapter: never trust a jack alone
- How to jack up your Fit and support it safely on jack stands
- Why hot parts under the hood and under the car can burn you
- How to handle engine oil, brake fluid, and other fluids without hurting yourself or your car
- What can go wrong when someone skips a safety step

## The short version

A jack lifts your car, but a jack can slip or roll off its point without warning. Jack stands, wedges you place under the car once it is up, are what actually holds the car's weight while you work underneath it. <!-- P-WP11-22 --> Every job in Chapters 24 through 27 that lifts the car depends on you doing this one step correctly.

## The full explanation

### Why a jack alone is not enough

A **jack** is a tool that lifts one corner or end of a car. A **jack stand** is a separate metal support you slide underneath once the car is up, so its weight rests on solid steel instead of the jack. <!-- P-WP11-22 --> The owner's manual itself warns that a car can easily roll off a jack and seriously injure anyone underneath it. <!-- P-WP11-22 --> That is why every maintenance job in this book that needs the car off the ground also needs jack stands, not the jack by itself.

Your Fit's trunk holds a small scissor jack, meant only for changing a flat tire on the side of the road. A **floor jack** is a separate, larger tool on wheels, rated to hold much more weight, and it is what you use for jack-stand work in the garage. <!-- P-WP11-22 --> Buy a floor jack and jack stands that are each rated for at least your car's weight. Do this before you start any job in Chapters 24 through 27 that needs the car raised. <!-- P-WP11-22 -->

### Where to lift, and where never to lift

Your owner's manual shows a molded mark on the underbody near each wheel. This is the lift point built for the small scissor jack. <!-- P-WP11-22 --> For jack-stand work, there are also reinforced seams along each rocker panel (the body panel below the doors) and along the front subframe, set well back from the bumper. <!-- P-WP11-22 --> These reinforced seam points come from community reports, not from the owner's manual itself, so check them against your own car and a second source before you rely on them. <!-- P-WP11-22 -->

Never set a jack or a jack stand under the floor pan, the exhaust, a plastic under-cover, or a random point on the frame. <!-- P-WP11-22 --> Any of these can bend, crush, or puncture under the car's weight.

### Hot parts, fluids, and other everyday hazards

Beyond jacking, a few hazards come up again and again in Chapters 24 through 27. An engine, its exhaust, and its brakes all get hot enough to burn skin, so let them cool before you touch anything nearby. <!-- P-WP11-1, P-WP11-6, P-WP11-2 --> Used and fresh engine oil can irritate your skin and eyes, so wear gloves and eye protection whenever you handle it. <!-- P-WP11-1 --> Brake fluid splashes easily, damages paint on contact, and hurts eyes, so eye protection matters there too. <!-- P-WP11-9 --> Brake dust should never be blown around with compressed air or brushed dry; use a brake-parts cleaner spray instead, and never breathe it in. <!-- P-WP11-7, P-WP11-8 -->

Used oil is hazardous to the environment. Never pour it in the trash or on the ground; take it to a recycling center in a sealed container. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->

## Step by step: Jack up the car and set it on jack stands

**Safety warnings, before you touch the jack:**
- Do not ever get any part of your body under a car supported only by a jack. The car can roll off a jack and seriously injure anyone underneath. <!-- P-WP11-22 -->
- Always work on a flat, level, hard surface. Never jack up a car on a slope, on soft ground, or on an incline. <!-- P-WP11-22 -->
- Always set the parking brake and chock the wheels that will stay on the ground before you start jacking. <!-- P-WP11-22 -->
- Always use the vehicle's marked or reinforced jacking points, never the floor pan, exhaust, plastic under-covers, or a random point on the frame. <!-- P-WP11-22 -->
- Do not exceed a jack's or a jack stand's rated weight capacity. Check the rating sticker before you buy or borrow one. <!-- P-WP11-22 -->
- Always give the car a firm shake test on the stands before working underneath, to confirm it will not slip. <!-- P-WP11-22 -->

**Steps:**
1. Park on a flat, level, solid surface and set the parking brake. <!-- P-WP11-22 -->
2. Chock the wheels that will remain on the ground. <!-- P-WP11-22 -->
3. Locate the lift point: the owner's manual shows a molded mark on the underbody near each wheel, for the factory scissor jack. <!-- P-WP11-22 -->
4. For jack-stand work, also find the reinforced pinch-weld seams on each rocker panel and the front subframe, well back from the bumper. <!-- P-WP11-22 -->
5. Treat step 4's points with caution: they are community-reported, not from the owner's manual, so double-check against your own car and a second reference. <!-- P-WP11-22 -->
6. Position the floor jack under the chosen point and raise the car slowly. <!-- P-WP11-22 -->
7. Stop as soon as the tire lifts a couple of inches and check that the jack is still centered on the point. <!-- P-WP11-22 -->
8. Continue raising to the height you need. <!-- P-WP11-22 -->
9. Slide a jack stand under a solid point near the jack and adjust it to the right height. <!-- P-WP11-22 -->
10. Slowly lower the floor jack until the car's weight rests on the jack stand. <!-- P-WP11-22 -->
11. Repeat steps 6 through 10 for the other end or side, if you are lifting the whole car. <!-- P-WP11-22 -->
12. Give the car a firm push and shake test before you get near or under it. <!-- P-WP11-22 -->
13. Leave the floor jack nearby as a backup, but do not rely on it to hold weight once the stands are set. <!-- P-WP11-22 -->
14. When done, reverse the process: raise slightly with the floor jack, remove the stands, then lower slowly and fully. <!-- P-WP11-22 -->

**How you know it worked:** the car sits solidly on the stands with no rocking when pushed. All four (or two) contact points look evenly loaded. The wheels you did not lift stay chocked and on the ground the whole time. <!-- P-WP11-22 -->

## What can go wrong

The most common mistake is placing a jack or a jack stand on the floor pan, a plastic cover, or the exhaust instead of a reinforced point. That mistake can bend the body or crush the part. <!-- P-WP11-22 --> The second most common, and most dangerous, mistake is working under a car held up by the floor jack alone, with no stands underneath. <!-- P-WP11-22 --> Jacking on a slope or soft ground can let the car shift, or let the jack sink into the ground while you work. <!-- P-WP11-22 --> Forgetting to chock the wheels that stay on the ground can let the whole car roll while one corner is in the air. <!-- P-WP11-22 -->

## Quick check

1. Why is a jack alone not enough to support the car while you work underneath it?
2. What is the difference between the trunk's scissor jack and a floor jack?
3. Name two places you should never set a jack or a jack stand.
4. What should you do before you fully trust the car's weight to the jack stands?
5. Why should you never blow brake dust around with compressed air?

<!-- Fact/Procedure IDs used: P-WP11-22, P-WP11-1, P-WP11-2, P-WP11-6, P-WP11-7, P-WP11-8, P-WP11-9 -->

Source IDs S-001, S-023, S-WP11-B (cited at the bottom of procedures/WP-11-22.md) are source references within that procedure card, not separate Fact or Procedure IDs, so they are not listed as citations here; the Procedure ID P-WP11-22 already covers everything sourced from that card. # Chapter 24: Oil and Filter Change

## What you will learn

- Why your Fit needs an oil and filter change, and what oil actually does
- The exact oil type and amount your Fit needs
- The full step-by-step process, from warming the engine to disposing of the old oil
- The one torque number that matters most in this job
- Common mistakes that cause leaks or engine damage

## The short version

Engine oil lubricates the moving parts inside your engine and carries away heat and dirt. Your Fit needs 3.8 US quarts (3.6 L) of 5W-20 oil and a new filter each time you change it. <!-- F-WP09-017, F-WP09-016 --> This is rated as a job that needs the car raised on jack stands, so read Chapter 23 first if you have not already. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->

## The full explanation

### Why oil matters, and what your Fit needs

Oil coats the metal parts inside your engine so they can move against each other without wearing out. Over time, oil picks up heat, dirt, and tiny bits of metal, and it needs to be drained out and replaced.

Your owner's manual calls for API Premium-grade 5W-20 detergent oil, carrying the API Certification Seal marked "For Gasoline Engines." <!-- F-WP09-016 --> Honda's own motor oil is the preferred choice, though a synthetic oil that meets the same seal and viscosity is acceptable too. <!-- F-WP09-016 --> No oil additive is needed or recommended. <!-- F-WP09-016 -->

A full oil change, with a new filter, takes 3.8 US quarts (3.6 L) of oil. <!-- F-WP09-017 --> Without a filter change, it takes slightly less, 3.6 US quarts (3.4 L). <!-- F-WP09-018 --> After a full engine rebuild, when the engine is completely dry, it takes 4.4 US quarts (4.2 L). <!-- F-WP09-019 --> You will not need that last figure for a routine oil change. It is here so you recognize it if you ever see it on a parts receipt or in a shop's paperwork.

### When to change it

Your Fit uses a computer called the Maintenance Minder instead of a printed mileage chart, as Chapter 21 explains in full. Even so, one fixed rule always applies: if a full 12 months pass without the display asking for an oil change, change the oil anyway. <!-- F-WP09-014 -->

### The one number to memorize: drain bolt torque

The bolt at the bottom of your oil pan that lets old oil drain out is called the drain bolt. Tighten it to 29 lbf·ft (39 N·m, 4.0 kgf·m) with a torque wrench, no more and no less. <!-- F-WP10-002 --> Honda gives no specific torque number for the oil filter itself. Instead, install a new filter according to the instructions printed on it. This usually means turning it by hand until it is snug against the engine, plus a small extra turn. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->

## Step by step: Change the engine oil and filter

**Safety warnings, before you start:**
- Always let the engine run first, then shut it off. The manual says to run the engine to normal temperature before draining so the oil flows and carries out contaminants. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->
- Always let the engine and exhaust cool before you touch anything underneath the car, once you shut it off. Hot oil and hot metal can burn you. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->
- Do not get under a vehicle held up only by a jack. Use jack stands, as Chapter 23 explains. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->
- Always wear eye protection and gloves. Used oil and fresh oil can irritate skin and eyes. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->
- Do not dump used oil in the trash or on the ground. It is hazardous to the environment. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->
- Always clean up any spilled oil right away. Spilled oil can damage engine parts and creates a slip hazard. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->

**Steps:**
1. Warm up the engine by running it, then shut it off and set the parking brake. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->
2. Raise the front of the car and support it on jack stands, following Chapter 23. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->
3. Open the hood and remove the engine oil fill cap so air can enter as oil drains. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->
4. Slide the drain pan under the drain bolt at the bottom of the engine. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->
5. Loosen and remove the drain bolt and its washer with your socket. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->
6. Let all the oil drain fully into the pan; this can take several minutes. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->
7. Wipe the drain area, then reinstall the drain bolt with a brand-new washer. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->
8. Tighten the drain bolt to 29 lbf·ft (39 N·m) with a torque wrench. <!-- F-WP10-002 -->
9. Move the drain pan under the oil filter and remove the old filter with the filter wrench. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->
10. Check that the old filter's rubber gasket did not stick to the engine; remove it if it did. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->
11. Wipe a thin film of new oil on the new filter's gasket, then thread it on by hand until snug. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->
12. Lower the car back to the ground and remove the jack stands. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->
13. Pour in 3.8 US quarts (3.6 L) of the recommended 5W-20 oil through the funnel. <!-- F-WP09-017, F-WP09-016 -->
14. Reinstall the oil fill cap and tighten it securely. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->
15. Start the engine and watch the oil pressure light; it should go out within 5 seconds. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->
16. Let the engine run a few minutes, then shut it off and check the drain bolt and filter for leaks. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->
17. Wait several more minutes, then check the level on the dipstick, the thin metal rod you pull out to see the oil level, and top up if needed. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->
18. Seal the used oil in a container and take it to a recycling center. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->

**How you know it worked:** the oil pressure warning light goes out within 5 seconds of starting the engine. There are no drips under the car after it has sat overnight. The dipstick reads between the marks. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->

## What can go wrong

Overfilling the engine past the dipstick's upper mark can damage the engine; the manual warns against this directly. <!-- P-WP11-1 --> Forgetting the new drain-bolt washer, called a crush washer, causes a slow leak. <!-- P-WP11-1 --> Over-tightening the oil filter by hand past snug can make it very hard to remove next time and can crush its gasket. <!-- P-WP11-1 --> Not fully re-torquing the drain bolt, or over-torquing it, can strip the pan's threads. <!-- P-WP11-1, F-WP10-002 --> And working under a car held up by only a jack, instead of jack stands, is the most dangerous mistake on this list. <!-- P-WP11-1 -->

## Quick check

1. What kind and amount of oil does your Fit need for a full oil change with a new filter?
2. What torque should you use on the oil drain bolt, and with what tool?
3. Why should you run the engine before draining the oil, and let it cool before you go underneath?
4. What is a crush washer, and what happens if you forget to replace it?
5. What should the oil pressure light do within 5 seconds of starting the engine, and what does that tell you?

<!-- Fact/Procedure IDs used: P-WP11-1, F-WP09-016, F-WP09-017, F-WP09-018, F-WP09-019, F-WP09-014, F-WP10-002 -->

F-WP09-001 through F-WP09-003, F-WP09-009 through F-WP09-013, F-WP09-015 (Maintenance Minder mechanics and other item codes): covered fully in Chapter 21; this chapter only needs the one fixed annual-oil-change rule (F-WP09-014).
F-WP10-001 (lug nut torque): not part of an oil change; used in Chapters 23, 26, and 27 instead.

# Chapter 25: Air Filter and Cabin Filter

## What you will learn

- What the engine air filter and the cabin air filter each do, and why they are different
- The exact part numbers Honda used for your Fit
- When to replace each filter
- How to replace both filters, step by step, with no jacking or fluids involved
- Common mistakes that reduce airflow instead of improving it

## The short version

Your Fit has two separate air filters. The engine air filter cleans air before it reaches the engine, and the cabin air filter cleans air coming through your heater and air conditioner. <!-- F-WP09-005, F-WP09-006 --> Both jobs are simple, need no tools beyond a screwdriver, and take well under 20 minutes combined. <!-- P-WP11-2, P-WP11-3 -->

## The full explanation

### Two filters, two jobs

The **engine air filter** sits in a black plastic housing in the engine bay and cleans the air the engine breathes before that air reaches the fuel injection system. <!-- P-WP11-2 --> Its part number is Honda 17220-PWA-505, and it fits the 2007-2008 Fit; this number replaced an older part, 17220-PWA-J10. <!-- F-WP09-040 -->

The **cabin air filter**, also called the dust and pollen filter, cleans the air that comes through your heater and air conditioner vents before it reaches you. <!-- F-WP09-006 --> Its part number is Honda 80291-SAA-J01, and it fits the 2007-2008 Fit; this number has since been superseded by 80291-SAA-316. <!-- F-WP09-042 --> This filter is fitted on models with air conditioning, per the owner's manual. <!-- P-WP11-3 -->

### When to replace each one

Both filters follow the Maintenance Minder's own timing under normal driving, with no fixed mileage number printed in the owner's manual. <!-- F-WP09-005, F-WP09-006 --> Two exceptions apply. If you drive mostly in dusty conditions, replace the engine air filter every 15,000 miles (24,000 km). <!-- F-WP09-005 --> If you drive mostly in urban areas with heavy soot from industry or diesel traffic, replace the cabin filter every 15,000 miles (24,000 km). <!-- F-WP09-006 --> Replace it sooner if airflow from your heater or air conditioner feels weaker than usual. <!-- F-WP09-006 -->

## Step by step

### Replace the engine air filter

**Safety warnings, before you start:**
- Always let the engine cool before opening the hood if it has just been running. Parts near the filter box can be hot. <!-- P-WP11-2 -->
- Do not start the engine with the air filter housing open or the element removed. <!-- P-WP11-2 -->

**Steps:**
1. Open the hood and find the air filter housing, a black plastic box on the driver's side of the engine bay. <!-- P-WP11-2 -->
2. Release the clips holding the two halves of the housing together (there are usually four). <!-- P-WP11-2 -->
3. Lift the top half of the housing up and out of the way. <!-- P-WP11-2 -->
4. Note which way the old filter element sits before you remove it. <!-- P-WP11-2 -->
5. Lift out the old, dirty filter element. <!-- P-WP11-2 -->
6. Wipe out any dust or leaves from inside the housing with a dry rag. <!-- P-WP11-2 -->
7. Place the new filter element in the same orientation as the old one. <!-- P-WP11-2 -->
8. Close the top half of the housing and press down until every clip snaps shut. <!-- P-WP11-2 -->
9. Check that all clips are fully closed before starting the engine. <!-- P-WP11-2 -->

**How you know it worked:** the housing halves sit flush together, every clip is snapped closed, and the engine starts and idles normally. <!-- P-WP11-2 -->

### Replace the cabin (dust and pollen) filter

**Safety warnings, before you start:**
- Do not force the glove box past its stops. You can break the hinge or the stop tabs. <!-- P-WP11-3 -->

**Steps:**
1. Open the glove box fully. <!-- P-WP11-3 -->
2. Squeeze both sides of the glove box inward to release the stop tabs. <!-- P-WP11-3 -->
3. Let the glove box swing down out of the way. <!-- P-WP11-3 -->
4. Find the filter tray in the housing behind the glove box opening. <!-- P-WP11-3 -->
5. Pinch the two clips on the tray's left and right sides and pull the tray out. <!-- P-WP11-3 -->
6. Slide the old filter out of the tray. <!-- P-WP11-3 -->
7. Look at the arrow printed on the new filter's frame. <!-- P-WP11-3 -->
8. Slide the new filter into the tray with the arrow pointing down. <!-- P-WP11-3 -->
9. Push the tray back into the housing until both clips click. <!-- P-WP11-3 -->
10. Swing the glove box back up and press it closed. <!-- P-WP11-3 -->

**How you know it worked:** the glove box closes and latches normally, and air from the vents feels stronger than before if the old filter was clogged. <!-- P-WP11-3 -->

## What can go wrong

Forcing a stiff clip on the engine air filter housing can crack the plastic. <!-- P-WP11-2 --> Installing the new engine air filter upside down or backward, or leaving one clip unlatched, lets unfiltered air leak past the filter instead of through it. <!-- P-WP11-2 --> On the cabin filter, installing it with the arrow pointing the wrong way can reduce airflow instead of improving it. <!-- P-WP11-3 --> Not seating both tray clips lets the tray rattle or fall loose later. <!-- P-WP11-3 -->

## Quick check

1. What is the difference between the engine air filter and the cabin air filter?
2. What is the part number for your Fit's engine air filter, and what older number did it replace?
3. Under what driving condition should you replace the engine air filter every 15,000 miles instead of waiting on the Maintenance Minder?
4. Which way should the arrow point when you install a new cabin filter?
5. What can happen if you force a stiff clip on the air filter housing instead of releasing it properly?

<!-- Fact/Procedure IDs used: P-WP11-2, P-WP11-3, F-WP09-005, F-WP09-006, F-WP09-040, F-WP09-042 -->
<!-- Unused IDs and reason:
F-WP09-041, F-WP09-043 (restated air/cabin filter intervals): duplicate F-WP09-005/F-WP09-006 already used above, so not cited again separately.
F-WP09-002 through F-WP09-004, F-WP09-007 through F-WP09-039, F-WP09-044 through F-WP09-061 (other Maintenance Minder items, fluids, spark plugs, bulbs, and battery): belong to other chapters (21, 24, 27-32), not to the two filters covered here.
F-WP10-* (torque and fitment numbers): no bolt in this chapter needs a torque spec, and no tire/wheel fitment number applies to an air filter, so none of Appendix C or E's facts are used here.

# Chapter 26: Tires — Pressure, Rotation, Wear, Replacement, the Spare

## What you will learn

- Your Fit's correct tire pressure, tire size, and wheel size
- How to check and set tire pressure the right way
- How to rotate your tires safely, and the torque your wheel nuts need
- What to look for when checking tires for wear
- Where to find the flat-tire and spare-tire change procedure in this book

## The short version

Your Fit's base trim uses 32 psi (220 kPa) in every tire, front and rear, and the compact spare needs 60 psi (420 kPa). <!-- F-WP10-010 --> Checking pressure takes about 10 minutes and needs only a gauge. <!-- P-WP11-4 --> Rotating all four tires needs jack stands and a torque wrench, and takes about 45 minutes. <!-- P-WP11-5 -->

## The full explanation

### Your Fit's tire and wheel numbers

Your base-trim Fit's factory tire size is P175/65R14 81S, mounted on a 14-inch by 5.5J steel wheel with a full wheel cover. <!-- F-WP10-011, F-WP10-012 --> The wheel bolts on a 4x100 mm pattern, meaning four lug nuts arranged around a circle 100 mm across. <!-- F-WP10-013 --> The wheel's center bore, the hole in the middle that centers the wheel on the hub, measures 56.1 mm. <!-- F-WP10-014 --> Offset means how far the wheel's mounting face sits from its centerline. The factory wheel's offset is ET45, within a reported range of 43 to 47 mm for this tire and wheel combination. <!-- F-WP10-015 -->

### Recommended pressure

Both base and Sport trims use the same cold tire pressure: 32 psi (220 kPa, 2.2 kgf/cm²), front and rear. <!-- F-WP10-010 --> The compact spare tire needs a higher pressure, 60 psi (420 kPa, 4.2 kgf/cm²), since it is a smaller, temporary tire. <!-- F-WP10-010 --> "Cold" means the tire has been parked for at least 3 hours, or driven less than 1 mile (1.6 km). <!-- P-WP11-4 --> A hot tire reads 4 to 6 psi higher than its true pressure, and this can fool you into underfilling it. <!-- P-WP11-4 -->

### Rotating your tires

Rotating tires means moving each wheel to a new position on the car, so all four tires wear more evenly. This is a Maintenance Minder sub item, with no fixed mileage number printed in the owner's manual; the Minder decides the timing. <!-- F-WP09-004 --> Whichever pattern you use, torque every wheel nut to 80 lbf·ft (108 N·m) once the car is back on the ground. <!-- F-WP10-001 -->

### Checking for wear

While you check pressure or rotate tires, look each tire over for bulges, cuts, or objects stuck in the tread. <!-- P-WP11-4 --> These sources do not give a specific tread-depth number for deciding when a tire needs replacing. We could not confirm a tread-wear replacement number for the 2008 Fit from this chapter's sources. Check your tire's own wear indicators, small raised bars molded into the tread, or ask a tire shop.

### The spare tire

Your Fit carries a compact spare tire for roadside use. Chapter 16 covers the full step-by-step procedure for changing a flat tire and using that spare, including the jacking steps, so this chapter does not repeat it. Come back to Chapter 16 if you have a flat on the road; use this chapter for routine pressure checks and rotations instead.

## Step by step

### Check and set your tire pressure

**Safety warnings, before you start:**
- Always check pressure when the tires are cold: parked 3+ hours, or driven less than 1 mile (1.6 km). Hot tires read 4-6 psi high and will fool you into underfilling them. <!-- P-WP11-4 -->
- Do not let air out of a hot tire to try to match the cold-pressure number; you will underinflate it. <!-- P-WP11-4 -->
- Always check the spare tire too, not just the four road tires. <!-- P-WP11-4 -->

**Steps:**
1. Find the recommended pressures on the driver's doorjamb sticker, or use 32 psi front and rear as a cold-pressure fallback. <!-- F-WP10-010 -->
2. Remove the valve cap from the first tire and set it somewhere you won't lose it. <!-- P-WP11-4 -->
3. Press the tire gauge firmly onto the valve stem until the hiss stops and read the number. <!-- P-WP11-4 -->
4. Compare the reading to the recommended pressure for that tire. <!-- P-WP11-4 -->
5. If the pressure is low, add air in short bursts, checking often, until it matches the number. <!-- P-WP11-4 -->
6. If the pressure is too high, press the small pin inside the valve to release a little air, then recheck. <!-- P-WP11-4 -->
7. Reinstall the valve cap. <!-- P-WP11-4 -->
8. Repeat steps 2 through 7 for the other three tires. <!-- P-WP11-4 -->
9. Check the compact spare the same way; it should be 60 psi (420 kPa). <!-- F-WP10-010, P-WP11-4 -->
10. While you are down there, look each tire over for bulges, cuts, or objects stuck in the tread. <!-- P-WP11-4 -->

**How you know it worked:** every tire, including the spare, reads within about 1 psi of its recommended cold pressure, and no tire shows a bulge, cut, or embedded object. <!-- P-WP11-4 -->

### Rotate the tires

**Safety warnings, before you start:**
- Do not work under a car held up only by a jack. Use four jack stands. <!-- P-WP11-5 -->
- Always loosen every wheel nut a bit before you raise that corner off the ground. <!-- P-WP11-5 -->
- Always chock the wheels that stay on the ground before you start jacking. <!-- P-WP11-5 -->
- Do not fully tighten wheel nuts with the wheel hanging free; snug them, lower the car, then torque them on the ground. <!-- P-WP11-5 -->

**Steps:**
1. Park on level, solid ground and set the parking brake. <!-- P-WP11-5 -->
2. Chock the wheels you are not lifting. <!-- P-WP11-5 -->
3. Loosen each wheel nut about a half turn while all four tires are still on the ground. <!-- P-WP11-5 -->
4. Raise one corner and support it on a jack stand; remove that wheel. <!-- P-WP11-5 -->
5. Repeat step 4 for the other three corners so all four wheels are off. <!-- P-WP11-5 -->
6. Move each wheel to its new position, front-to-back and side-to-side per the rotation chart, unless your tires are directional. A **directional tire** has a tread pattern designed to roll only one way, marked with an arrow on the sidewall. <!-- P-WP11-5 -->
7. If your tires are marked directional, only swap front-to-back on the same side, never side-to-side. <!-- P-WP11-5 -->
8. Put each wheel back on its new hub and thread the nuts on by hand. <!-- P-WP11-5 -->
9. Snug the nuts in a crisscross (star) pattern with the wrench, one corner at a time. <!-- P-WP11-5 -->
10. Lower the car until the tire touches the ground but still has some jack support. <!-- P-WP11-5 -->
11. Torque each wheel nut to 80 lbf·ft (108 N·m) in the crisscross pattern. <!-- F-WP10-001, P-WP11-5 -->
12. Lower the car fully and remove the jack stands. <!-- P-WP11-5 -->
13. Check and set the pressure in all four tires once they are all back on the ground. <!-- P-WP11-4, P-WP11-5 -->

**How you know it worked:** every wheel nut is torqued to 80 lbf·ft. No wheel wobbles when you drive at low speed. The tires are now in their new rotation positions. <!-- F-WP10-001, P-WP11-5 -->

## What can go wrong

Checking pressure right after driving gives a falsely high reading. <!-- P-WP11-4 --> Using a different gauge each time, without noticing it reads differently from your own gauge, causes confusion over time. <!-- P-WP11-4 --> Forgetting the spare tire is easy, and it slowly loses pressure over months even if it is never used. <!-- P-WP11-4 --> Ignoring a low-tire-pressure warning light and skipping the physical check anyway defeats the point of having the light. <!-- P-WP11-4 -->

During rotation, trying to fully torque a wheel nut while the wheel still hangs free in the air can leave it improperly seated. <!-- P-WP11-5 --> Skipping the crisscross tightening pattern can seat the wheel unevenly. <!-- P-WP11-5 --> Rotating directional tires side-to-side puts the tread pattern running backward. <!-- P-WP11-5 --> Forgetting to re-torque wheel nuts after the first 50 to 100 miles of driving on a re-used wheel is another common miss. <!-- P-WP11-5 -->

## Quick check

1. What pressure should every tire on your base-trim Fit hold, and what pressure does the spare need?
2. Why should you check tire pressure only when the tires are cold?
3. What torque should you use on your wheel nuts, and when in the rotation process should you apply it?
4. What is a directional tire, and how does rotating one differ from rotating a regular tire?
5. Where in this book should you look for the full flat-tire change procedure?

<!-- Fact/Procedure IDs used: P-WP11-4, P-WP11-5, F-WP09-004, F-WP10-001, F-WP10-010, F-WP10-011, F-WP10-012, F-WP10-013, F-WP10-014, F-WP10-015 -->
<!-- Unused IDs and reason:
F-WP10-016 (largest tire size the community runs without rubbing): tagged for Chapter 49 in facts/WP-10.md, not Chapter 26, and it is a modification-community fact rather than a stock-maintenance fact, so it is left for Part VIII.
F-WP10-002 through F-WP10-009 (oil, spark plug, caliper, strut, and battery torque values): not tire-related; used in Chapters 24, 27, 28, 29, and 41 instead.
Procedure P-WP07-1 (change a flat tire, in facts/procedures for Chapter 16): intentionally not repeated here per the writer brief; this chapter cross-references Chapter 16 instead of restating its steps.
-->
