# Chapter 27: Brakes — Inspection, Pads, Rotors, Fluid

## What you will learn

- Why your Fit's front and rear brakes are not the same type
- How to inspect your front brake pads without removing the wheel
- How to replace front brake pads, and how to replace rear brake shoes
- How to bleed your brakes and what fluid to use
- Safety warnings that apply to every brake job in this chapter

## The short version

Your 2008 Fit's front brakes are disc brakes, and its rear brakes are drum brakes, not disc. <!-- P-WP11-8 --> This matters because the rear job uses shoes and springs instead of pads and a caliper, and it is a different repair. Every brake job in this chapter needs jack stands, eye protection, and patience with springs under tension.

## The full explanation

### Front disc brakes, rear drum brakes

A **disc brake** uses a flat metal disc, called a **rotor**, that spins with the wheel. A **caliper** is a clamp that squeezes flat pads against both sides of the rotor to slow it down. <!-- P-WP11-6 --> A **drum brake** works differently. A hollow metal drum spins with the wheel, and curved brake **shoes** press outward against the inside of the drum to slow it down. <!-- P-WP11-8 --> Inside the drum, a small threaded part called the **self-adjuster** (or just the **adjuster**) takes up slack as the shoes wear down. This keeps the brake working correctly without you adjusting it by hand every time. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->

Honda's own owner's manual states plainly that your Fit's front brakes are disc, and its rear brakes are drum. <!-- P-WP11-8 --> This applies across the 2008 Fit lineup, with no separate statement carving out a different design for base trim. <!-- P-WP11-8 --> Do not assume your rear brakes have pads and a caliper like the front; they do not.

### Brake fluid

Your brake system, and your clutch system if you drive a manual, both use Honda Heavy Duty Brake Fluid DOT 3. <!-- F-WP09-032 --> A DOT 3 or DOT 4 fluid from a sealed container works as a temporary substitute. <!-- F-WP09-032 --> Never use DOT 5 brake fluid; it is not compatible with your Fit's braking system and can cause serious damage. <!-- F-WP09-032 --> On a manual-transmission Fit, the clutch pedal is operated by hydraulic fluid from the same specification, held in its own reservoir with a gray cap. <!-- F-WP09-033 --> Replace your brake fluid every 3 years, no matter what the Maintenance Minder display shows. <!-- F-WP09-013 -->

### Torque and bolt sizes: what is confirmed, and what is not

Your wheel nuts, front or rear, torque to 80 lbf·ft (108 N·m) whenever a wheel comes off for brake work. <!-- F-WP10-001 --> Owners report a front caliper bracket-to-knuckle bolt torque of around 80 lbf·ft. <!-- F-WP10-005 --> This number comes from two community sources discussing a Fit Sport, not a confirmed base-trim source, so treat it as a starting point, not a certainty. <!-- F-WP10-005 --> No confirmed number exists yet for a rear caliper bracket bolt on this car. <!-- F-WP10-006 --> Since your Fit's rear brakes are drum, not disc, there is no rear caliper to begin with. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->

## Step by step

### Inspect the front brake pads through the wheel

**Safety warnings, before you start:**
- Always let the brakes cool before you get close to the wheel if you just drove. The caliper and rotor can be very hot. <!-- P-WP11-6 -->
- Do not touch the wheel or brake parts with bare hands right after driving. <!-- P-WP11-6 -->

**Steps:**
1. Park on level ground and set the parking brake. <!-- P-WP11-6 -->
2. Turn the steering wheel toward you so you can see through the wheel spokes at that corner. <!-- P-WP11-6 -->
3. Shine the flashlight through the spokes at the caliper, the metal bracket that squeezes the pads onto the rotor. <!-- P-WP11-6 -->
4. Look for the pad material pressed against the flat, shiny rotor surface on each side of the caliper. <!-- P-WP11-6 -->
5. Estimate the pad's thickness; a healthy pad is clearly thicker than a thin metal backing plate. <!-- P-WP11-6 -->
6. Compare the inside and outside pad if you can see both; they should wear about evenly. <!-- P-WP11-6 -->
7. Repeat steps 2 through 6 on the other front wheel. <!-- P-WP11-6 -->
8. Listen for a metallic screeching sound the next time you brake gently; the manual says this warns you the pads need replacing. <!-- P-WP11-6 -->

**How you know it worked:** you can see both pads on each front wheel and estimate their thickness. Or, you learn you cannot see enough, and you need to remove the wheel or have a shop check them. <!-- P-WP11-6 -->

### Replace the front brake pads

**Safety warnings, before you start:**
- Always wear eye protection. Brake dust and springs under tension can hurt your eyes. <!-- P-WP11-7 -->
- Do not breathe brake dust; never use compressed air or a dry brush on brake parts, per the manual's own maintenance-safety caution. Use brake-parts cleaner instead. <!-- P-WP11-7 -->
- Do not work under a car held up only by a jack. Use jack stands. <!-- P-WP11-7 -->
- Always work on one side at a time and leave the other wheel on the ground so you have a reference for how parts go back together. <!-- P-WP11-7 -->
- Do not let the caliper hang by its brake hose; support or hang it so the hose has no weight on it. <!-- P-WP11-7 -->
- Always pump the brake pedal several times before driving, until it feels firm, before you rely on the brakes again. <!-- P-WP11-7 -->

**Steps:**
1. Loosen the front wheel nuts, then raise and support the car on jack stands. <!-- P-WP11-7 -->
2. Remove the wheel and set it aside. <!-- P-WP11-7 -->
3. Look at how the old pads and springs sit before you remove anything, or take a photo. <!-- P-WP11-7 -->
4. Remove the caliper slide-pin bolts and lift the caliper off the bracket. <!-- P-WP11-7 -->
5. Support the caliper with a hook or bungee so it does not hang by the hose. <!-- P-WP11-7 -->
6. Pull the old pads out of the bracket. <!-- P-WP11-7 -->
7. Use the C-clamp against the old pad to push the caliper piston back into its bore. <!-- P-WP11-7 -->
8. Clean the bracket and slide pins with a wire brush and brake-parts cleaner. <!-- P-WP11-7 -->
9. Apply a thin coat of brake grease to the pad backing and slide pins. <!-- P-WP11-7 -->
10. Place the new pads into the bracket. <!-- P-WP11-7 -->
11. Slide the caliper back over the new pads and reinstall the slide-pin bolts. <!-- P-WP11-7 -->
12. Reinstall the wheel and snug the nuts by hand. <!-- P-WP11-7 -->
13. Lower the car, then torque the wheel nuts to 80 lbf·ft (108 N·m) in a crisscross pattern. <!-- F-WP10-001, P-WP11-7 -->
14. Repeat steps 1 through 13 on the other front wheel. <!-- P-WP11-7 -->
15. Pump the brake pedal several times with the engine off until it feels firm before driving. <!-- P-WP11-7 -->
16. Check the brake fluid reservoir level and top it off if needed. <!-- P-WP11-7 -->
17. Drive slowly at first and test the brakes gently before returning to normal driving. <!-- P-WP11-7 -->

**How you know it worked:** the brake pedal feels firm, not soft or spongy. The car stops evenly with no pulling to one side. There is no grinding or squealing under light braking. <!-- P-WP11-7 -->

### Replace the rear brake shoes (drum brakes)

**Safety warnings, before you start:**
- Always wear eye protection. Springs are under strong tension and can snap free unexpectedly. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->
- Do not breathe brake dust; never use compressed air or a dry brush on brake parts. Use brake-parts cleaner instead. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->
- Do not work under a car held up only by a jack. Use jack stands. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->
- Always work on one side at a time, using the untouched side as your reference for how everything goes back together. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->
- Do not get your fingers between a compressed spring and the backing plate. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->
- Always keep grease off the brake shoe linings and the drum's inner surface; grease on the linings ruins braking. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->
- Always press the brake pedal several times after reassembly, before driving, to seat the shoes and set the self-adjuster. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->

**Steps:**
1. Loosen the rear wheel nuts, then raise and support the car on jack stands. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->
2. Remove the wheel and set it aside. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->
3. Release the parking brake, then pull the drum straight off the hub (tap gently if it is stuck). <!-- P-WP11-8 -->
4. Take a photo of the spring and lever layout before touching anything. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->
5. Unhook the lower return spring with your spring tool or pliers. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->
6. Unhook the upper return spring the same way. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->
7. Remove the hold-down springs and pins that pin each shoe to the backing plate. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->
8. Lift out the old shoes together with the adjuster and parking-brake link still attached. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->
9. Transfer the adjuster, parking-brake lever, and springs onto the new shoes in the same layout as the photo. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->
10. Apply a thin dab of high-temperature grease to the backing plate's contact pads and the parking-brake lever pivot, keeping grease off the linings. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->
11. Set the new shoes onto the backing plate and reinstall the hold-down pins and springs. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->
12. Hook the lower return spring back into place. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->
13. Use your C-clamps or spring pliers to compress the shoes enough to hook the upper return spring. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->
14. Check that the shoes sit fully in their bosses (the raised mounting pads that hold each shoe in place) and the adjuster turns freely. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->
15. Slide the drum back onto the hub. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->
16. Reinstall the wheel and snug the nuts by hand. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->
17. Lower the car, then torque the wheel nuts to 80 lbf·ft (108 N·m) in a crisscross pattern. <!-- F-WP10-001, P-WP11-8 -->
18. Repeat steps 1 through 17 on the other rear wheel. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->
19. Press the brake pedal firmly several times with the engine off until the pedal feels normal. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->
20. Adjust the parking brake if it now feels loose. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->
21. Drive slowly at first and test the brakes gently before returning to normal driving. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->

**How you know it worked:** the brake pedal feels firm, and the parking brake holds the car on a hill. Both rear wheels turn freely with light drag when the car is off the ground. There is no grinding or dragging noise while driving. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->

### Bleed the brakes

**Safety warnings, before you start:**
- Always wear eye protection. Brake fluid can splash and it damages paint and eyes. <!-- P-WP11-9 -->
- Do not let the brake fluid reservoir run dry during bleeding, or you will draw air back into the lines and have to start over. <!-- P-WP11-9 -->
- Do not reuse fluid that comes out of the bleeder; only use fresh fluid from a sealed container. <!-- P-WP11-9 -->
- Always keep brake fluid off painted surfaces; wipe up any spill immediately. <!-- P-WP11-9 -->
- Do not drive the car until the pedal feels firm and consistent, not soft or sinking. <!-- P-WP11-9 -->

**Steps:**
1. Remove old fluid from the reservoir with the baster, leaving a little so it does not run dry. <!-- P-WP11-9 -->
2. Fill the reservoir with fresh Honda Heavy Duty Brake Fluid DOT 3. <!-- F-WP09-032, P-WP11-9 -->
3. Slide clear tubing over the bleeder screw at the first wheel and route the other end into your catch bottle. <!-- P-WP11-9 -->
4. Have your helper press the brake pedal down and hold it. <!-- P-WP11-9 -->
5. Open the bleeder screw briefly to let fluid and air escape, then close it before the pedal is released. <!-- P-WP11-9 -->
6. Have your helper release the pedal only after the bleeder screw is closed. <!-- P-WP11-9 -->
7. Repeat steps 4 through 6 until no more air bubbles appear in the tubing. <!-- P-WP11-9 -->
8. Check the reservoir level and top it off before moving to the next wheel. <!-- P-WP11-9 -->
9. Move to the next wheel and repeat steps 3 through 8. <!-- P-WP11-9 -->
10. Recheck the reservoir level after all four wheels and fill to the MAX mark. <!-- P-WP11-9 -->
11. Press the brake pedal several times and confirm it feels firm and consistent, not soft. <!-- P-WP11-9 -->

**How you know it worked:** the pedal feels firm on the first press, not soft or sinking to the floor. No air bubbles appear in the tubing on a final check at each wheel. <!-- P-WP11-9 -->

## What can go wrong

On front pads, pushing the piston back without first opening the brake fluid reservoir cap slightly can push fluid out or damage a seal. <!-- P-WP11-7 --> Forgetting to support the caliper can stretch or kink the brake hose. <!-- P-WP11-7 --> Skipping the pedal-pumping step before your first drive can mean the pedal goes to the floor instead of feeling firm. <!-- P-WP11-7 -->

On rear drum shoes, losing track of how the springs and adjuster were arranged is exactly why a before photo matters so much. <!-- P-WP11-8 --> Getting grease on the shoe linings can cause the brakes to grab or fade. <!-- P-WP11-8 --> Forcing the return spring on without a tool can make it snap loose unexpectedly. <!-- P-WP11-8 --> Not resetting the self-adjuster can leave the parking brake or pedal feeling loose. <!-- P-WP11-8 --> Several independent do-it-yourself writeups single out the main return spring as the one step that trips up first-timers. If you are not comfortable with springs under tension, this is a reasonable job to hand to a shop. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->

On bleeding, letting the reservoir run empty partway through draws in fresh air, undoing your work. <!-- P-WP11-9 --> Opening the bleeder screw too far can let it come out, and over-tightening it can snap it. <!-- P-WP11-9 --> Skipping the final firm-pedal check and driving with air still in a line is the most dangerous mistake in this whole chapter. <!-- P-WP11-9 -->

## Quick check

1. Are your Fit's rear brakes disc or drum? How does that change the parts and tools you need?
2. What brake fluid type does your Fit use, and which type must you never use?
3. How often should you replace your brake fluid, no matter what the Maintenance Minder shows?
4. Name two safety warnings that apply to both the front pad job and the rear shoe job.
5. How do you know your brake bleeding worked?

<!-- Fact/Procedure IDs used: P-WP11-6, P-WP11-7, P-WP11-8, P-WP11-9, F-WP09-013, F-WP09-032, F-WP09-033, F-WP10-001, F-WP10-005, F-WP10-006 -->
<!-- Unused IDs and reason:

# Chapter 28: Battery: Testing, Cleaning, Replacing, Jump Starting

## What you will learn
- What size battery your 2008 Fit uses
- How to test your battery's voltage with a multimeter, a tool that reads electrical charge
- How to clean corrosion, the crusty buildup that forms on battery terminals
- How to replace the battery yourself, and why you need your radio code first
- Where to find the full jump-start steps if the battery goes dead on the road

## The short version

Your Fit uses a 12-volt battery, size group 151R.<!-- F-WP09-046 --> You should check its condition every month.<!-- F-WP09-061 --> A cheap multimeter can tell you if the battery is healthy, half-charged, or failing.<!-- P-WP11-10 --> Corrosion on the terminals is easy to clean with baking soda and water, and replacing the whole battery takes basic tools and about 20 minutes.<!-- P-WP11-11 --> If the battery is already dead and you need to get moving right now, turn to Chapter 16. It covers jump starting in full.<!-- P-WP07-2 -->

## The full explanation

### Your battery, and why you check it monthly

A battery stores electrical power and delivers a big burst of it to start your engine. Your Fit's owner's manual tells you to check the battery's condition once a month.<!-- F-WP09-061 --> Most batteries have a small test indicator window on top. A label on the battery explains what each color in that window means.<!-- F-WP09-061 --> The window's exact location can vary between battery brands, so check the label on your own battery.<!-- F-WP09-061 -->

The battery that came with your Fit is a group size 151R.<!-- F-WP09-046 --> A "group size" is a standard code for a battery's physical dimensions and terminal layout, so the right one fits your battery tray and connects correctly. Some owners report swapping in a group 51R battery instead, since it offers more cold-cranking amps, a rating of how much starting power a battery can deliver in cold weather. Owners report that a 51R is a noticeably tighter physical fit, and some say they had to modify the battery tray to make it work.<!-- F-WP09-046 --> For a straightforward replacement with no modification, stick with the original 151R size.<!-- F-WP09-046 -->

A battery does one thing you should always respect: it gives off hydrogen gas, an explosive gas, during normal operation. This is true every time you work near the battery, for any reason. Keep flames, sparks, and cigarettes away from it, and always wear eye protection when you work close to it.

### Testing the battery with a multimeter

A multimeter is a small handheld tool that reads electrical voltage, among other things. To test your battery, you touch its two probes to the battery's two posts and read the number on the screen. A rested battery, meaning the engine has been off at least an hour, should read about 12.6 volts or higher if it is fully charged.<!-- P-WP11-10 --> A reading near 12.2 volts means the battery is only about half charged, and anything below 12.0 volts usually points to a weak or failing battery.<!-- P-WP11-10 --> You can also have a helper crank the engine while you watch the meter; a healthy battery should not drop below about 9.6 volts while doing that.<!-- P-WP11-10 --> Full steps are below.

### Cleaning corroded terminals

Corrosion looks like a white or yellowish, crusty powder around the battery posts and cable clamps. It forms slowly over time as the battery works, and it can weaken your electrical connection or even stop the car from starting. Baking soda mixed with water reacts with this corrosion and breaks it apart, letting you rinse it away. A thin coat of grease afterward slows corrosion from coming right back.

### Replacing the battery

Before you disconnect any cable, write down your radio's 5-digit anti-theft code. Many Honda audio systems lock themselves as soon as the battery loses power, and you need that code to unlock the radio again afterward. Always disconnect the negative cable first and reconnect it last; this order lowers the risk of a spark shorting something in the car's electrical system. The new battery needs to be the same group size, 151R, so it fits the tray and hold-down bracket, the metal piece that clamps the battery in place, without modification.<!-- F-WP09-046 -->

### Jump starting: where to find it

This chapter does not repeat the jump-start steps. Chapter 16 already covers the full procedure, from checking your battery's condition to the exact order for connecting and disconnecting cables.<!-- P-WP07-2 --> If your battery is dead right now, go there.

## Step by step

### Test the battery with a multimeter

**Safety warnings, before you touch a probe to the battery:**
- Always wear eye protection when working near the battery. It gives off explosive hydrogen gas during normal operation.
- Do not smoke or bring sparks or flames near the battery while testing it.
- Do not let the multimeter's metal probes touch both terminals, or any metal, at the same time by accident. This can cause a spark.
- Always wash your hands after handling the battery or its terminals.

**Steps:**
1. Turn off the engine and every accessory, such as lights, radio, and fan, and let the car sit at least 1 hour.<!-- P-WP11-10 -->
2. Set the multimeter dial to 20 volts DC.<!-- P-WP11-10 -->
3. Touch the red probe to the positive (+) battery post.<!-- P-WP11-10 -->
4. Touch the black probe to the negative (-) battery post.<!-- P-WP11-10 -->
5. Read the number on the multimeter's screen. This is the resting voltage.<!-- P-WP11-10 -->
6. Compare the reading to this chart: 12.6 volts or higher means a full, healthy charge.<!-- P-WP11-10 -->
7. A reading near 12.2 volts means the battery is only about half charged.<!-- P-WP11-10 -->
8. A reading below 12.0 volts usually means a weak or failing battery.<!-- P-WP11-10 -->
9. Optional: have a helper crank the engine while you watch the meter. A healthy battery should not drop below about 9.6 volts while cranking.<!-- P-WP11-10 -->
10. If the reading is low, or the battery struggles to crank the engine, plan to charge or replace it.<!-- P-WP11-10 -->

**How you know it worked:** you have a clear resting-voltage number and, if you did the cranking check, a cranking-voltage number, and you know which category the battery falls into.

### Replace the battery

**Safety warnings, before you loosen a cable clamp:**
- Always wear eye protection and protective clothing, or have a skilled technician do this. The battery gives off explosive hydrogen gas.
- Do not smoke or bring sparks or flames near the battery.
- Always disconnect the negative (-) cable first, and reconnect it last, to reduce the risk of a spark shorting the vehicle's electrical system.
- Always wash your hands after handling the battery, its posts, or its terminals. They contain lead and lead compounds.
- Do not drop the battery. It is heavy and can crack, spilling acid.

**Steps:**
1. Turn off the engine and every accessory.<!-- P-WP11-11 -->
2. Write down or confirm you have your radio's 5-digit anti-theft code.<!-- P-WP11-11 -->
3. Loosen and remove the negative (-) cable clamp first, and set it aside so it cannot touch the terminal.<!-- P-WP11-11 -->
4. Loosen and remove the positive (+) cable clamp next.<!-- P-WP11-11 -->
5. Remove the bolt or clamp holding the battery hold-down bracket in place.<!-- P-WP11-11 -->
6. Lift the old battery straight out, using a strap or careful grip. It is heavy.<!-- P-WP11-11 -->
7. Set the new battery in the tray, in the same orientation as the old one.<!-- P-WP11-11 -->
8. Reinstall the hold-down bracket and its bolt.<!-- P-WP11-11 -->
9. Reconnect the positive (+) cable clamp first this time, and tighten it.<!-- P-WP11-11 -->
10. Reconnect the negative (-) cable clamp last, and tighten it.<!-- P-WP11-11 -->
11. Start the engine to confirm it runs normally.<!-- P-WP11-11 -->
12. Turn on the radio and enter your 5-digit code if "ENTER CODE" appears.<!-- P-WP11-11 -->
13. Reset the clock if it lost its setting.<!-- P-WP11-11 -->

**How you know it worked:** the engine starts normally, the radio accepts your code, or never asked for one, and the clock is reset if needed.

### Clean the battery terminals

**Safety warnings, before you mix any solution:**
- Always wear eye protection and protective clothing, or have a skilled technician do this. The battery gives off explosive hydrogen gas.
- Do not smoke or bring sparks or flames near the battery.
- Always wash your hands after handling the battery or its terminals. They contain lead and lead compounds.
- Always disconnect the negative (-) cable first if you need to remove a cable, and reconnect it last.

**Steps:**
1. Look at each terminal for a white or yellowish powder. This is corrosion.<!-- P-WP11-12 -->
2. Mix baking soda with a little water into a thick paste.<!-- P-WP11-12 -->
3. Cover the corroded terminal with the baking soda and water solution.<!-- P-WP11-12 -->
4. Watch it bubble up and turn brown as it reacts with the corrosion.<!-- P-WP11-12 -->
5. Once the bubbling stops, wash the terminal off with plain water.<!-- P-WP11-12 -->
6. Dry the battery and terminals with a cloth or paper towel.<!-- P-WP11-12 -->
7. Coat the clean terminals with a thin layer of grease to help prevent future corrosion.<!-- P-WP11-12 -->
8. Check that your radio still works. If it shows "ENTER CODE," enter your 5-digit code.<!-- P-WP11-12 -->

**How you know it worked:** the white or yellow powder is gone, the terminals look clean and shiny, and the cable clamps sit tight on the posts with no wobble.

## What can go wrong

- **Testing right after driving, before the battery has rested.** This gives a falsely high reading. Wait at least an hour with the engine off.
- **Assuming a battery that reads 12.6 volts resting is automatically fine.**<!-- P-WP11-10 --> A battery can pass a resting-voltage test and still fail under starter load. The cranking check catches that.
- **Disconnecting the positive cable first.** This raises the risk of a short if a tool accidentally touches metal. Always take the negative cable off first.
- **Forgetting the radio code before disconnecting the battery.** You may have to dig through old paperwork afterward to find it.
- **Not securing the hold-down bracket.** A loose battery can shift while driving.
- **Buying a non-original battery size without checking the physical fit first.** A group 51R battery is a tighter squeeze than the factory 151R.<!-- F-WP09-046 -->
- **Skipping the grease coating after cleaning terminals.** Corrosion often comes right back within months.
- **Not washing off all the baking soda paste.** This leaves a residue behind on the terminal.

## Quick check

1. What group size battery does your 2008 Fit use from the factory?
2. What voltage reading on a rested battery means a full, healthy charge?
3. Why do you always disconnect the negative cable first and reconnect it last?
4. What should you write down before you disconnect the battery, and why?
5. Where in this book do you go for the full jump-start procedure?

<!-- Fact/Procedure IDs used: F-WP09-046, F-WP09-061, P-WP11-10, P-WP11-11, P-WP11-12, P-WP07-2 (pointer only, not repeated). -->
<!-- Unused related IDs, with reason:
F-WP07-011 through F-WP07-014 (jump-start procedure detail): intentionally not repeated here per the writer task's instruction to cross-reference Chapter 16 rather than duplicate its steps.
F-WP09-060 (monthly owner checks list, general): covered chapter-by-chapter elsewhere; this chapter cites the battery-specific F-WP09-061 instead. -->

# Chapter 29: Spark Plugs

## What you will learn
- What a spark plug does, and which plugs fit your 2008 Fit's engine
- The correct gap and torque for a new spark plug
- How to replace all four spark plugs yourself, step by step
- Why valve clearance, a small engine gap checked at the same visits, is worth knowing about
- What can go wrong during a plug swap, and how to avoid it

## The short version

A spark plug ignites the fuel and air mixture inside your engine's cylinder. Your Fit takes an NGK IZFR6K-13 plug, or the equivalent Denso SKJ20DR-M13.<!-- F-WP09-036 --> The gap, the small space the spark jumps across, should be 1.2 to 1.3 millimeters (0.042 to 0.051 inches) on a new plug.<!-- F-WP09-037 --> Each plug tightens to about 13 pound-feet (18 newton-meters) of torque, a measure of twisting force.<!-- F-WP09-038 --> Replacing all four takes about 40 minutes the first time.<!-- P-WP11-13 -->

## The full explanation

### What spark plugs do, and when yours need changing

Every time a cylinder in your engine fires, a spark plug creates the spark that ignites the fuel and air inside it. Your Fit's owner's manual treats spark plug replacement as a Maintenance Minder sub item. This means the dashboard's Maintenance Minder computer decides when it is due, rather than a fixed mileage printed in the manual.<!-- F-WP09-009 --> No exact number is printed for your specific car.<!-- F-WP09-009 --> Your Fit's plugs are marketed as iridium-style plugs, plugs with a hard metal tip that lasts longer than a standard plug. Some general guidance for this kind of plug points to roughly 100,000 miles. This figure is not specific to the 2008 Fit. We could not confirm it as an exact number for your car, so check your owner's manual and dashboard display instead.<!-- F-WP09-039 -->

### Which plugs, what gap, what torque

Your Fit's Specifications table lists two matching plug part numbers: NGK IZFR6K-13 and Denso SKJ20DR-M13. Either one is correct; they are made to the same specification.<!-- F-WP09-036 -->

A new plug's gap, the small air space between the plug's electrodes where the spark jumps across, should measure 1.2 to 1.3 millimeters, or 0.042 to 0.051 inches.<!-- F-WP09-037 --> Most new plugs ship pre-gapped correctly from the factory. Use a gap gauge to check this measurement, but do not force-adjust a pre-gapped plug unless it is clearly wrong.

Torque means how tight a bolt or plug is turned, measured in force applied at a distance. Each spark plug tightens to about 18 newton-meters, which is 1.8 kilogram-force-meters or 13 pound-feet.<!-- F-WP09-038 --> A torque wrench, a tool that stops or signals once you reach a set tightness, keeps you from cracking the cylinder head's soft aluminum threads by overtightening.

### Valve clearance: a related check at the same visits

Valve clearance is the small gap that lets your engine's valves open and close correctly as they heat up and cool down. Your Fit's manual has the technician check this clearance at the same Maintenance Minder visits, labeled A, B, 1, 2, or 3, where other service happens.<!-- F-WP09-010 --> It only gets adjusted if the engine sounds noisy; there is no separate fixed mileage number for adjusting it.<!-- F-WP09-010 --> Check this on a cold engine, meaning the cylinder head is below 100 degrees Fahrenheit (38 degrees Celsius). The correct clearance is 0.15 to 0.19 millimeters (0.006 to 0.007 inches) on the intake valves.<!-- F-WP09-045 --> The correct clearance is 0.26 to 0.30 millimeters (0.010 to 0.012 inches) on the exhaust valves.<!-- F-WP09-045 --> We could not confirm a do-it-yourself procedure for adjusting valve clearance on this engine from this book's sources. This job is usually left to a technician, so check your owner's manual or a qualified shop if your engine is noisy.

## Step by step

### Replace the spark plugs

**Safety warnings, before you touch a coil or a plug:**
- Always let the engine cool completely before starting. The cylinder head and exhaust stay hot long after the engine is off.
- Do not drop anything into an open spark plug hole. A dropped bolt or tool can badly damage the engine.
- Always work on one plug at a time so you never mix up which coil and boot go where.
- Do not overtighten a plug. A torque wrench prevents cracking the cylinder head threads.

**Steps:**
1. Let the engine cool completely.
2. Open the hood and remove the plastic under-cowl or engine cover panel to reach the coils.
3. Unplug the electrical connector from the first ignition coil.
4. Remove the bolt holding that ignition coil, and lift the coil straight up and out.
5. Look inside the plug hole and clear away any loose dirt with a rag or brush.
6. Fit the spark plug socket over the old plug and turn it counterclockwise to remove it.
7. Inspect the old plug's tip for unusual color or heavy wear.
8. Check the new plug's gap against the specification on its box, but do not force-adjust a pre-gapped plug.
9. Apply a small amount of anti-seize compound, a paste that keeps metal threads from sticking together over time, to the new plug's threads.
10. Thread the new plug in by hand until it stops, to avoid cross-threading.
11. Tighten the plug the rest of the way with a torque wrench to about 13 pound-feet (18 newton-meters).<!-- F-WP09-038 -->
12. Reinstall that ignition coil, its bolt, and its electrical connector.
13. Repeat steps 3 through 12 for each remaining cylinder.
14. Reinstall the under-cowl or engine cover panel.
15. Start the engine and listen for smooth, even running with no misfire.

**How you know it worked:** the engine starts easily, idles smoothly with no shaking or misfire, and the check engine light does not come on after a short drive.

## What can go wrong

- **Threading a plug in with the socket instead of starting it by hand.** This risks cross-threading the soft aluminum cylinder head.
- **Skipping the torque wrench and guessing "snug."** This can under-tighten or over-tighten the plug.
- **Mixing up which coil connector plugs into which coil.** Work on one cylinder at a time to avoid this.
- **Dropping a bolt into the plug hole while the coil is off.** Keep loose tools and hardware well clear of open plug holes.
- **Assuming your engine's plug interval matches general online guidance.** Your Fit's own Maintenance Minder display is the only source that actually tracks your car's driving.<!-- F-WP09-009 -->

## Quick check

1. What two spark plug part numbers fit your 2008 Fit, and are they interchangeable?
2. What gap should a new spark plug have?
3. Why do you thread a new spark plug in by hand before using a torque wrench?
4. How tight should each spark plug be torqued to?
5. What is valve clearance, and when is it adjusted on your engine?

<!-- Fact/Procedure IDs used: F-WP09-009, F-WP09-010, F-WP09-036, F-WP09-037, F-WP09-038, F-WP09-039, F-WP09-045, P-WP11-13. -->
<!-- Unused related IDs, with reason: none from WP-09 tagged to Chapter 29 were left out. F-WP09-040 through F-WP09-043 (air/cabin filter part numbers, tagged Chapter 25) and F-WP09-044 (timing chain, tagged Chapter 31) are outside this chapter's scope and are used in their own chapters instead. -->

# Chapter 30: Coolant, Transmission Fluid, Brake Fluid, Power Steering, and Washer Fluid

## What you will learn
- What coolant type your 2008 Fit needs, and how much it holds
- How to check the coolant level and top it up, and how to do a full drain and fill
- What transmission fluid your car needs, and how to check and change it for your transmission type
- What brake and clutch fluid your Fit uses, and the one fixed rule that always applies
- Why this car has no power steering fluid to check at all
- What washer fluid to use, and what never to put in that reservoir

## The short version

Your Fit uses five separate fluids under the hood, besides engine oil: coolant, transmission fluid, brake fluid, clutch fluid on manual-transmission cars, and washer fluid.<!-- F-WP09-011, F-WP09-008, F-WP09-013, F-WP09-033, F-WP09-035 --> There is no power steering fluid on this car at all, because your Fit steers with an electric motor instead of hydraulic pressure.<!-- F-WP09-034 --> Each fluid has its own correct type; using the wrong one can cause real damage, so this chapter tells you exactly which one goes where.

## The full explanation

### Engine coolant

Coolant is the liquid that flows through your engine and radiator to carry heat away and keep the engine from overheating. Your Fit's owner's manual calls for Honda Long-Life Antifreeze/Coolant Type 2, which comes pre-mixed at 50 percent antifreeze and 50 percent water.<!-- F-WP09-020 --> Never add straight, undiluted antifreeze or plain water on its own.<!-- F-WP09-020 --> If Honda's coolant is not available, another major-brand coolant made for aluminum engines, and free of silicates, can be used temporarily. Flush the system and refill it with Honda coolant as soon as you reasonably can.<!-- F-WP09-020 -->

Replacing the coolant entirely is a Maintenance Minder sub item, so the dashboard display decides when it is due; no fixed mileage number is printed.<!-- F-WP09-011 --> The system's capacity depends on which transmission your car has. After a coolant change, a manual-transmission Fit holds 3.8 liters (1.00 US gallon), and an automatic-transmission Fit holds 3.7 liters (0.98 US gallon).<!-- F-WP09-021, F-WP09-022 --> After a full engine overhaul, the total capacity is 5.4 liters (1.43 US gallons) for a manual, or 5.3 liters (1.40 US gallons) for an automatic.<!-- F-WP09-023, F-WP09-024 --> The separate reserve tank, the see-through plastic tank you check day to day, holds 0.4 liters (0.11 US gallon) on its own.<!-- F-WP09-025 -->

### Transmission fluid

Transmission fluid keeps your gearbox's internal parts lubricated and, on an automatic, also carries the hydraulic pressure that shifts gears. Replacing it is a Maintenance Minder sub item on both transmission types, so the dashboard decides normal timing.<!-- F-WP09-008 --> Two fixed exceptions apply only to automatic-transmission Fits. If you regularly drive at low speed in mountains, change the fluid at 60,000 miles (100,000 kilometers), then every 30,000 miles (48,000 kilometers) after that.<!-- F-WP09-008 --> If you tow the car behind a motorhome, change it every 2 years or 30,000 miles (48,000 kilometers), whichever comes first.<!-- F-WP09-008 -->

If you have a manual transmission, your Fit uses Honda Manual Transmission Fluid (MTF).<!-- F-WP09-026 --> If it is not available, an SAE 10W-30 or 10W-40 motor oil carrying the API Certification Seal for gasoline engines works as a temporary substitute. Switch back to Honda MTF soon, since plain motor oil lacks the additives your gearbox needs and can make shifting stiffer over time.<!-- F-WP09-026 --> The **differential** is the gearbox part that splits engine power between your front wheels. A change takes about 1.6 US quarts (1.5 liters); the total amount, including the differential, is 1.7 US quarts (1.6 liters).<!-- F-WP09-027, F-WP09-028 -->

If you have an automatic transmission, your Fit uses Honda Genuine ATF-Z1 only. Mixing in another brand's automatic transmission fluid (ATF) can wear down your transmission, and Honda's own warranty does not cover damage caused that way.<!-- F-WP09-029 --> A drain-and-refill takes about 2.5 US quarts (2.4 liters); the total system capacity, only reached during a full teardown, is about 6.3 US quarts (6.0 liters).<!-- F-WP09-030, F-WP09-031 --> One note for buying fluid today: Honda's ATF-Z1 has since been discontinued across the industry and replaced by Honda ATF DW-1 for service fills. Ask your parts store for the replacement if the original is not on the shelf.<!-- F-WP09-029 -->

### Brake fluid and clutch fluid

Brake fluid transmits the force from your foot on the brake pedal to the brakes at each wheel. Your Fit's manual calls for Honda Heavy Duty Brake Fluid DOT 3, or a DOT 3 or DOT 4 fluid from a sealed container as a temporary replacement.<!-- F-WP09-032 --> Never use fluid marked DOT 5; it is not compatible with this car's brake system and can cause serious damage.<!-- F-WP09-032 -->

One rule for brake fluid always applies, no matter what the Maintenance Minder display shows: replace it every 3 years.<!-- F-WP09-013 --> This is one of the small set of fixed-schedule items in your whole maintenance schedule, alongside the annual oil change and the 160,000-mile idle speed check covered in Chapter 21.

If your Fit has a manual transmission, it also uses a hydraulic clutch, meaning fluid pressure, not a cable, disengages the clutch when you press the pedal. This clutch fluid shares the exact same specification as the brake fluid above.<!-- F-WP09-033 --> Check that its level sits between the MIN and MAX marks on the reservoir, which has a gray cap. A low level can point to a leak in the clutch hydraulic system.<!-- F-WP09-033 -->

We could not confirm a step-by-step, do-it-yourself procedure for replacing brake or clutch fluid from this book's sources at this pass. Check Chapter 27 for more on your braking system, and Chapter 40 for a full brake job, or have a qualified shop replace this fluid on schedule.

### Power steering: this car does not have hydraulic fluid to check

Power steering is the system that reduces how much effort it takes to turn your steering wheel. Many older cars use a hydraulic pump and a reservoir of power steering fluid for this. Your 2008 Fit does not. It uses Electric Power-Assisted Rack-and-Pinion Steering (EPS) instead, on every trim, which means an electric motor assists your steering rather than pressurized fluid.<!-- F-WP09-034 --> Your owner's manual's Fluid Locations diagram has no power steering fluid reservoir at all. It lists only engine oil, automatic transmission fluid, brake fluid, clutch fluid on manual-transmission cars, washer fluid, and the coolant reservoir.<!-- F-WP09-034 --> Your dashboard has its own dedicated electric power steering indicator instead, confirming this is an electrical system, not a hydraulic one.<!-- F-WP09-034 --> This also means there is no power steering fluid to check, top up, or ever change on your Fit.

### Washer fluid

Washer fluid sprays onto your windshield when you use the washers, so the wipers have something to clean with instead of dry glass. Your Fit's manual calls for any commercially available windshield washer fluid.<!-- F-WP09-035 --> Never put engine antifreeze, or a vinegar-and-water solution, into this reservoir.<!-- F-WP09-035 --> We could not confirm the exact reservoir capacity for the 2008 Fit from this book's sources. Check your owner's manual for that figure, and top the reservoir up when the level runs low.<!-- F-WP09-035 -->

## Step by step

### Check the coolant level and top it up

**Safety warnings, before you touch the radiator cap:**
- Do not remove the radiator cap while the engine is hot. Hot coolant can spray out and scald you badly. Wait until the engine and radiator are cool.
- Do not add plain water alone, or straight, undiluted antifreeze, to the reserve tank.
- Do not add rust inhibitors or other additives. They may not be compatible with the coolant or engine parts.

**Steps:**
1. Park on level ground and let the engine and radiator cool completely.
2. Open the hood and find the coolant reserve tank, a translucent plastic tank, not the radiator itself.
3. Look at the coolant level against the MIN and MAX marks on the side of the tank.
4. If the level is at or below MIN, remove the reserve tank cap.
5. Pour Honda Long-Life Antifreeze/Coolant Type 2 in slowly, watching the level.
6. Fill until the level reaches the MAX mark. Do not overfill.
7. Put the reserve tank cap back on, and make sure it seats fully.
8. Wipe up any spilled coolant right away. It can damage engine bay parts.
9. Look around the reserve tank and radiator hoses for any obvious wet spots or drips that would explain a low level.

**How you know it worked:** the coolant level sits at or near the MAX mark on the reserve tank, the cap is seated, and you find no active leak underneath.

### Drain and fill the coolant

**Safety warnings, before you raise the car or open the radiator:**
- Do not remove the radiator cap while the engine is hot. Hot coolant can spray out and scald you. Wait until the engine and radiator are cool.
- Do not work under a car held up only by a jack. Use jack stands or ramps rated for the weight.
- Always relieve pressure slowly. Turn the radiator cap counterclockwise to the first stop and let it hiss before pressing down and removing it.
- Do not add plain water alone as a permanent fix, and do not mix Honda's concentrated coolant with plain water beyond what the label describes.
- Always clean up spilled coolant immediately. It is toxic to pets and wildlife if left in a puddle.

**Steps:**
1. Let the engine and radiator cool completely.
2. Raise the front of the car and support it on jack stands, then remove the splash shield if one is fitted.
3. Relieve pressure at the radiator cap slowly, then remove the cap fully.
4. Place a drain pan under the radiator's drain plug, and loosen it to drain the radiator.
5. Place the drain pan under the drain bolt at the rear of the engine block, and remove it to drain the block.
6. Let all the coolant drain fully from both points.
7. Reinstall the block drain bolt with a new washer, and tighten it securely.
8. Tighten the radiator drain plug securely.
9. Remove, empty, and rinse the coolant reserve tank, then reinstall it.
10. Pour fresh Honda coolant into the radiator, up to the base of the filler neck.
11. Fill the reserve tank to the MAX mark.
12. Install the radiator cap loosely, not fully tight yet.
13. Start the engine and let it run until the radiator fan cycles on and off at least twice, to work air out of the system.
14. Turn off the engine, let it cool, and recheck the radiator and reserve tank levels.
15. Top off as needed, then put the radiator cap on fully and tightly.
16. Start the engine again and check underneath for leaks at both drain points.
17. Lower the car and remove the jack stands.

**How you know it worked:** the reserve tank sits at the MAX mark after a full warm-up-and-cool-down cycle, and there are no leaks at either drain point. The temperature gauge or warning light behaves normally on a test drive.

### Check and change the manual transmission fluid

**Safety warnings, before you raise the car:**
- Do not work under a car held up only by a jack. Use jack stands or ramps.
- Always check and change the fluid with the transmission at normal operating temperature, and the car on level ground.
- Do not overfill. Fluid should only reach the edge of the bolt hole, not spill out under pressure.
- Always clean up any spilled fluid immediately.

**Steps (level check only):**
1. Drive the car until the transmission reaches normal operating temperature.
2. Park on level ground and shut off the engine.
3. Remove the transmission filler bolt.
4. Carefully feel inside the bolt hole with a clean finger for the fluid level.
5. If fluid does not reach the edge of the hole, add Honda MTF until it starts to run back out.
6. Put a new washer on the filler bolt, and reinstall it, tightened securely.

**Steps (full drain and refill, continuing from a cool transmission):**
7. Raise the car and support it on jack stands.
8. Place the drain pan under the transmission's drain bolt.
9. Remove the drain bolt and let the old fluid fully drain.
10. Reinstall the drain bolt with a new washer, tightened securely.
11. Lower the car back to level ground.
12. Remove the filler bolt and add fresh Honda MTF through a pump or long funnel, until it reaches the edge of the hole.
13. Reinstall the filler bolt with a new washer, tightened securely.
14. Check for leaks at both bolts after a short drive.

**How you know it worked:** fluid sits right at the edge of the filler bolt hole, neither dripping out under pressure nor clearly low. There are no leaks at either bolt after driving, and shifting feels normal, not stiff or grindy.

### Check and change the automatic transmission fluid

**Safety warnings, before you raise the car:**
- Do not work under a car held up only by a jack. Use jack stands or ramps.
- Always check and fill the level with the engine at normal operating temperature, and the car on level ground.
- Do not fill above the dipstick's upper mark. Overfilling can damage the transmission.
- Always clean up any spilled fluid immediately.

**Steps (level check only):**
1. Park on level ground and start the engine, letting it run until the radiator fan comes on, then shut it off.
2. Wait about 60 seconds, but no longer than 90 seconds, before checking.<!-- P-WP11-17 -->
3. Remove the dipstick, the yellow-looped fluid gauge, and wipe it clean with a rag.
4. Insert the dipstick fully back into the transmission.
5. Remove it again, and read the fluid level against the upper and lower marks.
6. If the level is below the lower mark, add Honda ATF-Z1 through the dipstick tube until it is between the marks.
7. Reinsert the dipstick fully and securely.

**Steps (full drain and refill, continuing after the level check):**
8. Raise the car and support it on jack stands.
9. Place the drain pan under the transmission's drain plug.
10. Remove the drain plug and let the old fluid fully drain.
11. Reinstall the drain plug with a new washer, tightened securely.
12. Lower the car back to level ground.
13. Add fresh Honda ATF-Z1 through the dipstick tube using a pump or long funnel.
14. Recheck the level using steps 1 through 5 above, topping off as needed.
15. Check for leaks at the drain plug after a short drive.

**How you know it worked:** the dipstick reads between the upper and lower marks after the correct warm-up-and-wait routine, and there are no leaks at the drain plug. The car shifts smoothly, with no delay or harsh engagement.

## What can go wrong

- **Topping off coolant with plain water instead of the correct pre-mixed type.** This dilutes the mixture and can reduce its protection against freezing or overheating.<!-- F-WP09-020 -->
- **Opening a hot radiator cap and getting scalded by escaping steam and coolant.** Always let the system cool first.
- **Not letting the engine run through a full warm-up cycle after a coolant drain and fill.** This can leave a trapped air pocket that causes overheating later.
- **Using plain motor oil in a manual transmission long-term instead of switching back to Honda MTF.** Motor oil lacks the additives your gearbox needs, and shifting can get stiffer over time.<!-- F-WP09-026 -->
- **Using any automatic transmission fluid other than Honda ATF-Z1, or its replacement, ATF DW-1.** Mixing fluids can wear down your transmission, and the damage may not be covered under warranty.<!-- F-WP09-029 -->
- **Checking automatic transmission fluid cold, or without waiting the 60- to 90-second window after shutdown.**<!-- P-WP11-17 --> This gives an inaccurate reading.
- **Assuming your Fit has power steering fluid to check because an older car you know did.** This car steers electrically. There is no fluid at all.<!-- F-WP09-034 -->
- **Skipping the 3-year brake fluid change because the dashboard display never mentioned it.** This is a fixed rule, separate from the Maintenance Minder.<!-- F-WP09-013 -->
- **Pouring antifreeze or a vinegar solution into the washer fluid reservoir.** Use only commercial washer fluid.<!-- F-WP09-035 -->

## Quick check

1. What coolant type does your Fit require, and what should you never add to the system on its own?
2. Name the two fixed-mileage exceptions for automatic transmission fluid, and who they apply to.
3. What fluid does a manual-transmission Fit's clutch use, and where do you check its level?
4. Does your 2008 Fit have hydraulic power steering fluid? Why or why not?
5. What fixed rule applies to brake fluid no matter what the Maintenance Minder display says?

No WP-11-##.md procedure card exists in this book's research, at this pass, for bleeding or replacing brake fluid, or for topping off washer fluid — these were not among the procedure card inputs provided for this chapter, so no steps are invented for them; the chapter states this honestly and points to Chapters 27 and 40. -->

# Chapter 31: Belts, Hoses, and the Timing Chain

## What you will learn
- Why your 2008 Fit has a timing chain instead of a timing belt, and what that means for you
- What the drive belt is, and how your car's maintenance schedule handles it
- Why this book cannot give you a hose-replacement schedule for this chapter
- Why there is no do-it-yourself procedure in this chapter, and where to take the car instead

## The short version

Your Fit's engine uses a timing chain, not a timing belt.<!-- F-WP09-044 --> A timing chain is a metal chain, not rubber, and Honda treats it as a lifetime part with no scheduled replacement.<!-- F-WP09-044 --> Your car also has one drive belt, which the Maintenance Minder tracks for inspection and adjustment on its own schedule.<!-- F-WP09-007 --> This chapter has no do-it-yourself procedure, because none of this book's sources include one for these parts; a technician checks them for you.

## The full explanation

### Timing chain, not timing belt

A timing belt is a rubber belt. It keeps the engine's valves and pistons moving together. Many cars need this belt replaced on a strict mileage schedule. A skipped or broken timing belt can be a serious, sudden problem. Your Fit's engine does not have one of these belts at all. It uses a timing chain instead, a metal chain that does the same job.<!-- F-WP09-044 --> Your Fit's own maintenance item table lists two scheduled jobs: spark plug replacement and valve clearance inspection. It has no line at all for replacing a timing belt or timing chain.<!-- F-WP09-044 --> Honda treats the timing chain as a lifetime part under normal use. It has no scheduled replacement interval. It only needs service if a specific problem comes up. That problem could be unusual noise. It could be chain stretch, meaning the chain has worn and lengthened slightly. Or it could be the engine's timing actually skipping.<!-- F-WP09-044 -->

This is good news for your maintenance budget. Unlike a timing belt on many other cars, there is no expensive, scheduled chain replacement waiting for you at some fixed mileage. It is also a reason to pay attention if your engine ever sounds different or runs roughly. A timing chain problem is not something your maintenance schedule will warn you about ahead of time.

### The drive belt

Your Fit has one drive belt. It spins accessories such as the alternator, the part that recharges your battery while the engine runs. Inspecting and adjusting this belt is a Maintenance Minder sub item. The dashboard's own maintenance computer decides when it is due. There is no fixed mileage printed in your manual for this job.<!-- F-WP09-007 --> This book's sources do not include a do-it-yourself procedure card for inspecting or adjusting this belt. A technician should check it during your regular Maintenance Minder service visits.

### Hoses: what this chapter can honestly tell you

We could not confirm a specific inspection interval for coolant hoses from this book's sources at this pass. Your Fit's Maintenance Minder Item B inspection list does name some hoses. It lists brake hoses and lines, plus fuel lines and connections, as things a technician checks.<!-- F-WP09-003 --> It does not separately name engine coolant hoses in that list. Check your owner's manual for that item. Or ask your technician to look over your coolant hoses at your next service visit. This book does not have a confirmed, Fit-specific answer for that particular item.

## Step by step

This chapter has no do-it-yourself procedure. Your Fit's engine does not have a timing belt to replace, so this book's research did not turn up a timing-belt-style replacement job.<!-- F-WP09-044 --> It also did not turn up a home procedure card for inspecting or adjusting the drive belt, or for checking coolant hoses. A technician handles all three of these jobs. This usually happens at your regular Maintenance Minder service visits, described in Chapter 21. If your engine ever sounds unusual, or you notice a belt squeal, have it looked at promptly. Do not guess at a fix yourself.

## What can go wrong

- **Assuming your Fit needs a timing belt change at some mileage, the way many other cars do.** Your engine has a timing chain, not a belt, and Honda gives it no scheduled replacement interval.<!-- F-WP09-044 -->
- **Ignoring an unusual engine noise because "the timing chain doesn't need service."** A timing chain is only serviced when a problem shows up, such as noise, stretch, or a timing skip. Unusual noise is exactly the sign to have it checked.<!-- F-WP09-044 -->
- **Never having the drive belt inspected because the dashboard has not flagged it.** The Maintenance Minder decides the timing, but it still relies on your regular service visits to actually happen.<!-- F-WP09-007 -->
- **Assuming this chapter covers a hose-replacement schedule that this book could not confirm.** Check your owner's manual, or ask your technician, since this chapter is honest about that gap rather than guessing at a number.

## Quick check

1. Does your 2008 Fit have a timing belt or a timing chain? What is the difference between the two?
2. Does your Fit's timing chain have a fixed replacement mileage? If not, when does it get serviced?
3. What does the drive belt do, and how does your Fit's maintenance schedule decide when to inspect it?
4. Why does this chapter not include a step-by-step procedure?

<!-- Fact/Procedure IDs used: F-WP09-003 (Item B inspection list, cited for its brake-hose/fuel-line detail relevant to the hose discussion), F-WP09-007, F-WP09-044. -->
<!-- Unused related IDs, with reason: no procedure card (P-WP11-##) exists in this book's research inputs for a drive belt, hose, or timing chain job — none were provided for this chapter, and per the writer task's instruction, no belt- or chain-replacement procedure is invented to fill that gap. -->

# Chapter 32: Lights and Bulbs, Wipers, Fuses

## What you will learn
- Every bulb size and type in your 2008 Fit, from headlights to the dome light
- How to replace a headlight bulb, and tail, brake, or turn signal bulbs
- What size wiper blades your Fit uses, and how to replace them
- How to find and replace a blown fuse, and why you should never use the wrong amperage
- What can go wrong during each of these jobs

## The short version

Your Fit uses a mix of halogen bulbs, meaning bulbs with a heated filament inside a gas-filled glass bulb.<!-- F-WP09-048 --> It also uses small wedge-style bulbs for everything from turn signals to the dome light.<!-- F-WP09-058 --> Replacing most exterior bulbs takes about 15 minutes and basic tools.<!-- P-WP11-18, P-WP11-19 --> Wiper blades should be checked every 6 months and replaced at the first sign of trouble.<!-- F-WP09-047 --> A blown fuse, a small safety device that breaks an electrical circuit, is one of the easiest and safest repairs in your whole car.<!-- P-WP11-21 -->

## The full explanation

### Every light on your Fit, and its bulb

Your Fit's owner's manual and its Specifications table describe the bulb for nearly every light on the car. The table below lists them.

| Light | Bulb type | Fact ID |
|---|---|---|
| Headlight, high and low beam | HB2 (also cross-sold as 9003 or H4), halogen, 12 volt, 60/55 watt | F-WP09-048 |
| Fog light (Sport model or accessory) | H11, halogen | F-WP09-049 |
| Front turn signal | Amber, 12 volt, 21 watt | F-WP09-050 |
| Rear turn signal | 7443 | F-WP09-051 |
| Stop/taillight | 7443 | F-WP09-052 |
| Back-up light | 7443 | F-WP09-053 |
| License plate light | T10 | F-WP09-054 |
| High-mount brake light | UNVERIFIED trade number | F-WP09-055 |
| Front side marker/parking light | T10 | F-WP09-056 |
| Rear side marker | T10 | F-WP09-057 |
| Dome/ceiling light | C5W, 31 millimeter festoon, a tube-shaped bulb with metal caps on each end | F-WP09-058 |
| Cargo/luggage compartment light | T10 | F-WP09-059 |

Your headlight bulb carries the trade name HB2 in Honda's own Specifications table.<!-- F-WP09-048 --> Parts stores commonly sell this same physical bulb under the name H4 or 9003 as well; confirm the cross-reference against your own bulb's markings before you buy a replacement.<!-- F-WP09-048 --> If your Fit has fog lights, either from the Sport trim or added as an accessory, they use an H11 halogen bulb.<!-- F-WP09-049 -->

Your front turn signals use an amber, 21-watt bulb.<!-- F-WP09-050 --> Several of your rear lights share the same 7443 trade number: the turn signal, the stop/taillight, and the back-up light.<!-- F-WP09-051, F-WP09-052, F-WP09-053 --> Each one still plugs into its own socket in the assembly.<!-- F-WP09-051, F-WP09-052, F-WP09-053 --> Your license plate light, front and rear side marker lights, and cargo compartment light all use a small T10 wedge bulb.<!-- F-WP09-054, F-WP09-056, F-WP09-057, F-WP09-059 --> We could not confirm the exact trade number for your high-mount brake light bulb from this book's sources. The manual confirms it is a separate, owner-replaceable bulb, but does not print its part number.<!-- F-WP09-055 --> Check your owner's manual or a parts store for that specific number.<!-- F-WP09-055 --> Your dome light uses a festoon-style bulb, a small tube-shaped bulb with a metal cap on each end, listed as C5W in a 31-millimeter length.<!-- F-WP09-058 -->

### Wiper blades

Check your wiper blades' condition at least every 6 months, per your owner's manual, and replace any blade at the first sign of cracking, hardening, or streaking.<!-- F-WP09-047 --> Your front wiper blades are two different sizes: 24 inches on the driver's side, and 16 inches on the passenger's side.<!-- F-WP09-047 --> Your rear wiper blade, on the tailgate, is 14 inches.<!-- F-WP09-047 -->

### Fuses

A fuse is a small safety device that breaks an electrical circuit on purpose if too much current tries to flow through it. This protects the wiring behind it from damage or fire. Your Fit stores some spare fuses. Amperage rating, meaning the current level a fuse position is designed to carry, is printed on the fuse box's own lid diagram and in your owner's manual.<!-- P-WP11-21 --> Never substitute a fuse with a higher amperage rating than the one that was there originally. This raises the risk of damaging your electrical system.<!-- P-WP11-21 -->

## Step by step

### Replace a headlight bulb

**Safety warnings, before you touch a bulb:**
- Do not touch the glass part of a halogen bulb with bare skin. Oil from your fingers can make it overheat and shatter when lit. Handle it by its base only.
- Always clean the glass with denatured alcohol and a clean cloth before installing it, if you accidentally touch it.
- Always let the engine cool if it was recently running, since you will reach in near the engine bay.

**Steps:**
1. To reach the driver's side bulb, start the engine, turn the wheel fully right, then turn the engine off.
2. To reach the passenger's side bulb, instead turn the wheel fully left.
3. Use a flat-tip screwdriver to remove the holding clip from the inner fender.
4. Pull the inner fender cover back to expose the back of the headlight.
5. Remove the electrical connector from the bulb by pulling it straight back.
6. Remove the rubber weather seal by pulling on its tab.
7. Unclip the end of the hold-down wire from its slot.
8. Pivot the hold-down wire out of the way and remove the old bulb.
9. Install the new bulb, holding it by its base, making sure its tabs sit in their slots.
10. Pivot the hold-down wire back into place and clip its end into the slot.
11. Check the wire's installation through the inspection window on the headlight assembly.
12. Install the rubber weather seal back over the back of the headlight assembly.
13. Push the electrical connector onto the new bulb until it is securely connected.
14. Reinstall the inner fender cover and the holding clip, pressing the clip's center to lock it.
15. Turn on the headlights to confirm the new bulb works.

**How you know it worked:** the headlight comes on and looks similarly bright and evenly aimed as the other side. The inner fender cover and clip are back in place.

### Replace tail light and brake light bulbs

**Safety warnings, before you pry the cover:**
- Do not force the trim tool or screwdriver against painted body panels without a cloth underneath. You can scratch the paint.
- Always confirm which of the bulbs in the assembly is actually burned out before you disturb the others.

**Steps for the rear light assembly, on the tailgate side:**
1. Open the tailgate.
2. Place a cloth on the edge of the light assembly cover to protect the paint.
3. Pry the cover off carefully at the notch on its middle edge with a flat-tip screwdriver.
4. Identify which bulb is out: stop/taillight, back-up light, turn signal, or taillight.
5. Turn that bulb's socket one-quarter turn counterclockwise and remove it from the assembly.
6. Pull the burned-out bulb straight out of its socket.
7. Push the new bulb straight into the socket until it bottoms out.
8. Turn on the lights, or press the brake pedal for a stop/taillight bulb, to confirm it works.
9. Turn the socket clockwise back into the light assembly until it locks.
10. Reinstall the light assembly cover.

**Steps for the high-mount brake light, on the rear window:**
11. Open the tailgate.
12. Remove the light assembly cover by pushing in the tabs on both sides and pulling it off.
13. Turn the socket one-quarter turn counterclockwise and remove it from the assembly.
14. Pull the old bulb straight out of its socket.
15. Push the new bulb straight into the socket until it bottoms out.
16. Put the socket back into the light assembly and turn it clockwise to lock it.
17. Press the brake pedal to confirm the new bulb lights up.
18. Push the cover back onto the light assembly until it locks in place.

**How you know it worked:** the specific light you replaced turns on when tested, and both covers are fully seated and locked.

### Replace the wiper blades

**Safety warnings, before you raise a wiper arm:**
- Do not open the hood while the wiper arms are raised off the glass. The hood can hit and damage the arms.
- Always handle a raised wiper arm carefully. Do not let it snap down onto bare glass without a blade attached.

**Steps for the front wiper blades:**
1. Raise the wiper arms off the windshield, lifting the driver's side first, then the passenger's side.
2. Press and hold the lock tab on the blade assembly.
3. Slide the blade assembly toward the lock tab until it releases from the wiper arm.
4. Grasp the tabbed end of the old blade and pull firmly until it comes out of its holder.
5. If the new blade has no plastic or metal reinforcement strip, move the strip from the old blade into the new one's edge slots.
6. Make sure the new blade's rubber tabs fit into each notch of the reinforcement strip.
7. Slide the new blade into the wiper arm until it is engaged along its full length.
8. Lower the wiper arm back down onto the windshield.
9. Repeat steps 2 through 8 for the other front wiper arm.

**Steps for the rear wiper blade, if equipped:**
10. Raise the rear wiper arm off the rear glass.
11. Slide the old blade out of the wiper arm.
12. Move the reinforcement strip to the new blade if it does not already have one.
13. Slide the new blade assembly onto the wiper arm until it locks in place.
14. Lower the rear wiper arm back down against the glass.

**How you know it worked:** all wiper arms sit flat against the glass with the new blades locked in place. A test run of the wipers wipes cleanly, with no streaks, chatter, or missed spots.

### Replace a fuse

**Safety warnings, before you open a fuse box:**
- Always turn the ignition switch to LOCK (0) and turn off the headlights and every accessory before checking or changing fuses.
- Do not install a fuse with a higher rating than the circuit calls for. This greatly increases the risk of damaging the electrical system.
- Do not ignore a fuse that blows again right away with a same-rated replacement. That means there is a real electrical problem, and the vehicle should be checked by a qualified technician.

**Steps:**
1. Turn the ignition switch to the LOCK (0) position.
2. Make sure the headlights and all other accessories are off.
3. Figure out which box holds the fuse: primary under-hood, on the driver's side, interior, behind the coin tray, or secondary, on the battery.
4. Remove the cover from that fuse box.
5. Find the suspect fuse using the chart on the fuse box lid or in the manual.
6. Pull a small fuse straight out using the fuse puller from the interior box.
7. For a large fuse in an under-hood box, remove it with a Phillips screwdriver instead.
8. Look through the top of the fuse at the thin wire inside. A blown fuse shows a broken or melted wire.
9. If you are not sure which one is blown, check every fuse for that circuit, not only the first one.
10. Push in a new fuse of the exact same amperage rating.
11. Turn the ignition back on and test whether the device now works.
12. Reinstall the fuse box cover.

**How you know it worked:** the device that stopped working, such as a light, wiper, or power window, now works normally. The new fuse does not blow again right away.

## What can go wrong

- **Buying a bulb without checking its cross-reference against your old bulb's markings.** A headlight sold as "H4" is often the same bulb as an HB2, but confirm this before you install it.<!-- F-WP09-048 -->
- **Touching a new halogen bulb's glass with bare fingers.** Oil from your skin can make it overheat and shatter when lit.
- **Guessing which rear bulb is out instead of testing each function first.** Test the brakes, turn signal, and reverse gear before you open any cover.
- **Prying a light assembly cover without a cloth underneath.** This can scratch the paint around it.
- **Forgetting to transfer the reinforcement strip into a wiper blade that ships without one.** The new blade will not sit correctly on the glass without it.
- **Letting a raised, unblocked wiper arm snap down onto bare glass.** Always support or lower an arm carefully.
- **Installing a higher-rated fuse to make it stop blowing.** This risks damaging the wiring or causing a fire.<!-- P-WP11-21 -->
- **Checking only one fuse and missing that a different one in the same circuit is the actual problem.** If your device still does not work, check every fuse on that circuit.
- **Forgetting that removing the radio fuse can lock the radio.** You may need your 5-digit code to unlock it again.

## Quick check

1. What is the trade name for your Fit's headlight bulb, and what other name is it commonly sold under?
2. Name three of your Fit's rear lights that share the same 7443 bulb.
3. How often should you check your wiper blades, and what sizes does your Fit use, front and rear?
4. Why should you never install a fuse with a higher amperage rating than the original?
5. What should you do if a fuse blows again right away after you replace it?

# Chapter 33: Keeping It Clean Inside and Out

## What you will learn
- How to wash and wax your 2008 Fit's exterior without damaging the paint or wheels
- Why you should remove the rear antenna before a drive-through car wash
- How to clean your seat belts safely, and why they need to fully air-dry
- How to clean the interior and keep your floor mats from becoming a safety problem

## The short version

Washing and waxing your Fit, plus a full interior clean, takes about 90 minutes the first time.<!-- P-WP11-23 --> Before a drive-through car wash, unscrew and remove the rear roof antenna by hand; the wash's spinning brushes can damage it.<!-- P-WP11-23 --> Never clean your seat belts with bleach, dye, or cleaning solvents, and always let them fully air-dry before you use the car again.<!-- P-WP11-23 --> Always re-anchor your floor mats into their factory anchor points once they are dry, since a loose mat can interfere with the pedals.<!-- P-WP11-23 -->

## The full explanation

### Washing and waxing the exterior

A clean, well-waxed exterior protects your Fit's paint from the sun, road salt, and everyday dirt. Wax, or a sealant, is a protective coating you apply on top of clean paint; it helps water bead up and roll off instead of sitting on the surface. Your Fit's manual specifically warns against using a drive-through car wash without first removing the rear roof antenna by hand, since the wash's spinning brushes can damage it.<!-- P-WP11-23 --> Reinstall the antenna once you are done.<!-- P-WP11-23 -->

Your Fit's wheels are aluminum alloy, meaning a lightweight metal mix, and they have a protective clear-coat finish. Avoid harsh wheel cleaners or a stiff brush on them; these can damage that clear-coat.<!-- P-WP11-23 --> Use a car-wash soap made for vehicles, not dish soap, since dish soap can strip away wax.<!-- P-WP11-23 -->

### Cleaning seat belts and the interior

Your seat belts should never be cleaned with bleach, dye, or cleaning solvents.<!-- P-WP11-23 --> If they get dirty, clean them with a soft brush and mild soap and water instead, and let them air-dry fully before you use the car again.<!-- P-WP11-23 --> A damp seat belt that gets rolled back up into its retractor can stay damp for a long time, so give it time to dry completely first.

When you clean the rest of the interior, vacuum the seats, carpet, and floor.<!-- P-WP11-23 --> Wipe hard surfaces, like the dashboard and door panels, with a mild cleaner and a soft cloth.<!-- P-WP11-23 --> Take out your floor mats and clean them separately.<!-- P-WP11-23 --> Once everything is completely dry, put the mats back and re-anchor them into their factory anchor points. A loose mat can slide forward and interfere with the pedals, or with a seat's weight sensor, which is a real safety issue, not only a tidiness one.<!-- P-WP11-23 -->

## Step by step

### Wash and wax the exterior, and clean the interior

**Safety warnings, before you start:**
- Do not use a drive-through car wash without first unscrewing and removing the rear roof antenna by hand. The brushes can damage it.
- Do not use bleach, dye, or cleaning solvents on the seat belts.
- Always let seat belts air-dry fully before using the vehicle again.
- Do not clean aluminum alloy wheels with harsh chemicals or a stiff brush. It can damage their protective clear-coat.

**Steps for the exterior wash and wax:**
1. If you plan to use a drive-through wash, unscrew and remove the rear antenna by hand first.
2. Rinse the whole car with plain water to knock off loose dirt.
3. Wash a small section at a time with car-wash soap and a wash mitt, then rinse that section.
4. Clean the wheels separately with a mild detergent and a soft brush or sponge, avoiding harsh wheel cleaners on alloy wheels.
5. Dry the car with a clean microfiber towel before water spots form.
6. Apply wax or sealant in small sections, following the product's instructions.
7. Buff each section with a clean, dry microfiber towel.
8. Reinstall the antenna if you removed it.

**Steps for the interior:**
9. Remove the floor mats and shake or vacuum them separately.
10. Vacuum the seats, carpet, and floor, using an attachment to reach corners.
11. Wipe the dashboard, door panels, and other hard surfaces with a mild cleaner and a soft cloth.
12. Wipe the insides of the seat belt loops with a cloth dampened in mild soap water or isopropyl alcohol.
13. If the seat belts themselves are dirty, clean them with a soft brush and mild soap and water, then let them air-dry fully.
14. Make sure carpeting and floor mats are completely dry before you put the floor mats back.
15. Re-anchor the floor mats onto their factory anchor points. A loose mat can interfere with the pedals or a seat sensor.

**How you know it worked:** the exterior is clean with an even, beaded look where waxed, and the wheels are clean without dulled or damaged clear-coat. The interior looks and smells clean, and every floor mat is dry and re-anchored in place.

## What can go wrong

- **Washing the car in direct hot sun.** This makes soap and water dry into spots before you can rinse it off. Work in shade if you can.
- **Using a stiff brush or strong wheel cleaner on the aluminum alloy wheels.** This can damage their protective clear-coat finish.<!-- P-WP11-23 -->
- **Skipping the antenna removal before a drive-through wash.** The spinning brushes can bend or break it.<!-- P-WP11-23 -->
- **Cleaning seat belts with bleach or a solvent-based cleaner.** This can weaken the belt webbing itself.<!-- P-WP11-23 -->
- **Putting damp floor mats back down, or not re-anchoring them.** This can interfere with the pedals or a seat's weight sensor, which is a genuine safety concern.<!-- P-WP11-23 -->
- **Using dish soap instead of car-wash soap.** Dish soap can strip away any wax already on the paint.<!-- P-WP11-23 -->

## Quick check

1. What should you do to the antenna before a drive-through car wash, and why?
2. Name two things you should never use to clean your seat belts.
3. Why do your wheels need a gentler cleaner and softer brush than the rest of the car?
4. Why is re-anchoring your floor mats a safety issue, not only a tidiness one?

<!-- Fact/Procedure IDs used: P-WP11-23. -->
