# Part VI: Diagnosing Problems

---

# Chapter 34: How to Describe a Problem

## What you will learn
- Why a clear description helps you find the real cause of a car problem
- How to describe *when* a noise, vibration, smell, or leak happens
- How to describe *where* you feel or notice it
- How your speed and engine speed give useful clues
- How color and smell narrow down a leak or an odor
- The difference between a "Fit-specific" finding and general automotive knowledge, a difference you will see again in Chapter 37

## The short version
A good description has four parts: when it happens, where you notice it, how fast you were going, and what it looks or smells like. Chapters 35 through 37 sort information this same way, so a clear description lets you use those chapters fast. Write your notes down before you call a shop or look up a code.

## The full explanation

### Why this matters

"My car is making a weird noise" tells a mechanic almost nothing. It also will not help you use the tables in Chapter 37 or the flowchart in Chapter 36. The more specific you are, the faster anyone, including you, can narrow down the cause.

This chapter teaches you to notice four things about any problem: when it happens, where you notice it, your speed, and what it looks or smells like. These are the same four categories this book's research used to build the lookup tables in Chapter 37.<!-- WP-12 Sections C, D, E, F: table structure -->

### When does it happen?

Ask yourself when the noise, shake, or smell shows up. Common answers include: at idle, meaning while the engine runs but the car is not moving; while accelerating; while turning; while braking; or over bumps.<!-- WP-12 Section C: "When" column categories -->

For example, a ticking sound at idle often points to a low oil level. It can also point to a problem in the VTEC system, the mechanism that switches the intake valves between a low-lift and a high-lift setting using engine oil pressure.<!-- F-WP12-010 --><!-- F-WP12-011 --> A clunk that happens only when you shift into Drive or Reverse points to something different. It often means a worn engine or transmission mount, the rubber-and-metal part that holds the engine or transmission to the car's frame.<!-- F-WP02-016 --> Two very different sounds, both "at idle." But the timing detail, a constant tick versus a one-time clunk on shifting, points to two different systems.

### Where do you notice it?

Location matters too. Do you feel a vibration through the steering wheel, or through the seat and floor? Do you hear a noise from the front of the car, the middle, or the back? Owners report that a clicking noise while turning often points to a worn CV joint. A CV joint, short for constant velocity joint, is a flexible joint in the axle that lets power reach the wheel while it turns.<!-- WP-12 Section C: CV joint clicking while turning, community-reported --> That same clicking sound, if it does not change while you turn the wheel, more likely points to a loose exhaust heat shield instead. A heat shield is a metal panel that protects nearby parts from a hot exhaust pipe.<!-- WP-12 Section C: heat shield rattle, community-reported -->

### How fast were you going?

Speed is one of the most useful clues for a vibration. A shimmy in the steering wheel that appears below about 20 mph, then goes away above that speed, often points to a tire that is out of round. It can also point to a bent wheel, or a stuck brake part dragging.<!-- WP-12 Section D: low-speed shimmy, general automotive knowledge --> A stronger shake that grows worse between roughly 45 and 65 mph often points to a wheel or tire that is out of balance. Out of balance means its weight is not spread evenly around the wheel.<!-- WP-12 Section D: mid-to-highway speed shake, community-reported plus general knowledge --> Writing down the exact speed range helps you or a mechanic skip straight to the likely cause.

Engine speed matters too, separately from road speed. A whine or grind from the front of the engine that gets louder as the engine revs, not as the car speeds up, points toward the alternator. The alternator is the part that charges the battery while the engine runs, and this noise can also come from the belt that spins it.<!-- WP-12 Section C: alternator noise, community-reported -->

### What does it look or smell like?

For a leak, note the color and exactly where under the car you see it. Amber or dark brown usually means engine oil.<!-- WP-12 Section F: engine oil leak, general automotive knowledge --> Red or pink usually means automatic transmission fluid on this car. The 2008 Fit uses electric power steering, not a hydraulic system, so there is no separate power-steering fluid to confuse it with.<!-- F-WP02-008 --><!-- WP-12 Section F: ATF leak note --> Green, yellow-green, orange, pink, or blue, with a sweet smell, points to coolant, the fluid that keeps the engine's temperature down.<!-- WP-12 Section F: coolant leak, general automotive knowledge -->

For a smell, describe it in plain words: burning rubber, burning plastic, sweet and syrupy, rotten egg, raw gasoline, or hot oil.<!-- WP-12 Section E: smell categories --> Each points toward a different system, which Chapter 37 explains in full.

### Understanding urgency

Chapter 37's tables each give an urgency level: Low, Medium, Medium-high, or High.<!-- WP-12 Sections C, D, E, F: Urgency column --> Low means the problem is usually only annoying, not dangerous. High means you should stop driving soon, sometimes right away, because the problem could hurt you, hurt the car badly, or both. When you describe a problem to yourself or a shop, always mention any High-urgency sign. Examples include a burning smell, a grinding noise while braking, or a flashing warning light.<!-- WP-12 Sections C, E: high-urgency examples -->

### "Fit-specific" versus general knowledge

Some of what you will read in Chapters 35 through 37 comes from real 2007-2008 Honda Fit owners describing their own cars. This book marks those as "Owners report that..." Other information is true of almost any gasoline car and does not need a Fit-specific source, since it is standard automotive knowledge.<!-- WP-12 Sections C-F: "Grounded in" distinction --> Chapter 37 marks each row so you can tell which kind of information you are reading. Neither kind is more or less true. They are two different types of evidence.

## Step by step

Use this short routine any time something feels or sounds wrong with your car.

1. Stop and think before you drive further, especially if the problem feels sudden or serious.
2. Note when it happens: at idle, accelerating, turning, braking, or over bumps.<!-- WP-12 Section C -->
3. Note where you notice it: steering wheel, seat and floor, front, middle, or back of the car.<!-- WP-12 Sections C, D -->
4. Note your speed, or your engine speed, when it happens or gets worse.<!-- WP-12 Section D -->
5. Note the color of any fluid, and exactly where under the car it appears.<!-- WP-12 Section F -->
6. Note the smell, using plain words like burning, sweet, or rotten egg.<!-- WP-12 Section E -->
7. Write all of this down, or say it out loud to yourself, before you look anything up.
8. Use your notes with Chapter 35 (check engine light and codes), Chapter 36 (won't start), or Chapter 37 (lookup tables) to narrow down the cause.

## What can go wrong

- **Being too vague.** "It sounds funny" describes nothing. "It ticks at idle, only when the engine is cold" describes something you can look up.
- **Skipping the "when."** A noise that only happens once, right after starting, is very different from a noise that happens constantly. Both matter, but they point to different causes.
- **Guessing the cause instead of describing the symptom.** Say what you heard, felt, saw, or smelled first. Save your guess about the cause for after you check the tables.
- **Ignoring a High-urgency sign because the car "still drives fine."** A car can still move while something inside it is failing badly. Treat a burning smell or a flashing light seriously right away.<!-- WP-12 Section C: flashing MIL example -->
- **Forgetting to check both speed and engine speed for a vibration or a whine.** Road speed and engine speed are not the same thing, and they point to different systems.<!-- WP-12 Section C: alternator whine tied to engine speed, not road speed -->

## Quick check
1. What four things should you note about any car problem?
2. Why does the exact speed range matter for a vibration?
3. What is a CV joint, and what noise, at what time, points to a worn one?
4. What is the difference between a "Fit-specific" finding and general automotive knowledge?
5. Name one High-urgency sign you should never ignore, even if the car still seems to drive fine.

<!-- Fact IDs used: F-WP12-010, F-WP12-011, F-WP02-016, F-WP02-008. Also cited by section reference (WP-12 Sections C, D, E, F) where the source table row is a general-automotive-knowledge or community-reported entry without its own discrete Fact ID card (these rows exist only inside WP-12.md's Section C/D/E/F tables, not as separate F-WP12-0## cards). -->
<!-- Unused Fact IDs from WP-12.md: F-WP12-001 (OBD-II port location) and F-WP12-002 through F-WP12-021 (the 20 trouble codes) are not used in this chapter, because Chapter 34 teaches observation skill before any code lookup; they are fully used in Chapter 35. F-WP12-012 through F-WP12-021 individually are not repeated here for the same reason. -->

# Chapter 35: The Check Engine Light and OBD-II Codes

## What you will learn
- What OBD-II means and what the check engine light is actually telling you
- Where to find the OBD-II port in your 2008 Fit
- How reading and clearing a code works, in general terms
- All 20 trouble codes this book found linked to this exact car, in plain English, with the first thing to check for each
- Why 2 of those 20 codes are less certain than the other 18, and why this book says so honestly

## The short version
The check engine light, formally called the Malfunction Indicator Lamp (MIL), warns you that a system tied to emissions or engine control has a problem. OBD-II, short for On-Board Diagnostics, second generation, is the standard system every 1996-and-newer US gasoline car uses to store a trouble code describing that problem. A code reader plugged into the port under your dashboard shows you the code, and this chapter's table shows you what 20 common codes mean and where to start checking.

## The full explanation

### What OBD-II is, and what the check engine light means

OBD-II is a federal standard. Every gasoline car sold in the US since 1996 must have it, including your 2008 Fit.<!-- F-WP12-001 --> The system constantly watches the engine and emissions parts. When something goes outside its normal range, the car's computer, called the ECM (engine control module), saves a code describing the problem and turns on the check engine light.<!-- F-WP12-001 -->

Each stored code is called a DTC, short for Diagnostic Trouble Code. A DTC does not always mean a part is broken. It means the ECM saw a reading it does not expect, which could point to a broken part, a loose connector, a low fluid level, or something else entirely.<!-- F-WP12-002 --> That is why this chapter's table always lists a first thing to check, not only a part to replace.

### Where the OBD-II port is

The OBD-II port, also called the data link connector (DLC), is a 16-pin plug you connect a code reader to. On your 2008 Fit, it sits under the dashboard on the driver's side, at or a little above the brake and gas pedals, close to the steering column.<!-- F-WP12-001 --> It usually has no label, and it may sit tucked up against the underside of the dash instead of hanging down in plain view. You will likely need to crouch down and feel for it with your hand, or put your head down near the pedals to see it.<!-- F-WP12-001 -->

This location is not random. A federal standard called SAE J1962 sets the rules. The port must sit between the driver's end of the dashboard and a point slightly past the center of the car. It must mount to the dashboard structure and be reachable from the driver's seat without tools.<!-- F-WP12-001 --> That rule is why "under the dash, driver's side, near the steering column" is the right first place to look on almost any 1996-or-newer US car. A source naming the Fit by name confirms this location specifically for your car.<!-- F-WP12-001 -->

### Reading and clearing a code, in general terms

A code reader, sometimes called a scan tool, plugs into the OBD-II port. With the ignition turned on, it shows you any stored DTCs as a letter-and-number code, such as P0300. You then look up what that code means, which this chapter's table does for the 20 most common codes on this car.

Clearing a code turns off the check engine light, but it does not fix the problem that caused the code. If the underlying cause is still there, the code, and the light, will likely come back. This book could not confirm the exact menu steps for every code reader model, since readers vary by brand. Follow the instructions that come with your own reader.

### Should you keep driving with the light on?

This depends on how the light behaves. A steady check engine light means a problem exists, but usually is not an emergency by itself. A flashing check engine light is more serious. It means an active, severe misfire, a condition where a cylinder is not firing correctly, which can send unburned fuel into the hot catalytic converter and destroy it.<!-- F-WP12-002 --> If your check engine light is flashing, pull over and stop driving. Do not continue on to your destination first.<!-- F-WP12-002 -->

### The 20 codes this book found for the 2008 Fit

Every code below was checked against real reports from owners of 2007-2008 Honda Fits, not a generic list of what any car's computer can report.<!-- WP-12 Section B intro --> Most are backed by two independent owner reports describing this exact code on this exact generation of car. Two codes have only one such report each, and this book flags them honestly rather than presenting them as more certain than they are.

**Key to the Source column:**
- **TSB** — Backed by an actual Honda Technical Service Bulletin, the strongest evidence in this list.
- **2+** — Backed by two or more independent 2007-2008 Fit owner reports.
- **1 only** — Backed by only one 2007-2008 Fit owner report found. Treat this as a lead, not a confirmed pattern.

| Code | What it means | First thing to check | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| P0300 | Random or multiple cylinder misfire. More than one cylinder is skipping a spark. | Spark plugs first, then the ignition coil packs. Owners report that more than one coil pack can fail around the same time on this car.<!-- F-WP12-002 --> | 2+ |
| P0301 | Cylinder 1 misfire. | Spark plug and ignition coil for cylinder 1. If that does not fix it, check the upstream oxygen sensor next.<!-- F-WP12-003 --> | 2+ |
| P0302 | Cylinder 2 misfire. | Spark plug and ignition coil for cylinder 2. Owners report this code often appears alongside P0300.<!-- F-WP12-004 --> | 2+ |
| P0303 | Cylinder 3 misfire. | Spark plug and ignition coil for cylinder 3. If plugs and coils don't fix it, worn fuel injectors are a real possibility on this engine.<!-- F-WP12-005 --> | 2+ |
| P0304 | Cylinder 4 misfire. | Spark plug and ignition coil for cylinder 4.<!-- F-WP12-006 --> | 2+ |
| P0135 | The heater inside the upstream oxygen sensor, the sensor that checks exhaust before the catalytic converter, is not drawing the current expected. | Check the 15-amp fuse for this sensor's heater circuit first. If the fuse is fine, check the sensor's wiring before replacing the sensor.<!-- F-WP12-007 --> | 2+ |
| P0141 | Same heater problem as P0135, but on the downstream oxygen sensor, located after the catalytic converter. | Check the downstream sensor's connector and wiring first. This exact code and repair has been directly confirmed on a base-trim 2007 Fit.<!-- F-WP12-008 --> | 2+ (base-trim confirmed) |
| P0420 | The catalytic converter, the part that cleans exhaust gases, is not working as well as the computer expects. | Rule out an active misfire or a bad upstream oxygen sensor first. Both are cheaper to fix than a converter, and both can trigger this code without the converter actually being bad.<!-- F-WP12-009 --> | 2+ |
| P2646 | Part of the VTEC system, which uses engine oil pressure to change the intake valve lift. This code means the switch that reports oil pressure to the computer is reading a lower voltage than expected. | Check engine oil level and condition first. Honda's own bulletin says this is the single most common actual cause.<!-- F-WP12-010 --> | TSB |
| P2647 | The same VTEC oil pressure switch as P2646, but reading a higher voltage than expected. | Same as P2646: check engine oil level and condition first, before replacing the switch.<!-- F-WP12-011 --> | TSB |
| P2649 | The VTEC oil control solenoid, the valve that actually moves oil pressure into the VTEC system. This is a different part from the switch in P2646/P2647. | Owners report a small, inexpensive O-ring or gasket at the solenoid as a common fix. This code often appears together with misfire codes on this car.<!-- F-WP12-012 --> Note: this code is not covered by the same Honda bulletin as P2646/P2647, unlike an earlier version of this book's notes claimed.<!-- F-WP12-012 --> | 2+ (owner reports only) |
| P0335 | The crankshaft position sensor, which tracks engine speed and position for timing and fuel, is sending a signal the computer cannot use. | Check the sensor's electrical connector and wiring first. Owners report replacing the sensor and having the problem return right away, pointing to wiring more than the sensor itself.<!-- F-WP12-013 --> | 2+ |
| P0339 | Same sensor and circuit as P0335, but the signal drops out only sometimes instead of failing completely. | Same as P0335: check the wiring and connector first.<!-- F-WP12-014 --> | 2+ |
| P0500 | The vehicle speed sensor, which reports how fast the car is moving to the computer and speedometer, is not sending a usable signal. | Check the wiring at the transmission connector first. Owners on this car have twice found a broken wire, not the sensor itself, to be the actual cause.<!-- F-WP12-015 --> | 2+ |
| P0171 | The fuel mixture keeps coming back leaner, meaning less fuel relative to air, than the target, and the computer is adding extra fuel to correct it. | Check for a vacuum or intake leak first, such as a loose or cracked hose. If that comes up clean, worn fuel injectors are a real second possibility on this car.<!-- F-WP12-016 --> | 2+ |
| P0172 | The opposite of P0171: the fuel mixture keeps coming back richer, meaning more fuel than the target, than expected. | Check for a stuck-open EGR valve first, a part that recirculates exhaust gas into the intake. Then check fuel pressure before assuming a fuel injector problem.<!-- F-WP12-017 --> | 2+ |
| P2A00 | The upstream air/fuel ratio sensor, Honda's more advanced version of an oxygen sensor, is reading outside its normal range. | Check the sensor's connector and wiring first. Watch out for a bad-fit aftermarket sensor if this code appears right after a do-it-yourself sensor replacement.<!-- F-WP12-018 --> | 2+ |
| P0451 | The sensor that reports pressure inside the sealed EVAP system, which is the fuel tank, canister, and lines that trap gasoline vapor instead of releasing it into the air, is sending a signal the computer does not trust. | Check the sensor's electrical connector and its small vacuum hose fitting first, before replacing the sensor.<!-- F-WP12-019 --> | 1 only |
| P0401 | The EGR system, which feeds some exhaust gas back into the intake to lower combustion temperature, is not flowing as much as the computer expects. | Clean the EGR valve and its passage first. Carbon buildup restricting the valve is the most commonly cited cause.<!-- F-WP12-020 --> | 2+ |
| P0113 | The intake air temperature sensor is sending a reading the computer considers too high, or possibly reading as an open circuit. | Check whether the code appeared right after disconnecting the battery, cleaning the throttle body, or unplugging the sensor. A code caused this way can sometimes clear itself after normal driving.<!-- F-WP12-021 --> | 1 only |

### Being honest about the "1 only" codes

This book's research found only one 2007-2008 Fit-specific report for P0451 and for P0113, not the two independent reports found for every other code in this table.<!-- F-WP12-019 --><!-- F-WP12-021 --> Both are still real, specific reports about this exact generation of car, not invented entries. But treat them as a lead worth checking, not a settled pattern the way the other 18 codes are.<!-- F-WP12-019 --><!-- F-WP12-021 -->

## What can go wrong
- **Assuming a code always means a broken part.** A DTC often points to a wiring problem, a loose connector, or a low fluid level instead. Always start with the "first thing to check" column, not a parts order.<!-- F-WP12-002 -->
- **Ignoring a flashing check engine light.** A flashing light means stop driving, not "get to it this week."<!-- F-WP12-002 -->
- **Clearing a code and assuming the problem is fixed.** Clearing only turns off the light. If the cause is still there, the code will likely return.
- **Treating P0451 or P0113 as a fully confirmed pattern.** Both only have one Fit-specific report behind them in this book's research.<!-- F-WP12-019 --><!-- F-WP12-021 -->
- **Skipping the cheap check for P2646 or P2647.** Honda's own bulletin says checking the oil level and condition comes before replacing the switch.<!-- F-WP12-010 --><!-- F-WP12-011 -->

## Quick check
1. What does OBD-II stand for, and what does DTC stand for?
2. Where is the OBD-II port on your 2008 Fit, and why is it in that general area on almost any car built since 1996?
3. What does a flashing check engine light mean, and what should you do about it?
4. Which two codes in this chapter's table are backed by an actual Honda service bulletin, and which one code was previously, incorrectly, linked to that same bulletin?
5. Which two codes in the table have only one Fit-specific report, and how should you treat that information?

All 21 fact cards in facts/WP-12.md Sections A and B are used. -->
<!-- Unused Fact IDs: none from Sections A/B. Section C, D, E, F, G table content is reserved for Chapters 34, 36, and 37 and is not repeated here, to avoid duplicating whole chapters. -->

# Chapter 36: Won't Start, Won't Crank, or Cranks But Won't Start

## What you will learn
- The difference between "won't crank" and "cranks but won't start"
- A 9-step flowchart to work through when your Fit will not start
- What the immobilizer light tells you, and what to do if it blinks
- When the problem is likely the battery, and when it is likely something else
- When to try something yourself, and when to call for a tow

## The short version
Cranking means the starter is spinning the engine over; starting means the engine actually fires and keeps running on its own. If the dashboard is completely dark, check the battery first. If the dash lights up but the engine will not crank, check your clutch pedal or shift position and the immobilizer light next. If the engine cranks normally but will not fire, think about flooding, fuel, or ignition.

## The full explanation

### Two different problems that feel similar

"My car won't start" can mean two very different things, and telling them apart is the fastest way to find the cause.

**Won't crank** means when you turn the key to START, nothing happens, or you hear only a click or a slow grinding sound. The starter, the motor that spins the engine to get it running, is not turning the engine over at a normal speed.<!-- WP-12 Section G -->

**Cranks but won't start** means the starter spins the engine at a normal speed, but the engine never fires up and keeps running on its own.<!-- WP-12 Section G -->

This chapter's flowchart below asks you a short question at each step, then tells you exactly where to go next depending on your answer. No step sends you down more than 3 possible paths.<!-- WP-12 Section G -->

### The immobilizer

Your Fit has an immobilizer, an anti-theft system built into the key. The key has a chip inside it that the car's computer must recognize before the engine will start.<!-- F-WP04-029 --> A key-shaped light on the dashboard shows you what the immobilizer is doing. Step 3 of the flowchart below tells you what to watch for.

## Step by step

### The 9-step flowchart

Work through these steps in order. Each step tells you exactly which step to go to next, based on what you find.

**Step 1.** Turn the key to ON (the second position) without starting the engine. Do the dashboard warning lights and gauges light up normally?
- If **yes**, go to Step 2.
- If **no**, meaning the dashboard is completely dark, go to Step 8.<!-- WP-12 Section G -->

**Step 2.** Check your starting position. On a manual transmission, is the clutch pedal pressed all the way to the floor? On an automatic, is the shift lever fully in Park or Neutral, with the brake pedal pressed? The starter will not engage at all unless this is correct.<!-- F-WP04-002 --><!-- F-WP04-003 -->
- If this is correct and the car still will not start, go to Step 3.
- If it was **not** correct, fix it and try again. If it now starts, you are done.<!-- WP-12 Section G -->

**Step 3.** Turn the key to ON and watch the key-shaped immobilizer light. Does it flash, or stay lit, instead of going out normally?
- If **yes**, go to Step 4.
- If **no**, go to Step 5.<!-- WP-12 Section G -->

**Step 4.** Try your spare key.
- If the spare key **starts** the car, your primary key or its chip is likely damaged. Stop here and have the primary key replaced by a dealer.
- If the spare key **also fails** to start the car, the immobilizer system itself may be at fault. Stop here and see a dealer or a qualified shop.<!-- WP-12 Section G -->

**Step 5.** Turn the key to START. Does the engine crank, meaning turn over or spin, at a normal speed?
- If **yes**, go to Step 6.
- If it makes **no sound** and does not move at all, go to Step 8.
- If you hear a **single click**, or a slow grinding sound and the engine barely turns, go to Step 8.<!-- WP-12 Section G -->

**Step 6.** The engine cranks normally but does not fire. Do you smell strong raw gasoline, or did you pump the gas pedal several times before trying to start?
- If **yes**, this may be a flooded engine, meaning too much fuel has reached it. Wait a few minutes without touching the pedal, then try again without pumping the pedal. If it still will not fire, go to Step 7.
- If **no**, go to Step 7.<!-- WP-12 Section G -->

**Step 7.** Check the fuel gauge. Is the tank empty or very close to empty?
- If **yes**, add fuel and try again.
- If **no**, this points to a fuel-delivery or ignition problem. It could be the fuel pump, a fuse, the spark plugs, or the ignition coils. This needs a scan tool and a shop visit, so stop here and arrange service.<!-- WP-12 Section G -->

**Step 8.** Check the battery terminals, the metal connection points on top of the battery. Are they clean, tight, and free of white or blue corrosion?
- If **no**, clean and tighten them, then go back to Step 1.
- If **yes**, go to Step 9.<!-- WP-12 Section G -->

**Step 9.** Jump-starting has real risks, including sparks near battery gases. Always follow every safety warning in Chapter 16 before you connect a single cable.<!-- F-WP07-014 --> Once you have read those warnings, jump-start the car using the terminal order already covered in Chapter 16. Connect positive to positive on both batteries first. Then connect negative on the booster battery, then the unpainted metal ground point on your car.<!-- F-WP07-012 -->
- Does the car **start**? If yes, drive for at least 20 to 30 minutes to recharge the battery, then have the battery and charging system tested soon.
- If **no**, the starter, the alternator, or a deeper electrical fault is likely. Stop here and arrange a tow to a shop.<!-- WP-12 Section G -->

**How you know it worked:** the engine starts and keeps running on its own. The dashboard lights behave normally, and the immobilizer light goes out after its brief flash at key-ON.

## What can go wrong
- **Skipping Step 2.** Many "won't start" calls turn out to be a clutch pedal not fully pressed, or a shifter not fully in Park. Always check this before assuming a bigger problem.<!-- F-WP04-002 --><!-- F-WP04-003 -->
- **Missing the immobilizer light at Step 3.** A blinking key light means the chip in your key was not recognized. Trying to force a start without checking this wastes time.<!-- F-WP04-029 -->
- **Continuing to pump the gas pedal after a possible flooded engine.** This adds more fuel and makes flooding worse, not better.<!-- WP-12 Section G -->
- **Jumping straight to a jump-start before checking the battery terminals.** Dirty or loose terminals are checked at Step 8. That is an easy first fix that a jump-start alone would not solve.<!-- WP-12 Section G -->
- **Connecting jumper cables in the wrong order, or to the wrong point.** Follow the exact order in Step 9 and in Chapter 16 to avoid sparks near the battery.<!-- F-WP07-012 --><!-- F-WP07-014 -->

## Quick check
1. What is the difference between "won't crank" and "cranks but won't start"?
2. What two starting-position checks does Step 2 ask about, and why do they matter?
3. If the immobilizer light keeps blinking even with your spare key, what does that suggest, and what should you do?
4. What should you do first if you suspect a flooded engine?
5. In what order do you connect jumper cables at Step 9?

<!-- Fact IDs used: F-WP04-002, F-WP04-003, F-WP04-029, F-WP07-012, F-WP07-014. Also cited by section reference (WP-12 Section G) for the flowchart's own step logic and branching, which exists in facts/WP-12.md only as the numbered decision tree in Section G, not as separate F-WP12-0## fact cards. -->

# Chapter 37: Noises, Vibrations, Smells, and Leaks: Lookup Tables

## What you will learn
- How to use four lookup tables to narrow down a noise, a vibration, a smell, or a leak
- What each table's urgency rating means for how soon you should act
- How to tell a "Fit-specific" finding from general automotive knowledge, at a glance, in each table
- Where this book's research found a gap, meaning no Fit-specific source at all

## The short version
These four tables sort common car problems by when they happen, how fast you were going, what they smell like, or what color they are. Each row also names an urgency level, from Low to High, so you know how soon to act. A "Fit" tag means real 2007-2008 Fit owners reported this pattern; a "General" tag means the row is true of gasoline cars broadly.

## The full explanation

### How to read these tables

Every row below has a short **Source** tag:
- **Fit** — Owners report that this specific pattern shows up on real 2007-2008 Honda Fits.
- **General** — This is true of gasoline cars broadly, and did not need, or have, a Fit-specific report.<!-- WP-12 Sections C-F: "Grounded in" distinction -->

Use Chapter 34's method before you open these tables: note when it happens, where you notice it, your speed, and any color or smell.<!-- WP-12 Sections C, D, E, F --> Then find the closest matching row below.

Urgency levels mean:
- **Low** — Usually only annoying, not dangerous. Fix it when convenient.
- **Medium** — Worth checking soon. It can get worse or more expensive if ignored.
- **Medium-high** — Get it checked promptly. Waiting has a real cost.
- **High** — This can hurt you, seriously damage the car, or both. Act now.<!-- WP-12 Sections C-F: Urgency column -->

### Table 1: Noises, by when they happen

| When | Noise | Likely cause | Urgency | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| At idle | Ticking or tapping from the top of the engine | Low oil level, or a fault in the VTEC oil pressure switch or solenoid. VTEC is the system that uses engine oil pressure to change the intake valve lift (see Chapter 35's P2646, P2647, and P2649) | Medium — check oil level today | Fit<!-- F-WP12-010 --><!-- F-WP12-011 --><!-- F-WP12-012 --> |
| At idle | Clunk when shifting into Drive or Reverse | A worn engine or transmission mount, the part that holds the engine or transmission to the car's frame | Medium — annoying now, but a worsening clunk can mean a fully failed mount | Fit<!-- F-WP02-016 --> |
| At idle, rough or unstable | Rough or hunting idle speed | A vacuum leak, a dirty throttle body, or a misfire code (see Chapter 35's P0300-series codes) | Medium — get it scanned for codes soon | Fit<!-- F-WP12-002 --><!-- F-WP12-016 --><!-- F-WP12-017 --> |
| Accelerating | Clicking that speeds up with engine speed but does not change with steering | An exhaust heat shield, a metal panel that protects nearby parts from a hot exhaust pipe, rattling loose | Low — annoying, but worth fixing before the shield falls off | Fit<!-- WP-12 Section C: heat shield rattle --> |
| Accelerating, especially under load | Misfire-related roughness, popping, or a flashing check engine light | Spark plugs, ignition coils, or fuel injectors (see Chapter 35's misfire codes) | High if the check engine light is flashing, stop driving and arrange a tow; Medium if the light is steady | Fit<!-- F-WP12-002 --><!-- F-WP12-003 --><!-- F-WP12-004 --><!-- F-WP12-005 --><!-- F-WP12-006 --> |
| Turning, especially sharp turns | Clicking that only happens while turning | A worn outer CV joint, the flexible joint in the axle that lets power reach the wheel while it turns | High — a fully failed CV joint can separate and cause loss of drive; get it inspected soon | Fit<!-- WP-12 Section C: CV joint clicking --> |
| Turning, low speed, such as in a parking lot | Rubbing or grinding | A brake pad wear indicator touching the rotor, or a worn CV joint or steering part | Medium-high — have brakes and the front end checked soon | Fit<!-- WP-12 Section C: rubbing/grinding --> |
| Braking | High-pitched squeal | Worn brake pads, whose built-in wear tab is designed to squeal on purpose, or a missing anti-squeal shim on aftermarket pads | Medium-high — check pad thickness soon; worn-out pads can damage rotors | Fit<!-- WP-12 Section C: brake squeal --> |
| Braking | Grinding, metal-on-metal | Brake pads worn down to the metal backing plate | High — stop driving on it; rotors are likely being damaged and stopping distance is affected | Fit + General<!-- WP-12 Section C: brake grinding --> |
| Releasing the parking brake | Squeak only at that moment | Usually normal friction in the parking-brake mechanism | Low, if it only happens on release and brakes otherwise feel normal | Fit + General<!-- WP-12 Section C: parking brake squeak --> |
| Over bumps | Rattle from the dashboard or glovebox area | Loose interior trim clips, often the cowl panel under the windshield or a part behind the factory radio | Low — annoying, not a safety issue | Fit<!-- F-WP02-022 --> |
| Over bumps | Metallic clunk from the front end | Worn sway-bar end links or strut top-mount bushings, small parts in the front suspension | Medium — have the front suspension inspected; can worsen into a handling problem | Fit<!-- F-WP02-021 --> |
| Over bumps | Rattling from underneath, near the middle or rear of the car | A loose or cracked exhaust heat shield | Low-medium — usually only noise, but a shield that falls off can drag or become a fire risk near dry material | Fit<!-- WP-12 Section C: rear heat shield rattle --> |
| Any time, gets louder with engine speed, not road speed | Whining or grinding from the front of the engine | The alternator's bearing wearing out, or the serpentine belt and its tensioner | Medium-high — a failing alternator can leave you stranded with a dead battery; also check the Smells table below | Fit<!-- WP-12 Section C: alternator whine --> |

### Table 2: Vibrations, by speed range

| Speed range | Vibration felt where | Likely cause | Urgency | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Idle or stopped | Through the seat or floor, not the steering wheel | A worn engine or transmission mount, or a rough idle from a misfire or vacuum leak code | Medium | Fit<!-- F-WP02-016 --><!-- F-WP12-002 --><!-- F-WP12-016 --><!-- F-WP12-017 --> |
| Low speed, under about 20 mph, goes away above that | Shimmy in the steering wheel | A tire out of round, a bent wheel, or a stuck brake caliper part dragging | Medium | General<!-- WP-12 Section D: low-speed shimmy --> |
| Mid-to-highway speed, roughly 45 to 65 mph, through the steering wheel | A shake that gets stronger with speed | A wheel or tire out of balance, meaning its weight is not spread evenly, or a bent rim | Medium-high — have wheels balanced and inspected soon; can speed up tire and bearing wear | Fit + General<!-- WP-12 Section D: mid-highway shake --> |
| Highway speed, roughly 55 to 65 mph, felt through the whole car, automatic transmission only | Shudder, jerk, or hesitation, not a steady vibration | Owners report a split opinion: it could be the automatic transmission itself, or worn ignition coils, valve clearance, or spark plugs presenting as a shudder | Medium-high — get it scanned for codes; do not assume it is only the transmission without a check, since the ignition-related cause is cheaper to fix | Fit<!-- F-WP02-019 --> |
| Any speed, a growl or vibration that grows with speed, sometimes louder in one turning direction | Vibration paired with a grinding or humming noise | A worn wheel bearing, the part that lets the wheel spin smoothly | Medium-high — a bearing that fails completely can cause a wheel to lock up or separate | General<!-- WP-12 Section D: wheel bearing --> |
| Braking, any speed | Pulsation felt in the brake pedal | A warped or worn brake rotor | Medium — schedule a brake inspection; not usually an immediate emergency, but it will worsen | General<!-- WP-12 Section D: brake pulsation --> |

### Table 3: Smells

| Smell | Likely cause | Urgency | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Burning rubber, especially after highway driving or with the heater on | The heater blower motor or its resistor overheating, or a drive belt slipping | Medium — have it checked soon; a slipping belt can eventually fail and strand you | Fit<!-- WP-12 Section E: burning rubber --> |
| Burning plastic or electrical | The alternator or another electrical part overheating; also check for a stuck power window switch, given this car's known switch recall | High — this can be a fire risk. Pull over, shut off the engine, and have it inspected before driving further | Fit<!-- WP-12 Section E: burning plastic --><!-- F-WP02-001 --><!-- F-WP02-002 --> |
| Sweet, syrupy smell, especially from the heater vents or under the hood | Leaking coolant, the fluid that keeps the engine's temperature down | Medium-high — check coolant level; do not keep driving if the temperature gauge or light also shows overheating | General + Fit<!-- WP-12 Section E: sweet smell --> |
| Rotten egg | A catalytic converter running too rich, or, in one specific report on this car, a faint exhaust smell only with the A/C on, while idling, after hard acceleration in hot weather | Medium — get it scanned for the fuel-trim, misfire, and catalyst codes covered in Chapter 35; a converter that keeps overheating from a rich mixture can be destroyed | General + Fit<!-- WP-12 Section E: rotten egg --> |
| Raw gasoline | A fuel leak, an EVAP system leak (the system that traps gasoline vapor), or an overfilled gas tank right after fueling | High if it is a steady, strong smell; do not start the engine near an open flame or smoking. Medium if it is a faint smell right after topping off the tank | General + Fit<!-- WP-12 Section E: raw gasoline --> |
| Hot oil or a burning-oil smell | An oil leak dripping onto a hot exhaust part | Medium — check the oil level and look underneath for drips; oil pooling near hot exhaust for a long time can become a fire risk | General<!-- WP-12 Section E: hot oil --> |
| Clear, odorless puddle in humid or rainy weather | Normal air conditioning condensation | None — this is normal and not a leak to worry about | General<!-- WP-12 Section E: A/C condensation --> |

### Table 4: Leaks, by color and location

Two things about this car matter for reading this table. First, the 2008 Fit uses electric power steering, not a hydraulic pump, so there is no separate red or pink power-steering fluid puddle to worry about.<!-- F-WP02-008 --> Any red fluid you see is almost certainly automatic transmission fluid instead, on automatic-transmission cars.<!-- WP-12 Section F --> Second, on a manual transmission, the clutch and the brakes share the same fluid reservoir and fluid type. A clear-to-amber leak near the driver's side firewall could point to either system.<!-- F-WP02-018 -->

| Location | Color | Likely fluid or cause | Urgency | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Under the front of the car, roughly under the engine | Amber to dark brown, slightly oily | Engine oil | Medium-high — check the dipstick; do not run the engine low on oil | General<!-- WP-12 Section F: engine oil --> |
| Under the front, closer to the center or passenger side, thin and slippery | Red or pink | Automatic transmission fluid; this car has no hydraulic power-steering fluid to confuse it with | Medium-high — have the transmission checked; low fluid can cause the highway shudder symptom in Table 2 | General<!-- F-WP02-008 --><!-- F-WP02-019 --> |
| Under the front, sweet-smelling | Green, yellow-green, orange, pink, or blue, depending on which coolant was last used | Coolant | Medium-high — do not keep driving if it is also overheating; top up and have the leak found and fixed | General + Fit<!-- WP-12 Section F: coolant --> |
| Under the middle of the car, near the driver's side firewall | Clear to light amber, slippery, not sweet-smelling | Brake fluid or clutch fluid, since they share one reservoir on a manual-transmission Fit | High — brake fluid loss is a safety issue; stop driving and have it inspected right away if the pedal feels low or soft, or a brake warning light is on | General + Fit<!-- F-WP02-018 --> |
| Under the middle-front area | Thin, dark, smells strongly of gear oil | Manual transmission fluid | Medium — top up or have it checked; low fluid can cause hard shifting or gear noise | General<!-- WP-12 Section F: MTF --> |
| Under the middle of the car | Clear, odorless, only in hot or humid weather with the A/C running | Air conditioning condensate water | None — this is normal | General<!-- WP-12 Section F: A/C condensation --> |
| Under the rear of the car | Any fluid color | This book's research found no Fit-specific pattern for rear-of-car leaks; the fuel tank and rear brake and axle parts are the main candidates | High if it smells like gasoline; Medium otherwise, pending identification | General (gap: no Fit-specific source found)<!-- WP-12 Section F: rear leaks, flagged gap --> |

## What can go wrong
- **Treating any table as a final diagnosis.** These tables narrow things down. A shop visit or a code check may still be needed to confirm the exact cause.
- **Ignoring a High-urgency row because the car "seems fine."** Brake fluid loss, a burning-plastic smell, and a flashing check engine light are all High for a reason.
- **Mixing up red transmission fluid with power-steering fluid.** This car has no hydraulic power-steering fluid, so red or pink almost always means automatic transmission fluid instead.<!-- F-WP02-008 -->
- **Assuming the rear-of-car leak row is settled.** This book's research found no Fit-specific source for that row, and says so honestly rather than guessing.<!-- WP-12 Section F: rear leaks gap -->
- **Skipping Chapter 34's four questions before opening these tables.** When, where, at what speed, and what color or smell, make these tables far faster to use.

## Quick check
1. What does a "Fit" tag mean in these tables, and what does a "General" tag mean?
2. What should you do if a row's urgency is listed as High?
3. Why is red or pink fluid under the front of this car almost always transmission fluid, not power-steering fluid?
4. Name one noise and one vibration that both point toward a worn CV joint or wheel bearing, and explain why.
5. Which row in the Leaks table does this book admit has no Fit-specific source, and what does that row cover instead?

# Chapter 38: Known Weak Spots on the 2007-2008 Fit

## What you will learn
- What "owners report that" means, and how it is different from a recall.
- Symptoms for 11 wear problems that first-generation Fit owners report.
- A rough shop repair cost for each problem, at today's prices.
- Whether each problem is a good DIY job, or one to leave to a shop.
- The one problem whose repair cost this book could not confirm for the 2008 Fit.

## The short version
Owners of 2007-2008 Fits report 11 wear problems that show up again and again. None of these are recalls. They are patterns spotted by real drivers, not confirmed defects from Honda or the government. Every dollar figure below is a 2024-2026 shop price, not a 2008 price, so an actual 2008 repair bill would have been lower.

## The full explanation

### Why this is different from Chapter 18
Chapter 18 covered recalls and TSBs. A recall is a safety fix Honda must offer for free. A TSB is a repair instruction Honda sends to dealers. Both come from Honda or the government. This chapter is different. It covers patterns that owners on Fit forums have reported seeing again and again on their own cars. This book marks each one with the phrase "owners report that." That phrase means the pattern comes from many individual drivers, not from an official investigation. A community-reported problem can still be real and common. It only means no official body has confirmed it.

### A word about the prices in this chapter
Every dollar amount below comes from repair-cost websites and forum posts checked in 2026. These are today's shop prices, for cars generally 16 to 20 years old. They are not what a repair would have cost when your Fit was new, in 2007 or 2008. Prices also change by region, by shop, and by whether a part is new, aftermarket, or used. Treat every number here as a rough guide, not an exact quote.

### The 11 weak spots

**1. Motor mounts.** Owners report that motor mounts wear out over the life of the car <!-- F-WP02-016 -->. A motor mount is a rubber-and-metal part that bolts the engine and transmission to the car's frame <!-- F-WP02-016 -->. When a mount wears out, you may feel a clunk when you shift into Drive or Reverse <!-- F-WP13-001 -->. You may also feel a clunk under hard acceleration <!-- F-WP13-001 -->. You may feel extra vibration at idle <!-- F-WP13-001 -->. Shop cost for one mount runs roughly $495 to $908, depending on which price page you check <!-- F-WP13-001 -->. New Honda mounts, called OEM parts, cost about $130 each <!-- F-WP13-001 -->. OEM means Original Equipment Manufacturer, the same brand Honda used when it built your car <!-- F-WP13-001 -->. Generic aftermarket mounts run about $30 to $40 each <!-- F-WP13-001 -->. Aftermarket means a part made by a company other than Honda <!-- F-WP13-001 -->. This job is only partly DIY-able <!-- F-WP13-001 -->. DIY means "do it yourself," without paying a shop <!-- F-WP13-001 -->. You need a way to support the engine's weight while a mount is off <!-- F-WP13-001 -->. You also need jack stands and a torque wrench <!-- F-WP13-001 -->. A first-timer should have a helper or a shop manual close by <!-- F-WP13-001 -->.

**2. Timing chain tensioner noise.** Owners report that a ticking or rattling noise comes from the front of the engine <!-- F-WP02-017 -->. It is most noticeable at a cold startup, and it quiets down as the engine warms up <!-- F-WP02-017 -->. A timing chain tensioner keeps the timing chain tight, so the engine's valves open and close at the right moment <!-- F-WP13-002 -->. This is the weakest-sourced item in this chapter <!-- F-WP02-017 -->. Researchers found only one usable online report of this exact problem on a 2008 Fit, not the two separate reports this book normally requires <!-- F-WP02-017 -->. Treat this as a possible pattern, not a settled fact <!-- F-WP02-017 -->. **We could not confirm this for the 2008 Fit.** <!-- F-WP13-002 --> For other small cars, this same kind of tensioner job can run from several hundred dollars to more than a thousand dollars <!-- F-WP13-002 -->. The job costs so much because a mechanic has to remove the front cover of the engine to reach the part <!-- F-WP13-002 -->. This is not a beginner job <!-- F-WP13-002 -->. A chain that slips out of time can bend the engine's valves <!-- F-WP13-002 -->. Take this repair to a shop <!-- F-WP13-002 -->.

**3. Clutch master or slave cylinder wear (manual transmission only).** Owners report that the clutch pedal can suddenly go spongy or soft <!-- F-WP02-018 -->. Sometimes the pedal drops to the floor with little resistance <!-- F-WP02-018 -->. Sometimes there is no fluid puddle under the car, because the leak happens inside the cylinder <!-- F-WP02-018 -->. A master cylinder and a slave cylinder work together to make the clutch disengage when you press the pedal <!-- F-WP13-003 -->. Shops usually replace both cylinders together <!-- F-WP13-003 -->. They do this because the second cylinder tends to fail soon after the first <!-- F-WP13-003 -->. A full job, with both cylinders and a fluid bleed, commonly runs $700 to $1,000 or more <!-- F-WP13-003 -->. This job is only partly DIY-able <!-- F-WP13-003 -->. Swapping the cylinders themselves is not hard <!-- F-WP13-003 -->. Bleeding air out of the hydraulic line afterward is easy to get wrong the first time <!-- F-WP13-003 -->.

**4. Automatic transmission shudder (automatic transmission only).** Owners report that the car shudders, jerks, or hesitates at about 55 to 65 miles per hour on flat ground <!-- F-WP02-019 -->. Forum owners disagree on the cause <!-- F-WP02-019 -->. Some blame the transmission itself <!-- F-WP02-019 -->. Others blame worn spark plugs or ignition coils instead <!-- F-WP02-019 -->. A torque converter is a part inside the automatic transmission that links the spinning engine to the transmission <!-- F-WP13-004 -->. If a worn torque converter turns out to be the cause, expect a bill around $800 to $1,600 <!-- F-WP13-004 -->. This is a general repair-shop price, not a number specific to the Fit <!-- F-WP13-004 -->. Diagnosing this shudder is not a DIY job <!-- F-WP13-004 -->. It needs real test time and, ideally, a scan tool that reads transmission codes <!-- F-WP13-004 -->. Changing the transmission fluid and filter first is a cheap, DIY-able step worth trying before paying for deeper diagnosis <!-- F-WP13-004 -->.

**5. Air conditioning compressor and condenser problems.** Owners report that two separate air conditioning complaints show up on this car <!-- F-WP02-020 -->. First, the condenser can get damaged by road debris <!-- F-WP02-020 -->. A condenser is a radiator-like part, mounted low at the front of the car, that cools the refrigerant <!-- F-WP02-020 -->. One 2008 Fit owner told the government this exact repair cost over $800 <!-- F-WP02-020 -->. Second, some owners report a rattling compressor pulley when the air conditioning turns on <!-- F-WP02-020 -->. Others report air conditioning that cools for a minute and then stops <!-- F-WP02-020 -->. Shop cost for a full compressor replacement runs roughly $1,100 to $1,800, and can run higher <!-- F-WP13-005 -->. This is not a DIY job <!-- F-WP13-005 -->. Opening an air conditioning system needs a special license <!-- F-WP13-005 -->. That license is called EPA Section 609 certification <!-- F-WP13-005 -->. You also need equipment to safely remove the old refrigerant gas <!-- F-WP13-005 -->. A home garage does not have this equipment <!-- F-WP13-005 -->.

**6. Front suspension clunk or knock.** Owners report that a metallic clunk or knock comes from the front of the car over bumps <!-- F-WP02-021 -->. Forum owners most often trace this to one of two parts <!-- F-WP02-021 -->. The first is a worn sway bar end link <!-- F-WP02-021 -->. A sway bar end link is a small linking part that connects the sway bar to the suspension <!-- F-WP02-021 -->. The second is a worn strut top mount <!-- F-WP02-021 -->. A strut top mount is the rubber bushing where the top of a strut bolts to the car's body <!-- F-WP02-021 -->. Owner advice is to check the end links first, before paying for a full strut job <!-- F-WP02-021 -->. Sway bar end links cost roughly $75 to $200 to replace on one side, or $200 to $300 to do both sides at once <!-- F-WP13-006 -->. A full pair of struts, with new top mounts, runs roughly $400 to $900 <!-- F-WP13-006 -->. End links are a real beginner DIY job <!-- F-WP13-006 -->. The job needs only a floor jack, jack stands, and hand tools <!-- F-WP13-006 -->. Struts need a spring compressor tool and real caution <!-- F-WP13-006 -->. A released spring can badly hurt you <!-- F-WP13-006 -->.

**7. Dashboard or glovebox rattle.** Owners report that a rattle comes from the dashboard or glovebox area over bumps <!-- F-WP02-022 -->. It is most often traced to a loose plastic panel under the windshield <!-- F-WP02-022 -->. Sometimes it is a part behind the factory radio instead <!-- F-WP02-022 -->. The rattle tends to get worse in cold weather <!-- F-WP02-022 -->. There is no set price for this job, since it depends on finding the cause first <!-- F-WP13-007 -->. Shops typically bill it as one diagnostic fee visit <!-- F-WP13-007 -->. A diagnostic fee is the charge for the time a shop spends finding a problem before fixing it <!-- F-WP13-007 -->. That fee runs roughly $50 to $150 at an independent shop <!-- F-WP13-007 -->. It can run $100 to $400 or more at a dealer <!-- F-WP13-007 -->. Once found, the actual fix often costs under $20 in parts, such as a broken clip <!-- F-WP13-007 -->. This is a good DIY job for a patient beginner <!-- F-WP13-007 -->. It needs only basic plastic trim-removal tools <!-- F-WP13-007 -->.

**8. Clearcoat peeling.** Owners report that the clearcoat flakes or peels on flat, horizontal surfaces after several years <!-- F-WP02-023 -->. The roof and hood are the surfaces owners mention most <!-- F-WP02-023 -->. Clearcoat is the clear, protective top layer of a car's paint <!-- F-WP02-023 -->. Owners, and a paint-repair professional in one forum discussion, blame thin or improperly cured factory clearcoat <!-- F-WP02-023 -->. Fixing one panel, like only the roof, commonly costs $500 to $1,500 or more <!-- F-WP13-008 -->. A full repaint is sometimes needed once the damage has spread across several panels <!-- F-WP13-008 -->. A full repaint can run $2,000 to $5,000, and sometimes over $10,000 <!-- F-WP13-008 -->. This is not a DIY job <!-- F-WP13-008 -->. Matching factory paint color takes real skill <!-- F-WP13-008 -->. Spraying an even clearcoat without runs also takes real skill and equipment <!-- F-WP13-008 -->.

**9. Rust at the rear quarter panel and wheel well.** Owners report that rust starts where the rear quarter panel meets the rear bumper cover <!-- F-WP02-024 -->. It also starts along a hidden seam near the rocker panel and wheel well <!-- F-WP02-024 -->. This spot collects road grit and moisture <!-- F-WP02-024 -->. Left alone, it can eat through to holes in the driver's rear wheel well <!-- F-WP02-024 -->. This mostly affects cars driven where roads get salted in winter <!-- F-WP02-024 -->. Cleaning and treating surface rust early costs roughly $150 to $900 <!-- F-WP13-009 -->. A real patch repair, once metal has to be cut and welded, runs roughly $800 to $1,800 <!-- F-WP13-009 -->. A full panel replacement can run up to about $2,500 <!-- F-WP13-009 -->. Catching rust early is a real DIY job <!-- F-WP13-009 -->. It uses a rust converter product you can buy cheaply <!-- F-WP13-009 -->. Welding in a new patch panel needs skill and equipment most beginners do not have <!-- F-WP13-009 -->.

**10. Battery drain.** Owners report that the battery goes dead after sitting for two or three days <!-- F-WP02-025 -->. Some owners report the battery draining overnight instead <!-- F-WP02-025 -->. Reported causes include a faulty door switch, an electrical short, or an aftermarket add-on wired into the fuse box, such as an alarm <!-- F-WP02-025 -->. One technical forum post says a healthy 2008 Fit should draw only about 15 to 17 milliamps with everything off <!-- F-WP02-025 -->. A milliamp, written mA, is a small unit of electric current <!-- F-WP02-025 -->. A draw above roughly 40 to 50 mA points to a real fault worth chasing <!-- F-WP02-025 -->. Basic diagnosis at a shop runs $50 to $100 <!-- F-WP13-010 -->. Tracing a harder short can run $150 to $400 or more <!-- F-WP13-010 -->. After that, a new battery costs $50 to $200 <!-- F-WP13-010 -->. A new alternator runs $200 to $600 <!-- F-WP13-010 -->. A wiring repair starts around $100 and climbs with complexity <!-- F-WP13-010 -->. The diagnostic step is DIY-able with a simple multimeter and patience <!-- F-WP13-010 -->. Replacing a battery or a simple switch is beginner-easy <!-- F-WP13-010 -->. Chasing an intermittent wiring short is much harder <!-- F-WP13-010 -->.

**11. Weak factory radio and speakers.** Owners report that the factory speaker system sounds thin <!-- F-WP02-026 -->. Some trims are missing rear door speakers <!-- F-WP02-026 -->. Some head units lack a way to plug in an aftermarket amplifier <!-- F-WP02-026 -->. This is a comfort complaint, not a safety defect <!-- F-WP02-026 -->. A professional head-unit swap commonly costs $150 to $550 all-in for a basic unit <!-- F-WP13-011 -->. A unit with Bluetooth or a phone-mirroring screen costs more <!-- F-WP13-011 -->. Replacing all four speakers plus the head unit at a shop runs roughly $600 to $1,100 <!-- F-WP13-011 -->. This is one of the most beginner-friendly car projects there is <!-- F-WP13-011 -->. It needs only a screwdriver and a trim-panel tool <!-- F-WP13-011 -->. A wiring adapter is a good idea too, so you do not have to cut the factory wiring <!-- F-WP13-011 -->.

### Putting it together
Most of these 11 items are wear-and-tear, not danger <!-- F-WP02-016 -->. None of them stop you from driving your car safely on their own <!-- F-WP02-016 -->. Still, always fix a real safety issue, like a shuddering transmission or a suspension clunk, before it gets worse <!-- F-WP02-019 -->. The next chapter shows you how to find a fair shop for any repair on this list that you cannot, or should not, do yourself.

## Quick check
1. What does "owners report that" mean, and how is it different from a recall?
2. Name three of the 11 weak spots and their main symptom.
3. Which weak spot's repair cost could this book not confirm for the 2008 Fit, and why not?
4. Name one weak spot that is a good DIY job for a beginner, and one that is not.
5. Why are the dollar amounts in this chapter higher than what a 2008 Fit owner actually paid back in 2008?

# Chapter 39: When to Go to a Shop, and How to Not Get Ripped Off

## What you will learn
- The difference between an independent shop and a dealership, and why their prices differ.
- Typical hourly labor rates for each, at today's prices.
- Three situations where paying a dealer's higher rate still makes sense.
- How to read every line of a repair estimate before you agree to pay.
- Simple habits that protect you from a surprise bill.

## The short version
Independent shops usually charge less per hour than dealerships. A dealer is still the right choice in a few specific cases. Every repair estimate has the same five parts: a diagnostic fee, labor, parts, shop supplies, and tax. Read every line before you agree to a repair, and compare the final bill to that estimate before you pay.

## The full explanation

### Independent shop versus dealership: labor rates
A labor rate is the hourly price a shop charges for a mechanic's time <!-- F-WP13-012 -->. It is separate from the cost of parts <!-- F-WP13-012 -->. An independent shop is a repair shop not owned by a car maker <!-- F-WP13-012 -->. Independent shops commonly charge $80 to $130 per hour <!-- F-WP13-012 -->. Dealerships commonly charge $120 to $180 per hour <!-- F-WP13-012 -->. A separate national study found that nearly half of all US repair shops price their labor somewhere between $120 and $159 per hour <!-- F-WP13-012 -->. Dealerships tend to charge more for a few reasons <!-- F-WP13-012 -->. Their mechanics go through the car maker's own training programs <!-- F-WP13-012 -->. They must buy diagnostic tools the maker requires <!-- F-WP13-012 -->. They also carry higher costs from running a franchise and a larger building <!-- F-WP13-012 -->.

For a routine job you already understand, like brakes or a motor mount, an independent shop can often do equal-quality work for meaningfully less money <!-- F-WP13-012 -->. Look for a shop with mechanics who hold ASE certification, a widely recognized test for mechanics <!-- F-WP13-012 -->. A dealer becomes the better choice in three cases <!-- F-WP13-012 -->. First, the repair needs a special tool only the maker sells <!-- F-WP13-012 -->. Second, the repair is still covered by a warranty, a recall, or a TSB; see Chapter 18 for your car's own recall list <!-- F-WP13-012 -->. Third, the needed part is sold only through the dealer's own parts network <!-- F-WP13-012 -->.

### How to read a repair estimate
Ask for a written estimate before any work starts <!-- F-WP13-013 -->. Do not settle for a spoken number only <!-- F-WP13-013 -->. A complete estimate has five parts <!-- F-WP13-013 -->.

**Diagnostic fee.** This is the charge for the time a shop spends figuring out what is wrong <!-- F-WP13-013 -->. It comes before any repair is agreed to <!-- F-WP13-013 -->. It commonly covers 60 to 90 minutes of labor <!-- F-WP13-013 -->. Many shops apply this fee toward your repair bill if you have the work done there <!-- F-WP13-013 -->. Ask this question before you say yes to the diagnosis <!-- F-WP13-013 -->.

**Labor.** This line shows the job name, a number of hours, and the shop's hourly rate <!-- F-WP13-013 -->. Hours are often written in tenths, so 3.5 means three and a half hours <!-- F-WP13-013 -->. You can check the math yourself by multiplying the hours by the rate <!-- F-WP13-013 -->.

**Parts.** Each parts line should say exactly what is being replaced <!-- F-WP13-013 -->. It should also say what kind of part it is <!-- F-WP13-013 -->. A part can be new OEM, meaning Original Equipment Manufacturer, the same brand Honda used <!-- F-WP13-013 -->. A part can be new aftermarket, made by a different company <!-- F-WP13-013 -->. A part can be remanufactured, meaning a used part rebuilt to work like new <!-- F-WP13-013 -->. A part can also be plain used <!-- F-WP13-013 -->. A cheaper price on this line often means a lower-tier part, which is worth asking about <!-- F-WP13-013 -->.

**Shop supplies.** This line covers small extra costs, like rags, cleaning fluid, and small fasteners <!-- F-WP13-013 -->. It is normal to see this charge <!-- F-WP13-013 -->. It is usually about 5 to 10 percent of the labor total <!-- F-WP13-013 -->. A vague fee, with no clear dollar amount or percentage, is worth questioning <!-- F-WP13-013 -->.

**Tax.** Sales tax applies to parts in most states <!-- F-WP13-013 -->. Whether labor also gets taxed depends on your state <!-- F-WP13-013 -->. This line will vary by where you live <!-- F-WP13-013 -->.

### Habits that protect you
Get every estimate in writing before work starts <!-- F-WP13-013 -->. Check that the paperwork lists your own car's VIN and mileage, not another car's <!-- F-WP13-013 -->. A VIN is your car's own 17-character serial number, covered in Chapter 18 <!-- F-WP13-013 -->. When the final bill arrives, compare it line by line against the original estimate <!-- F-WP13-013 -->. A bill that grew without a phone call explaining why is a warning sign <!-- F-WP13-013 -->.

### Tying it back to Chapter 38
Chapter 38 told you which of the 11 weak spots a beginner can safely try at home. When a repair on that list is marked "no," or needs tools you do not have, use this chapter's habits before you pay. Ask for a written estimate. Read every line. Compare the final bill against it. Doing this each time protects you far more than trying to memorize a "fair price" for every possible repair.

## Quick check
1. What is the usual hourly labor-rate range at an independent shop, and at a dealership?
2. Name three situations where paying a dealer's higher rate makes sense.
3. What are the five parts of a complete repair estimate?
4. What does OEM mean, and how is it different from aftermarket and remanufactured?
5. What two things should you always compare before paying a repair bill?

<!-- Fact IDs used: F-WP13-012, F-WP13-013 -->
<!-- Fact IDs from facts/WP-13.md tagged Chapter 39 but NOT used in this chapter, and why:
None. Both Chapter-39-tagged facts in WP-13.md (F-WP13-012, F-WP13-013) are used above.
-->

# Part VII: Repairs You Can Do at Home

---

# Chapter 40: Brake job, full

## What you will learn

- The difference between a basic pad or shoe swap and a full brake job
- How to check a front brake rotor and replace it along with the pads
- How to check a rear brake drum and replace or resurface it
- The extra tools a full brake job needs beyond a basic pad or shoe job
- What can go wrong on these harder steps, and how to catch it

## The short version

Chapter 27 already showed you how to swap front brake pads and rear brake shoes on their own. <!-- P-WP11-7 --> <!-- P-WP11-8 --> A full brake job goes one step further: you also check the front rotor and the rear drum, and replace either one if it is worn out. <!-- P-WP14-1 --> <!-- P-WP14-2 --> You do this work with the same jack stands, the same care, and mostly the same tools as Chapter 27, plus a few extras for the rotor and the drum.

## The full explanation

A rotor is the flat metal disc that spins with your front wheel. The caliper squeezes your brake pads against it to slow the car. A drum is the metal cylinder that spins with your rear wheel. Your brake shoes press outward against the inside of the drum to slow the car. <!-- P-WP11-8 -->

Pads and shoes wear out on their own schedule, and so do rotors and drums. A rotor or drum can wear thin, get scored with deep grooves, or crack, even while the pads or shoes next to it still have life left. Both the rotor and the drum carry a wear-limit number stamped into the metal. If your measurement goes past that number, or the metal is cracked or deeply grooved, you replace the part instead of reusing it. <!-- P-WP14-1 --> <!-- P-WP14-2 -->

This chapter does not repeat the pad and shoe steps from Chapter 27. It focuses only on what a full brake job adds: taking the rotor or drum off, checking it, and putting a new or resurfaced one back on.

## Step by step

### Front brakes: pads and rotors together

Do this job with the safety rules from Chapter 27 in mind. Always wear eye protection, since brake dust and rust flakes can hurt your eyes. <!-- P-WP14-1 --> Do not breathe brake dust, and never use compressed air or a dry brush on brake parts; use brake-parts cleaner instead. <!-- P-WP14-1 --> Never work under a car held up only by a jack; always use jack stands. <!-- P-WP14-1 --> Work on one wheel at a time and leave the other wheel on the ground, so you always have a reference for how the parts go back together. <!-- P-WP14-1 --> Never let the caliper hang by its brake hose; support it so the hose carries no weight. <!-- P-WP14-1 --> Never resurface or reuse a rotor that is cracked, deeply grooved, or thinner than its stamped minimum thickness; replace it instead. <!-- P-WP14-1 --> Always pump the brake pedal several times before you drive, until it feels firm again. <!-- P-WP14-1 -->

1. Loosen the front wheel nuts, then raise and support the car on jack stands, and remove the wheel. <!-- P-WP14-1 -->
2. Remove the caliper slide-pin bolts and lift the caliper off its bracket. Hang the caliper so it does not pull on the brake hose. <!-- P-WP14-1 -->
3. Remove the two caliper mounting-bracket bolts and lift the bracket off the steering knuckle, the metal part the wheel bearing sits in. <!-- P-WP14-1 -->
4. Check the rotor's edge for a minimum-thickness number stamped into the metal, and write it down before you remove the rotor. <!-- P-WP14-1 -->
5. If the rotor is stuck to the hub, tap it near its center with a rubber mallet. Never hit the braking surface. <!-- P-WP14-1 -->
6. Pull the old rotor off the hub studs. <!-- P-WP14-1 -->
7. Clean rust and debris off the hub face with a wire brush, then wipe it clean. <!-- P-WP14-1 -->
8. Apply a thin coat of anti-seize compound to the hub face only. Keep it off the braking surface. <!-- P-WP14-1 -->
9. Slide the new rotor onto the hub studs. <!-- P-WP14-1 -->
10. Clean the caliper bracket and slide pins with a wire brush and brake-parts cleaner. <!-- P-WP14-1 -->
11. Reinstall the caliper bracket. Owners report torquing its mounting bolts to 80 lbf-ft (108 N-m), but this number is not independently confirmed for this car. <!-- P-WP14-1 -->
12. Use a C-clamp against an old pad, or a block of wood, to push the caliper piston back into its bore. <!-- P-WP14-1 -->
13. Coat the new pads' metal backing and the slide pins with a thin layer of brake grease, then place the pads into the bracket. <!-- P-WP14-1 -->
14. Slide the caliper back over the new pads and reinstall the slide-pin bolts. <!-- P-WP14-1 -->
15. Reinstall the wheel, snug the nuts by hand, then lower the car and torque the wheel nuts to 80 lbf-ft (108 N-m) in a crisscross pattern. <!-- P-WP14-1 -->
16. Repeat steps 1 through 15 on the other front wheel. <!-- P-WP14-1 -->
17. Pump the brake pedal several times with the engine off, until it feels firm. Then check the brake fluid reservoir and top it off if needed. <!-- P-WP14-1 -->
18. Drive slowly at first and test the brakes gently in an empty area before you return to normal driving. <!-- P-WP14-1 -->

### Rear brakes: drums

Chapter 27 already walked you through the rear brake shoe swap on this car, including the return spring, which is the hardest single step in that job. <!-- P-WP11-8 --> This section only adds the drum check. Do it in the same session as the shoe swap, since the drum has to come off to reach the shoes anyway. <!-- P-WP14-2 -->

Always wear eye protection when you inspect or clean a drum. <!-- P-WP14-2 --> Never reinstall a drum that is cracked, deeply scored, or worn past its stamped maximum diameter; replace it instead. <!-- P-WP14-2 --> Never work under a car held up only by a jack; always use jack stands. <!-- P-WP14-2 -->

1. Follow the first three steps of Chapter 27's rear brake job: raise the car, remove the wheel, and pull the drum off the hub. <!-- P-WP14-2 -->
2. Look for a maximum-diameter number cast or stamped into the drum's outer face. <!-- P-WP14-2 -->
3. Measure the drum's inside diameter with a micrometer or caliper, checking several points around the circle. <!-- P-WP14-2 -->
4. If any measurement goes past the stamped maximum diameter, or the surface has deep grooves or cracks, plan to replace both rear drums as a pair. <!-- P-WP14-2 -->
5. If the drum is within its limit but glazed or lightly scored, a machine shop can resurface it instead of replacing it. Ask the shop whether resurfacing costs less than a new drum. <!-- P-WP14-2 -->
6. If you are replacing the drum, do not transfer anything from the old drum itself. Only the shoes, springs, and adjuster carry over. <!-- P-WP14-2 -->
7. Finish the rest of Chapter 27's rear brake shoe steps, using the new or resurfaced drum in place of the old one. <!-- P-WP14-2 -->

## What can go wrong

Getting anti-seize compound or grease on the rotor's braking surface, or on the pad linings, causes squealing and cuts your stopping power. <!-- P-WP14-1 --> Reusing a rotor or drum that is warped, cracked, or past its wear limit is the most common mistake in a full brake job. Always check the stamped number before you decide to reuse a part. <!-- P-WP14-1 --> <!-- P-WP14-2 --> Pushing a caliper piston back without first loosening the brake fluid reservoir cap can push fluid out or damage a seal. <!-- P-WP14-1 --> Letting a caliper hang by its hose can stretch or kink the hose. <!-- P-WP14-1 --> Replacing only one side's drum, instead of both as a pair, can cause the car to pull or brake unevenly. <!-- P-WP14-2 --> And skipping the "pump the pedal first" step, on either job, can mean the pedal goes soft or all the way to the floor on your first drive. <!-- P-WP14-1 --> <!-- P-WP14-2 -->

## Quick check

1. What two extra parts does a full brake job add on top of Chapter 27's basic pad and shoe swap?
2. How do you know a rotor or drum has to be replaced instead of reused?
3. Why should you never let a caliper hang by its brake hose?
4. Why should you check both rear drums, instead of only the one side that looks worn?
5. What should you do with the brake pedal before your first drive after the job, and why?

<!-- Procedure IDs used: P-WP14-1, P-WP14-2, P-WP11-7, P-WP11-8, P-WP11-22 -->
