# Chapter 48: Stage 0 — Get Your Baseline Right First

## What you will learn

- What "Stage 0" means, and why it comes before any performance part
- Why fresh fluids matter before you add stress to your Fit
- Why good tires and good brakes matter more than any bolt-on part
- Why this book cannot hand you an alignment procedure, and what to do instead

## The short version

Before you buy a single performance part, make sure your Fit's basic maintenance is already current. Stage 0 means fresh oil, tires in good shape, and brakes that already work correctly. Stage 0 costs less than most single mods, and it protects every mod you add after it.

## The full explanation

### What "Stage 0" means

Most modding guides number their steps: Stage 1, Stage 2, and so on, from mild to wild. **Stage 0** is the step before all of those. It means bringing your Fit's existing maintenance up to date before you spend a dollar on power, handling, or looks. A stiffer suspension on old, worn shocks will not behave the way a review video shows. A grippier tire on a car with a slipping alignment will wear out fast and unevenly.

This chapter is short by design. Every Stage 0 item already has a full procedure card written in an earlier chapter of this book. This chapter tells you which chapter to open and why that item matters before you mod, instead of repeating steps you can already find there.

### Fresh fluids

The first Stage 0 item is fresh engine oil and a fresh filter. <!-- F-WP15-015 --> Old oil has already picked up dirt and lost some of its ability to protect your engine. A car that will work harder, longer, or hotter than normal deserves oil that is not already tired. Chapter 24 covers the full oil-change procedure, including the tools, the drain-bolt torque spec, and every safety warning. <!-- F-WP15-015 --> Open Chapter 24 and follow its steps rather than guessing at your own order.

### Good tires

The second Stage 0 item is good tires: correct, even air pressure, and no uneven wear from a rotation you skipped. <!-- F-WP15-016 --> This is a check, not a rebuild. Chapter 26 already covers how to check and set your tire pressure, and how to rotate your tires so they wear evenly. <!-- F-WP15-016 --> Read Chapter 26 and confirm both are already current before you spend money on new wheels or tires, which Chapter 49 covers next.

Stage 0 is not about buying new tires yet. It is about confirming the tires you already have are properly maintained, so any new tire or wheel choice starts from a clean baseline instead of hiding an existing problem.

### Good brakes

The third Stage 0 item is good brakes. This means pads or shoes and fluid that are already in solid condition before you add track driving or spirited street driving to the mix. <!-- F-WP15-017 --> Your Fit's front brakes use pads and rotors, and its rear brakes use shoes inside a drum, which is a different design with different parts and a different repair. Chapter 27 covers inspecting the front pads through the wheel, replacing the front pads, replacing the rear shoes, and bleeding the brake fluid. <!-- F-WP15-017 --> Work through Chapter 27's checks before you plan any brake upgrade in Chapter 51.

### Alignment: the one gap in this book

Here is where this book has to be honest with you instead of guessing. **Alignment** is the process of adjusting your wheels' angles, camber, caster, and toe, so your car drives straight, handles predictably, and wears its tires evenly. This book does not include a step-by-step alignment procedure. <!-- F-WP15-018 --> We could not confirm a home alignment procedure for the 2008 Fit in this book's research. Check your owner's manual, and plan on a shop visit instead. <!-- F-WP15-018 -->

There is a practical reason for this gap. Alignment normally needs an **alignment rack**, a specialized shop machine that measures your wheel angles precisely and then adjusts them. <!-- F-WP15-018 --> This is not a typical home-garage job the way an oil change or a brake-pad swap is. For Stage 0, the right move is to have a shop check your alignment, especially if the car has ever been in a curb strike or a minor accident. Once you get to Chapter 50, you will also want a fresh alignment any time you change springs, coilovers, or camber parts.

### A simple Stage 0 checklist

- Fresh engine oil and filter, per Chapter 24. <!-- F-WP15-015 -->
- Correct, even tire pressure and a current tire rotation, per Chapter 26. <!-- F-WP15-016 -->
- Healthy front brake pads, healthy rear brake shoes, and fresh brake fluid, per Chapter 27. <!-- F-WP15-017 -->
- An alignment check at a shop, especially after any curb strike, accident, or suspension work. <!-- F-WP15-018 -->

## Quick check

1. What does "Stage 0" mean, and why does it come before power or handling mods?
2. Which chapter should you open for the exact oil-change steps at Stage 0?
3. Name the two tire-related checks that count toward Stage 0, and which chapter covers them.
4. Why does "good brakes" mean checking two different repair types on your Fit, not only one?
5. Why does this book send you to a shop for alignment instead of giving you home steps?

# Chapter 49: Tires and Wheels — Sizes, Offset, and Weight

## What you will learn

- What the numbers in a tire size and a wheel offset actually mean
- Which wheel and tire sizes owners report fitting a GD3 without rubbing
- Why a bigger wheel is not automatically a better wheel
- What autocross class a stock Fit runs in, and how tire choice connects to it

## The short version

Fitting a tire and wheel to your Fit depends on diameter, width, and offset working together, not any single number. Owners of first-generation Fits report that a 195/50R15 tire on a 15x6.5-to-7-inch wheel fits without rubbing. Bigger wheels and wider tires get progressively tighter, and they also add weight your engine has to turn, which can work against your handling goal instead of helping it.

## The full explanation

### Reading a tire size, and what "offset" means

A tire size like 195/50R15 has three parts. The 195 is the tire's width in millimeters. The 50 is the **aspect ratio**, the tire's sidewall height as a percentage of that width. The R15 means it is a radial tire built for a 15-inch wheel diameter.

**Offset** is a separate number, measured in millimeters, that describes how far the wheel's mounting face sits from its true centerline. A higher offset number pulls the tire in toward the engine and suspension. A lower offset number pushes the tire out toward the fender. Offset, tire width, and wheel diameter all work together to decide whether a tire **rubs**. A tire rubs when it touches the fender, a suspension part, or a body panel while turning or going over a bump.

### Sizes owners report fitting without rubbing

Owners report that 195/50R15 on a 15x6.5-to-7-inch wheel, at +40 to +42mm offset, is the combination that fits a stock-height GD3 without rubbing. <!-- F-WP16-001 --> A 205/45R16 tire on a 16x7-inch wheel is described as more marginal. It only works at a minimum offset of +42mm, and some owners report rear rubbing, especially with passengers in the back seat. <!-- F-WP16-001 -->

Owners also report on a 205/50R15 tire, one size wider than the 195/50R15 baseline. <!-- F-WP16-002 --> The consensus from those reports is that a 205/50R15 fits without severe rubbing. It runs tighter than the baseline size, though, and it may need the fender lip rolled back or a lower ride height to stay fully clear at full steering lock. <!-- F-WP16-002 -->

One owner who autocrossed a GD3 for four to five seasons reports a staggered setup. Up front sit 225/45R15 tires on 15x7.5-inch wheels, and the rear runs narrower 205/50R15 tires. <!-- F-WP16-003 --> A **staggered** setup means the front and rear tires are different sizes on purpose. This particular combination is reported by only one source, so treat it as one experienced owner's setup, not a confirmed community consensus. <!-- F-WP16-003 -->

### Weight: why bigger is not always better

A wheel and tire are part of your car's **unsprung weight**, the mass that your springs do not support, since it sits below them and moves with the road surface. Heavier unsprung weight generally makes a suspension react slower and feel less precise, even if the tire itself grips well. This is one reason the autocross setup above pairs a wider front tire with a narrower, lighter rear wheel. The wider front tire adds grip up front, without adding weight to the rear too. <!-- F-WP16-003 -->

Every size owners report above stays inside the 15-inch wheel diameter family. This matters because your Fit's Sport trim already used a 15-inch factory wheel. Staying at 15 inches keeps your speedometer and anti-lock brake system reading closer to correct than jumping straight to a 16-inch or 17-inch wheel would. <!-- F-WP16-006 -->

### Autocross tire sizes and classing

**Autocross** is a timed event where you drive one car at a time through a course marked with cones, usually in a parking lot. A stock, unmodified 2008 Fit runs in **H Street (HS)** class under SCCA's national Solo rules, since the rulebook's "Fit" entry does not carry a model-year restriction. <!-- F-WP16-004 --> Once you add aftermarket wheels, tires, and other Street Touring-eligible parts, your Fit typically moves into **Street Touring FWD (STF)** class instead. STF allows a wider range of parts than H Street does. <!-- F-WP16-005 -->

Combining every source found for this chapter, two sizes come up most often for GD3 autocross: 195/50R15 and 205/50R15, both usually run on 15x7-to-7.5-inch wheels. <!-- F-WP16-006 --> Chapter 58 covers autocross classing and event prep in full detail; this chapter only connects your tire choice to that bigger picture.

### A buying tip before you spend

Every size above is community-reported, meaning real owners shared it online, not a guarantee written by Honda or a tire manufacturer. Your own car's ride height, past accident history, and even factory tolerances can shift what actually clears. Before you buy a full set, ask the seller or a shop whether they have fit that exact size to a GD3 before. Check clearance on your own car at full steering lock before you commit.

## Quick check

1. What do the three parts of a tire size like 195/50R15 tell you?
2. What is "offset," and why does changing it affect whether a tire rubs on the fender?
3. Which size do owners report as the safest baseline fit for a GD3, and which size is described as marginal?
4. Why can a bigger, heavier wheel work against your handling goal instead of helping it?
5. What SCCA class does a stock 2008 Fit run in, and what class do many modified GD3s move into?

# Chapter 50: Suspension — Springs, Coilovers, Sway Bars, and Alignment

## What you will learn

- What a spring, a coilover, and a sway bar each actually do
- 9 real suspension products confirmed to fit your GD3, and how each was checked
- 7 real products that looked right but were rejected, and exactly why
- How to run that same fitment check yourself before you ever buy a part
- Street and track alignment targets, and why they are not the same numbers

## The short version

A lowering spring drops your ride height and changes how stiff your suspension feels, and a coilover is a fully adjustable spring-and-shock unit. A sway bar reduces how much your car leans in a corner. This chapter lists 9 real products confirmed, from each product's own listing text, to fit your 2007-2008 GD3 Fit. It also lists 7 look-alike products this book rejected, because they were actually built for the different 2009-2014 GE8 Fit. Learning to spot that difference yourself is one of the most useful skills a Fit modder can build.

## The full explanation

### What these parts actually do

A **spring** is the coiled metal part that holds your car's weight up and compresses when the wheel hits a bump. A shorter, stiffer aftermarket spring usually lowers your ride height and reduces how much the car leans and dives. A **coilover** combines a spring around a shock absorber, called a **damper**, into one adjustable unit. Most coilovers let you set ride height independent of how hard the spring is preloaded, and many also let you adjust damping, meaning how quickly the shock compresses and rebounds.

A **sway bar**, also called an anti-roll bar, is a metal bar that connects the left and right suspension. It twists slightly in a corner to resist body lean. A thicker sway bar generally means a stiffer, less-leaning car.

Three more terms matter for the alignment section later in this chapter. **Camber** is how much a wheel tilts inward or outward at the top, viewed from the front of the car, measured in degrees. **Caster** is how much the steering axis tilts, viewed from the side. **Toe** is whether the wheels point slightly toward each other or away from each other, viewed from above.

### The single most important rule in this chapter

Chapter 47 warned you that a part built for the 2009-2014 Fit, chassis code GE8, does not automatically fit your 2007-2008 Fit, chassis code GD3, even when it looks identical. This chapter puts that warning into practice with real parts, real listings, and real mistakes this book's own research caught.

Here is how you run the same check yourself. Read the product listing's own text, not only its headline. Look for the words "GD3," "2007-2008," or "07-08," or for two separate fitment lines, one for 2007 and one for 2008. A generic claim like "fits Honda Fit" is not enough on its own, since that same wording can quietly cover the GE8 too. Watch for three specific traps. A trusted brand can sell a near-identical part for the GE8 under a similar name. A part number can look right while sitting on a different generation's page. And a Japanese-market production-date window can fail to line up with the US 2007-2008 model years, even when the chassis code looks correct.

### 9 real products confirmed for the GD3

Every product below states, in its own listing text, that it fits the 2007-2008 Fit or the GD3 chassis code by name. Confirm this yourself again at the time you buy, since listings change.

| Product | Type | GD3 confirmation in the listing | Reported spec |
|---|---|---|---|
| BC Racing BR Series | Coilover | "2007-2008 Honda Fit FWD (GD3)," two separate year lines | 5 kg/mm front and rear, ride height and damping adjustable <!-- F-WP16-007 --> |
| TEIN BASIC (DSB36-LUAS2) | Coilover | "07+ GD3 Honda Fit FF" | Twin-tube, 22mm piston front and rear, ride height adjustable <!-- F-WP16-008 --> |
| Skunk2 Pro-C (541-05-6990) | Coilover | "06-08 Honda Fit" / "2006-2008 Honda Fit" | 12-way adjustable, 8 kg/mm front, 6 kg/mm progressive rear, race-oriented <!-- F-WP16-009 --> |
| Fortune Auto 510 Series | Coilover | "Honda Fit (GD3) USDM 2007-2008," repeated on the page | Customer-selectable spring rates, 4k-16k kg/mm range <!-- F-WP16-010 --> |
| F2 Suspension Type 2 | Coilover | "Honda Fit GD (2006-2008)" | Fitment confirmed; specific spring rates not confirmed this pass <!-- F-WP16-011 --> |
| TEIN S.Tech | Lowering spring | "2007-2008 Honda Fit FWD (GD3)," two separate year lines | Front 2.8 kg/mm, rear 6 kg/mm, drop about 1.8 in front / 1.2 in rear <!-- F-WP16-012 --> |
| H&R Sport Spring (51830) | Lowering spring | Fitment table names 2007 and 2008 Fit Base, and Sport, separately | Drop about 1.6 in front / 1.3 in rear <!-- F-WP16-013 --> |
| Eibach Pro-Kit (4069.140) | Lowering spring | Listing's own fitment list names only "2007 Honda Fit" and "2008 Honda Fit," no 2009+ line | Drop 1.6 in front / 1.3 in rear <!-- F-WP16-014 --> |
| Fortune Auto 500 Series | Coilover | "Honda Fit (GD3) USDM 2007-2008," same title pattern as the 510 Series | Entry-level version of the 510 Series above <!-- F-WP16-015 --> |

That is 9 real spring and coilover products, across 6 different brands, each confirmed by its own listing text to fit the GD3. Notice that the H&R spring's own fitment table names your base trim directly, not only the Sport trim. <!-- F-WP16-013 -->

### 7 rejected products — the year-confusion trap, caught in the act

While researching the list above, this book also found 7 similar-looking products that failed the same check. Every one is recorded here so you can recognize the pattern, not only avoid these specific parts.

| # | Category | Product | Why it was rejected |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Lowering spring | Eibach Pro-Kit, part 4081.140 | Listing says "2009-2013" / "09-12 Honda Fit," a GE8-only part with a different part number than the correct GD3 part above |
| 2 | Coilover | TEIN Street Basis Z | Listing title and URL say "2009-2014 Honda Fit FWD (GE8)" |
| 3 | Coilover | TEIN Street Advance Z | Listing tags itself "(GE8)," even though "2008" also appears in the title text |
| 4 | Camber bolt | Eibach Pro-Alignment 5.81260K | Listing says "Honda Fit (2009-2014)" (GE8) |
| 5 | Brake pad | EBC Yellowstuff | Listing says "Honda Fit (09-15)" (GE8/GK); no listing naming "2007-2008" or "GD3" was found |
| 6 | Camber bolt | SPC EZCam 81260, on a "09-14" page | Same physical part number as a confirmed GD3 fact below, but this specific page is tagged for the GE8, so it is not cited as a GD3 source |
| 7 | Coilover | TEIN FLEX Z | Listing does say "GD3," but its own Japanese production-date window, 2003.10 to 2007.09, ends before the US 2008 model year begins |

Row 3 is worth reading twice. A listing can say "2008" in its title and still not be for your car. That happens when the same listing also tags itself with a trim or region code, like "RS," that Honda never sold as a US 2008 base Fit. Row 7 shows an even sneakier version of the same trap: the chassis code is right, but the production-date window quietly excludes your car's actual build year. Read every date and code on a listing, not only the ones that jump out at you.

This chapter researched three categories: springs and coilovers, camber bolts, and brake pads. Across all three, 7 total candidates were found and rejected for not actually fitting the GD3, judged by their own listing text. <!-- S-WP16-AA, S-WP16-AB, S-WP16-AC, S-WP16-AD, S-WP16-AE, S-WP16-AF, S-WP16-AG -->

### The same part, three different listings

One more case study drives the lesson home. The retailer Heeltoe Automotive sells the identical SPC 81260 camber bolt on three separate product pages. One page is tagged "07-08 Honda Fit" for your GD3. Another is tagged "09-14 Honda Fit" for the GE8, and a third is tagged "15-20 Honda Fit" for the newer GK generation. <!-- F-WP16-021 --> A buyer who searches only by the bare part number, 81260, could click into the wrong page and never notice. <!-- F-WP16-021 --> Always confirm you are on the generation-matched page, not only the correct part number.

### Sway bars for the rear

The Progress Technology 22mm rear sway bar, part 62.1060, is listed for "07-08 Honda Fit," and its own body copy adds "Compatible with GD Honda Fit." <!-- F-WP16-016 --> The Ultra Racing 16mm rear sway bar is listed for "Honda Fit (GD) 2001-2008," a thinner alternative to the Progress bar above. <!-- F-WP16-017 -->

One listing deserves a caution instead of a recommendation. A Whiteline sway bar link is sold under a web address that says "rear sway bar link." The same page's own title and body text, read directly, say "Front Sway Bar Link" throughout instead. <!-- F-WP16-018 --> Because the page disagrees with itself about which end of the car this part is for, this book does not use it as a confirmed rear-bar part. <!-- F-WP16-018 --> As a bonus, a Cusco 24mm bar listed for "Honda Fit-Jazz GD1/GD3" was also found this pass. It is explicitly a front sway bar, not a rear one, so skip it if you are specifically shopping for a rear bar. <!-- F-WP16-019 -->

### Camber and alignment adjustment parts

The SPC EZCam 14mm camber bolt, part 81260, gives roughly plus or minus 1.75 degrees of front camber adjustment when both bolts on a strut are replaced. <!-- F-WP16-020 --> It installs by swapping out one or both of your strut's existing pinch bolts for this eccentric, off-center bolt. Buy it from the page tagged "07-08 Honda Fit," not the "09-14" or "15-20" pages selling the identical part number. <!-- F-WP16-020 -->

Honed Developments, a shop focused specifically on the GD3, sells a strut-top part as part of its published GD3 suspension recipe. This part increases both front caster and front camber beyond what a camber bolt alone provides, though no numeric adjustment figure for it was confirmed this pass. <!-- F-WP16-022 --> Your Fit's rear suspension is a **torsion-beam axle**, a solid rear beam with no built-in camber or toe adjustment from the factory. <!-- F-WP16-023 --> Honed Developments also sells a rear alignment shim kit, listed for "GD & GK Jazz/Fit," that adds camber and toe adjustment where none exists from the factory. <!-- F-WP16-023 -->

### Alignment specs: street versus track

These two alignment targets come from different sources and different goals, so this book labels each one clearly rather than blending them into a single number.

One GD3-focused suspension recipe publishes a **track or performance setup**. Up front: camber -3.5 to -4.5 degrees, caster +2 to +2.5 degrees, and toe 0 degrees. <!-- F-WP16-024 --> In the rear: camber -2 to -2.5 degrees, and toe +2 to 0 (in). <!-- F-WP16-024 --> This target came from a single source, so treat it as a strong starting point for a dedicated track car, not a universal rule. <!-- F-WP16-024 -->

GD3 owners report a gentler **street or daily-driving setup** instead: roughly -1.25 to -2.0 degrees of front camber, which balances comfortable driving with acceptable tire wear. <!-- F-WP16-025 --> Toe is kept as close to 0 as the front and rear allow. <!-- F-WP16-025 --> Owners report that rotating your tires regularly helps manage the extra wear that negative camber causes on a street-driven car. <!-- F-WP16-025 --> Notice how much less aggressive this is than the track numbers above; a track alignment on a daily driver chews through tires fast.

One number this book could not confirm is your Fit's original factory alignment spec. We could not confirm this for the 2008 Fit's factory alignment; check your owner's manual or a dealer. <!-- F-WP16-026 --> A source found this pass gave factory numbers alongside a tire fitment and pressure spec. That pressure spec conflicts with this book's own verified owner's-manual data elsewhere in this book. <!-- F-WP16-026 --> This book's research could not confirm which generation or market that source actually describes. <!-- F-WP16-026 --> Rather than guess, this book flags the conflict and sends you to your own door-jamb sticker and factory manual instead.

## Quick check

1. Name the three alignment angles, camber, caster, and toe, and say what each one describes.
2. What is the difference between a lowering spring and a coilover?
3. List two ways a suspension listing can quietly reveal it is actually built for the GE8, not your GD3.
4. Why does the same SPC camber bolt appear on three separate product pages, and why does that matter to you as a buyer?
5. How do the track alignment numbers in this chapter differ from the street numbers, and why does that difference exist?

# Chapter 51: Brakes for Mods — Pads, Fluid, Lines, Rotors, and Cooling

## What you will learn

- Two brake pad compounds built for street use and track use, both confirmed for your GD3
- How to read a brake fluid boiling point, and why a bigger DOT number is not always the winner
- Three stainless steel brake line kits confirmed for your GD3
- Two upgrade areas, rotors and brake cooling, that this chapter could not confirm real GD3 products for

## The short version

A track-oriented brake pad, a high-boiling-point brake fluid, and stainless steel brake lines all work together to give you a firmer, more consistent pedal under repeated hard braking. This chapter presents real manufacturer data on two pad compounds and two fluid types, all confirmed to fit or apply to your GD3. Fresh, correct fluid matters as much as any pad choice, since fluid that boils turns your brake pedal soft right when you need it most.

## The full explanation

### Two pad compounds, one confirmed fitment list

Hawk Performance sells two pad compounds worth knowing for a modified Fit. Hawk's own fitment database lists its HPS ("High Performance Street") front pad, part HB418F.646, for both the 2007 and 2008 Honda Fit, Base and Sport, by name. <!-- F-WP16-027 --> HPS is a street-oriented compound: lower noise and dust for daily driving, positioned below Hawk's track pad for outright heat capacity. <!-- F-WP16-027 -->

Hawk's HP+ ("High Performance Plus") front pad, part HB418N.646, appears on the identical fitment list, again naming both 2007 and 2008, Base and Sport, by name. <!-- F-WP16-028 --> HP+ is Hawk's higher-temperature track and autocross compound, generally preferred for repeated hard sessions, at the cost of more noise, more dust, and a longer bed-in period on the street. <!-- F-WP16-028 --> Both parts are confirmed for your GD3 by Hawk's own manufacturer fitment list naming your car and your base trim directly, not by a generic aftermarket claim.

### Brake fluid: DOT numbers and boiling points

**Brake fluid** transfers the force from your foot on the pedal to the pads or shoes at each wheel, through a sealed hydraulic system. Brake fluid absorbs a small amount of water over time, and hot brakes can heat that fluid enough to boil it. Fluid that boils turns to gas bubbles, which compress instead of pushing the pads, and your pedal goes soft or sinks to the floor.

A **DOT rating** is a US safety standard that sets a minimum boiling point for a brake fluid. **Dry boiling point** is the boiling point of fresh, unused fluid. **Wet boiling point** is the boiling point after the fluid has absorbed a set amount of water, closer to how fluid behaves after real-world use.

| Fluid | Dry boiling point | Wet boiling point |
|---|---|---|
| FMVSS 116 DOT 4, regulatory minimum | 446°F (230°C) | 311°F (155°C) |
| FMVSS 116 DOT 5.1 (non-silicone), regulatory minimum | 500°F (260°C) | 356°F (180°C) |
| Motul DOT 5.1, actual product data | 522°F (272°C) | 365°F (185°C) |
| Motul RBF 600 Factory Line, a racing DOT 4 fluid | 594°F (312°C) | 399°F (204°C) |

<!-- F-WP16-029 -->

Read that table carefully, because it teaches an important lesson. The racing-grade DOT 4 fluid at the bottom has a higher boiling point than the plain DOT 5.1 fluid above it. This is true even though 5.1 is technically the "higher" number. <!-- F-WP16-029 --> A DOT rating is a minimum standard, not a guarantee that a higher-numbered fluid always beats a lower-numbered one. <!-- F-WP16-029 --> When you shop for a track-capable fluid, compare actual boiling points on the data sheet, not only the DOT number printed on the bottle.

This chapter could not confirm which brake fluid your Fit's owner's manual specifies from the factory. We could not confirm this specific fact in this chapter's own research; Chapter 27 covers your factory-specified fluid type in full. <!-- F-WP16-030 --> Use this chapter's boiling-point table to compare upgrade options once you know your factory baseline from Chapter 27.

### Stainless steel brake lines

A **stainless steel brake line** replaces the factory rubber brake hose with a braided steel outer layer over a sealed inner tube. Rubber hoses can swell slightly under hard, repeated braking, which can make the pedal feel soft even with good pads and fluid; a stainless line resists that swelling.

Three stainless line kits were confirmed for the GD3 this pass. A StopTech kit, part 950.40017, is listed as a front kit for "Honda Fit 2007-2008." The listing found this pass marks it discontinued at that retailer, so treat it as a lead to chase down, not a guaranteed current purchase. <!-- F-WP16-031 --> A Fastline Performance kit carries the chassis code directly in its own part number, FLP-BLKIT-GD3, in addition to a "2007-08 Honda Fit" title. <!-- F-WP16-032 --> A Techna-Fit kit, part HN-2600, is listed for "2007-2008 Honda Fit" by two independent retailers. <!-- F-WP16-033 -->

Notice that the Fastline part number is the strongest fitment confirmation of the three. The manufacturer built the chassis code into the part number itself, not only into marketing text on a web page. <!-- F-WP16-032 -->

### Rotors and brake cooling: an honest gap

Every product in this chapter so far came with real, checked fitment text. Rotors and dedicated brake-cooling parts, like brake ducts that route outside air to a hot rotor, did not turn up a confirmed GD3-specific product in this chapter's own research. We could not confirm specific rotor or brake-cooling products for the 2008 Fit in this pass. Check current listings yourself, and apply the same GD3-versus-GE8 check from Chapter 50 to anything you find.

A general point still applies even without a specific product to name. Any rotor you buy should be sized and drilled for your Fit's specific caliper and hub. Any brake-cooling duct should be checked for physical clearance against your specific bumper and fender, the same way a wheel or tire needs a clearance check in Chapter 49.

## Quick check

1. Which two Hawk pad compounds are confirmed for your GD3, and which one suits daily street driving better?
2. What is the difference between a dry boiling point and a wet boiling point?
3. Why can a DOT 4 fluid sometimes have a higher boiling point than a DOT 5.1 fluid?
4. Name the three confirmed stainless brake line kits, and which one has the strongest fitment proof, and why.
5. Why did this chapter not name a specific rotor or brake-cooling product for your GD3?

# Chapter 52: Intake, Header, and Exhaust

## What you will learn
- What an intake, a header, and an exhaust system each do on your Fit's engine.
- The real dyno numbers researchers found for each part on this exact car.
- Why a manufacturer's own claim is weaker proof than two separate independent tests.
- Why no clean, isolated dyno number exists for an exhaust system alone.
- How these parts perform together, instead of one at a time.

## The short version
An intake, a header, and an exhaust each free up a small amount of power on your L15A1 engine <!-- F-WP17-001 -->. Every real dyno number found for these parts on this car is modest, in the single digits <!-- F-WP17-003 -->. No clean, isolated dyno test for an exhaust system alone was found for this car <!-- F-WP17-008 -->.

## The full explanation

### Words you need first
A dynamometer, or dyno, is a machine that measures how much power an engine makes <!-- background -->. Some dynos measure power at the wheels <!-- background -->. That number is written as whp <!-- background -->. Wheel numbers are usually lower than crankshaft numbers <!-- background -->. Power gets lost on the way to the wheels <!-- background -->. A cold air intake replaces the factory air box and tubing <!-- background -->. It feeds air into your engine <!-- background -->. A header replaces the factory exhaust manifold <!-- background -->. That is the metal piece that collects exhaust gas right off the engine <!-- background -->. A cat-back exhaust replaces the pipe and muffler after the catalytic converter <!-- background -->. The catalytic converter is the part that cleans exhaust gas <!-- background -->. An axle-back exhaust replaces only the section behind the rear axle <!-- background -->. It is a smaller piece than a full cat-back <!-- background -->. A test pipe, sometimes called a catless downpipe, is a straight pipe <!-- background -->. It replaces the catalytic converter itself <!-- background -->.

### Intake gains
Researchers found dyno numbers for three intake products on the L15A1 <!-- F-WP17-001 -->. Injen's SP1511 cold air intake is built specifically for the 2007-2008 Fit <!-- F-WP17-001 -->. Injen advertises a gain of up to 7 hp, plus up to 8 lb-ft of extra torque <!-- F-WP17-001 -->. That same figure appears on a second retailer's page <!-- F-WP17-002 -->. Both pages trace back to the same original Injen claim <!-- F-WP17-002 -->. They are not two separately run tests <!-- F-WP17-002 -->. This book reports the number as Injen's own claim, not as two independent tests <!-- F-WP17-002 -->.

A different, independent video test used a Dynapack dyno on a car identified as a GD3 <!-- F-WP17-003 -->. An HPS Performance cold air intake gained a reported 5.9 whp at 5,685 rpm <!-- F-WP17-003 -->. It also gained 7.6 lb-ft of torque at 3,655 rpm <!-- F-WP17-003 -->. An HPS Performance short-ram intake pulls air from inside the engine bay instead of outside <!-- F-WP17-004 -->. It gained a reported 7.1 whp at 5,645 rpm and 6.4 lb-ft at 5,590 rpm <!-- F-WP17-004 -->. Three different intake products, tested two different ways, all landed in the same narrow range <!-- F-WP17-001 -->. Every reported gain is roughly 5 to 8 hp, and roughly 6 to 8 lb-ft <!-- F-WP17-001 -->. No source found claims a double-digit horsepower gain from an intake alone <!-- F-WP17-001 -->.

### Header gains
Only one number-based dyno claim for a header alone was found <!-- F-WP17-005 -->. It came from a test on an otherwise completely stock GD3 Fit <!-- F-WP17-005 -->. A fitfreak.net forum thread reported a gain of "5+HP" <!-- F-WP17-005 -->. Owners report that figure; it was not independently re-tested by this research <!-- F-WP17-005 -->. That single number sits below this book's normal bar of two separate sources <!-- F-WP17-005 -->. Treat it as a rough hint, not a settled fact <!-- F-WP17-005 -->. A second header claim of "+9 hp" turned up during research <!-- F-WP17-005 -->. It named a header built for the 2009-2014 Fit <!-- F-WP17-005 -->. That is a different car, with a different engine, so it does not count here <!-- F-WP17-005 -->. Header products built specifically for the 2007-2008 GD3 Fit are still sold today <!-- F-WP17-006 -->. No published dyno number is attached to that current product's own listing <!-- F-WP17-006 -->.

### Exhaust gains, and the honest gap
Stop and remember Chapter 47 before you read this section. A test pipe removes your car's catalytic converter, the part that cleans exhaust gas. Removing it is exactly the kind of change the Clean Air Act calls a defeat device. Federal law makes that illegal on a car driven on public roads, with real fines attached. <!-- F-WP15-001 --> A test pipe belongs on a car built only for closed-course track or competition use. It does not belong on a car you also drive to school or work, unless a specific part carries a CARB EO that covers your exact car.

Community discussion describes a test pipe alone as "usually" adding about 3 to 5 whp <!-- F-WP17-007 -->. One owner reported a header, a test pipe, and a cat-back exhaust installed together <!-- F-WP17-007 -->. That combination raised the car from 92 whp to 100 whp <!-- F-WP17-007 -->. That is an 8 whp gain for all three parts combined, not the test pipe alone <!-- F-WP17-007 -->.

Here is the honest gap in this research. No clean, isolated dyno number for a cat-back or axle-back exhaust alone was found for this exact car <!-- F-WP17-008 -->. Every full-exhaust dyno result found was reported together with a header, a test pipe, or an engine tune <!-- F-WP17-008 -->. There is no way to know what the exhaust piece alone contributed <!-- F-WP17-008 -->. Rather than guess at a number, this book leaves it unconfirmed <!-- F-WP17-008 -->. Real cat-back and axle-back exhaust products built for the 2007-2008 Fit do exist today <!-- F-WP17-009 -->. A'PEXi and HKS both sell one <!-- F-WP17-009,F-WP17-010 -->. Neither brand's own product page publishes a specific dyno figure <!-- F-WP17-009 -->. Both use only general marketing language instead <!-- F-WP17-010 -->.

### What happens when you combine all of them
Two community reports show what these parts can do together <!-- F-WP17-011 -->. Neither report isolates any single part's own share of the gain <!-- F-WP17-012 -->. One 2008 Fit owner started with a stock rating near 107 hp <!-- F-WP17-011 -->. He reported reaching about 115 whp <!-- F-WP17-011 -->. He got there by adding an intake, a header, a catless downpipe, a full exhaust, and an engine tune together <!-- F-WP17-011 -->. A second build reported 115.46 whp from a header, a downpipe, an axle-back muffler, and a tune <!-- F-WP17-012 -->. A third build, with an intake, a header, and a downpipe, reached 101 whp with a different tune <!-- F-WP17-012 -->. These are real reports from real owners <!-- F-WP17-012 -->. Treat them as "several parts together," not proof of what any one part adds by itself <!-- F-WP17-012 -->.

### Putting it together
An intake, a header, and an exhaust are each real, modest upgrades on the L15A1 <!-- F-WP17-001 -->. None of them is a magic power boost <!-- F-WP17-001 -->. Every isolated number found for these parts is small, in the single digits <!-- F-WP17-003 -->. The header claim rests on one source, not two, so treat it carefully <!-- F-WP17-005 -->. No isolated exhaust-only number exists at all <!-- F-WP17-008 -->. This book will not invent one <!-- F-WP17-008 -->. If you want a real, noticeable gain, expect it to come from combining several parts, plus a tune, not any single part alone <!-- F-WP17-011 -->.

## Quick check
1. What is the difference between horsepower measured at the wheels and at the crankshaft?
2. Why does this book treat the Injen intake's "7 hp" claim as one piece of evidence, even though it appears on two web pages?
3. What single number could this book not confirm for the exhaust system, and why not?
4. Why was a "+9 hp" header claim left out of this chapter?
5. If a reported combo build gains power from an intake, a header, and a tune together, what can you NOT conclude from that number?

<!-- Fact IDs used: F-WP17-001, F-WP17-002, F-WP17-003, F-WP17-004, F-WP17-005, F-WP17-006, F-WP17-007, F-WP17-008, F-WP17-009, F-WP17-010, F-WP17-011, F-WP17-012, F-WP15-001 (cross-referenced from Chapter 47's own legal framework, added on review to warn that a test pipe removes the catalytic converter, per D9/R2) -->
<!-- Fact IDs from facts/WP-17.md tagged Chapter 52 but NOT used, and why: none — every fact card tagged Chapter 52 (F-WP17-001 through F-WP17-012) is used above. -->

---

# Chapter 53: Engine Management

## What you will learn
- What an ECU is, and what "tuning" or "reflashing" it actually means.
- The single most surprising fact in this whole book about aftermarket support: which Fit generation Hondata actually supports.
- What Hondata sells for your exact car, and what it costs.
- What does NOT exist for your car, so you do not waste money chasing it.
- Why a header dyno number "for a Honda Fit" you find online might not even be for your car.

## The short version
A company called Hondata sells a full engine-tuning system built specifically for your 2007-2008 Fit, right now, today <!-- F-WP17-013 -->. Hondata has never sold anything like it for the newer 2009-2014 Fit, which is a different car with a different engine <!-- F-WP17-017 -->. If you assumed the newer Fit has better tuning support than yours, that assumption is backwards <!-- F-WP17-022 -->.

## The full explanation

### Words you need first
An ECU, short for Engine Control Unit, is the small computer that runs your engine <!-- background -->. It decides things like fuel amount and spark timing <!-- background -->. "Tuning" an ECU means changing those settings, usually to make more power or run differently <!-- background -->. A "reflash" rewrites the software inside the factory ECU itself, through the car's OBD-II port <!-- background -->. OBD-II is the standard data port under your dashboard, used on all cars built after 1996 <!-- background -->. A reflash tool does not need to physically cut any wires <!-- background -->. A "piggyback" is a separate small box that sits between the ECU and its sensors <!-- background -->. It changes some signals without touching the factory ECU's own software <!-- background -->. A "standalone" system fully replaces the factory ECU with a new one <!-- background -->. CARB is the California Air Resources Board, the state agency that sets emissions rules <!-- background -->. A CARB EO, or Executive Order, is a specific approval number from that agency <!-- background -->. It lets an aftermarket part be sold as legal for street use in every state, not only outside California <!-- background -->.

### The headline finding
Here is the single most important finding in this chapter, so read it twice <!-- F-WP17-013 -->. Hondata's FlashPro system is a real, full-reflash tuning product <!-- F-WP17-013 -->. Hondata's own product page lists it for "Vehicle: Fit, Year: 2007-2008, Market: US/Canada" <!-- F-WP17-013 -->. That is your exact car <!-- F-WP17-013 -->. This is not a guess or an inference <!-- F-WP17-013 -->. It is a direct statement from the manufacturer's own current catalog <!-- F-WP17-013 -->.

Now for the surprising part <!-- F-WP17-017 -->. A direct check of Hondata's own product catalog, filtered specifically to the Fit, shows only two products <!-- F-WP17-017 -->. Both of them are for the 2007-2008 car, your car <!-- F-WP17-017 -->. Zero products exist in that same catalog for the 2009-2014 Fit <!-- F-WP17-017 -->. Hondata's own customer forum has a thread from a 2009-2014 Fit owner asking "Still nothing for the L15A7?" <!-- F-WP17-017 -->. The L15A7 is the engine in the 2009-2014 Fit <!-- F-WP17-017 -->. A reader might expect the newer car to have more aftermarket support, since it is newer <!-- F-WP17-022 -->. For Hondata specifically, the exact opposite is true <!-- F-WP17-022 -->. Never assume the newer Fit has better tuning support without checking for yourself <!-- F-WP17-022 -->.

### What Hondata sells for your car
Hondata sells two different FlashPro versions for the 2007-2008 Fit <!-- F-WP17-013 -->. The full version, product code FP-FIT-US, costs $795 <!-- F-WP17-013 -->. It unlocks complete custom tuning and full datalogging <!-- F-WP17-013 -->. Datalogging means the system records engine data over time, so a tuner can study it later <!-- F-WP17-013 -->. It comes with 20 hours of on-board datalogging memory <!-- F-WP17-013 -->. The second version, product code FP-FIT-US-CARB, costs $595 <!-- F-WP17-014 -->. It offers datalogging plus a limited menu of preset tuning options, not fully open custom tuning <!-- F-WP17-014 -->. Hondata states this CARB version carries CARB EO number D-742-1, which keeps it legal for street use in every state, including California <!-- F-WP17-014 -->.

Here is the real tradeoff <!-- F-WP17-014 -->. The $595 CARB version keeps you street-legal everywhere, but limits you to Hondata's own preset calibrations <!-- F-WP17-014 -->. The $795 full version unlocks real custom tuning instead <!-- F-WP17-014 -->. That kind of tuning is what you need to get the most out of a header or other bolt-ons <!-- F-WP17-014 -->. Hondata does not represent the full version as CARB-legal for street use, though <!-- F-WP17-014 -->. Choose based on whether legality or full tuning matters more to you <!-- F-WP17-014 -->.

Both versions connect through your car's OBD-II port <!-- F-WP17-013 -->. Neither one requires cutting into your factory ECU <!-- F-WP17-013 -->. Hondata's own specification page lists the exact factory ECU part numbers this system supports <!-- F-WP17-015 -->. That range is RME-A11 through RME-A89 for US cars, and RME-C11 through RME-C89 for Canadian cars <!-- F-WP17-015 -->. An independent retailer's own product page reproduces that same exact part-number range, word for word <!-- F-WP17-015 -->. That second, separate confirmation makes this an unusually solid fact <!-- F-WP17-015 -->.

### What does NOT exist for your car
Hondata also makes older products called K-Pro and s300 <!-- F-WP17-018 -->. Neither one applies to any Honda Fit, in either generation <!-- F-WP17-018 -->. K-Pro is built only for Honda's K-series engines, used in cars like the Civic Si and Acura RSX <!-- F-WP17-018 -->. Your Fit does not have a K-series engine <!-- F-WP17-018 -->. The s300 line is built for much older, 1992-1999 OBD1-era Hondas <!-- F-WP17-018 -->. Your Fit is a newer OBD2 car <!-- F-WP17-019 -->. Very old-school Honda ECU-chipping tools, such as Uberdata, Neptune, or Crome, are also built only for those same 1992-1995 OBD1 Hondas <!-- F-WP17-019 -->. None of them can work on your car's ECU <!-- F-WP17-019 -->.

A plug-and-play standalone system, like an AEM Infinity harness, is a different kind of product than FlashPro <!-- F-WP17-021 -->. AEM sells this kind of harness for some older Honda engines <!-- F-WP17-021 -->. No dedicated, named harness for the Fit or the L15A1 was found in this research <!-- F-WP17-021 -->. That does not prove one does not exist somewhere <!-- F-WP17-021 -->. It only means none was found <!-- F-WP17-021 -->. This is recorded as unverified, not as a confirmed "no" <!-- F-WP17-021 -->.

### A real piggyback option, with real limits
At least one owner has used a piggyback controller, an Apexi VAFC, on a GD3 Fit <!-- F-WP17-020 -->. A piggyback, remember, sits between the ECU and its sensors rather than replacing the ECU <!-- F-WP17-020 -->. Owners report that the VAFC could adjust the air-fuel mixture <!-- F-WP17-020 -->. Owners also report that trying to use it to affect VTEC engagement triggered the check engine light <!-- F-WP17-020 -->. VTEC is Honda's variable valve-timing system <!-- F-WP17-020 -->. This is a single community report, so treat the specific limitation as reported, not fully confirmed <!-- F-WP17-020 -->.

### A trap this research caught
While researching this chapter, a claim about a "+9 hp" header surfaced online <!-- F-WP17-016 -->. Tracing it back showed the header itself was built for the "2009-2012 Honda Fit," the newer car <!-- F-WP17-016 -->. The forum discussion about it also sat in a section for that newer car, not yours <!-- F-WP17-016 -->. This is exactly the kind of mix-up this book is built to catch <!-- F-WP17-016 -->. A number you find online for "a Honda Fit" might actually be for the newer, different car, not the one you own <!-- F-WP17-016 -->. Always check the model year before trusting a number <!-- F-WP17-016 -->.

### Putting it together
Real, current, full-reflash ECU tuning exists for your exact car, sold today by a mainstream company, for $595 or $795 <!-- F-WP17-022 -->. This is not a niche or discontinued method <!-- F-WP17-022 -->. Hondata sells the same kind of product family for many other current Honda and Acura models <!-- F-WP17-022 -->. The newer 2009-2014 Fit, by contrast, has no equivalent Hondata product at all <!-- F-WP17-022 -->. Do not assume the newer car has better tuning support only because it is newer <!-- F-WP17-022 -->. For this brand and this exact question, your 2007-2008 car is the one Hondata actually built for <!-- F-WP17-022 -->.

## Quick check
1. What is the difference between an ECU reflash and a piggyback controller?
2. What does Hondata's own current product catalog show for the 2009-2014 Fit's engine, the L15A7?
3. What is the price difference between Hondata's two FlashPro versions for your car, and what do you give up with the cheaper one?
4. Why does neither Hondata's K-Pro nor its s300 line apply to any Honda Fit?
5. Why was a "+9 hp" header claim rejected from this chapter, even though it mentioned "a Honda Fit"?

<!-- Fact IDs used: F-WP17-013, F-WP17-014, F-WP17-015, F-WP17-016, F-WP17-017, F-WP17-018, F-WP17-019, F-WP17-020, F-WP17-021, F-WP17-022 -->
<!-- Fact IDs from facts/WP-17.md tagged Chapter 53 but NOT used, and why: none — every fact card tagged Chapter 53 (F-WP17-013 through F-WP17-022) is used above. -->

# Chapter 54: Power Adders

## What you will learn
- What a turbocharger and a supercharger are, and how they differ from a header or an exhaust.
- The three real, GD3-specific turbo or supercharger kits researchers found.
- Why all three of those kits are hard, or impossible, to buy new today.
- What real reliability problems owners and builders report.
- What else you would likely need to change if you add real boost: clutch, fuel system, and more.

## The short version
Exactly three real, brand-named turbo or supercharger kits were ever built specifically for your GD3 Fit's L15A1 engine <!-- F-WP18-001 -->. All three are hard to buy new today; two are fully discontinued, and the third is small-batch and often out of stock <!-- F-WP18-002 -->. Adding real boost is reported to also demand a stronger clutch and a bigger fuel system, not only the turbo or supercharger kit itself <!-- F-WP18-019 -->.

## The full explanation

### Words you need first
A turbocharger is a small turbine that uses your engine's own exhaust gas to force extra air into the engine <!-- background -->. A supercharger does the same job, but it is spun by a belt connected to the engine, not by exhaust gas <!-- background -->. Both are ways to force more air in, so the engine can burn more fuel and make more power <!-- background -->. This forced air is called boost, and it is measured in psi, or pounds per square inch <!-- background -->. An intercooler is a small radiator that cools the forced air down before it enters the engine <!-- background -->. Cooler air is denser and safer for the engine <!-- background -->. A CARB EO is a California approval number that lets a part be sold as legal for street use in every state <!-- background -->. A clutch is the part that connects your engine to the transmission on a manual car <!-- background -->. A flywheel is a heavy spinning disc the clutch grabs onto <!-- background -->.

### The three real kits
KraftWerks made a bolt-on supercharger kit built specifically for the manual-transmission 2007-2008 US Fit <!-- F-WP18-001 -->. It was also sold under the related brand name Jackson Racing <!-- F-WP18-001 -->. The kit used a Rotrex C15-60 supercharger, plus Hondata engine management and an intercooler, included in the box <!-- F-WP18-001 -->. It was never built for the automatic-transmission Fit <!-- F-WP18-001 -->. Neither KraftWerks' own current shop nor Jackson Racing's own current shop lists a Fit kit today <!-- F-WP18-002 -->. This kit is discontinued <!-- F-WP18-002 -->. Used kits can sometimes be found on sites like eBay instead <!-- F-WP18-002 -->.

Two different power figures were reported for this kit, from two different points in its life <!-- F-WP18-003 -->. An early write-up, at the kit's first announcement, reported 121 whp for the "base kit" <!-- F-WP18-003 -->. A later write-up described a "High-Boost" version, reporting 164 whp and 132 lb-ft of torque at 10 psi of boost <!-- F-WP18-003 -->. Both numbers are reported by enthusiast media, not independently re-tested by this research <!-- F-WP18-003 -->.

This kit's retail price was reported at $4,495 plus shipping, from a single source <!-- F-WP18-004 -->. Reliability reports mostly cover the same Rotrex supercharger design used across other Honda models, not the Fit specifically <!-- F-WP18-004 -->. Owners of those other cars report belt and tensioner problems <!-- F-WP18-004 -->. One report describes a tensioner bolt snapping and the belt coming off during highway driving <!-- F-WP18-004 -->. Another describes belt edge wear from rubbing on the pulley <!-- F-WP18-004 -->. KraftWerks itself states it added an automatic belt tensioner specifically for the Fit kit, and reports no belt issues on it <!-- F-WP18-004 -->. That is the manufacturer's own claim, though, not an independent owner report <!-- F-WP18-004 -->.

HKS made a bolt-on turbo kit named specifically for "2007 and 2008 model year Honda Fits" with the 1.5L engine <!-- F-WP18-005 -->. This is confirmed directly by California's own CARB Executive Order record, D-186-27 <!-- F-WP18-005 -->. The kit used a Garrett T25 turbocharger, a front-mount intercooler, and HKS's own F-Con IS engine management, which plugs into the OBD-II port <!-- F-WP18-005 -->. Maximum boost was set at 6.0 psi per that same official record <!-- F-WP18-005 -->. HKS described the kit as "legal in all 50 states," based on that CARB approval <!-- F-WP18-005 -->. HKS reported roughly a 30 percent gain in peak horsepower <!-- F-WP18-006 -->. That kit is also reported discontinued today, with only used or old-stock units left to find <!-- F-WP18-006 -->.

Unequal Fabrication, a small aftermarket parts maker, currently lists a "Honda Fit GD3 (07-08) Turbo Kit" at $2,300 <!-- F-WP18-007 -->. The listing describes a bolt-on kit that keeps your air conditioning, heat, and fog lights working <!-- F-WP18-007 -->. The seller states this kit can reach "170hp on the stock internals," given a healthy clutch <!-- F-WP18-007 -->. That is a manufacturer claim, not an independent test <!-- F-WP18-007 -->. Separate tuning software, such as the Hondata FlashPro covered in Chapter 53, is required and not included in the price <!-- F-WP18-007 -->. The listing states the kit is "FOR OFF ROAD USE" <!-- F-WP18-007 -->. At the time of this research, the kit was shown as "Out of Stock," built to order with a variable wait time <!-- F-WP18-007 -->.

### A warning about generic listings
Unbranded turbo kits also show up for sale on sites like eBay, labeled for the "GD3" or "L15A" <!-- F-WP18-008 -->. One such listing claims a huge 485 horsepower gain <!-- F-WP18-008 -->. That number is far above every other figure found in this research and is not backed up anywhere <!-- F-WP18-008 -->. This book does not use that number, and you should not trust it either <!-- F-WP18-008 -->. A GD3-specific community thread, from experienced builders, gives a more realistic picture instead <!-- F-WP18-009 -->. They describe the stock L15A1 as able to hold about 8 psi of boost safely <!-- F-WP18-009 -->. They suggest a realistic target of roughly 160 to 200 whp on stock internals <!-- F-WP18-009 -->. One named builder blew his engine at 220 whp on a custom turbo setup <!-- F-WP18-009 -->. He then rebuilt it with a larger bore to reach about 260 whp <!-- F-WP18-009 -->. This is a single community thread, so treat it as a reported estimate, not a lab-tested fact <!-- F-WP18-009 -->.

One well-written article about a turbocharged Fit was found and read in full, then set aside <!-- F-WP18-010 -->. The car in that article used the L15A7 engine, which is the newer 2009-2014 Fit's engine, not yours <!-- F-WP18-010 -->. It was excluded from this chapter entirely, so no number from it appears anywhere above <!-- F-WP18-010 -->.

### What else you would likely need
Owners report that the stock clutch can slip once real boost is added <!-- F-WP18-020 -->. Exedy sells Sport Clutch kits built specifically for the 2007-2008 Fit in two strength levels <!-- F-WP18-019 -->. The Stage 1 kit is rated for 128 lb-ft of torque to the wheels, and costs about $454 <!-- F-WP18-019 -->. The Stage 2 kit is rated for 160 lb-ft <!-- F-WP18-019 -->. For a daily-driven, non-boosted car, though, community advice says you should not need to upgrade the stock clutch at all <!-- F-WP18-020 -->. Some race-style clutches are reported to feel "too grabby" for comfortable daily driving <!-- F-WP18-020 -->. Save the clutch upgrade for if, and only if, you actually add boost <!-- F-WP18-020 -->.

Fuel system upgrades are also reported as necessary once you add real power <!-- F-WP18-021 -->. For the KraftWerks supercharger kit specifically, owners report upgrading to larger 410cc fuel injectors at 10 psi of boost <!-- F-WP18-021 -->. Changing injector size always requires a matching ECU retune, to avoid running dangerously lean <!-- F-WP18-021 -->. For custom turbo builds, owners report that aftermarket fuel pumps, such as a Walbro or Deatschwerks pump, are commonly used instead of the stock pump <!-- F-WP18-021 -->.

No source found in this research shows the stock axles failing specifically because of added horsepower <!-- F-WP18-022 -->. This is left honestly unverified, rather than guessed at <!-- F-WP18-022 -->.

### Putting it together
Three real turbo or supercharger kits were built for your exact car, and all three are hard to buy new today <!-- F-WP18-002 -->. Reported power gains for these kits range from about 121 whp up to about 260 whp on a heavily built engine <!-- F-WP18-009 -->. Reliability problems are real and reported, mostly around belts, tensioners, and the need for a stronger clutch and fuel system <!-- F-WP18-004 -->. Treat any huge, unsupported power claim you find online, like the 485 horsepower eBay listing, with real suspicion <!-- F-WP18-008 -->.

## Quick check
1. What is the difference between how a turbocharger and a supercharger force air into the engine?
2. Name the three real, GD3-specific turbo or supercharger kits found in this research.
3. Why is a huge power claim on a generic eBay turbo-kit listing not trustworthy?
4. What two other systems do owners report needing to upgrade once they add real boost?
5. Why was a well-written turbo-Fit build article excluded from this chapter entirely?

<!-- Fact IDs used: F-WP18-001, F-WP18-002, F-WP18-003, F-WP18-004, F-WP18-005, F-WP18-006, F-WP18-007, F-WP18-008, F-WP18-009, F-WP18-010, F-WP18-019, F-WP18-020, F-WP18-021, F-WP18-022 -->
<!-- Fact IDs from facts/WP-18.md tagged Chapter 54 but NOT used, and why: none — every fact card tagged Chapter 54 (F-WP18-001 through F-WP18-010 and F-WP18-019 through F-WP18-022) is used above. -->

---

# Chapter 55: Engine Swaps

## What you will learn
- What an engine swap is, and why it is a much bigger job than a bolt-on part.
- The one documented engine family that has actually been swapped into a GD3 Fit.
- The real obstacles builders report: mounts, wiring, the ECU, the transmission, and more.
- Why this is one of the thinnest, smallest topics in this whole book.
- What was searched for and not found, so you know the honest limits of this research.

## The short version
An engine swap means removing your car's factory engine and installing a different one <!-- background -->. Exactly one engine family has a real, documented swap into a GD3 Fit: Honda's K-series engine <!-- F-WP18-011 -->. This is a genuinely small, niche corner of the Fit hobby; one community estimate puts the total number of K-swapped GD3 Fits worldwide at fewer than 50 <!-- F-WP18-014 -->.

## The full explanation

### Words you need first
An engine swap replaces a car's original engine with a different one, often from another model <!-- background -->. Honda's K-series is a family of engines used in cars like the Civic Si and the Acura TSX <!-- background -->. A wiring harness is the bundle of wires that connects an engine and its ECU to the rest of the car <!-- background -->. An immobilizer is an anti-theft system that stops the engine from starting without the correct key <!-- background -->. A vehicle speed sensor, or VSS, tells the ECU how fast the car is moving <!-- background -->. A limited slip differential, or LSD, is a transmission part that helps both drive wheels get power during hard cornering <!-- background -->.

### A legal reminder before you start
An engine swap is a much bigger emissions question than any bolt-on part in this book. Chapter 47 already covered the basic rule: federal law bans removing or defeating emissions equipment, and many states, especially California, apply extra rules specifically to swapped engines. This book's research did not confirm the exact swap-approval steps for this exact car, so this book will not guess at them. Check your own state's rules on engine swaps before you start, the same way Chapter 17 already told you to check your state's inspection and emissions rules.

### The one documented swap family
Researchers found real, detailed build documentation for exactly one swapped-in engine family: Honda's K-series <!-- F-WP18-011 -->. Two specific K-series engines have documented builds: the K24A2, pulled from a wrecked Acura TSX, and the K20Z3, pulled from a Civic Si <!-- F-WP18-011 -->. No documented, completed swap of any other engine family was found for a GD3 chassis <!-- F-WP18-011 -->. That includes other Honda engine families, non-Honda engines, and electric motor conversions <!-- F-WP18-011 -->.

Two independent, named builders documented their own K-series swaps <!-- F-WP18-012 -->. One builder runs a website, pbomers.com, showing his own K24A2 swap into a GD3 Fit in detail <!-- F-WP18-012 -->. He says he has a running K24-swapped Fit <!-- F-WP18-012 -->. He also points to his brother's YouTube channel for video coverage of four completed swaps between the two of them <!-- F-WP18-012 -->. A second builder posted a 2014 forum thread about a K20Z3 swap into a 2007 GD Fit <!-- F-WP18-012 -->. He used an engine, ECU, and wiring harness pulled from a Civic Si <!-- F-WP18-012 -->. A different forum member replied that he had "just went through this" the same way, about a month earlier <!-- F-WP18-012 -->. That second, independent reply shows this is a known method within the small community, not a one-off guess <!-- F-WP18-012 -->.

### The real obstacles
Builders report real problems in several different car systems <!-- F-WP18-013 -->. On motor mounts: without stiffer aftermarket mounts, or reinforcing the stock ones, the engine is reported to rock and rub inside the tight engine bay <!-- F-WP18-013 -->. Hasport is the one mount brand specifically named by builders <!-- F-WP18-013 -->. On body clearance: the front support structure often has to be cut to fit the larger K24 intake manifold <!-- F-WP18-013 -->. A smaller K-series intake needs less cutting <!-- F-WP18-013 -->. The stock radiator and its fans can also foul the taller intake manifold, so some builders relocate the radiator and switch to slimmer fans <!-- F-WP18-013 -->.

Wiring is reported as one of the hardest parts of this swap <!-- F-WP18-013 -->. One builder said he contacted more than half a dozen harness companies and was ignored by all of them <!-- F-WP18-013 -->. His solution was to hand-build a harness, using an Acura TSX ECU, since the TSX shares similar wiring architecture with the Fit <!-- F-WP18-013 -->. The car's immobilizer has to be disabled or re-synced to the new ECU and key, at a Honda dealership <!-- F-WP18-013 -->. Builders also report that only certain donor transmissions work correctly <!-- F-WP18-013 -->. Honda changed its vehicle speed sensor design at some point <!-- F-WP18-013 -->. An older-design sensor will not read correctly on a newer ECU <!-- F-WP18-013 -->. Builders report using axles from a 2002-2004 Acura RSX, instead of the stock Fit axles, to make everything fit together <!-- F-WP18-013 -->. Because the Fit uses electric power steering, an idler arm borrowed from a Honda Civic Si is also reported as necessary <!-- F-WP18-013 -->. Even the exhaust header is reported as a genuinely tough part to source for this swap <!-- F-WP18-013 -->.

### Cost and rarity
No total build-cost figure was found for a full K-series swap into a GD3 <!-- F-WP18-014 -->. This is left honestly unverified, rather than estimated or guessed at <!-- F-WP18-014 -->. One real, single component price point was found instead: a commercial Hasport-brand engine mount kit for this exact swap sells for $249 to $285, depending on the retailer <!-- F-WP18-014 -->. That is only a small fraction of what a complete swap would actually cost <!-- F-WP18-014 -->. On rarity, one experienced forum member estimated that fewer than 50 K-swapped GD3 Fits exist worldwide <!-- F-WP18-014 -->. That is a rough personal guess, not an official count <!-- F-WP18-014 -->. A second, separate long-time forum member said he could not remember any currently active members with a K-swapped Fit <!-- F-WP18-014 -->. Both statements point the same direction: this swap is genuinely rare <!-- F-WP18-014 -->.

### A real parts market, even so
Even though this swap is rare, a real commercial parts market exists to support it <!-- F-WP18-015 -->. Three separate retailers each independently sell the same Hasport-brand engine mount kit, specifically titled for the "07-08 Honda Fit" K-series swap <!-- F-WP18-015 -->. That does not confirm any one builder's total cost, but it does show real, ongoing commercial demand, from more than one seller <!-- F-WP18-015 -->.

### Claims checked and set aside
One claim, that a professional shop called King Motorsports built a K24-swapped Fit for the SEMA car show, was searched for directly and could not be confirmed <!-- F-WP18-016 -->. Only an ordinary parts listing for a mount kit was found, no build article or show record <!-- F-WP18-016 -->. This claim is not used anywhere in this book <!-- F-WP18-016 -->.

A forum thread asking about swapping in a B-series or D-series Honda engine, a different engine family than K-series, was found <!-- F-WP18-017 -->. No completed, documented build of that kind into a GD3 chassis was found, though <!-- F-WP18-017 -->. This is recorded honestly as "few to no documented examples," not as a confirmed impossibility <!-- F-WP18-017 -->.

An electric motor, or EV, conversion was also searched for <!-- F-WP18-018 -->. One forum thread shows a GD3 owner asking questions about an EV conversion, which suggests at least planning-stage interest <!-- F-WP18-018 -->. No completed, documented GD3-specific EV conversion build was found <!-- F-WP18-018 -->. General EV conversion kits exist for other cars, but none found named the Fit specifically <!-- F-WP18-018 -->.

### Putting it together
This is one of the smallest, most niche topics in this entire book <!-- F-WP18-014 -->. Exactly one engine family, Honda's K-series, has real documented swaps into a GD3 Fit <!-- F-WP18-011 -->. The obstacles are real and touch nearly every system in the car: mounts, wiring, the ECU, the transmission, the axles, even the steering <!-- F-WP18-013 -->. No total cost figure exists, and the swap is genuinely rare, at well under 50 known worldwide by one rough estimate <!-- F-WP18-014 -->. If you are considering this, expect a long, expensive, mostly self-taught project, not a weekend job <!-- F-WP18-013 -->.

## Quick check
1. What is the one documented engine family that has been swapped into a GD3 Fit?
2. Name three different systems in the car that builders report as real obstacles for this swap.
3. Why does this book leave the total swap cost as unverified, instead of estimating it?
4. About how many K-swapped GD3 Fits does one community estimate suggest exist worldwide?
5. Why was a claim about a professional shop building one for a car show left out of this chapter?

<!-- Fact IDs used: F-WP18-011, F-WP18-012, F-WP18-013, F-WP18-014, F-WP18-015, F-WP18-016, F-WP18-017, F-WP18-018 -->

# Chapter 56: Weight reduction and aerodynamics

## What you will learn

- Which parts owners commonly remove from a base 2008 Fit to save weight
- How much weight the spare tire, its cover, and the jack system add up to
- How much weight the air conditioning system adds
- Why SCCA's Street category limits what you can remove
- Why aerodynamic parts are a thin topic for this specific car

## The short version

Removing the spare tire, its cover, and the jack and tools together saves you roughly 30 to 35 pounds. <!-- F-WP19-017 --> Removing the air conditioning system saves about 23.46 pounds more. <!-- F-WP19-018 --> Aerodynamic parts like spoilers and splitters exist for this car, but they rarely help much at Fit speeds, and SCCA's Street category will not let you use them anyway. <!-- F-WP19-024, F-WP19-025 -->

## The full explanation

### Why weight matters

A lighter car turns, stops, and accelerates better with the same engine. <!-- F-WP19-022 --> Removing weight is one of the cheapest ways to make a stock Fit quicker at autocross or a track day. <!-- F-WP19-022 --> Autocross is a timed driving event where you drive one car at a time through a course marked with cones. <!-- F-WP19-024 --> SCCA stands for the Sports Car Club of America. It is a group that runs autocross and racing events and writes the rulebooks for them. <!-- F-WP19-024 -->

### The spare tire system

In this book, the "spare tire system" means the spare tire itself, its cover, and the jack and tools that go with it.

Community-reported figures found for a first-generation Fit like yours put the spare tire itself at about 21 pounds (9,525 g). <!-- F-WP19-014 --> The fabric cover some Fits carry over the spare weighs about 7.76 pounds (3,520 g). <!-- F-WP19-015 --> The jack, wrench, tow hook, and mounting bracket together weigh about 4.91 pounds (2,227 g). <!-- F-WP19-016 --> Add those three together and you get roughly 30 to 35 pounds. <!-- F-WP19-017 --> These numbers come from a single online community source, so treat them as a good estimate, not an exact figure. <!-- F-WP19-017 -->

Removing the spare tire system is easy and reversible. You can put it back before you drive home. But driving without a spare means you have no backup if you get a flat tire on the way to or from the event. Plan around that risk. <!-- F-WP19-017 -->

### The air conditioning system

Owners report that removing the entire air conditioning system — the condenser, compressor, refrigerant lines, brackets, and belt pulley — saves about 23.46 pounds (10,645 g). <!-- F-WP19-018 --> This job also frees up a small amount of engine power, since the compressor no longer drags on the engine's belt. <!-- F-WP19-018 --> This is a bigger job than pulling the spare tire, and it is usually a track-only or Street-Prepared-category modification, not something you do for a single event. <!-- F-WP19-022 --> **Always** have a licensed shop recover the refrigerant with the correct equipment before any A/C line is opened. Venting refrigerant straight into the air is illegal under federal law and harms the environment, so this is not a driveway job.

### Sound deadening and rear seats

Owners report removing sound-deadening material — the tar-like padding under the carpet that quiets road noise — for further weight savings. <!-- F-WP19-019 --> One community source lists about 11 pounds of sound deadening found so far, but the source itself calls this tally incomplete. <!-- F-WP19-019 --> Sound deadening is a modest weight item on this car compared to the seats or the air conditioning system. <!-- F-WP19-019 -->

Rear seat weight is a different story. We could not confirm this for the 2008 Fit. Check your owner's manual. <!-- F-WP19-020 --> A number exists for the newer, second-generation Fit's rear seat, but that is a different car, so this book will not print it as if it applied to yours. <!-- F-WP19-020 --> The Fit's fold-flat Magic Seat design — Honda's name for rear seats that fold down flat to open up cargo space — makes removing the rear seat an easy, reversible job. <!-- F-WP19-020 --> That is likely why owners still do it, even without a confirmed weight figure. <!-- F-WP19-020 -->

### The realistic number, and the extreme number

One community source describes a full "race trim" build that removes about 534.67 pounds (242,525 g) total. <!-- F-WP19-021 --> That number assumes gutting most of the interior, fitting lighter wheels and tires, a lightweight flywheel and battery, and a dedicated racing seat. <!-- F-WP19-021 --> That is not a realistic goal for a typical weekend autocrosser. <!-- F-WP19-021 -->

A more honest number keeps the car street-legal and Street-category-legal. <!-- F-WP19-022 --> Combining the spare tire system and the air conditioning system saves about 55 to 60 pounds. <!-- F-WP19-022 --> SCCA's Street category rule requires the car to run "as specified by the manufacturer with only standard equipment." <!-- F-WP19-022 --> That means a Street-classed Fit cannot legally remove its rear seats, sound deadening, or air conditioning at all. <!-- F-WP19-022 --> Owners who want to remove more have to move up into Street Prepared, Street Modified, Prepared, or Modified category first. <!-- F-WP19-022 -->

### Aerodynamics: an honest, thin topic

Aerodynamic parts use the shape of moving air to push a car down onto the road, which can help it grip better in fast corners. <!-- F-WP19-025 --> For the base 2008 Fit, this is honestly a thin topic, for three reasons. <!-- F-WP19-025 -->

First, only a few parts are made specifically for this exact chassis. <!-- F-WP19-023 --> Rear spoilers are the most common part sold for a GD3 Fit, and most of them are small, styling-focused pieces rather than parts built to add real downforce. <!-- F-WP19-023 --> Front and side splitters — panels that redirect air under the front of the car — are also sold specifically for the GD3 chassis, by at least two retailers. <!-- F-WP19-023 --> Canards, small wing-like fins added to the front bumper, are the thinnest category. No canard was found molded specifically for this car's bumper shape; the parts sold are generic pieces trimmed to fit. <!-- F-WP19-023 -->

Second, SCCA's Street category will not let you use most of these parts anyway. <!-- F-WP19-024 --> The rulebook does not list splitters, canards, or non-factory wings as allowed Street-category modifications. <!-- F-WP19-024 --> You would need to move up to Street Prepared, Street Modified, Prepared, or Modified category before you could legally add them. <!-- F-WP19-024 -->

Third, aerodynamic parts work best at higher speeds, because the extra grip they create grows with the square of your speed. <!-- F-WP19-025 --> A Fit at typical street or track-day speeds is well below the speed range where a splitter or wing meaningfully outweighs the extra weight and drag it adds. <!-- F-WP19-025 --> Treat a rear spoiler as a styling choice, and a splitter as a niche option for a higher-category or track-only build, not a priority modification for this car. <!-- F-WP19-025 -->

### A general checklist, beyond the specific numbers

Beyond the confirmed figures above, owners of small economy hatchbacks commonly remove other items too. <!-- F-WP19-026 --> These include the factory first-aid or warning-triangle kit, any aftermarket subwoofer or amplifier, and floor mats. <!-- F-WP19-026 --> They also empty the trunk of anything not needed that day. <!-- F-WP19-026 --> These items do not have specific weight figures behind them in this book; they are general community practice, not measured facts. <!-- F-WP19-026 -->

## Quick check

1. Roughly how much weight does removing the spare tire system save, and what risk do you take on by removing it before an event?
2. About how much does the air conditioning system weigh if fully removed?
3. Why can a Street-classed Fit not legally remove its rear seats or air conditioning?
4. Name the three reasons aerodynamics is described as a thin topic for this car.
5. What is a canard, and why are GD3-specific canards hard to find?
