# Chapter 57: Seats, harnesses, roll bars

## What you will learn

- What SCCA autocross rules require for seat belts and helmets in a stock Fit
- Why a stock Fit does not need a roll bar or harness for autocross
- What NASA requires for seat belts and roll bars at a track day
- Why NASA's roll bar rule depends on your car's body style, not your speed
- What happens once you move up to wheel-to-wheel racing

## The short version

A stock, closed-body Fit does not need a roll bar or an aftermarket harness for autocross or a typical track day. <!-- F-WP19-003, F-WP19-010 --> Both SCCA and NASA only require a roll bar once a car is either heavily modified or has an open top, like a convertible. <!-- F-WP19-004, F-WP19-010 --> Neither rulebook ties the roll bar requirement to how fast you drive. <!-- F-WP19-010, F-WP19-013 -->

## The full explanation

### Two organizations, two kinds of event

This chapter covers rules from two organizations. SCCA, the Sports Car Club of America, runs autocross events, called Solo. <!-- F-WP19-001 --> NASA, the National Auto Sport Association, runs High Performance Driving Events, known as HPDE, along with Time Trial and full wheel-to-wheel racing. <!-- F-WP19-007 --> An HPDE is a track day where you drive your own car on a real racetrack. An instructor rides along at first, to help you build skill rather than to race other cars. <!-- F-WP19-007 -->

A roll bar is a metal hoop or frame built into the car to protect your head in a rollover crash. <!-- F-WP19-003 --> A roll cage is a fuller, more complete version of the same idea, with multiple bars protecting more of the cabin. <!-- F-WP19-011 --> A harness is a set of racing seat belts with more attachment points than a factory belt, usually four, five, six, or seven points instead of the factory's three. <!-- F-WP19-001 -->

### SCCA autocross: what a stock Fit needs

For autocross, SCCA's current rulebook requires a lap belt in every car. <!-- F-WP19-001 --> A lap belt is the strap that crosses your hips; a shoulder belt is the strap that crosses your chest. <!-- F-WP19-001 --> Using a shoulder belt or a harness is strongly recommended, but it is not required in a closed car like your Fit. <!-- F-WP19-001 --> Your Fit's factory-installed belt system already meets this rule. <!-- F-WP19-001 -->

Here is a rule worth remembering carefully: SCCA only allows a non-factory harness in an open car. <!-- F-WP19-001 --> An open car means a convertible, or a car with its targa top or T-tops removed. <!-- F-WP19-001 --> Even then, the car also needs a roll structure that meets the rulebook's Appendix C standard. <!-- F-WP19-001 --> The top of that structure must sit at or above the top of your helmet. <!-- F-WP19-001 --> Your Fit is a closed hardtop with no factory open-top version, so this rule does not open a path to installing a racing harness in it. <!-- F-WP19-001 --> The rulebook does not let you swap a racing harness into a closed car at all. <!-- F-WP19-001 -->

Some autocrossers install a harness bar, a bar that lets you mount alternate belts, without a full roll cage. <!-- F-WP19-006 --> A harness bar is only a belt-mounting fixture. It is not a certified roll structure by itself. <!-- F-WP19-006 --> So installing a harness bar alone does not satisfy the roll-structure requirement above, and does not open the door to using a harness in a closed car either. <!-- F-WP19-006 -->

Helmets are a firmer rule. Every competitor, in every class, needs a helmet meeting one of a specific list of Snell, SFI, or FIA safety standards. <!-- F-WP19-002 --> Snell, SFI, and FIA are organizations that test and certify helmets for motorsport use, and none of them offer an exception based on speed or class. <!-- F-WP19-002 --> The chin strap must be fastened securely. <!-- F-WP19-002 --> Most cars with a normal-size windshield, like your Fit, do not need a full-face helmet, but open-wheel cars and karts do. <!-- F-WP19-002 -->

### SCCA autocross: when a roll bar becomes mandatory

A roll bar or cage is not required for a closed car running in SCCA's Street, Street Touring, Street Prepared, or Street Modified categories. <!-- F-WP19-003 --> These are the categories a base, lightly-modified Fit would run in; see Chapter 58 for exactly which class. <!-- F-WP19-003 --> A roll bar only becomes mandatory in specific, heavily-modified classes: A Modified, B Modified, C Modified, F Modified, open cars in the Prepared category, D Modified, and E Modified. <!-- F-WP19-004 --> These "Modified" and "Prepared" classes are SCCA's most heavily-built, purpose-made-for-racing categories, well beyond a car with bolt-on street parts. <!-- F-WP19-004 --> Building a car into one of these classes is a major step beyond the stock or lightly-modified Fit this book focuses on. <!-- F-WP19-004 -->

If a roll bar is added to a car in one of the more modified categories, SCCA's Appendix C sets rules for it. <!-- F-WP19-005 --> The top of the bar cannot sit below the top of the driver's helmet. <!-- F-WP19-005 --> The rulebook also suggests it extend at least 3.0 inches (76.2 mm) above the helmet. <!-- F-WP19-005 --> It must use seamless steel tubing, not a bolted-together post design, and must attach to the car's structure at two points, forming a hoop shape. <!-- F-WP19-005 -->

### NASA HPDE and Time Trial: what a stock Fit needs

For an HPDE or Time Trial day, NASA requires modern, good-condition factory seat belts, or a DOT-approved restraint system. <!-- F-WP19-007 --> DOT stands for the U.S. Department of Transportation, which sets safety standards for belts sold for street cars. <!-- F-WP19-007 --> A lap belt alone, with no shoulder strap, is not allowed. <!-- F-WP19-007 --> Your Fit's stock three-point belt already meets this rule, with no changes needed. <!-- F-WP19-007 -->

NASA also requires a helmet meeting Snell SA2010 or newer, or an equivalent standard, for both the driver and any passenger. <!-- F-WP19-009 --> Eye protection like goggles or a face shield is recommended, but is not a hard requirement the way the helmet is. <!-- F-WP19-009 -->

### The roll bar rule that surprises a lot of people

Here is the most important, and most misunderstood, rule in this chapter. NASA's roll bar requirement for HPDE and Time Trial depends entirely on whether your car is open or closed. <!-- F-WP19-010 --> It does not depend on how fast you drive, or which run group you are in. <!-- F-WP19-010 --> NASA's rulebook states that open cars, meaning convertibles or open T-tops, should have a roll bar installed. <!-- F-WP19-010 --> Nothing in NASA's rules ties a roll bar requirement to a driver's lap time or top speed. <!-- F-WP19-010 --> Your base 2008 Fit is a closed hardtop with no factory open-top version. It does not need a roll bar for any NASA HPDE or Time Trial run group, no matter how fast you get. <!-- F-WP19-010 -->

Many drivers assume that going faster, or moving into a higher run group, eventually triggers a roll bar requirement. <!-- F-WP19-013 --> That assumption is wrong for both organizations covered in this chapter. <!-- F-WP19-013 --> The real trigger is always event type and car body style, never a specific speed or lap time. <!-- F-WP19-013 -->

There is one thing that does change your equipment needs on a closed car: adding a hard-shell, fixed-back racing seat. <!-- F-WP19-008 --> NASA's rulebook says stock belts should not be worn with that kind of seat, so a car with one needs a harness meeting a specific certification standard instead. <!-- F-WP19-008 --> This does not apply to your Fit's stock seats. It only matters if you later install an aftermarket racing seat. <!-- F-WP19-008 -->

### Wheel-to-wheel racing: the one place equipment is always mandatory

Once you move beyond HPDE and Time Trial into wheel-to-wheel Competition — racing where more than one car is on the track at once, with finishing positions — the rules change completely. <!-- F-WP19-011 --> A full roll cage is required in every car, with no exception for car type or speed. <!-- F-WP19-011 --> The cage must meet detailed construction standards. It needs a continuous main hoop close to the roof, and a diagonal brace. <!-- F-WP19-011 --> Padding must cover anywhere your head or limbs could hit it. <!-- F-WP19-011 --> The cage also needs a minimum of six mounting points to the car's floor or rocker panels. <!-- F-WP19-011 --> A 5-, 6-, or 7-point harness, certified to a specific SFI or FIA standard, is also required, and that certification carries a hard expiration date with no exceptions. <!-- F-WP19-012 -->

Building a Fit to this level is a large step past autocross or an HPDE day, and beyond the scope of a first project car. <!-- F-WP19-011 --> If you ever consider it, treat this chapter as only a starting point, and work from the full current rulebook. <!-- F-WP19-011 -->

## Quick check

1. Does a stock, closed 2008 Fit need a roll bar for SCCA autocross? Why or why not?
2. What is the real trigger for NASA's roll bar requirement at an HPDE or Time Trial day — speed, lap time, or something else?
3. Under SCCA's rules, when is an aftermarket harness legal, and why can you not install one in your Fit as it comes from the factory?
4. What is the difference between a roll bar and a roll cage?
5. What piece of aftermarket equipment can require a harness on a closed car, even without a roll bar rule forcing it?

# Part IX: Getting Ready for the Track

---

# Chapter 58: Autocross

## What you will learn

- What autocross is and how it works
- What SCCA class a stock 2008 base Fit runs in
- What kinds of changes move your Fit into a faster class
- Whether an older Fit is still allowed to compete
- What to know before your first event

## The short version

Autocross is a timed event where you drive one car at a time through a course marked out with cones, trying to beat the clock without hitting anything. <!-- F-WP20-001 --> A stock 2008 base Fit currently runs in SCCA's H Street class. <!-- F-WP20-001 --> Adding suspension, brake, or wheel and tire upgrades moves it up into a faster class, and adding race tires and engine tuning moves it up again. <!-- F-WP20-003, F-WP20-004 -->

## The full explanation

### What autocross actually is

SCCA, the Sports Car Club of America, calls its autocross program "Solo." <!-- F-WP20-001 --> In Solo, you drive one car at a time through a course laid out with cones on a large paved lot, usually a parking lot or an unused runway. <!-- F-WP20-001 --> You are timed, not racing side by side against another driver, so your only competition is the clock and everyone else's best run of the day. <!-- F-WP20-001 --> Cars are sorted into classes so that similar cars compete against each other, which is why knowing your class matters. <!-- F-WP20-001 -->

### Your Fit's class: H Street

The current SCCA National Solo rulebook lists the Fit in H Street, written as HS. <!-- F-WP20-001 --> This classing was checked in two separate rulebook editions and is identical in both, so it has not changed recently. <!-- F-WP20-001 --> Street is SCCA's entry-level category. The rulebook itself calls it "the lowest barrier of entry." <!-- F-WP20-001 --> It allows only limited changes: mainly shocks, anti-roll bars — suspension parts that resist body lean in turns — and replacement tires. <!-- F-WP20-001 --> Beyond those changes, a Street-classed Fit is expected to stay close to how it left the factory. <!-- F-WP20-001 -->

Your 2008 model-year Fit is still fully eligible to compete. <!-- F-WP20-002 --> SCCA's own age rule allows a car to run in National events through the end of the 30th calendar year after its model year. <!-- F-WP20-002 --> For a 2008 car, that rule stays in effect through 2038, so age is not a concern for many years to come. <!-- F-WP20-002 --> Regional-only Solo events do not use this same age limit at all. <!-- F-WP20-002 -->

### Moving up a class: Street Touring and Street Prepared

If you add the kinds of parts Street category does not allow, your Fit moves into a different class instead of staying in H Street. <!-- F-WP20-003 --> The next class up is E Street Touring, written EST. <!-- F-WP20-003 --> SCCA describes Street Touring as sitting between Street and the more heavily modified Street Prepared category. <!-- F-WP20-003 --> Moving into EST means adding aftermarket suspension parts, like shocks, springs, or sway bars. <!-- F-WP20-003 --> It can also mean a different differential — the part that lets your two drive wheels spin at different speeds through a turn — bolt-on engine parts, or a bigger brake kit. <!-- F-WP20-003 --> Wider or different-sized wheels and tires than Street category allows are permitted too. <!-- F-WP20-003 --> A car in this class is still expected to stay street-legal. <!-- F-WP20-003 --> It must keep its emissions equipment and OBD-II system — the car's onboard diagnostic computer, which tracks emissions and engine problems — working. <!-- F-WP20-003 --> Its factory anti-lock brakes must also keep working, though they may be turned off electronically. <!-- F-WP20-003 -->

Going further still moves your Fit into F Street Prepared, written FSP. <!-- F-WP20-004 --> Street Prepared builds on everything Street Touring allows, then adds a further step that is not really meant for driving on public roads. <!-- F-WP20-004 --> This includes race-compatible tires and permanent bodywork changes, such as cutting or flaring fenders for tire clearance. <!-- F-WP20-004 --> It also includes front splitters, rear spoilers, and an aftermarket ECU — an aftermarket computer that controls engine tuning — for engine tuning. <!-- F-WP20-004 --> Emissions equipment can be removed, and the intake and exhaust become unrestricted. <!-- F-WP20-004 --> A Street Prepared car is also allowed further weight-reduction changes, like removing the air conditioning system, and more suspension upgrades than Street Touring allows. <!-- F-WP20-004 -->

Put together, the modification ladder for your Fit looks like this. Street or H Street keeps the car stock, aside from shocks, sway bars, and tires. <!-- F-WP20-001, F-WP20-003 --> Street Touring or EST adds bolt-on suspension, brake, and wheel upgrades while staying street-legal. <!-- F-WP20-003 --> Street Prepared or FSP adds race tires, tuning, and body changes, and is no longer necessarily street-legal. <!-- F-WP20-004 -->

### Getting ready for your first event

Before your first event, confirm which class you plan to run. If your Fit is stock, or close to it, that class is H Street. <!-- F-WP20-001 --> Read Chapter 57 for what your Fit needs, and does not need, in terms of seat belts, harnesses, and helmets. <!-- F-WP20-001 --> Read Chapter 60 for a full checklist of what to bring and do the night before, the morning of, and between runs. <!-- F-WP20-001 --> Knowing your class ahead of time means you register correctly and compete against the right group of cars from your very first run. <!-- F-WP20-001 -->

## Quick check

1. What class does a stock 2008 base Fit run in under SCCA's current rulebook?
2. What is the difference between Street, Street Touring, and Street Prepared, in terms of what each one allows you to change?
3. Through what year does SCCA's age rule keep a 2008 model-year car eligible for National events?
4. Name two changes that would move your Fit from H Street into E Street Touring.
5. Where in this book should you look for the safety-gear rules and the full event-day checklist?

# Chapter 59: HPDE / track days

## What you will learn

- What HPDE means and how it differs from racing
- What NASA's tech inspection checks on the car itself
- What safety and general-condition items tech inspection checks
- What the actual paper tech form looks like
- The most common reasons cars fail tech inspection

## The short version

HPDE stands for High Performance Driving Event, a track day where you drive your own car on a real track to build skill, not to race other drivers. <!-- F-WP20-005 --> Before you can drive, your car has to pass a tech inspection covering its mechanical condition and its safety equipment. <!-- F-WP20-005, F-WP20-006 --> A well-maintained, stock Fit with good tires and brakes should pass without trouble. <!-- F-WP20-007 -->

## The full explanation

### What tech inspection checks on the car itself

NASA's Club Codes and Regulations, the rulebook covering NASA's HPDE program, lists specific mechanical items every car must pass. <!-- F-WP20-005 --> Wheels and tires must show no cracked or damaged wheels and no exposed cords or bubbles on the tire, with every lug nut present and tight. <!-- F-WP20-005 --> Steering and suspension get checked for tightness, since very little or no play is allowed in the wheel bearings, suspension, or steering. <!-- F-WP20-005 --> The engine bay must show no significant fluid leaks from the engine, radiator, or hoses, and hoses must be in good condition with no cracks. <!-- F-WP20-005 --> The radiator needs an overflow container holding at least one liter. <!-- F-WP20-005 --> Any oil breather or vent line must either return to the engine, or route to a catch tank of at least one liter. <!-- F-WP20-005 --> Brakes must work well enough to stop the car in a reasonable, safe distance, with adequate pedal pressure. <!-- F-WP20-005 --> Fluid must sit at or above the minimum level, and brake lines must be in good condition. <!-- F-WP20-005 --> The car's general appearance also gets checked against what the rulebook calls the "50/50 rule": it should look undamaged and straight at 50 mph from 50 feet away. <!-- F-WP20-005 -->

### What tech inspection checks beyond the mechanicals

Tech inspection also covers safety items that are not part of the car's mechanical systems. <!-- F-WP20-006 --> Seatbelts and harnesses must be in good, undamaged, factory configuration, or use an approved aftermarket harness if the car has an aftermarket hard-shell seat. <!-- F-WP20-006 --> Roll bars are required on all open cars, but not on a closed car like your Fit. <!-- F-WP20-006 --> The battery must be securely fastened — a bungee cord or rubber cord alone is not acceptable — and its positive terminal must be covered with a non-conductive material. <!-- F-WP20-006 --> The fuel cap must seal well enough that fuel does not spill under hard driving. <!-- F-WP20-006 --> No exposed, live wiring is allowed inside the cabin, and seats must be securely fastened to the car's structure. <!-- F-WP20-006 --> Every loose object must come out of the passenger compartment before you go on track. <!-- F-WP20-006 --> At least one working rear-view mirror and at least two working brake lights, visible from 300 feet, are both required. <!-- F-WP20-006 --> Tow eyes — front and rear hooks a tow truck can safely pull from — are strongly recommended, though not always required. <!-- F-WP20-006 -->

On the process itself, you review the Technical Inspection Form before the event, then either have the car pre-teched at an authorized shop or teched at the track. <!-- F-WP20-006 --> Once the form is signed, you place it on the dash or windshield so a grid marshal can collect it and apply a tech sticker. <!-- F-WP20-006 -->

### The real tech form, item by item

A real NASA HPDE/Time Trial Technical Inspection Form organizes its checklist into five boxes. <!-- F-WP20-007 --> Wheels and tires covers tire condition, all lug nuts present and tight, and no hubcaps or beauty rings left on. <!-- F-WP20-007 --> Steering and suspension covers front and rear wheel-bearing play and steering play. <!-- F-WP20-007 --> Engine covers fluid leaks from oil, fuel, or water, the radiator overflow and hose condition, and a battery secured with no bungee cords and covered terminals. <!-- F-WP20-007 --> Brakes covers pedal pressure, fluid level, line condition, and brake lights. <!-- F-WP20-007 --> Safety Equipment covers roll bars on open cars, seat belts or a harness, a Snell SA2005-or-newer helmet, and a covered battery if it sits inside the cockpit. <!-- F-WP20-007 --> Miscellaneous covers a secured gas cap, no exposed wires, and seats bolted down tight. <!-- F-WP20-007 -->

The form also reminds you to put contrasting-color numbers, at least 10 inches tall, on both sides of the car. <!-- F-WP20-007 --> It tells you to remove all loose objects, including the spare tire and floor mats. <!-- F-WP20-007 --> Finally, place your tech sticker at the top center of the windshield. <!-- F-WP20-007 --> The most common first-timer tech-inspection failures are worn brake pads, a loose battery, corded or cracked tires, leaking fluids, loose wheel bearings, and a missing tow hook. <!-- F-WP20-007 --> Inspect your car several days before the event, not the morning of, so you have time to fix or order parts for anything you find. <!-- F-WP20-007 -->

## Quick check

1. What does HPDE stand for, and how is it different from wheel-to-wheel racing?
2. Name three mechanical items NASA's tech inspection checks under the car's hood.
3. Does a stock, closed Fit need a roll bar to pass tech inspection? Why or why not?
4. What are the five checklist boxes on a real NASA tech inspection form?
5. What are the most common reasons a first-timer's car fails tech inspection, and how can you avoid them?

<!-- Fact IDs used: F-WP20-005, F-WP20-006, F-WP20-007 -->

# Chapter 60: Track-day prep checklist

## What you will learn

- What to do the night before a track day
- What to check and bring the morning of the event
- What to check between track sessions
- Why finishing car prep early matters more than doing it at the last minute

## The short version

Finish your car prep several days before the event, not the night before, and pack your gear bag completely so the morning is simple. <!-- F-WP20-008 --> On the morning of, check your cold tire pressure and dress for the day's required clothing. <!-- F-WP20-009 --> Between sessions, recheck tire pressure and wheel torque, and take time to eat, drink, and rest. <!-- F-WP20-010 -->

## The full explanation

### The night before

Finish any car repairs or upgrades several days ahead of the event, not the night before. <!-- F-WP20-008 --> A last-minute repair that goes wrong, like a broken bolt or a surprise problem, is one of the most common ways a driver arrives at the track exhausted and unprepared. <!-- F-WP20-008 --> The night before, pack your car and gear bag completely, so the morning of the event stays simple. <!-- F-WP20-008 --> Pack your helmet and gloves, your completed or shop-signed tech inspection form, a pen, and a tire pressure gauge. <!-- F-WP20-008 --> Also pack a small tool bag with a jack, a wheel-lock key if your car uses one, and zip ties. <!-- F-WP20-008 --> Also pack a filled gas container if the track does not sell fuel, and a cooler with water or a sports drink and food. <!-- F-WP20-008 --> Bring cash for tow-truck tips or tolls, and a toll transponder if you will drive toll roads to get there. <!-- F-WP20-008 --> Many event organizers hold check-in the evening before the event. <!-- F-WP20-008 --> Attending it avoids a morning-of line for waivers, the tech form, car numbers, and your event wristband or tech sticker. <!-- F-WP20-008 -->

### The morning of

Check your tire pressures while the tires are still cold, right at the start of the day. <!-- F-WP20-009 --> Tire pressure rises once the tires warm up on track. <!-- F-WP20-009 --> Note your cold baseline before you leave, so you can adjust it correctly later. <!-- F-WP20-009 --> Confirm the car has no new fluid leaks and no dashboard warning lights before you leave home. <!-- F-WP20-009 --> Dress for the event's required clothing rather than showing up in shorts and sandals. <!-- F-WP20-009 --> Many organizations, including NASA, require long sleeves and pants, and closed-toe shoes. <!-- F-WP20-009 --> Bring sunscreen, comfortable weather-appropriate clothing, snacks, and plenty of water. <!-- F-WP20-009 --> A good rule of thumb is roughly one quart of fluid for every 50 pounds of body weight over a full track day. <!-- F-WP20-009 --> Heat and nervous energy both increase how much fluid you lose. <!-- F-WP20-009 --> Arrive with enough time before the mandatory drivers' meeting to unload, get through registration and tech, and not feel rushed. <!-- F-WP20-009 -->

### Between sessions

After each session, recheck tire pressure, and tire temperature too if you have a temperature gun on hand. <!-- F-WP20-010 --> Track surface temperature and how hard you drive both change your tire pressure through the day. <!-- F-WP20-010 --> Recheck your wheel and lug torque periodically, since track driving can work fasteners loose faster than everyday street driving. <!-- F-WP20-010 --> Top off engine oil if your car is a known oil-consumer, or at least check the level, using oil and a funnel packed for exactly this purpose. <!-- F-WP20-010 --> Use the break between sessions to rehydrate, eat something, and rest in the shade, rather than only working on the car. <!-- F-WP20-010 --> Staying hydrated and fed directly affects how well, and how safely, you drive later in the day. <!-- F-WP20-010 --> Many organized programs hold an instructor debrief or driver meeting between sessions. <!-- F-WP20-010 --> Taking a few notes on what changed between sessions is a widely repeated habit. <!-- F-WP20-010 --> A braking point, an odd noise, or a new tire pressure are all worth writing down, for tracking progress and problems across the day. <!-- F-WP20-010 -->

## Quick check

1. Why should you finish car repairs several days before the event instead of the night before?
2. Name three things you should pack in your gear bag the night before.
3. Why do you check tire pressure while the tires are still cold in the morning?
4. What should you check on your wheels between sessions, and why does track driving make this more important than usual?
5. Besides working on the car, what should you do during the break between sessions?

<!-- Fact IDs used: F-WP20-008, F-WP20-009, F-WP20-010 -->
<!-- All Chapter 60 fact cards (from WP-20 Section C) were used. No unused facts. -->

# Chapter 61: Driver skills

## What you will learn

- Where to look with your eyes while driving on track
- Why smooth steering and pedal inputs make you faster, not just safer
- What trail braking means and why drivers use it
- How these skills connect to what you already do behind the wheel

## The short version

Look far ahead, toward the path you want, not at a hazard or the track's edge. <!-- F-WP20-011 --> Smooth, gentle steering and pedal inputs keep more grip in your tires than sudden, jerky ones do. <!-- F-WP20-012 --> Trail braking means letting off the brake gradually as you steer into a corner, instead of coming off it all at once beforehand. <!-- F-WP20-013 -->

## The full explanation

### Where to look

A well-known professional driving coach's free instructional material teaches vision as one of the very first skills to learn, ahead of lines or braking technique. <!-- F-WP20-011 --> Train your eyes to look far down the track, not just at the pavement right in front of your car. <!-- F-WP20-011 --> Point your eyes toward the path you want the car to take. <!-- F-WP20-011 --> Avoid fixing your gaze on a hazard or the track's outer edge, since your eyes tend to pull the car toward wherever they are aimed. <!-- F-WP20-011 --> Turning your head to see into a corner, instead of keeping it pointed straight ahead, helps you spot that path earlier. <!-- F-WP20-011 --> Treat this as a constant scanning habit. Always pick up your next reference point further down the track, rather than glancing once and then forgetting about it. <!-- F-WP20-011 --> Your overall awareness of the drive shapes how well you react. That means what you see, what you feel through the seat as the car grips or slides, and what you hear from the engine and tires. <!-- F-WP20-011 -->

### Why smoothness matters

The same source teaches keeping your hands near the 9-and-3 o'clock position on the wheel, like the hands of a clock, through most corners on most tracks. <!-- F-WP20-012 --> A car that needs less wheel movement tends to be smoother and more predictable to drive. <!-- F-WP20-012 --> Keeping your hand movements small is directly tied to how smooth, and ultimately how fast, you can drive. <!-- F-WP20-012 -->

Here is why smoothness matters so much: weight transfer. <!-- F-WP20-012 --> Weight transfer is the way a car's weight shifts from one end, or one side, to the other as you brake, speed up, or turn. <!-- F-WP20-012 --> Braking shifts weight toward the front tires. Speeding up shifts it toward the rear. Turning shifts it toward the outside tires. <!-- F-WP20-012 --> A sudden, jerky input causes a bigger, more sudden weight shift than the same input made gradually. <!-- F-WP20-012 --> A tire's grip does not grow at the same rate as the extra load it carries. <!-- F-WP20-012 --> That is why a car actually loses more overall grip from a jerky input than a smooth one. <!-- F-WP20-012 --> That is the real reason smooth driving is fast driving, not only safer driving. <!-- F-WP20-012 -->

### Trail braking, explained simply

Trail braking means gradually letting off the brake pedal with your foot as you steer into a corner, instead of coming completely off the brake before you begin turning. <!-- F-WP20-013 --> This trade swaps some of the brake's stopping force for turning force, right as the corner asks for it. <!-- F-WP20-013 --> Braking keeps extra weight, and therefore extra grip, on your front tires right when you are also asking them to change the car's direction. <!-- F-WP20-013 --> Coming off the brake gradually, instead of all at once, helps keep the front tires closer to their best grip as you turn in. <!-- F-WP20-013 --> Many quick drivers pay less attention to their braking point, and more attention to exactly when they finish coming off the brake. <!-- F-WP20-013 --> A smooth, late release keeps the car balanced, and keeps your eyes up on the corner instead of down at your feet. <!-- F-WP20-013 -->

### Putting it together

These three skills work as a set, not on their own. <!-- F-WP20-011, F-WP20-012, F-WP20-013 --> Looking far ahead gives you the time you need to make your steering and pedal inputs smooth, instead of rushed. <!-- F-WP20-011, F-WP20-012 --> Smooth inputs keep your tires gripping their best, which is exactly what trail braking is trying to use as you enter a corner. <!-- F-WP20-012, F-WP20-013 --> None of these skills require a faster car. They only take practice, and they work just as well in a stock Fit as in any other car. <!-- F-WP20-011, F-WP20-012, F-WP20-013 -->

## Quick check

1. Where should your eyes look while driving on track, and why does this matter?
2. What is weight transfer, and how does it connect braking, speeding up, and turning to your tires' grip?
3. Why is a smooth input often faster than a jerky one, not just safer?
4. What is trail braking, in your own words?
5. Why do many faster drivers pay more attention to how they finish releasing the brake than to where they start braking?

<!-- Fact IDs used: F-WP20-011, F-WP20-012, F-WP20-013 -->
<!-- All Chapter 61 fact cards (from WP-20 Section D) were used. No unused facts. All content is paraphrased from the fact cards, per D7; the underlying source (Ross Bentley's "Performance Driving Illustrated") is never quoted directly. -->

# Chapter 62: After the track

## What you will learn

- What to do to your car right after you finish your last session
- Why you should avoid the parking brake right after hard driving
- What to check before you drive home
- What to check once you get home, for a fuller post-event inspection

## The short version

Take a slow cool-down lap at the end of your last session, instead of braking hard and parking right away. <!-- F-WP20-014 --> Once the car has cooled, recheck your tire pressure, tread, and brake pedal feel before you drive home. <!-- F-WP20-014 --> At home, check for fresh leaks, fluid levels, pad wear, tire wear, and lug torque before you put the car back into everyday use. <!-- F-WP20-015 -->

## The full explanation

### Right after your last session

Drive one slower, gentle lap at the end of your last session, instead of braking hard and parking the car right away. <!-- F-WP20-014 --> This cool-down lap lets air move through the brakes, engine, and transmission, bringing their temperatures down gradually rather than all at once. <!-- F-WP20-014 --> Avoid using the parking brake, also called the hand brake, right after a hot session. <!-- F-WP20-014 --> A very hot brake rotor, clamped tight by the parking brake, can warp or damage the pad or rotor as it cools. <!-- F-WP20-014 --> Let the car cool with the parking brake off instead. <!-- F-WP20-014 -->

### Before you drive home

Once the car and its tires have cooled, recheck your tire pressure and tread condition before you leave for home. <!-- F-WP20-014 --> Pressures measured right after a hot session read higher than your correct driving pressure, so wait until the tires cool before trusting the number. <!-- F-WP20-014 --> A hard session can also reveal cuts, exposed cords, or uneven wear that were not there that morning. <!-- F-WP20-014 --> Check your brakes too: the pedal should still feel firm, not soft or noticeably longer in travel than it felt that morning. <!-- F-WP20-014 --> Brake fluid can boil or fade under sustained hard track use, and a spongy or long pedal is a sign that has happened. <!-- F-WP20-014 -->

### The fuller inspection, once you get home

Look under the car and around the engine bay, on clean, dry ground, for any fresh drips of oil, coolant, or brake fluid that were not there before the event. <!-- F-WP20-015 --> Check that your engine oil, coolant, and brake fluid are all still at the correct levels. <!-- F-WP20-015 --> A hard day of high-RPM driving and heavy braking uses up and stresses fluids faster than ordinary street driving does. <!-- F-WP20-015 --> Check the visible brake pad thickness at each wheel, and look for fresh cracks, heavy discoloration, or fluid residue near the calipers. <!-- F-WP20-015 --> Pad wear is one of the fastest-consumed items on a track day, so this check matters even if the car seemed fine on the drive home. <!-- F-WP20-015 --> Inspect all four tires for uneven wear, cuts, or a change in the tread surface. <!-- F-WP20-015 --> Re-torque every wheel's lug nuts once the car and wheels are fully cool. <!-- F-WP20-015 --> Track driving's repeated heating and cooling, combined with harder cornering loads, can work fasteners loose faster than normal driving does. <!-- F-WP20-015 --> Finally, walk around the car and listen for anything that came loose — in the engine bay, under the car, or inside. <!-- F-WP20-015 --> Check trim panels, undertray fasteners, and exhaust hangers before you put the car back into everyday use. <!-- F-WP20-015 -->

## Quick check

1. Why should you drive a slower cool-down lap instead of stopping and parking right away?
2. Why should you avoid using the parking brake right after a hard session?
3. Why does tire pressure right after a session not tell you the correct driving pressure?
4. Name three fluids you should check the level of once you get home.
5. Why does track driving make it more important to re-torque your lug nuts than everyday driving does?

<!-- Fact IDs used: F-WP20-014, F-WP20-015 -->

# Appendix A: Full specifications

This appendix lists every specification found for your 2008 Fit base trim: engine, transmission, dimensions, weight, cargo, fuel economy, crash ratings, and price. Most numbers below come straight from Honda's own 2008 press materials. Where a source disagreed with another, or where no reliable number could be found at all, that is written out honestly instead of leaving the row blank. A few terms are defined the first time they appear. **Redline** is the highest engine speed (in RPM, or revolutions per minute) the engine is designed to reach before the driver should shift or ease off. **Compression ratio** compares the volume inside a cylinder when the piston is at the bottom of its stroke to the volume when it is at the top; a higher ratio generally squeezes the air/fuel mixture more tightly before it burns. **Curb weight** is how much the car weighs by itself, with a full tank of fuel and all standard equipment, but no passengers or cargo. **Wheelbase** is the distance between the centers of the front and rear wheels. **MSRP** stands for manufacturer's suggested retail price — the sticker price Honda recommended before tax, license, registration, and destination charge.

| Torque | 105 lb-ft @ 4,800 rpm | F-WP01-004 |
| Compression ratio | 10.4:1 | F-WP01-005 |

| Recommended fuel | Regular unleaded gasoline. Honda's own specification sheet and fueleconomy.gov both say "regular" fuel, but neither one prints an exact minimum octane number for this car. The commonly repeated "87 octane" figure is the standard US definition of "regular" gasoline in general, not a number confirmed for the Fit specifically, so it is not stated here as a verified Fit-specific fact. | F-WP01-007 |
| Redline | 6,500 rpm | F-WP01-008 |

# Appendix B: Fluid capacities and types

This appendix lists every fluid in your 2008 Fit: the type or spec to buy, and how much your car holds. A few terms come up more than once. **Viscosity** describes how thick or thin a fluid flows, written as a code like 5W-20. **MTF** stands for manual transmission fluid, the fluid inside a manual gearbox. **ATF** stands for automatic transmission fluid, the fluid inside an automatic transmission. **DOT** stands for Department of Transportation. A DOT number on brake fluid is a US safety-grade rating, and a higher number is not automatically "better" for your car. Using the wrong one can cause damage. Numbers marked M/T are for cars with a manual transmission; numbers marked A/T are for cars with an automatic.

## Engine oil

| What | Value | Fact ID |
|---|---|---|
| Type | API Premium-grade 5W-20 detergent oil, with the API Certification Seal ("For Gasoline Engines"). Honda's own motor oil is the preferred brand. Synthetic oil is acceptable if it carries the same seal and viscosity. No oil additives are recommended. | F-WP09-016 |
| Capacity, oil change including filter | 3.8 US qt (3.6 L) | F-WP09-017 |
| Capacity, oil change without filter | 3.6 US qt (3.4 L) | F-WP09-018 |
| Capacity, total (after a full engine overhaul or dry fill) | 4.4 US qt (4.2 L) | F-WP09-019 |

## Engine coolant

Coolant, also called antifreeze, is the liquid that flows through your engine and radiator to keep the engine from overheating.

| What | Value | Fact ID |
|---|---|---|
| Type | Honda Long-Life Antifreeze/Coolant Type 2 only, pre-mixed 50% antifreeze and 50% water. Never add straight antifreeze or plain water. If Honda coolant is not available, a major-brand non-silicate coolant rated for aluminum engines may be used temporarily, then the system should be flushed and refilled with Honda coolant as soon as possible. | F-WP09-020 |
| Capacity after a coolant change, manual transmission | 3.8 L (1.00 US gal) | F-WP09-021 |
| Capacity after a coolant change, automatic transmission | 3.7 L (0.98 US gal) | F-WP09-022 |
| Capacity total, after engine overhaul, manual transmission | 5.4 L (1.43 US gal) | F-WP09-023 |
| Capacity total, after engine overhaul, automatic transmission | 5.3 L (1.40 US gal) | F-WP09-024 |
| Reserve tank capacity | 0.4 L (0.11 US gal) | F-WP09-025 |

## Manual transmission fluid (MTF)

| What | Value | Fact ID |
|---|---|---|
| Type | Honda Manual Transmission Fluid (MTF) preferred. If unavailable, an SAE 10W-30 or 10W-40 motor oil with the API Certification Seal ("For Gasoline Engines") may be used as a temporary replacement, but motor oil is missing the additives a gearbox needs, and using it for a long time can leave your shifts feeling stiffer. | F-WP09-026 |
| Capacity, fluid change | 1.6 US qt (1.5 L) | F-WP09-027 |
| Capacity, total (including the differential, after overhaul) | 1.7 US qt (1.6 L) | F-WP09-028 |

The differential is the set of gears inside the transmission housing that lets your two front wheels spin at different speeds when you turn. It shares the same fluid as the manual transmission on your Fit.

## Automatic transmission fluid (ATF)

| What | Value | Fact ID |
|---|---|---|
| Type | Honda Genuine ATF-Z1 only. Do not mix it with other transmission fluids. Using a different fluid can cause the transmission to wear out faster, and damage from using the wrong fluid is not covered by Honda's new-vehicle warranty. Note for today's buyer: Honda ATF-Z1 has since been discontinued industry-wide and replaced by Honda ATF DW-1 for service fills on many older Hondas; ask your parts store for the current equivalent. | F-WP09-029 |
| Capacity, fluid change | 2.5 US qt (2.4 L) | F-WP09-030 |
| Capacity, total (after overhaul) | 6.3 US qt (6.0 L) | F-WP09-031 |

## Brake fluid and clutch fluid

| What | Value | Fact ID |
|---|---|---|
| Type | Honda Heavy Duty Brake Fluid DOT 3 preferred, or a DOT 3 or DOT 4 brake fluid from a sealed container as a temporary replacement. Brake fluid marked DOT 5 does not work with your Fit's braking system, and can cause extensive damage. | F-WP09-032 |
| Clutch fluid (manual transmission only) | Manual-transmission Fits use a hydraulic clutch that shares the same fluid specification as the brake system above. Fluid level should sit between the MIN and MAX marks on the clutch fluid reservoir, which has a gray cap; a low level can point to a leak in the clutch hydraulic system. | F-WP09-033 |
| Capacity | This work package's sources do not give a brake or clutch fluid capacity number for the 2008 Fit. | — |

## Power steering fluid

| What | Value | Fact ID |
|---|---|---|
| Type / capacity | Not applicable. Your 2008 Fit uses Electric Power-Assisted Rack-and-Pinion Steering (EPS) on every trim. EPS uses an electric motor to help you turn the wheel, not a hydraulic pump and fluid reservoir, so there is no power steering fluid to check or change. | F-WP09-034 |

# Appendix C: Torque specifications

**Torque** is a measure of twisting force — how hard you turn a bolt or nut with a wrench. A **torque wrench** is a tool that stops you (or clicks, or shows a reading) once you have reached a set amount of twisting force, so you do not over-tighten or under-tighten a fastener. Over-tightening can strip threads or crack parts; under-tightening can let a part loosen and fail while you drive. This appendix gives every torque number this project's research turned up for the 2008 Fit base trim, and it says so plainly whenever a number could not be confirmed. The service manual that mechanics normally use for this exact number (the Honda factory service manual) was not available as a free source during this research, so most values below come from the owner's manual (which prints a few) or from cross-checked online forum and repair-site sources (which print the rest, with lower confidence). The **confidence tier** column tells you how much to trust each number: Tier 1 means it came from Honda's own document; Tier 3 means a published repair reference or parts site; Tier 4 means an online forum or Q&A post. A "community-reported" value has not been checked against an official document — treat it as a helpful starting point, not a guarantee, and check it against your own shop's torque chart or the fastener's own markings before you rely on it.

| Item | Torque value | Confidence / tier | Fact ID |
|---|---|---|---|
| Wheel lug nut (wheel nut) | 80 lbf·ft (108 N·m, 11 kgf·m) — same value for a normal wheel or the compact spare | High / Tier 1 (owner's manual) | F-WP10-001 |
| Oil drain plug (drain bolt) | 29 lbf·ft (39 N·m, 4.0 kgf·m) | High / Tier 1 (owner's manual) | F-WP10-002 |
| Oil filter | No numeric torque published by Honda. The owner's manual says to install the filter "according to the instructions that come with it" — hand-tighten per the filter maker's own directions (typically until the gasket touches the mounting surface, then turn an additional fraction of a turn as stated on the filter's box). | High confidence that no number exists / Tier 1 (owner's manual) | F-WP10-003 |
| Spark plug | 13 lbf·ft (18 N·m, 1.8 kgf·m) | Medium / Tier 3, corroborated by a Tier 4 source. Caution: the main source for this number files its page under a different generation of Fit than the 2007-2008 GD3 this book covers; it is used anyway because a second, independent source names a 2007 Fit specifically with the same number, and this engine family is not expected to change spark plug thread torque between adjacent generations — but that expectation was not independently confirmed against a document naming GD3 by name. | F-WP10-004 |
| Front brake caliper bracket (mounting bracket-to-knuckle) bolt | 80 lbf·ft (community-reported) | Low / Tier 4. Both corroborating sources actually discuss a Fit Sport, not confirmed identical on base trim. | F-WP10-005 |
| Rear brake caliper bracket bolt | **UNVERIFIED.** A figure of "41 ft-lbs" turned up repeatedly in general web searches, but every time it was traced back, it came from Honda Accord/Civic assembly-plant-specific threads, not the Fit. No Fit-specific source with this number was found. We could not confirm this for the 2008 Fit. Check your owner's manual, a Honda factory service manual, or ask a Honda parts counter or dealer before you rely on a number for this bolt. | Not applicable | F-WP10-006 |
| Caliper slide pin (guide bolt), front | Approximately 25-26 lbf·ft (community-reported; the front number varies slightly between sources found, so it is given as a range rather than a single picked value) | Low / Tier 4, single independent source for the front value naming this exact car | F-WP10-007 |
| Caliper slide pin (guide bolt), rear | Approximately 17 lbf·ft (community-reported) | Low / Tier 4 | F-WP10-007 |
| Strut top nut | **UNVERIFIED.** Only one online forum thread with specific numbers was found (front strut lower nut 25 lb-ft, upper nut 33 lb-ft, shock flange nuts 75 lb-ft, rear upper strut nut 22 lb-ft), and this project's rule requires two independent forum/community sources before using a number, not just one. A repair-reference excerpt confirms the general procedure and torque-spec diagrams exist for this car's front suspension, but its actual numbers were in an image that could not be read as text. We could not confirm this for the 2008 Fit. Check your owner's manual, a Honda factory service manual, or ask a Honda parts counter or dealer before you rely on a number for this nut. | Not applicable | F-WP10-008 |
| Battery hold-down | **UNVERIFIED.** The owner's manual describes cleaning, testing, and jump-starting the battery, but gives no clamp or bracket torque number. No source with a stated number for this car was found despite multiple searches. Small-car battery hold-down bolts are sometimes described generally as a "snug, do not overtighten" spec with no published torque number at all, but that general claim could not be confirmed as Honda's own guidance for this car either. We could not confirm this for the 2008 Fit. Check your owner's manual, a Honda factory service manual, or ask a Honda parts counter or dealer before you rely on a number for this bolt. | Not applicable | F-WP10-009-torque |

# Appendix D: Fuse and relay maps

Your 2008 Fit has three fuse boxes: one inside the car, and two in the engine compartment. This appendix lists every fuse position in all three boxes, with its amperage and the circuit it protects, straight from the owner's manual. It also tells you honestly where the manual does not give you a relay map, instead of making one up.

## Fuse box locations

| Fuse box | Location | How to open it | Fact ID |
|---|---|---|---|
| Interior (passenger compartment) fuse box | Behind the driver's coin tray, on the dashboard on the driver's side | Turn the coin tray's dial counterclockwise, then pull the tray toward you to remove it | F-WP21-002 |
| Primary under-hood fuse box | Engine compartment, driver's side | Push the tabs on the fuse box cover to release the lid | F-WP21-003 |
| Secondary fuse box | On the positive terminal of the battery, in the engine compartment | This box holds a single 80 A fuse that protects the battery circuit | F-WP21-004 |

<!-- F-WP21-001 -->
Your Fit has three fuse boxes in total, not two. Many cars have only one box under the dash and one under the hood. Your Fit adds a third, very small fuse box mounted directly on the battery's positive terminal.

## Fuse table — Interior (passenger compartment) fuse box

This box has 33 numbered positions. Six of them (No. 2, 5, 19, 20, 24, 30) are marked "Not Used" in the manual — they are empty slots with no fuse installed. <!-- F-WP21-011 -->

| Fuse # | Amperage | Circuit(s) protected |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | 10 A | Back Up Light |
| 2 | — | Not Used |
| 3 | 10 A | METER |
| 4 | 10 A | Turn Light |
| 5 | — | Not Used |
| 6 | 30 A | Front Wipers |
| 7 | 10 A | SRS |
| 8 | 7.5 A | Daytime Running Light (Canadian models) |
| 9 | 20 A | Rear Defogger |
| 10 | 7.5 A | HAC (Hill-start Assist Control) |
| 11 | 15 A | Fuel Pump |
| 12 | 10 A | Rear Wiper |
| 13 | 10 A | SRS |
| 14 | 15 A | IGP (Ignition Power) |
| 15 | 20 A | Left Rear Power Window |
| 16 | 20 A | Right Rear Power Window |
| 17 | 20 A | Right Front Power Window |
| 18 | 7.5 A | TPMS — Tire Pressure Monitoring System (if equipped) |
| 18 | 10 A | Daytime Running Light (Canadian models) — same slot, other equipment variant |
| 19 | — | Not Used |
| 20 | — | Not Used |
| 21 | 20 A | Fog Light (if equipped) |
| 22 | 10 A | Small Light (parking/side-marker lights) |
| 23 | 10 A | LAF (Linear Air/Fuel sensor) |
| 24 | — | Not Used |
| 25 | 7.5 A | ABS |
| 26 | 7.5 A | Radio |
| 27 | 15 A | ACC Socket (accessory power socket / cigar lighter) |
| 28 | 20 A | Power Door Lock (if equipped) |
| 29 | 20 A | Driver's Power Window |
| 30 | — | Not Used |
| 31 | 7.5 A | LAF (Linear Air/Fuel sensor) |
| 32 | 15 A | DBW (Drive-By-Wire / electronic throttle) |
| 33 | 15 A | Ignition Coil |

<!-- F-WP21-010, F-WP21-014 -->
Position No. 27 is your accessory power socket (cigar lighter) fuse — the one to check first if a phone charger or other plug-in accessory stops working. Position No. 18 is listed twice in the manual because it is an equipment-dependent slot: on a car with a Tire Pressure Monitoring System, it is a 7.5 A TPMS fuse; on a Canadian-market car, it is a 10 A daytime running light fuse instead. A US base-trim 2008 Fit without TPMS and without Canadian daytime running lights would not use either circuit at that position, so treat position 18 as reserved rather than assuming one fixed value.

## Fuse table — Primary under-hood fuse box

This box has 16 numbered positions (No. 1-16), and all 16 are occupied — there are no "Not Used" slots in this box. <!-- F-WP21-012 -->

| Fuse # | Amperage | Circuit(s) protected |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | 80 A | Battery |
| 2 | 60 A | EPS (Electric Power Steering) |
| 3 | 50 A | Ignition |
| 4 | 30 A | ABS |
| 5 | 40 A | Blower Relay (HVAC blower motor) |
| 6 | 40 A | Power Window |
| 7 | 30 A | HAC Option (Hill-start Assist Control, if equipped) |
| 8 | 10 A | Back Up |
| 9 | 30 A | Small Light (parking/side-marker lights) |
| 10 | 30 A | Cooling Fan |
| 11 | 30 A | Condenser Fan, MG Clutch (A/C, if equipped) |
| 12 | 20 A | Right Headlight |
| 13 | 20 A | Left Headlight |
| 14 | 10 A | Hazard (hazard warning flashers) |
| 15 | 30 A | ABS F/S |
| 16 | 15 A | Horn, Stop (stop/brake lights) |

## Fuse table — Secondary fuse box (on the battery)

<!-- F-WP21-013 -->
This box holds exactly one fuse.

| Fuse # | Amperage | Circuit(s) protected |
|---|---|---|
| 1 (only fuse) | 80 A | Battery |

## Checking and replacing fuses

<!-- F-WP21-007, F-WP21-008 -->
**Always** replace a blown fuse with one of the exact same amperage rating shown in the tables above. Replacing a fuse with a higher-rated one greatly increases the chance of damaging your electrical system. If you do not have the correct-rated fuse on hand, the manual says to install a lower-rated one instead of a higher one, and to put in the correctly-rated fuse as soon as you can — a fuse with a lower rating may blow again on its own, which by itself does not mean something else is wrong.

**Do not** keep replacing a fuse that blows again in a short time with another fuse of the same rating. The manual says this points to a serious electrical problem. Leave the blown fuse in that circuit and have the car checked by a qualified technician instead.

<!-- F-WP21-006 -->
To check whether a fuse is blown: (1) turn the ignition switch to LOCK (0) and make sure the headlights and other accessories are off; (2) remove the cover from the fuse box; (3) for the large fuses in the under-hood boxes, look through the top of the fuse at the wire inside; (4) for the smaller fuses in the primary under-hood box and all fuses in the interior box, pull each one out with the fuse puller; (5) look for a blown wire inside — a blown fuse's wire is broken or melted. Check the fuse listed for the affected device first, but check all the fuses before you decide a blown fuse is the cause.

<!-- F-WP21-005 -->
A fuse puller is stored in the interior fuse box. Use it on the smaller fuses in the interior box and the smaller fuses in the primary under-hood box. The larger under-hood fuses are checked by looking through the top of the fuse, and removed with a Phillips-head screwdriver instead of the puller.

<!-- F-WP21-009 -->
If you pull the radio fuse, or if the battery is disconnected, the audio system disables itself as an anti-theft feature. The next time you turn the radio on, "ENTER CODE" appears in the frequency display, and you must enter the audio system's 5-digit code using the radio's preset buttons to turn it back on. That code should be on a radio code card that came with the vehicle. You get 10 tries; after 10 failed attempts, you must leave the radio powered on for 1 hour before trying again. If the code card is lost, a dealer can look up the code using the audio system's serial number.

## Relay map

<!-- F-WP21-015 -->
We could not confirm this for the 2008 Fit. Check your owner's manual. The owner's manual documents fuses in detail, but it does not contain a separate relay location diagram, relay numbering, or a relay amperage/function chart anywhere in its "Taking Care of the Unexpected" chapter, its Specifications section, or its index. A physical relay box may still exist on the car, as it does on many similar cars, but no owner-facing map of it was found in this source.

# Appendix E: Tire and wheel fitment table

This appendix gathers the numbers you need to buy tires or wheels that actually fit your 2008 Fit base trim. A few terms are defined here since they show up together for the first time. **Lug pattern** (also called bolt pattern or PCD) describes how many wheel bolts there are and the diameter of the circle they sit on — a wheel with the wrong lug pattern will not bolt onto your car at all. **Center bore** is the size of the round hole in the middle of the wheel that centers it on the hub; a wheel with too large a center bore needs a plastic ring (a hub-centric ring) to sit properly, and one with too small a bore will not fit over the hub at all. **Offset** describes whether the wheel's mounting surface sits toward the inside or outside of the wheel compared to its centerline, measured in millimeters; changing offset changes how far the tire sticks out from (or tucks into) the fender. **Cold tire pressure** means the pressure measured before you have driven the car, while the tires are still at outside-air temperature — driving heats up the air inside a tire and raises the reading, so checking "cold" gives you a consistent, accurate number.

| Item | Value | Confidence / tier | Fact ID |
|---|---|---|---|
| Lug pattern (bolt pattern / PCD) | 4x100 mm (4 lugs, bolt circle 100 mm across) | Medium / Tier 3, single source, no independent second source found | F-WP10-013 |
| Center bore | 56.1 mm | Medium / Tier 3, single source, no independent second source found | F-WP10-014 |
| Factory wheel offset, base trim | ET45 (offset range given as 43-47 mm for the factory 5.5J x 14 wheel with the 175/65R14 tire) | Medium / Tier 3, single source; two other attempts to confirm this independently either failed to load or contradicted themselves and were not treated as a genuine competing claim | F-WP10-015 |
| Factory tire size, base trim | P175/65R14 81S | High / Tier 1 (owner's manual), matches Appendix A | F-WP10-011 |
| Factory wheel size, base trim | 14 in x 5.5J (14 x 5 1/2JJ), steel wheel with full wheel covers | High / Tier 1 (owner's manual), matches Appendix A | F-WP10-012 |
| Cold tire pressure, base trim, front and rear | 32 psi (220 kPa, 2.2 kgf/cm²) — the same number front and rear | High / Tier 1 (owner's manual) | F-WP10-010 |
| Cold tire pressure, compact spare tire | 60 psi (420 kPa, 4.2 kgf/cm²) | High / Tier 1 (owner's manual) | F-WP10-010 |
| Largest tire that fits without rubbing (community-reported) | 195/50R15, on a 15x6.5 or 15x7 wheel with a +40 to +42 mm offset, is the size two independent online communities agree "fits without rubbing" at stock ride height. A wider 205/45R16 (on a 16x7 wheel) is reported to work only with a high offset (+42 mm minimum), and even then some rubbing was reported at the rear, especially with passengers in the back seat — treat 205/45R16 as a borderline size, not a clean fit. One source also suggested a narrower 195/45R16 as a safer 16-inch alternative. | Low / Tier 4, two independent community sources, both discussing this generation of Fit generally rather than confirming base trim specifically against Fit Sport | F-WP10-016 |

Note on the Fit Sport's numbers, for comparison only: the 2008 Fit Sport uses a different factory tire size, P195/55R15 84H, and a different factory wheel, 15 x 6JJ — both larger than the base trim's numbers above. These Sport-trim numbers are not this book's subject car but are included here so you do not confuse a Sport parts listing for a base-trim one.

# Appendix F: Part numbers for common maintenance items

This appendix lists the Honda genuine part numbers, sizes, and specifications for the maintenance items you are most likely to buy for your 2008 Fit base trim: filters, spark plugs, the battery, wiper blades, and light bulbs. A **part number** is the unique code a manufacturer or parts store uses to identify one exact part, so you can order the correct one instead of guessing from a picture. A **trade number** is a standardized code (like "7443" or "T10") that many bulb makers use for the same size and shape of light bulb, so the same trade number generally works across different car brands. Where this project's research could not find a real part number, that is written out honestly below rather than left blank or guessed — do not buy a part based on a made-up number; ask a Honda parts counter or dealer, or bring your old part in for a match.

## Filters

| Item | Part number | Notes | Fact ID |
|---|---|---|---|
| Engine air filter | Honda 17220-PWA-505 | Replaces older part number 17220-PWA-J10. Fitment confirmed for 2007-2008 Fit. | F-WP09-040 |
| Cabin (dust and pollen) filter | Honda 80291-SAA-J01 | Later superseded by part 80291-SAA-316. Fitment confirmed for 2007-2008 Fit. Present on models with air conditioning. | F-WP09-042 |
| Oil filter | **UNVERIFIED.** No Tier 1 or Tier 2 source with a confirmed part number for this engine's oil filter was found during this project's research. Do not guess a number — ask your parts counter for "2008 Honda Fit 1.5L oil filter," or bring your old filter in for a match. | Not applicable | P-WP11-1 |

## Spark plugs

| Item | Part number | Notes | Fact ID |
|---|---|---|---|
| Spark plug (NGK brand) | NGK IZFR6K-13 | Iridium-type plug per retailer listings; the owner's manual's own specifications table gives the bare part number without the word "iridium." | F-WP09-036 |
| Spark plug (Denso brand) | DENSO SKJ20DR-M13 | Genuine-equivalent to the NGK part above; either one is correct for this engine. | F-WP09-036 |
| Spark plug gap | 1.2-1.3 mm (0.042-0.051 in), standard/new | Confirm against the physical plug's own box or stamped specification before use; a second, independent web summary gave slightly different, overlapping numbers. | F-WP09-037 |

## Battery

| Item | Value | Notes | Fact ID |
|---|---|---|---|
| Battery group size | 151R | This is the original-equipment size for the 2008 Fit. Group 51R is a commonly reported upgrade with more cold-cranking amps, but it is a noticeably tighter physical fit, and some owners report needing to modify the battery tray to use it. This number was not independently confirmed by a Honda document during this research pass, so treat it as medium confidence rather than certain. | F-WP09-046 |

## Wiper blades

| Item | Size | Fact ID |
|---|---|---|
| Front, driver's side | 24 in | F-WP09-047 |
| Front, passenger's side | 16 in | F-WP09-047 |
| Rear (hatchback wiper) | 14 in | F-WP09-047 |

Note: the 2009-2014 Fit (a different car, chassis GE8, covered in this project's rule D8) uses different wiper sizes — 28 in / 14 in / 14 in — which confirms the sizes above are genuinely specific to your 2007-2008 first-generation Fit and not a general "Fit" number that also fits the later car.

| Rear brake shoes, base trim (drum brakes) | **UNVERIFIED.** No Tier 1 or Tier 2 source with a confirmed part number was found. Ask a Honda parts counter, or bring your old shoes in for a match. | P-WP11-8 |
| Replacement wheel lug nuts | **UNVERIFIED.** No confirmed part number found. Ask a Honda parts counter for the correct lug nut if one is damaged or lost. | P-WP11-5 |

All other F-WP09-### IDs (Maintenance Minder mechanics, fluid types and capacities, valve clearance, timing chain, owner's checks): not part-number facts for this appendix; used in Appendix B and various chapters instead.
