# Appendix G: Glossary

This glossary defines every technical term used in this book, in one sentence each, at a level a new driver can follow. Terms are alphabetized. Where the dashboard or the owner's manual uses an abbreviation, such as "MIL" or "TPMS," the full name is given first.

**ABS (Anti-lock Brake System)**: A safety system that pumps the brakes very fast on its own so your wheels don't lock up and skid when you brake hard.

**Accessory position**: The ignition key position that powers things like the radio and the power outlet without starting the engine.

**Aftermarket**: A part or accessory made by a company other than Honda, instead of by the car's original maker.

**AKI (octane rating)**: The number on a gas pump's label that tells you how well that fuel resists engine knocking; the 2008 Fit needs at least 87 AKI.

**Alignment**: The process of adjusting a car's wheel angles — camber, caster, and toe — so it drives straight, handles predictably, and wears its tires evenly.

**Alignment rack**: A specialized shop machine that measures a car's wheel angles precisely and then adjusts them.

**Alloy wheels**: Wheels made from a lightweight metal blend instead of plain steel; the 2008 Fit's base trim has steel wheels with covers instead.

**Aspect ratio**: The tire's sidewall height, given as a percentage of the tire's width; it is the middle number in a tire size like 195/50R15.

**Auto-down window**: A power window feature where one firm push on the switch lowers the whole window by itself, without you holding the switch down.

**Autocross**: A timed driving event, usually held in a large parking lot or empty lot, where drivers race one at a time against the clock through a course marked out with cones.

**Automatic transmission**: A transmission that changes gears for you on its own, so there's no clutch pedal to press.

**Auxiliary (aux) jack**: A small audio plug on some car stereos that lets you plug in an outside music player; the US base 2008 Fit does not have one.

**Axle-back exhaust**: A replacement exhaust part that swaps out only the last section, from about the rear axle back to the tailpipe, usually just to change the sound.

**Bill of sale**: The dealer's or private seller's own sales contract for a car, showing the price, taxes, and fees.

**Bleeder screw**: A small screw at each brake that a wrench opens to push old fluid and trapped air out of the brake lines.

**Bolt pattern (lug pattern)**: The number and spacing of the bolt holes on a wheel, which must match the studs on the car for the wheel to bolt on correctly.

**Boost**: Extra air pressure that a turbocharger or supercharger pushes into the engine, above normal air pressure, to help the engine make more power.

**Bore and stroke**: Two engine measurements — how wide each cylinder is (bore) and how far the piston travels inside it (stroke).

**Brake fluid**: The hydraulic fluid that transfers the force from a driver's foot on the pedal to the pads or shoes at each wheel.

**Brake shoes**: The curved parts inside a drum brake that press outward against the inside of the drum to slow the wheel.

**Brake-parts cleaner**: A solvent spray made for cleaning brake dust off brake parts during a pad or drum brake job.

**Break-in period**: The first several hundred miles of driving a new car, when the maker asks you to drive gently so engine parts can settle in properly.

**Breaker bar**: A plain, longer handle with no ratcheting action, used with a socket when a bolt is stuck and needs extra force.

**Buyers Guide**: A federally required window sticker on a used car at a dealer's lot that discloses the car's warranty status and major systems.

**C-clamp**: A tool that pushes a brake caliper's piston back into its housing to make room for new brake pads.

**Cabin air filter**: A filter, also called the dust and pollen filter, that cleans the air coming through a car's heater and air conditioner vents.

**Caliper**: A clamp that squeezes flat brake pads against both sides of a disc brake's rotor to slow the wheel.

**Camber**: How much a tire tilts inward or outward at the top when you look at the car from the front.

**Campaign number**: The specific ID number NHTSA assigns to one safety recall, used to look that exact recall up online.

**Canard**: A small, wing-shaped piece bolted to the front bumper that is meant to press the front of a fast-moving car down slightly.

**CARB (California Air Resources Board)**: The California state agency that adds its own emissions rules on top of federal ones, mainly for cars titled or registered in California.

**CARB EO number**: A number the California Air Resources Board gives to an aftermarket part after testing, showing that part can legally be used on a car driven on public roads in California.

**Cargo volume**: How much space is available for luggage or gear, usually measured in cubic feet.

**Caster**: The tilt of the steering axis when viewed from the side of the car, which affects how the steering wheel returns to center and how stable the car feels driving straight.

**Cat-back exhaust**: A replacement exhaust system that starts right after the catalytic converter and runs back to the tailpipe, usually installed to change the exhaust sound or let gases flow out easier.

**Catalytic converter**: A part in the exhaust system that cleans harmful gases out of the exhaust before they leave the car.

**Center bore**: The hole in the middle of a wheel that fits around a matching part on the car's hub to help keep the wheel centered.

**Certification label**: A sticker on the driver's doorjamb that lists your VIN, along with the recommended tire sizes and tire pressures.

**Charging system**: The parts, mainly the alternator, that keep the car battery topped up while the engine is running.

**Chassis code**: A short factory code, like GD3, that identifies exactly which body and underlying platform a specific car was built on.

**Check Fuel Cap message**: A message that appears on the dashboard display when the gas cap is loose or missing.

**Childproof lock**: A small lever on a rear door that stops that door from being opened using the inside handle.

**Clean Air Act**: The federal law that sets emissions rules for every car sold in the United States and makes it illegal to remove or disable a car's emissions-control devices.

**Clutch (aftermarket)**: A stronger replacement clutch, built to grip harder than the factory clutch so it can handle more engine power without slipping.

**Coilover**: A shock absorber and spring built together as one adjustable unit, often letting an owner change the car's ride height and how stiff it feels.

**Cold air intake**: A replacement air-intake system meant to pull in cooler outside air instead of the warmer air already inside the engine bay, which can add a small amount of power.

**Combination switch**: The single lever on the steering column that also controls the turn signals and headlight high/low beams.

**Compact spare tire**: A small, lightweight temporary tire (sometimes called a "donut") meant only for short trips at reduced speed until the real tire is fixed.

**Complaint (NHTSA)**: A report a vehicle owner files with the government describing a problem with their car, whether or not it turns into a recall.

**Compression ratio**: A number describing how much an engine squeezes the air-fuel mixture before it ignites it.

**Consumer notice**: The letter a manufacturer mails to a car's registered owner explaining a recall and what to do about it.

**Cool-down lap**: One slow, gentle lap driven at the end of a track session so the brakes, engine, and transmission cool down gradually instead of being parked while still very hot.

**Cranks but won't start**: A no-start problem where the starter spins the engine at a normal speed, but the engine never fires up and keeps running on its own.

**Cruise control**: A feature that holds the car at a steady speed on its own, so the driver doesn't need to keep a foot on the gas; not included on the 2008 Fit's base trim.

**Curb weight**: How much the empty car weighs with a full tank of gas but no passengers or cargo.

**D3 (gear position)**: An automatic-transmission setting that works like Drive but keeps the transmission from shifting into 4th or 5th gear, useful for engine braking downhill.

**Damper**: The shock absorber inside a coilover, which controls how quickly the spring compresses and rebounds.

**Daytime Running Lights (DRL)**: Lights that turn on by themselves whenever the engine is running, making the car easier to see in daylight; not fitted on the US 2008 Fit.

**Defeat device**: An aftermarket part built mainly to bypass, defeat, or disable a device that controls a car's exhaust emissions, which is illegal to manufacture, sell, or install.

**Destination charge**: A flat fee added to a new car's price to cover shipping it from the factory to the dealer.

**Diagnostic fee**: The charge a repair shop bills for the time it spends finding what is wrong with a car, before any repair is agreed to.

**Diagnostic Trouble Code (DTC)**: A short code the car's computer stores when it detects a problem, which a mechanic reads with a scan tool.

**Differential**: The gearbox part that splits engine power between a car's drive wheels so they can spin at different speeds through a turn.

**Directional tire**: A tire with a tread pattern designed to roll only one way, marked with an arrow on its sidewall.

**Disc brake**: A brake that uses a flat metal disc, called a rotor, spinning with the wheel, squeezed by a caliper to slow it down.

**Displacement**: The total volume swept by all of an engine's cylinders, usually given in liters, which roughly shows how big the engine is.

**Doorjamb**: The frame area you see when a door is open; the certification label and VIN sticker are usually placed there.

**DOT brake fluid rating**: A number (like DOT 3, DOT 4, or DOT 5.1) that a federal safety standard uses to set a brake fluid's minimum boiling point; a higher DOT number does not always mean a higher boiling point for every specific product.

**Downpipe (test pipe)**: The section of exhaust pipe right after the turbocharger or catalytic converter; a "test pipe" is a downpipe with the catalytic converter removed, usually meant only for track use.

**Drum brake**: A brake in which a hollow metal drum spins with the wheel while curved brake shoes press outward against the inside of the drum to slow it down.

**Dry boiling point**: The boiling point of fresh, unused brake fluid.

**Dyno (dynamometer)**: A machine that measures how much power an engine or a car's wheels actually produce.

**ECU (Engine Control Unit)**: The car's onboard computer that decides things like how much fuel to add and when to fire the spark plugs, based on readings from its sensors.

**Electric Power Steering (EPS)**: A power-steering system that uses an electric motor, instead of hydraulic fluid pressure, to help you turn the steering wheel.

**Electrolyte**: The liquid inside a car battery that lets it store and release electricity; in very cold weather it can thicken or freeze.

**Electronic Brake Distribution (EBD)**: A system that automatically balances how much braking force goes to the front wheels versus the rear wheels.

**Emissions control system**: The group of parts that work together to reduce pollution coming out of the engine and exhaust.

**Endorsement (insurance)**: An add-on to an existing insurance policy, sometimes called "custom parts and equipment coverage," that covers modified parts a factory policy would not.

**Engine air filter**: A filter that sits in the engine bay and cleans the air the engine breathes before that air reaches the fuel injection system.

**Engine swap**: Removing a car's original engine and installing a different one, usually to get more power or better reliability.

**EPA (Environmental Protection Agency)**: The US government agency that tests and publishes the official fuel-economy (MPG) numbers for new cars.

**Fast idle**: Running an engine a little faster than normal idle speed, often done to a booster vehicle's engine during a jump start.

**FIA (Fédération Internationale de l'Automobile)**: An international motorsports organization whose safety standards, such as harness and helmet certifications, are often required at racing and track events.

**Final drive ratio**: An extra gear ratio built into the transmission that affects how the engine's speed relates to the wheels' speed in every gear.

**Flat-tip (flathead) screwdriver**: A screwdriver with a flat blade tip, used along with a Phillips screwdriver for the clips and screws on things like an air filter housing.

**Flat-tow**: Towing a car with all four wheels rolling on the ground behind another vehicle; the 2008 Fit's owner's manual does not support this method for either transmission.

**Flatbed towing**: Towing a car by loading all four wheels onto the back of a truck, so none of the car's wheels touch the road.

**Floor jack**: A larger tool on wheels, rated to hold much more weight than a trunk's scissor jack, used to lift a car for jack-stand work in the garage.

**Flywheel**: A heavy spinning disc attached to the engine that smooths out its power pulses; a lighter aftermarket flywheel lets the engine change speed faster.

**Fog lights**: Extra, low-mounted lights that help light the road edge in fog; standard on the Fit Sport trim only, not on base.

**Friction point**: The spot while slowly releasing a clutch pedal where the engine and transmission start to connect, and the car begins to move.

**Fuel vapor recovery system**: A system that catches gasoline vapor at the pump instead of letting it escape into the air; a fault in it can make the pump nozzle click off early and repeatedly.

**Gear ratio**: A number describing how many times the engine turns for every one turn of the wheels in a given gear; smaller numbers generally mean higher top speed, larger numbers mean more pulling power.

**Ground clearance**: The distance between the lowest point of the car's underside and the ground.

**Ground point ("the stay")**: A metal point in the engine bay, away from the battery itself, used as the final connection spot when jump-starting a car to reduce spark risk.

**H Street (HS)**: An entry-level SCCA autocross class, meant for cars that are still mostly stock, with only limited changes like tires and shocks allowed.

**Harness (racing harness)**: A multi-point seat belt system, with straps over both shoulders and between the legs, used in place of a factory 3-point belt for extra crash protection.

**Harness bar**: A bar bolted across the car behind the front seats, used to anchor a racing harness's shoulder straps.

**Hazard warning lights**: All four turn signals flashing together at once, used when parked in a dangerous spot or when a car is disabled.

**Head restraint**: The padded part above a seatback that supports your head and helps prevent whiplash in a crash; often just called a headrest.

**Header**: A set of pipes that connects each engine cylinder to the rest of the exhaust system; a performance header is shaped to let exhaust gases flow out more easily than the factory manifold does.

**High beam**: The brighter, longer-reaching headlight setting meant for dark roads with no oncoming traffic nearby.

**Hood release**: The handle inside the car, usually under the dashboard, that pops the hood partway open.

**Horsepower**: A measurement of how much power an engine can produce.

**HPDE (High Performance Driving Event)**: A track day where drivers practice driving skills at speed with instruction, rather than racing wheel-to-wheel against other cars.

**i-VTEC / VTEC**: Honda's system for adjusting how and when engine valves open, which helps the engine make good power and stay efficient across a range of speeds.

**IIHS (Insurance Institute for Highway Safety)**: An independent group, funded by insurance companies, that crash-tests vehicles and rates how well they protect the people inside.

**Immobilizer**: An anti-theft system that keeps the engine from starting unless it recognizes the electronic chip built into your key.

**Intercooler**: A small radiator-like part that cools down air after a turbocharger or supercharger has heated and pressurized it, before that air enters the engine.

**Investigation (NHTSA)**: A formal government review into a possible safety defect, which may or may not lead to an official recall.

**Jack stands**: Metal supports slid under a lifted car so its weight rests on solid steel instead of on the jack itself.

**Jacking point**: A specific spot marked on the underside of a car's body where it is safe to place a jack.

**Jump start**: Starting a car with a dead battery by temporarily connecting jumper cables to a charged battery in another vehicle or a portable booster pack.

**Keyless entry**: A remote fob that lets you lock or unlock the doors by pressing a button instead of using the physical key; not included on the base 2008 US Fit.

**Long Mode**: One of the Fit's Magic Seat cargo layouts, used for sliding long items like lumber or a surfboard through to the front of the car.

**Low beam**: The normal, everyday headlight setting used whenever other traffic is nearby.

**Lug wrench**: A socket that fits a car's wheel nuts, paired with a breaker bar, used any time a wheel is taken off.

**Magic Seat**: Honda's name for the Fit's flexible 60/40-split rear seat, which can fold flat or flip up into several different cargo and passenger layouts.

**Maintenance Minder**: A dashboard system that tracks the car's condition over time and tells you, with a wrench-shaped light and a code, when a specific service is due.

**Malfunction Indicator Lamp (MIL)**: The dashboard light — often called the "check engine light" — that comes on when the emissions-control system detects a problem.

**Milliamp (mA)**: A small unit of electrical current, used to describe how much power a parked car's electrical system draws with everything off.

**MMT**: A manganese-based fuel additive that Honda recommends avoiding where possible because it can cause the check-engine light to come on.

**Modding**: Changing a car from how the factory built it, usually to improve how it handles, stops, sounds, or looks.

**Moderate overlap front test**: A crash test where only part of the front of the car strikes a barrier, similar to many real-world head-on crashes that aren't perfectly centered.

**Modified category (SCCA)**: A group of SCCA autocross classes (labeled A through F Modified) built for cars taken far past a factory street car, closer to a dedicated race car; a roll bar or cage is mandatory in these classes.

**Motor mount**: A rubber-and-metal part that holds the engine and transmission in place and absorbs vibration; a worn one can cause a clunk when shifting gears.

**MPG (miles per gallon)**: A measurement of how far a car can travel using one gallon of fuel.

**MSRP (Manufacturer's Suggested Retail Price)**: The price a car's maker suggests dealers charge, before tax, fees, and any negotiating.

**MTBE**: A fuel additive some gasoline blends contain; the Fit's manual allows fuel with up to 15% MTBE by volume.

**NHTSA (National Highway Traffic Safety Administration)**: The US government agency that manages vehicle recalls, safety ratings, and owner complaint records.

**Occupant Detection System (ODS)**: Sensors that check the front passenger seat for a person, a child seat, or nothing at all, to help decide whether the airbag should go off.

**Occupant Position Detection System (OPDS)**: Sensors that check exactly where a front passenger is sitting, so the airbag can be adjusted or turned off if someone is sitting too close to it.

**Odometer**: The gauge that shows the total distance a car has been driven over its whole life.

**Odometer disclosure statement**: The mileage reading at the time of a car's sale, signed by the seller, which federal law requires for most cars under 20 model years old.

**OEM (Original Equipment Manufacturer) part**: A replacement part made by, or to the same standard as, the part the car originally came with from the factory.

**Oil filter wrench**: A tool shaped to grip the round oil filter so it can be twisted loose.

**Overlap crash test**: A type of crash test that hits only part of a vehicle's front end, rather than the whole width, to better match many real-world crashes.

**Paddle shifters**: Levers mounted behind the steering wheel that let a driver manually bump an automatic transmission up or down a gear; found on the Fit Sport's automatic only, not on base.

**Parasitic draw**: A small amount of electrical current a parked, switched-off car still uses; too much of it drains the battery even when the car is left alone.

**Parking brake**: A separate brake, set by hand, that holds a parked car in place; also called an emergency brake.

**Piggyback (tuning device)**: A small aftermarket box that changes the signals going to the factory ECU without replacing it, often used to adjust the fuel mixture.

**Powertrain**: The engine, transmission, and connected parts that work together to move the car.

**Pre-purchase inspection (PPI)**: A check of a used car before you buy it, done by you or by a paid independent mechanic, covering the body, tires and brakes, engine bay and underside, interior and electronics, and a test drive.

**Prepared category (SCCA)**: An SCCA autocross category for cars modified well past Street Prepared; an open car (no factory roof) in this category must have a roll bar.

**Ratchet**: A handle that turns a socket over a bolt head in one direction without lifting off between turns.

**Readiness codes**: Internal checks the emissions computer runs to confirm all its own monitoring systems have finished testing; some states check these during an emissions test.

**Recall (safety recall)**: A free, manufacturer-run repair program ordered after a safety-related defect is found in a group of vehicles.

**Redline**: The highest engine speed considered safe to reach; going past it risks damaging the engine.

**Refresh Mode**: A Magic Seat layout where the front seats recline flat to rest, usable only while the car is parked.

**Registration**: The state's record that a car is legally allowed on the road.

**Reverse lockout**: A mechanism in a manual transmission that stops the driver from shifting straight from the highest gear into reverse without pausing.

**Ride height**: How high the bottom of the car sits above the ground; lowering it usually helps handling but reduces ground clearance.

**Roll bar**: A metal bar or hoop mounted behind the seats to help keep the roof from collapsing onto the people inside during a rollover crash.

**Roll cage**: A full frame of metal tubing built inside a car to protect the people inside during a serious crash; more extensive than a simple roll bar.

**Rollover rating**: A safety score estimating how likely a specific vehicle is to roll over in a single-vehicle crash.

**Rotor**: The flat metal disc that spins with the wheel in a disc brake and is squeezed by the caliper's pads to slow the car.

**RPM (revolutions per minute)**: How fast the engine is spinning, shown on the tachometer.

**Rub (tire rub)**: When a tire touches the fender, a suspension part, or a body panel while turning or going over a bump.

**SAE Class S**: A size and type classification for tire chains; the Fit's manual only allows this cable-type chain, and only on the front tires.

**Salt-belt states**: A group of US states where winter road salt is heavily used, which some recalls (like rust or corrosion-related ones) apply to instead of the whole country.

**Security system**: An anti-theft alarm system, standard on the Fit Sport trim only, not on the 2008 US base Fit.

**Self-adjuster (adjuster)**: A small threaded part inside a drum brake that takes up slack as the brake shoes wear down, keeping the brake working correctly without manual adjustment.

**SFI (SFI Foundation)**: A US organization that tests and certifies motorsports safety equipment, such as racing harnesses and helmets, to a set standard.

**Shift interlock (shift lock)**: A safety mechanism that keeps an automatic transmission's shift lever locked in Park unless the brake pedal is pressed.

**Shift lever**: The lever a driver moves to choose a gear, in either a manual or an automatic transmission.

**Side airbag**: An airbag that deploys out of the seat or door area to protect against a side-impact crash.

**Splitter**: A flat panel added to the front or sides of a car's bumper, meant to help manage airflow underneath the car.

**Spoiler**: A wing-shaped piece mounted on the trunk or hatch; on a light, slower car this is mostly for looks, though on faster cars it can help push the rear tires down.

**Spring (suspension)**: The coiled metal part that holds a car's weight up and compresses when a wheel hits a bump.

**Spring rate**: A number describing how stiff a spring is, based on how much force it takes to compress it a set distance; a higher number means a stiffer, firmer ride.

**SRS (Supplemental Restraint System)**: The umbrella term for a car's airbags and their related crash sensors, working alongside the seat belts.

**Stage 0**: The step before any performance modification, meaning bringing a car's existing maintenance — fluids, tires, and brakes — up to date before spending money on power, handling, or looks.

**Staggered (fitment)**: A wheel-and-tire setup where the front and rear tires are different sizes on purpose.

**Stainless brake lines**: Brake hoses wrapped in a woven stainless-steel sleeve instead of plain rubber, which resist expanding under hard, repeated braking.

**Standalone engine management**: A complete aftermarket computer that fully replaces the factory ECU, instead of working alongside it.

**Street Modified**: An SCCA autocross class, one step past Street Prepared, that allows major aerodynamic parts and other extensive changes on cars still built from a factory street car.

**Street Prepared**: An SCCA autocross class that allows bigger changes than Street Touring, such as race-only tires, engine tuning, and body modifications, and is no longer necessarily street-legal.

**Street Touring**: An SCCA autocross class that allows more modifications than Street classes, like aftermarket suspension, brakes, and wheels, while the car must still stay street-legal.

**Supercharger**: A device, driven by a belt from the engine, that forces extra air into the engine to help it make more power.

**Sway bar**: A suspension part connecting the left and right wheels that reduces how much the car's body leans in turns.

**Synchronized transmission**: A manual transmission design that lets gears mesh smoothly during a normal shift, without extra tricks like a double-clutch.

**Tachometer**: The gauge that shows how fast the engine is spinning, in RPM.

**Takata airbag inflator**: A now-recalled type of airbag part, made by the Takata company, that can explode and send metal fragments into the car after long exposure to heat and humidity.

**Tall Mode**: A Magic Seat layout where the rear seat cushions flip up, opening floor space for tall items.

**Tech inspection**: A safety check of a car, done before a track day or race, that looks at things like tires, brakes, fluids, and seat belts.

**Technical Service Bulletin (TSB)**: A repair instruction a manufacturer sends to its own dealers about a known problem; it is not a safety recall and is not always a free repair.

**Time Trial**: A track event where each car is timed individually on an open track, instead of racing wheel-to-wheel against other cars at the same time.

**Tire chain**: A traction device wrapped around a tire to improve grip on snow or ice.

**Title (vehicle title)**: The legal document that proves who owns a car; it should never be left in the glovebox.

**Toe**: How much a car's tires point inward or outward, viewed from above, compared to pointing straight ahead.

**Torque**: A twisting force; for a car this means both the pulling power an engine produces and the exact tightness a bolt or nut must be tightened to.

**Torque wrench**: A tool that tells you exactly how hard you are tightening a bolt, by feel or by a dial, measured in pound-feet or newton-meters.

**Torsion beam**: A simple, solid rear suspension design, used on the Fit, where a single beam connects the two rear wheels instead of letting them move fully independently.

**TPMS (Tire Pressure Monitoring System)**: A system that watches tire pressure and warns the driver on the dashboard if a tire gets significantly low.

**Trail braking**: Easing off the brake pedal gradually while turning into a corner, instead of releasing the brake fully before starting to turn.

**Tread wear indicator**: A raised bar built into a tire's grooves that becomes visible once the tire is worn down and needs replacing.

**Trim level**: A specific version of a car model, such as "base" or "Sport," that comes with its own defined set of standard features.

**Trip meter**: A mileage counter, separate from the odometer, that a driver can reset to track the distance of a single trip.

**Turbocharger**: A device powered by the engine's own exhaust gases that forces extra air into the engine to help it make more power.

**Unsprung weight**: The mass of a car's wheels and tires that the springs do not support, since it sits below them and moves with the road surface.

**Utility Mode**: A Magic Seat layout where the rear seatback(s) fold down flat to make a larger, flat cargo floor.

**VDS (Vehicle Descriptor Section)**: The middle part of a VIN that encodes details like the car's platform, engine, and body type.

**VIN (Vehicle Identification Number)**: A unique 17-character code stamped on every car that identifies its maker, features, and production details.

**Warranty extension**: A manufacturer's decision to cover a specific repair for longer than the original warranty period, usually issued after a known problem turns up.

**Wet boiling point**: The boiling point of brake fluid after it has absorbed a set amount of water, closer to how the fluid behaves after real-world use.

**Wheel chocks**: Wedges placed against a tire that stays on the ground so the car cannot roll while another corner is lifted.

**Wheel nut**: One of the nuts that holds a wheel onto the car; these must be tightened to an exact torque spec, not just "as tight as possible" (also commonly called a lug nut).

**Wheel offset**: How far a wheel's mounting surface sits from the exact center-line of the wheel, which affects how far the tire sticks out from or tucks under the car.

**Wheel-lift towing**: Towing a car by lifting only its front (or rear) wheels off the ground on a tow truck's frame, while the other wheels stay on the road.

**Wheelbase**: The distance between the centers of the front and rear wheels.

**Whp (wheel horsepower)**: Horsepower measured at the wheels, after some power is lost through the drivetrain, instead of measured at the engine itself.

**Wing bolt**: A bolt with flat, wing-shaped tabs that can be loosened or tightened by hand; used to hold the 2008 Fit's spare tire in its well.

**WMI (World Manufacturer Identifier)**: The first three characters of a VIN, which identify the company and country that built the vehicle.

**Won't crank**: A no-start problem where turning the key to START produces nothing, or only a click or a slow grinding sound, because the starter is not turning the engine over at a normal speed.

**Zone A/B/C recall**: A way NHTSA and automakers roll out some large recalls in stages by region (zone) instead of notifying every owner nationwide at once, often based on local climate or humidity risk.

<!-- Formatted from facts/WP-22-glossary.md, no new content added except during R1 review. 165 terms, alphabetized. No definitions were changed in meaning; only light copy-editing for consistent formatting. Review pass added 3 terms (Street Modified, Modified category (SCCA), Prepared category (SCCA)) that were used undefined in Chapters 56-57; definitions paraphrase F-WP19-004's own summary of these SCCA classes, already cited in those chapters. R2 completeness pass (glossary-completeness check against all 62 chapters) added 50 more terms that appeared bolded in the chapter text but had no glossary entry (see /home/user/hyperwave-community/research/honda_fit_2008_manual/fit-book/review/glossary_completeness.md for the full list and method); no existing definition's meaning was changed. 215 terms total, alphabetized. -->

# Appendix H: Resources

This appendix lists places you can actually go to look something up about your 2008 Fit: official Honda pages, government safety tools, owner communities, parts and fitment lookups, and track/autocross rulebooks. Each entry gives the resource's name, its URL, and one line on what it is good for.

## Official Honda

**Honda Owners site** — https://owners.honda.com/vehicle-information/information/2008/Fit/manuals
Official Honda site to download your 2008 Fit's owner's manual and look up other vehicle-specific documents.

**Helm Inc. — factory service manual** — https://www.helminc.com/helm/product2.asp?class_2=AHM&Sku=61SAA01&itemtype=N
The official Honda factory service manual distributor; buy the real 2007-2008 Fit (KA) shop manual here if you want the same repair procedures Honda dealers use (paid).

**Honda News — 2008 Fit specifications** — https://hondanews.com/en-US/releases/release-2911fc22bc993ed9e80deb004c34be5b-2008-honda-fit-specifications
Honda's own archived press release with the full factory spec sheet (engine, dimensions, weights, gear ratios) for the 2008 Fit and Fit Sport.

**hfitinfo.com** — https://www.hfitinfo.com/
A free, page-by-page mirror of the full Honda Fit 2001-2008 (first-generation) owner's manual — useful if you don't want to deal with Honda's own PDF download flow.

**ManualsLib — 2008 Fit Owner's Manual** — https://www.manualslib.com/manual/524850/Honda-2008-Fit.html
A free page-image/OCR viewer of the actual Honda-published 2008 Fit owner's manual, handy for searching a specific page or section.

## Government / Safety

**NHTSA vehicle lookup (recalls)** — https://www.nhtsa.gov/vehicle/2008/HONDA/FIT
Look up every official safety recall ever issued for your exact car by VIN or by year/make/model — always check this before assuming your car has had all its recall repairs done.

**NHTSA vehicle lookup (complaints and investigations)** — https://www.nhtsa.gov/vehicle/2008/HONDA/FIT
The same NHTSA tool also shows owner-submitted complaints and any formal government investigations for the 2008 Fit, which can reveal problems before they become a recall.

**IIHS ratings — 2008 Honda Fit** — https://www.iihs.org/ratings/vehicle/honda/fit-4-door-wagon/2008
Independent, insurance-industry-funded crash test ratings and results for the 2008 Fit 4-door wagon.

**fueleconomy.gov — 2008 Honda Fit** — https://www.fueleconomy.gov/feg/Find.do?action=sbs&id=24920
The official US government fuel-economy page for the 2008 Fit, with EPA city/highway/combined MPG for both transmissions.

## Community forums

**FitFreak.net — 1st Generation (GD 01-08) forum** — https://www.fitfreak.net/forums/1st-generation-gd-01-08/
The largest dedicated first-generation Fit owner community; go here for real-world troubleshooting threads, "does anyone else have this problem" discussions, and DIY write-ups specific to the GD3 chassis. This is community-reported information only — always double-check anything safety-related against your own owner's manual.

**Honda-Tech.com — Honda Fit forum** — https://honda-tech.com/forums/honda-fit-73/
A second large Honda-focused forum with its own active Fit section, useful as a second opinion when a FitFreak thread doesn't have enough replies to be confident in.

## Parts and fitment

**HondaPartsNow.com** — https://www.hondapartsnow.com/2008-honda-fit--5dr_base-ka_5at-parts.html
An authorized Honda dealer parts site where you can look up genuine part numbers and diagrams for your exact 2008 Fit trim and transmission.

**Wheel-Size.com — Honda Fit GD [2001-2008]** — https://www.wheel-size.com/size/honda/fit/gd-2001-2008/
A quick-reference page for factory and compatible wheel/tire sizes, bolt pattern, and offset for the first-generation Fit — useful before buying replacement wheels or tires.

**Haynes Repair Manual 42030 (Honda Fit 07-13)** — https://us.haynes.com/products/honda-fit-07-13-haynes-repair-manual
A widely available, affordable aftermarket repair manual covering the 2007-2013 Fit; it spans both the GD3 (2007-2008) and GE8 (2009-2013) generations, so check that any given page applies to your model year before following it (paid).

**workshop-manuals.com — Fit L4-1.5L fluid/spark plug/valve specs** — see the book's source records for the exact reachable URL
A free reference page listing fluid capacities, spark plug part numbers, and valve clearance specs for the Fit's 1.5L engine — handy for a quick maintenance lookup without buying a full service manual.

**Boslla — 2007-2018 Honda Fit bulb size guide** — https://www.boslla.com/2007-2018-honda-fit-base-dx-ex-lx-sport-bulb-size-guide/
A free lookup table for every exterior and interior bulb size on the Fit, broken out by trim and model year.

**AutoPadre — Honda Fit battery and wiper blade sizes** — https://www.autopadre.com/battery-size/honda-fit and https://www.autopadre.com/wiper-size/honda-fit
Quick-reference pages for the correct replacement battery group size and wiper blade lengths for the Fit across its model years.

## Track / autocross organizations

**SCCA — National Solo Rules** — https://www.scca.com/downloads
The Sports Car Club of America's official rulebook for autocross ("Solo") competition, including the class structure a stock 2008 Fit would compete in.

**NASA — Club Codes and Regulations (CCR)** — https://nasa-assets.s3.amazonaws.com/document/document/282/2026.3.pdf
The National Auto Sport Association's official rulebook for HPDE (track day) and time-trial events, including safety-gear and tech-inspection requirements.

# Appendix I: Recall and TSB list

This appendix is the complete, standalone reference for every NHTSA safety recall and Technical Service Bulletin (TSB) confirmed for the 2007-2008 Honda Fit. A **recall** is a free, manufacturer-run repair program ordered after a safety-related defect is found in a group of vehicles. A **TSB** is a repair instruction a manufacturer sends to its own dealers about a known problem; it is not a safety recall and is not always a free repair. Always check your own VIN at a recall lookup tool (see Appendix H), since coverage can depend on where a car was sold or registered, and on whether a part was already replaced under an earlier campaign.

## All 8 confirmed NHTSA recalls at a glance

<!-- F-WP02-001, F-WP02-002, F-WP02-003, F-WP02-004, F-WP02-005, F-WP02-027, F-WP02-028, F-WP02-029 -->
A direct pull of NHTSA's own recalls database for model year 2008 Honda Fit returns exactly 8 campaigns.

| Campaign number | Recall began | Component | Fact ID |
|---|---|---|---|
| 07V549000 | December 12, 2007 | Frontal airbag ODS/OPDS wire harness corrosion (salt-belt states) | F-WP02-027 |
| 10V033000 | March 19, 2010 | Power window master switch water intrusion (1st campaign) | F-WP02-001 |
| 10V624000 | January 24, 2011 | Headlight low-beam wiring harness / combination switch | F-WP02-003 |
| 13V260000 | June 24, 2013 (approx.) | Power window master switch water intrusion (re-recall) | F-WP02-002 |
| 16V344000 | June 27, 2016 | Passenger frontal airbag inflator, Takata, original-equipment and replacement, Zone A/B/C | F-WP02-028 |
| 17V029000 | March 2017 | Passenger frontal airbag inflator, Takata, same defect family, later zone expansion | F-WP02-029 |
| 19V501000 | August 12, 2019 (approx.) | Passenger frontal airbag inflator, Takata, remedy-part-only | F-WP02-004 |
| 20V770000 | December 10, 2020 (approx.) | Drive shaft corrosion, manual transmission | F-WP02-005 |

## Recall details

### 07V549000 — Frontal airbag ODS/OPDS wire harness corrosion

<!-- F-WP02-027 -->
- **Recall began:** December 12, 2007
- **Applies to:** Certain 2007-2008 Fit vehicles originally sold, or currently registered, in 21 listed salt-belt states plus Washington, DC: Connecticut, Delaware, Illinois, Indiana, Iowa, Kentucky, Maine, Maryland, Massachusetts, Michigan, Minnesota, Missouri, New Hampshire, New Jersey, New York, Ohio, Pennsylvania, Rhode Island, Vermont, Virginia, West Virginia, and Wisconsin.
- **Component:** Frontal airbags — Occupant Detection System (ODS) / Occupant Position Detection System (OPDS) wire harness.
- **Defect:** The wire harness for the front passenger ODS/OPDS is routed underneath the carpet on the driver's side floorboard. In salt-belt areas, road salt tracked in on a driver's shoes can melt and seep through the carpet, reaching the harness. If the harness corrodes and a wire breaks, the SRS indicator light comes on, and the ODS/OPDS may fail to detect a child seat or an out-of-position passenger, so the passenger's frontal and side airbags will not be suppressed when they should be.
- **Consequence:** In a crash, a deploying front passenger airbag or side airbag increases the risk of injury to small or out-of-position occupants.
- **Remedy:** Dealers inspect and, if necessary, replace the wire harness, free of charge. Contact Honda at 1-800-999-1009.

### 10V033000 — Power window master switch water intrusion (1st campaign)

<!-- F-WP02-001 -->
- **Recall began:** March 19, 2010 (Honda recall no. R28)
- **Applies to:** Certain model year 2007-2008 Fit, approximately 141,140 units sold in the US. This is an all-trims recall, not base-only.
- **Component:** Power window master switch, driver's door.
- **Defect:** Under severe conditions, rain water or spilled liquid entering through an open driver's window can reach the power window master switch and damage its printed circuit board. A damaged circuit board can short and overheat the switch connectors, which can melt and, in rare cases, produce smoke or fire.
- **Remedy:** Dealer inspects the switch and installs a cover around the switch housing to prevent water entry; if the switch is already damaged, it is replaced and a cover installed — free of charge. Contact Honda at 1-800-999-1009.

### 10V624000 — Headlight low-beam wiring harness / combination switch

<!-- F-WP02-003 -->
- **Recall began:** January 24, 2011
- **Applies to:** 143,083 model year 2007-2008 Fit vehicles in the US. All-trims recall.
- **Component:** Exterior lighting — headlights, high/low beam dimmer (combination) switch and its wiring harness.
- **Defect:** An installation error in the wiring harness for the combination switch caused the low-beam circuit wires to be pulled tighter than intended. Repeated use of the headlight switch can wear the electrical connector terminal for the low-beam circuit, lowering conductivity and letting copper oxide build up at the contacts.
- **Consequence:** Low-beam headlights can become inoperative, reducing the driver's visibility and the vehicle's visibility to other traffic, increasing crash risk.
- **Remedy:** Dealer inspects the low-beam terminal and performs the necessary repair, free of charge. Contact Honda at 1-800-999-1009.

### 13V260000 — Power window master switch water intrusion (re-recall)

<!-- F-WP02-002 -->
- **Recall began:** Approximately June 24, 2013; Honda's own recall notice states that by October 18, 2013, replacement parts were in stock in sufficient quantity to complete repairs on all affected units.
- **Applies to:** Certain model year 2007-2008 Fit. All-trims recall.
- **Component:** Power window master switch, driver's door — the same switch as 10V033000.
- **Defect:** Honda determined the 2010 remedy was not sufficient to prevent water intrusion into the switch. Under severe conditions, water can still reach the switch and impair its function, and a damaged switch can overheat and cause smoke, melting, or fire.
- **Remedy:** Dealer replaces the power window master switch, free of charge, using an updated part.

### 16V344000 — Passenger frontal airbag inflator, Takata, original-equipment and replacement, Zone A/B/C

<!-- F-WP02-028 -->
- **Recall began:** June 27, 2016 (Honda numbers KA0, KA1, KA2, KA3)
- **Applies to:** Certain 2007-2008 Fit vehicles, among many other Honda/Acura models, in a geographic Zone A/B/C recall — the 2007-2008 Fit is named in every zone, though exact notification timing is zone-dependent. This campaign covers **both** original-equipment-installed and replacement passenger frontal airbag inflators.
- **Component:** Airbags, frontal, passenger side, inflator module.
- **Defect:** Passenger frontal airbag inflators, whether original equipment or replacement, may rupture due to propellant degradation after long-term exposure to absolute humidity and temperature cycling.
- **Consequence:** An inflator rupture may send metal fragments toward vehicle occupants, causing serious injury or death.
- **Remedy:** Dealers replace the passenger frontal airbag inflator, free of charge. Contact Honda customer service at 1-888-234-2138.

### 17V029000 — Passenger frontal airbag inflator, Takata, later zone expansion

<!-- F-WP02-029 -->
- **Recall began:** March 2017 (Honda numbers KD9, KE0)
- **Applies to:** Certain 2007-2008 Fit vehicles, among other Honda/Acura models, in a later geographic Zone A/B/C expansion of the same defect family as 16V344000.
- **Component:** Airbags, frontal, passenger side, inflator module.
- **Defect:** Same as 16V344000 — passenger frontal airbag inflators, original equipment or replacement, may rupture due to propellant degradation from long-term exposure to absolute humidity and temperature cycling.
- **Consequence:** An inflator rupture may send metal fragments toward vehicle occupants, causing serious injury or death.
- **Remedy:** Dealers replace the front passenger airbag inflator, free of charge. Contact Honda customer service at 1-888-234-2138.

### 19V501000 — Passenger frontal airbag inflator, Takata, remedy-part-only

<!-- F-WP02-004 -->
- **Recall began:** On or about August 12, 2019 (Honda internal numbers E5D and X5C)
- **Applies to:** Certain model year 2007-2008 Fit vehicles specifically equipped with a passenger frontal airbag inflator that was itself installed as a recall remedy or replacement service part — in other words, a Fit that already had its original passenger airbag inflator swapped under an earlier Takata campaign.
- **Component:** Passenger frontal airbag inflator.
- **Defect:** These replacement/remedy inflators may explode due to propellant (phase-stabilized ammonium nitrate) degradation from long-term exposure to high absolute humidity, high temperature, and temperature cycling — the same underlying defect mechanism as the original mass recall, but affecting the second-round replacement parts.
- **Consequence:** An inflator explosion can send sharp metal fragments toward the driver or other occupants, risking serious injury or death.
- **Remedy:** Dealer replaces the passenger frontal airbag inflator again, free of charge. Contact Honda customer service at 1-888-234-2138.

### 20V770000 — Drive shaft corrosion, manual transmission

<!-- F-WP02-005 -->
- **Recall began:** American Honda notified NHTSA on or about December 10, 2020.
- **Applies to:** 2007-2008 Fit vehicles with a **manual transmission** (nationwide), plus 2009-2013 Fit vehicles with a manual transmission in specific salt-belt states or with a salt-belt registration history. Automatic-transmission 2008 Fits are not affected by this recall.
- **Component:** Powertrain — driveline — drive shaft.
- **Defect:** The protective coating on certain drive shafts may not have been applied properly during manufacturing, making the shaft more susceptible to corrosion from road salt or other contaminants; a sufficiently corroded shaft can break.
- **Consequence:** A broken drive shaft can cause a sudden loss of drive power.
- **Remedy:** Dealer inspects the drive shaft(s) and replaces them if necessary, free of charge.

## Recall-sounding campaigns checked and confirmed to NOT apply to the 2008 Fit

<!-- F-WP02-006, F-WP02-007 -->
It is easy to see "Honda Fit" in a recall name and assume your 2008 car is covered. These two campaigns were checked directly and do **not** apply to the 2008 model year:

| Campaign number | Component | Why it does not apply |
|---|---|---|
| 18V268000 | Front passenger airbag module, incorrect installation | Names only 2007 and 2009-2013 Fit; 2008 is not listed. |
| 19V182000 | Driver frontal airbag inflator, Takata-related replacement part | Names only "2007 Fit" in its affected-vehicle list; 2008 is not present. (The 2008 Fit's passenger-side inflator is covered separately — see 19V501000, 16V344000, and 17V029000 above — but its driver-side inflator is not.) |

## Technical Service Bulletins (TSBs)

<!-- F-WP02-008, F-WP02-009, F-WP02-010 -->
Two TSBs are confirmed applicable to the 2007-2008 Fit:

| Bulletin number | Date | Title | Component |
|---|---|---|---|
| 14-058 | Approx. September 23, 2014 | Warranty Extension: Electric Power Steering With DTC 32-09 Or 61-04 | Electric power steering (EPS) system |
| 13-021 | Version 3, September 16, 2015 | Rocker arm oil pressure switch causing Malfunction Indicator Lamp with DTC P2646/P2647/P2651/P2652 | Rocker arm oil pressure switch |

**14-058 — Electric power steering warranty extension.** Explicitly names the 2006-2009 Honda Civic and 2007-2008 Fit. American Honda extended the EPS warranty to 10 years from the original purchase date or 150,000 miles, whichever comes first, for vehicles with a stored diagnostic trouble code 32-09 (EPS current sensor) and/or 61-04 (open or short in the EPS motor harness). The bulletin describes the symptom as steering that feels heavier than normal or is hard to turn, particularly when the vehicle is not moving.

**13-021 — Rocker arm oil pressure switch.** The bulletin's "AFFECTED VEHICLES" table lists "2007-11 Fit — ALL — ALL" as one of several rows in a multi-model bulletin. It addresses an intermittently failing rocker arm oil pressure switch that causes the Malfunction Indicator Lamp to illuminate with DTC P2646/P2651 (circuit low voltage) or P2647/P2652 (circuit high voltage); the bulletin notes the symptom may be intermittent and may not be reproducible after clearing the code. The corrective action is to replace the rocker arm oil pressure switch (Honda part number 37250-PNE-G01) and its O-ring (part number 91319-PAA-A01).

**Total TSB count.** We could not confirm this for the 2008 Fit. Check your owner's manual, or ask a Honda dealer, for a full list of every bulletin issued for your specific car. Different aggregator sites reported conflicting totals (19 versus approximately 42), likely because they count bulletins differently, and neither count could be independently confirmed.

## How to check for new recalls and TSBs yourself

Use the NHTSA vehicle lookup tool listed in Appendix H to search by your VIN or by year/make/model. This is the fastest way to see whether a new recall or TSB has been issued since this book was written, and it also shows owner-submitted complaints and formal government investigations, which can reveal problems before they turn into an official recall.
